313A Answer Keys and Explanations

Numbered to match the practice tests in your printed study guide.

Practice Test 1 Answer Key

  1. The correct answer is D (To confirm every physical field point communicates correctly and matches its BAS graphic label): Point to point verification confirms each physical sensor, actuator, and status point in the field actually communicates with the BAS and is correctly labeled and mapped on the graphics, which must be confirmed before any functional sequence testing can be considered valid. It has nothing to do with calculating equipment purchase costs. It does not calculate energy consumption, which is a separate performance analysis task. It is unrelated to cosmetic decisions such as mechanical room paint colour.
  2. The correct answer is C (Push-pull recovery, moving liquid refrigerant using the pressure difference between vessels): Push-pull recovery uses the pressure differential between the system and the recovery cylinder to move liquid refrigerant quickly, which is well suited to systems holding a large liquid charge. Option A describes a different method that relies on the appliance's own compressor rather than a pressure differential. Option B describes vapor-only recovery, which is slower for large liquid charges. Option D describes standard self-contained recovery, which does not specifically use the push-pull pressure differential technique.
  3. The correct answer is A (Evaporative condenser, combining a wetted coil with forced air for lower condensing temperature): an evaporative condenser sprays recirculated water over the condenser coil while a fan draws air across it, using the evaporative cooling effect to achieve a lower condensing temperature than a dry air-cooled unit while using substantially less water than a once-through water-cooled system. A water-cooled shell and tube condenser using a once-through supply consumes far more water. An air-cooled condenser relies only on dry bulb temperature, and a cascade condenser links two separate refrigerant systems rather than addressing water use.
  4. The correct answer is B (A resistance reading far lower than the winding's normal rated resistance value): A shorted winding has some of its coil turns bypassed by unintended contact, so it measures noticeably lower resistance than the manufacturer's rated value for that winding, though not necessarily zero. Infinity indicates an open winding with no continuity at all. A reading matching the rated value indicates a healthy winding rather than a fault. Zero resistance to the compressor shell describes a grounded winding, a different fault tested between a terminal and the case, not between two winding terminals.
  5. The correct answer is C (there is no significant standing leak detectable by the pressure decay method used): holding a system at a set nitrogen test pressure and watching for pressure loss over time is a standard method to confirm the piping and joints do not have a leak large enough to bleed off pressure in that period. A steady gauge reading over the hold time supports that conclusion for the test performed. This pressure test does not remove moisture from the system, which is the separate job of evacuation, and it says nothing about compressor mechanical condition under running load, since the compressor is not operating during a static nitrogen hold.
  6. The correct answer is A (Throttle the near balancing valves first, then proceed toward the farthest circuit last): Hydronic proportional balancing works from the circuit closest to the pump outward, since throttling the near circuits pushes more available flow toward the circuits farther down the loop. Adjusting only the far circuit without touching the near ones ignores the actual cause of the imbalance. Working systematically from near to far, then rechecking, produces a properly balanced loop rather than a temporary fix.
  7. The correct answer is C (The oversized line holds more liquid volume, increasing the estimated required charge): a liquid line with a larger internal diameter than the manufacturer's recommended size holds a greater volume of liquid refrigerant per metre of length, so the design-stage charge estimate must account for this additional volume rather than using the standard sized-line charge allowance. Oversizing does not reduce required charge, since the extra internal volume must still be filled. The effect is not limited to the discharge line, since liquid line diameter directly determines held liquid volume in that section.
  8. The correct answer is D (Wearing a properly fitted respirator rated for metal fume exposure): cadmium bearing filler rods release toxic metal fumes when heated, and inhaling them can cause acute lung injury or long term kidney damage, so a fit tested respirator rated for metal fumes is the required control when adequate ventilation is not achievable. Safety glasses in A protect the eyes from splatter but do nothing for inhaled fumes. Steel toed boots in C and a cotton shirt in D address impact and thermal burn hazards, which are real concerns during brazing but do not control the inhalation hazard described in this scenario.
  9. The correct answer is C (Compressed air contains oxygen and moisture that risk explosion and corrosion): Compressed shop air mixed with residual refrigerant oil inside the piping creates a real combustion hazard under pressure, and the moisture typically present in shop air lines promotes internal corrosion. Dry nitrogen is inert, moisture-free, and available at cylinder pressures well above typical refrigerant system test pressures, making it the standard and code-required medium for pressure testing.
  10. The correct answer is B (Approximately 8 ohms, dividing 24 volts by 3 amps): resistance equals voltage divided by current, so R = 24V ÷ 3A = 8Ω, meaning the total circuit resistance must stay at or above this value to keep current at or below the fuse rating. If the combined coil and wiring resistance drops below this figure, perhaps from a partial short, the fuse will open to protect the transformer and wiring. This calculation is a useful sanity check when a control fuse repeatedly fails.
  11. The correct answer is B (The blue hose is connected to the low pressure side): standard manifold gauge sets use blue for the low side gauge and hose, red for the high side gauge and hose, and yellow or green as the center utility hose for charging or evacuating. The red hose described in option A is actually the high side connection, not the low side. Yellow in option C is reserved for the utility hose rather than either pressure side, and option D is incorrect because this color convention is a widely adopted industry standard, not an arbitrary manufacturer choice.
  12. The correct answer is D (A2L refrigerants carry flammability risk that changes ventilation and detection requirements under CSA B52): A2L refrigerants carry flammability risk that changes ventilation and detection requirements under CSA B52. A2L refrigerants are classified as lower flammability, which triggers additional CSA B52 provisions beyond what applies to a non-flammable A1 refrigerant, including refrigerant detection, ignition source separation, and ventilation sized with flammability in mind rather than toxicity alone. Simply reusing an A1-based ventilation calculation ignores this classification difference and could leave the machine room non-compliant and unsafe. Machine room requirements in B52 apply broadly across refrigerant classes, not only to ammonia, so this reasoning also misstates the scope of the code.
  13. The correct answer is B (The metered state point, since it shows the lowest listed refrigerant temperature.): The diagram shows the metered state at approximately -10°C, the lowest temperature of the four labeled points, because the pressure drop across the metering device causes some liquid to flash into vapour and cool the remaining refrigerant sharply. This cold, low pressure mixture then enters the evaporator coil where it absorbs heat from the conditioned space. The suction point at 2°C is warmer because the refrigerant has already picked up superheat while traveling through the evaporator and suction line. Discharge and liquid are both high pressure, high temperature points found after the compressor and condenser stages.
  14. The correct answer is A (The suction line is severely restricted or the charge is critically low): A compound gauge reading a vacuum on the low side while the compressor runs indicates the compressor is pulling harder than the system can supply, which happens when a restriction blocks refrigerant flow or the charge is so low there is almost no vapour returning. Option B describes overcharging, which would instead push the low side pressure higher than normal, not into a vacuum. Option C would isolate the gauge from the system rather than produce a running vacuum reading. Option D would raise, not lower, the suction pressure by dumping hot discharge gas backward.
  15. The correct answer is D (The capacity equals approximately 5 tons of refrigeration, or about 17.6 kilowatts.): One ton of refrigeration is defined as 12,000 BTU per hour, so dividing 60,000 BTU per hour by 12,000 gives exactly 5 tons of cooling capacity. Converting tons to kilowatts uses the standard factor of 3.517 kilowatts per ton, so multiplying 5 tons by 3.517 gives approximately 17.6 kilowatts. This conversion lets a technician compare imperial-rated equipment directly against metric specification sheets without guessing at an approximate value. Getting this conversion correct matters when sizing a replacement unit, since an undersized system will fail to maintain the required box temperature.
  16. The correct answer is A (The time delay relay must finish timing before its contact closes): the two rungs are linked only through the timing relay itself, since the Y contact energizes the timer coil but the contactor coil depends on a separate contact that only closes once the relay finishes its timed delay. This staged design deliberately delays compressor start, whether for anti-short-cycle protection or a controlled startup sequence. The contactor cannot pull in on the instant the thermostat calls, and neither the high pressure switch nor the fan motor appear in this particular timing path.
  17. The correct answer is A (It sets a phase-down schedule that reduces the production and import of HFC refrigerants over time): The Kigali Amendment extends the Montreal Protocol, which originally addressed ozone-depleting substances, by adding a scheduled phase-down of high-global-warming-potential HFC refrigerants. Canada is a party to this amendment, so future HFC availability and pricing will tighten on the published schedule. Option B is false because no immediate blanket ban exists. Option C is false because CEPA 1999 remains Canada's domestic legal authority, and option D misstates who the amendment applies to.
  18. The correct answer is C (24 volts alternating current, the industry standard control voltage): control transformers step incoming line voltage down to a nominal 24 volts AC, which is the accepted industry standard for thermostat and control circuits across residential and light commercial refrigeration and air conditioning equipment. This lower voltage is considered safer for field wiring and thermostat handling than full line voltage. Contactor coils, relays, and most control components are selected specifically to match this 24 volt rating.
  19. The correct answer is C (Applying bubble solution to the joint and watching for forming bubbles): brushing or spraying bubble solution directly onto a suspected joint is a simple, low cost method that shows a visible stream of bubbles forming exactly where refrigerant is escaping under pressure. Wrapping the joint in foil, as in option A, does nothing to reveal a leak and is not a recognized detection method. Plain water in option B will not reliably show a small leak through bubbling or temperature change, and a stethoscope in option D is more suited to detecting mechanical noise than a small refrigerant leak.
  20. The correct answer is B (Force outdoor air conditions in the BAS point override and observe the damper's actual response): Sequence verification requires actively forcing or simulating the trigger condition, such as an outdoor air temperature point override, through the BAS and confirming the damper physically responds as the written sequence describes. Relying on a building operator's memory does not confirm the actual programmed logic and physical response. A damper being physically installed says nothing about whether its control logic actually functions correctly. Reading the written specification alone confirms design intent but not that the installed and programmed system actually behaves that way in the field.
  21. The correct answer is B (The contact is normally closed and opens when its coil is energized): the diagonal line drawn through the contact symbol is the standard convention indicating a normally closed contact, meaning current flows through it at rest and it opens when its associated coil or device energizes. This is commonly used for safety contacts, such as a switch that must open to interrupt a circuit when an unsafe condition occurs. A plain gap without the diagonal line represents a normally open contact instead. Misreading this symbol can lead a technician to expect the opposite behavior from the actual wired circuit.
  22. The correct answer is D (Replace the capacitor, since bulging and leakage indicate internal failure risk): a bulged case and leaking residue are visible signs that the internal dielectric material has broken down, often from overheating, age, or an internal short, and the capacitor can fail completely or even rupture further during operation. This is a clear physical indicator of failure that does not require electrical testing to confirm before replacement is warranted. The replacement capacitor should match the original microfarad and voltage rating, not an increased microfarad value, since the wrong rating can itself cause motor problems or repeat failures.
  23. The correct answer is C (The technician should select a polyol ester oil since it mixes well with HFC refrigerants.): Polyol ester, or POE, oil was specifically developed to provide good miscibility with HFC refrigerants such as R-410A, which do not mix well with the mineral oils traditionally used with older CFC and HCFC refrigerants. Good miscibility ensures oil returns properly to the compressor along with the circulating refrigerant rather than pooling in the evaporator or suction lines and starving the compressor of lubrication. Mineral oil and HFC refrigerants can separate into distinct layers rather than forming a stable mixture, which is why using the wrong oil type is a common and serious service error. Manufacturer service literature always specifies POE oil compatibility for HFC-charged compressors like the one in this system.
  24. The correct answer is A (Recycling, an on-site process that reduces contaminants but does not meet reclamation purity.): Recycling reduces moisture, acidity, and particulate contamination through devices like filter driers and multiple passes, but it is performed on-site without laboratory verification against AHRI Standard 700. Reclamation requires chemical analysis and processing at a licensed off-site facility before the refrigerant can be resold as new-equivalent product. Recovery is simply the removal of refrigerant into a cylinder without any contaminant reduction at all.
  25. The correct answer is D (Compare the suction pressure and superheat reading against the manufacturer's target charging chart): on a fixed-orifice system, suction pressure alone never confirms a correct charge because ambient conditions shift the saturation point constantly. The technician must pair the suction pressure with the measured suction line temperature to calculate superheat and compare it to the manufacturer's target. Declaring the charge complete on pressure alone, swapping to discharge pressure, or equalizing high and low side pressure while running are all incorrect and can leave the system critically undercharged or overcharged.
  26. The correct answer is D (The reversing valve is not fully shifting to the defrost position.): During a proper defrost cycle the discharge pressure should climb as hot gas is directed to the frosted outdoor coil to melt the ice, so an abnormally low discharge pressure reading during defrost indicates the reversing valve has not fully shifted and hot gas is only partially reaching the outdoor coil. This explains why the frost is failing to melt even though the cycle has been initiated. Normal defrost progression would show a rising, elevated discharge pressure rather than this abnormally low reading, and overcharge or excess indoor airflow would not produce this specific low pressure pattern during defrost.
  27. The correct answer is C (Residual refrigerant lingering in the surrounding air is triggering false alarms): opening a system releases some refrigerant into the surrounding air, and a sensitive electronic detector can pick up that ambient concentration everywhere the probe passes, producing alarms at fittings that are not actually leaking. Option A does not match the scenario since an empty system would not still be producing detector alarms at all. Option B describes normal detector function and does not explain the pattern of widespread false alarms, and option D is incorrect because leak detectors respond to refrigerant concentration in air, not directly to system pressure.
  28. The correct answer is A (Approximately 30°C, matching the value listed for R-404A at that pressure): A standard R-404A pressure-temperature chart lists approximately 1408 kPa gauge as the saturation pressure corresponding to a 30°C condensing temperature. R-404A operates at noticeably higher pressures than R-134a at a comparable temperature, though R-410A actually runs at even higher pressures than R-404A for the same temperature, which is exactly why service equipment and gauges must always be rated for the specific refrigerant in the system rather than assumed interchangeable. Confirming this saturation temperature is a necessary step before the technician can calculate subcooling using the measured liquid line temperature.
  29. The correct answer is B (The sensible heat ratio for this space is approximately 0.80.): Sensible heat ratio is calculated by dividing the sensible heat gain by the total heat gain, which is the sum of sensible and latent heat combined. Dividing 8 kW of sensible heat by the total of 10 kW, the sum of 8 kW sensible plus 2 kW latent, gives a sensible heat ratio of 0.80. This value tells equipment selectors that 80 percent of the cooling load is temperature-related while only 20 percent involves removing moisture from the air. Selecting equipment with a matching sensible heat ratio ensures the system removes heat and moisture in proportions that actually match the space's real load, avoiding an overly humid or an overly dry conditioned space.
  30. The correct answer is C (A2L refrigerant flammability requires greater separation from ignition sources than a non-flammable A1 refrigerant): because A2L refrigerant carries a mild flammability rating, CSA B52 requires additional consideration of clearance from ignition sources such as electrical panels and from sensitive locations such as a mechanical room air intake, which a non-flammable A1 refrigerant installation does not require to the same degree. Applying identical A1 clearance rules to an A2L installation overlooks this real flammability hazard and could place equipment closer to an ignition source than the code allows for that refrigerant class. Rooftop location alone does not exempt an installation from flammability related clearance requirements. Ignition source proximity is a genuine concern for A2L refrigerants specifically, not only for the more flammable A3 classification.
  31. The correct answer is D (Position the unit so service access space is reserved on the side with valves and disconnects): planning the layout to deliberately reserve clear working space adjacent to the valves, gauge ports, and electrical disconnects ensures a technician can safely perform future service without removing adjacent equipment or working in an unsafe confined position. Placing the unit tight against a wall on all sides eliminates this necessary access. Assuming leftover space will be adequate leaves service access to chance rather than deliberate planning, and shipping crate dimensions do not reflect the operational clearance needed for ongoing maintenance.
  32. The correct answer is D (Whether the confined space has adequate ventilation to prevent vapour accumulation.): Because R-32 is mildly flammable, charging it inside a small, poorly ventilated mechanical closet raises the risk of flammable vapour accumulating to a concentration that could ignite if a leak occurs during the procedure. The technician must evaluate whether adequate ventilation exists, and improve it if necessary, before beginning work in that confined space. Sealing the door for insulation, shelf space, and operating noise are unrelated to the flammability hazard this scenario presents.
  33. The correct answer is B (Fully back-seated, which closes the gauge port and allows full refrigerant flow.): Turning a two-valve service stem all the way back, toward the back seat, opens a full, unrestricted path for refrigerant through the valve body while simultaneously closing off the small side gauge port, which is the correct position for normal running operation. Cracking the stem off the back seat, called mid-seating, opens the gauge port slightly so pressure readings can be taken while flow still passes through. Front-seating closes the main refrigerant path entirely for isolation, the opposite of normal running position.
  34. The correct answer is C (An anti-short-cycle delay, used to protect the compressor from rapid restarts): this time delay relay holds the contactor coil circuit open for a set period, typically several minutes, after a call for cooling drops out or power is interrupted, allowing internal pressures to equalize before the compressor is permitted to restart. Restarting a compressor against high head pressure can cause excessive current draw and mechanical strain on the motor and valves. This delay is a standard protective feature on most modern compressors.
  35. The correct answer is B (The vapour temperature rises above the saturation temperature, producing measurable superheat): Once all of the liquid refrigerant has boiled away, any further heat absorbed by the vapour causes its actual temperature to climb above the saturation temperature for that pressure, and this rise above saturation is what superheat measures. This superheated vapour then travels through the suction line back to the compressor. Subcooling is a liquid line concept that occurs after the condenser, not inside the evaporator.
  36. The correct answer is D (The flow rate through the coil is too high relative to the load, reducing delta T): A delta T that reads well below the design value, with the load otherwise similar to design conditions, commonly indicates the water flow rate through the coil is higher than intended relative to the actual heat transfer occurring. This can point toward an oversized pump, an open bypass, or a balancing valve that is not properly set. Replacing the chiller compressor based on a single delta T reading, without further investigation, would be premature and is not supported by this finding alone.
  37. The correct answer is A (Real compression adds mechanical work as heat, which increases the refrigerant's enthalpy during the process.): The compressor does mechanical work on the refrigerant vapour, and that work energy is converted into additional heat within the vapour itself, raising both its pressure and its enthalpy as it travels from the suction state to the discharge state. A perfectly vertical line would represent an idealized process where enthalpy never changes, which does not match real compressor behavior. This rightward shift on the diagram is exactly why the discharge vapour leaves the compressor noticeably hotter than a simple pressure increase alone would explain. Recognizing this real, non-vertical compression line helps a technician understand why discharge temperature readings run higher than the saturated condensing temperature would suggest.
  38. The correct answer is C (Item 3, the electrical panel deficiency, since it carries the highest listed priority rating): Item 3 is the only entry rated High priority on the exhibit, meaning it should be addressed before the two Low priority items and the one Medium priority item. Item 1's listing position on the page has no bearing on urgency, since the exhibit rates it Low priority. Item 4 is also rated Low priority regardless of how many people pass through the corridor. Item 2 is rated Medium priority, which ranks below the electrical panel deficiency's High rating.
  39. The correct answer is D (A1 means the refrigerant is nonflammable and has low acute toxicity to workers.): ASHRAE Standard 34 assigns a letter for toxicity and a number for flammability, where A indicates lower toxicity and 1 indicates no flame propagation under standard test conditions. R-410A carries this A1 rating, which is why standard non-flammable-rated recovery cylinders and gauges can be used without the additional precautions required for A2L refrigerants. This is different from newer lower-GWP refrigerants such as R-32 and R-454B, which are rated A2L for mild flammability. Understanding the classification on the cylinder label directly affects what tools, ventilation, and ignition source precautions are required on site.
  40. The correct answer is C (It prevents liquid refrigerant and oil from draining back into the evaporator): During the off cycle, gravity would otherwise pull refrigerant and oil in the vertical riser back down into the evaporator coil. A trap at the base of the riser catches that drainage in a small pocket, and on the next start-up the gas velocity sweeps the trapped oil upward with the refrigerant instead of leaving it stranded low in the system.
  41. The correct answer is D (Shine an ultraviolet lamp along suspected joints and look for a glowing dye trace): Fluorescent leak detection dye circulates with the refrigerant oil and leaks out at the exact leak point, where it fluoresces brightly under an ultraviolet lamp even in conditions where air movement defeats an electronic sniffer. Soap solution has no chemical role in making the dye glow, since fluorescence requires UV light. The dye is designed to persist at the leak site, not evaporate away before it can be found. Deliberately overcharging a system is unsafe and unnecessary since the correct charge already carries enough dye to be detected.
  42. The correct answer is B (A solenoid valve, electrically actuated to stop flow when the compressor stops): a solenoid valve is drawn as a rectangle with a coil symbol beside the line because it is electrically operated, and it is commonly wired to close automatically when the compressor cycles off, preventing liquid refrigerant from migrating into the evaporator during the off cycle. A check valve is mechanically actuated by pressure differential rather than electrically controlled. A pressure regulating valve maintains a set pressure during operation rather than stopping flow on shutdown, and a sight glass provides only a visual indication with no valve function.
  43. The correct answer is D (The unit will short cycle and fail to properly dehumidify the space): a condensing unit sized at nearly double the actual calculated load will satisfy the thermostat very quickly and shut off before running long enough to remove adequate moisture from the air, producing frequent short cycling and poor humidity control. Suction pressure reaching design levels is not the primary concern with oversizing in this scenario. Discharge line velocity exceeding code limits is a piping sizing issue, not a direct result of oversizing the compressor itself, and coil icing is not a guaranteed outcome of oversizing alone.
  44. The correct answer is D (Non-condensable air trapped in the system alongside the refrigerant charge): With a clean, unobstructed condenser and a normal condenser fan, airflow restriction and fouling are ruled out as the cause of elevated head pressure. Air trapped in the system occupies space in the condenser and adds its own partial pressure on top of the refrigerant's condensing pressure, raising discharge pressure while suction pressure stays close to normal. A restricted filter drier or a leaking reversing valve would instead show abnormal suction pressure or reduced discharge pressure, not this specific pattern. The system needs to be evacuated properly and recharged to remove the trapped air.
  45. The correct answer is D (The dye can take time to circulate and reach the leak location): after injection, the dye needs the system to run long enough for it to circulate with the oil and refrigerant and actually reach the point of the leak, so an inspection performed too soon may show nothing even though dye was added. Option A is false because dye alone does not quantify a leak rate, it only helps locate the leak visually. Option B is incorrect since circulation is not instantaneous, and option C is wrong because a UV light source is still required to make the dye fluoresce and become visible.
  46. The correct answer is C (Sensible heat changes dry bulb temperature, while latent heat changes moisture content only): sensible heat is heat that raises or lowers dry bulb air temperature through conduction, convection, and infiltration. Latent heat is the heat absorbed or released when moisture changes state, such as when water vapor condenses on a coil, and it does not itself change dry bulb temperature. A cooling load calculation must total both components separately before they are combined, since equipment must be sized to handle each. Confusing the two leads to undersized dehumidification capacity even when total kW appears adequate.
  47. The correct answer is D (The system has excess resistance somewhere in the airstream, reducing delivered airflow below design): A static pressure reading above design combined with airflow below design indicates the fan is working against more resistance than planned, commonly from a dirty filter, closed damper, or coil fouling, which reduces the actual airflow it can deliver. Higher static pressure with lower airflow is the opposite of good performance, not confirmation of it. Jumping straight to motor replacement skips the logical troubleshooting step of first identifying the source of excess resistance. Disregarding the design value on the schedule is not justified simply because the field reading differs from it.
  48. The correct answer is B (The system is undercharged, since the expansion valve can mask low superheat as subcooling drops): A thermostatic expansion valve actively regulates flow to maintain target superheat even as charge drops, so superheat can look normal on an undercharged system for a while even as subcooling falls well below specification and suction pressure drifts slightly low from reduced mass flow. This is why subcooling is considered the more reliable charge indicator on a TXV system, while superheat is more reliable on a fixed orifice system that has no active flow control. Overcharge would instead tend to raise subcooling and head pressure rather than lowering them as seen here.
  49. The correct answer is C (Recover the remaining refrigerant into another approved cylinder before scrapping the damaged one.): Even a damaged cylinder that can no longer be safely requalified must have any residual refrigerant recovered into another approved cylinder before it is scrapped, since deliberate venting remains prohibited regardless of the cylinder's own condition. Assuming pressure will simply dissipate ignores that the refrigerant itself does not disappear on its own. Residual refrigerant in damaged equipment is not exempt from recovery requirements just because the container itself is being retired.
  50. The correct answer is A (Their certification is recognized without having to write another provincial exam): the entire purpose of the Red Seal endorsement is to certify that a tradesperson has met a common interprovincial standard, so a technician holding it can have their qualification recognized in the new province without repeating the certification exam process. Option B contradicts the fundamental labour mobility purpose of the Red Seal program. Option C incorrectly assumes a partial re-examination is required, which is not how Red Seal recognition works. Option D is also incorrect because the endorsement supports mobility for provincially regulated trades work generally, not only federally regulated sites.
  51. The correct answer is A (Apply the manufacturer's effective area factor and take multiple readings across the face): A vane anemometer reads the velocity of air moving through its rotor, not the true volume of air leaving the grille, so the reading must be corrected using the grille's effective area factor. Taking several readings across the grille face and averaging them accounts for uneven airflow patterns. Skipping the correction factor or taking only one reading produces a CFM value that does not match reality.
  52. The correct answer is A (The contents are under pressure and may explode or cause frostbite if released): the gas cylinder pictogram under WHMIS 2015 specifically warns that a product is a compressed, liquefied, or dissolved gas that can rupture its container under heat or physical damage, and rapid release of liquid refrigerant can also cause frostbite injuries. R-410A is non-flammable under normal conditions, so the flame pictogram in option B does not apply. The corrosion pictogram in C and the health hazard pictogram in D describe different classes of product entirely and would appear on chemicals like acids or carcinogens, not on a standard refrigerant cylinder.
  53. The correct answer is B (Discharge pressure rises because reduced airflow limits heat rejection from the coil): A condenser coil rejects heat from the refrigerant to outdoor air, so any restriction to that airflow forces the refrigerant to condense at a higher pressure and temperature to reject the same heat load. Debris packed into the fins acts exactly like a fan failure in reducing effective airflow across the coil. This is one of the three classic causes of elevated discharge pressure, alongside overcharge and non-condensable gases. Cleaning the coil restores normal airflow and returns discharge pressure to the expected range for the outdoor temperature.
  54. The correct answer is A (The G contact must close to energize the fan relay coil first): the fan motor rung depends entirely on the fan relay contact, and that contact only closes once the fan relay coil is energized on the separate rung above it, which itself only happens when the G contact closes. This two-stage arrangement lets a low current thermostat signal safely control a higher current fan motor circuit through the relay. The fan motor cannot start on its own rung until this upstream condition is satisfied, and the contactor coil for the compressor is a completely separate circuit not linked to this fan path.
  55. The correct answer is C (The evaporator and the condenser, both constant-pressure heat exchange components): Heat is absorbed in the evaporator and rejected in the condenser, and both processes occur at essentially constant pressure across the coil. The compressor raises pressure through mechanical work, while the metering device drops pressure through a restriction, so neither of those is a constant-pressure process. Mixing a pressure-changing component with a heat-exchanging one, as the other options do, misidentifies the cycle's structure.
  56. The correct answer is C (It sets requirements for refrigerant classification, machine room design, piping, and safety devices nationally): CSA B52 is the Canadian Mechanical Refrigeration Code, and it provides requirements covering refrigerant safety group classification, machine room construction and ventilation, piping design, and required safety devices across refrigeration systems generally, not just household appliances. It works alongside, rather than replacing, provincial building codes and pressure vessel regulations, which each retain their own jurisdiction. It also addresses far more than electrical wiring methods, covering the full mechanical design and installation of a system. Understanding this broad scope is essential before planning any new refrigeration installation in Canada.
  57. The correct answer is D (A lockout hasp or group lock box allowing each worker their own lock): group lockout procedures use a hasp or lock box so that every worker applies their own personal padlock to the isolation point, and the equipment cannot be reactivated until every single lock has been removed by its owner. Option A concentrates control in one person, which fails if that person leaves or forgets a worker is still present. Verbal agreement in option B provides no physical barrier against accidental re-energization, and sharing a single padlock in option C defeats the purpose of individual personal accountability.
  58. The correct answer is C (Radiation, since electromagnetic heat transfer needs no medium or direct contact): Radiant heat transfer travels as electromagnetic waves from a warm surface and can be felt at a short distance without any physical contact or airflow, which matches this scenario exactly. Conduction requires direct contact between materials and would not explain heat felt across an air gap. Vibration alone does not constitute forced convection, and latent heat describes energy tied to a phase change inside the refrigerant, not the warmth radiating from the outer shell.
  59. The correct answer is D (No voltage present at the relay's output terminal in low speed.): If the motor itself already proved good by running correctly in high speed, a missing voltage reading at the low speed relay's output terminal when it should be energized confirms the fault is inside that relay rather than the motor windings. This isolates the problem to the switching device controlling that specific speed tap. Full voltage at the motor, normal overheating behavior, or correct amperage in low speed would all indicate the relay and wiring path are functioning properly, which contradicts the reported failure to run.
  60. The correct answer is C (The reversing valve body is mechanically stuck despite correct electrical signal to the solenoid): Correct voltage confirmed at the solenoid whenever heating is called for rules out an electrical signal problem, so a valve that still fails to shift positions points to the valve body itself being mechanically stuck, often from internal debris or a worn slide, rather than an electrical fault. An open solenoid coil would show zero or abnormal voltage under load, which contradicts the confirmed correct voltage reading. A backward-wired thermostat would produce inappropriate calls for heating or cooling, not a valve that receives the correct call but fails to physically shift. Continuous cold air delivery in heating mode is a clear malfunction, not normal winter behavior.
  61. The correct answer is B (A low, gentle flow just enough to displace air without pressurizing the line): the nitrogen purge only needs to maintain a slow, steady flow through the piping during brazing so oxygen is displaced and internal scale formation is prevented, without building enough pressure to blow molten filler out of the joint. A high flow rate as in option A risks disturbing the braze joint itself and wastes gas. No flow as in option C defeats the entire purpose of purging during the heat application, and a pulsing flow as in option D would let air back into the line during each off cycle.
  62. The correct answer is C (Compression of refrigerant vapour taking place inside the compressor): The compression line moves upward because pressure increases and to the right because enthalpy increases as the compressor adds mechanical work to the refrigerant vapour, carrying it from the saturated vapour line into the superheated region. Heat rejection in the condenser instead plots as a mostly horizontal line moving left at constant high pressure. Flow through the metering device plots as a vertical line at constant enthalpy, and evaporator heat absorption plots as a horizontal line at constant low pressure.
  63. The correct answer is A (Evaporation of water from the wick absorbs heat and cools the bulb): As air moves across the dampened wick, water evaporates and draws latent heat from the bulb, which lowers the wet bulb reading below the dry bulb temperature by an amount that depends on how much moisture the air can still absorb. Option B is not a real mechanism since reflectivity has nothing to do with a psychrometer's operating principle. Option C describes a physical property that does not explain the temperature drop caused by evaporation. Option D is false because the wet bulb is deliberately exposed to airflow to promote evaporation.
  64. The correct answer is A (Ozone depletion potential, or ODP, and global warming potential, or GWP): ODP rates how much a released refrigerant can damage the stratospheric ozone layer compared to a reference substance, while GWP rates how much a released refrigerant contributes to atmospheric warming compared to an equal mass of carbon dioxide over a set time period. Neither rating has anything to do with equipment efficiency measurements such as COP, EER, or SEER, which instead describe how much cooling or heating output a system produces per unit of energy consumed. Lower ODP and GWP values are generally preferred when selecting a replacement refrigerant.
  65. The correct answer is B (Latent heat changes a substance's physical state while its temperature remains constant throughout.): Latent heat is the energy absorbed or released during a phase change, such as liquid refrigerant boiling into vapour in the evaporator, and it produces no thermometer reading change during the process. Sensible heat, by contrast, changes a substance's temperature without changing its state, such as vapour superheating after leaving the evaporator coil. Distinguishing the two is essential because a technician reading only a thermometer cannot tell whether heat added is doing useful cooling work through evaporation or simply raising vapour temperature. This distinction underlies why the evaporator can absorb large amounts of heat while its coil temperature stays nearly constant.
  66. The correct answer is B (A record of every deficiency found during testing that must be corrected before final acceptance): The deficiency list captures every item found during commissioning testing that does not yet meet design intent, so it can be tracked and resolved before the project is considered fully complete. It is a working punch list for the parties responsible for correcting issues, not a billing summary, marketing piece, or spare parts recommendation. A clear deficiency list keeps outstanding work visible until every item is closed out.
  67. The correct answer is B (A severely undercharged system tripping the low pressure switch repeatedly.): A rapid rise then rapid fall in suction pressure right after each start is consistent with a severely low charge that pulls suction pressure down fast enough to trip a low pressure safety switch, shutting the compressor off until pressure re-equalizes and it restarts. This repeating pattern matches classic undercharge-driven short cycling rather than a wiring or airflow issue. An oversized condenser, a correctly set thermostat anticipator, and a clean filter are all normal conditions that would not cause this cycling behavior.
  68. The correct answer is C (One-third to one-half full, and a low level often signals poor oil return.): Most reciprocating and scroll compressors are designed to run correctly with the sight glass showing roughly one-third to one-half full under normal operating conditions, allowing the manufacturer's crankcase reservoir to properly lubricate moving parts. A level that stays consistently low often points to poor oil return from the system, commonly caused by undersized piping, low refrigerant velocity, or a leak that has also let oil escape. A completely full or empty sight glass, or an overcharge assumption, does not match this standard interpretation.
  69. The correct answer is A (An uncalibrated gauge can drift and report a false superheat value that hides an incorrect charge): Digital gauge sets, like any measuring instrument, can drift out of accuracy over time and require periodic calibration verification against a known standard. A drifted gauge can display a superheat value that looks correct while the actual refrigerant charge is wrong, leading to poor system performance that goes undetected until later. This is exactly why calibration checks are part of the commissioning documentation process, not an optional formality.
  70. The correct answer is B (It pinpoints the exact location of a leak rather than just confirming one exists): A gauge showing pressure loss over time only tells the mechanic that a leak exists somewhere in the system, not where. Sweeping an electronic leak detector along each individual joint lets the technician isolate the exact fitting or braze that is leaking, so it can be reworked without guessing or retesting the entire piping run repeatedly.
  71. The correct answer is B (Mineral oil, since it has long served as the standard lubricant for R-22 systems): Mineral oil has traditionally been the standard lubricant for R-22 and other older HCFC refrigerants, since it is miscible with them and provides adequate lubrication and oil return under normal operating conditions. Alkylbenzene oil is sometimes blended with mineral oil to improve miscibility during certain retrofit situations, but it is not the original standard oil for R-22 itself. Polyol ester oil was instead developed later specifically to address the miscibility problems that arise when mineral oil is used with modern HFC refrigerants.
  72. The correct answer is D (The fan runs continuously whenever the unit has power, ignoring the thermostat): because the auxiliary contact rung is wired in parallel with the G contact rung, a permanently closed auxiliary contact provides a continuous path to the fan relay coil regardless of what the thermostat G terminal is doing. The G contact path still works normally, but it becomes irrelevant since the parallel path never opens. This fault typically shows up as an indoor fan that never shuts off, even with the thermostat fully satisfied.
  73. The correct answer is D (Each nitrogen break dilutes residual moisture and non condensables for easier removal.): each time the vacuum is broken with dry nitrogen, any residual moisture vapour or trapped non condensable gas becomes diluted within the added nitrogen, so the following evacuation removes a larger proportion of the original contaminant than a single continuous pull could achieve on its own. Repeating this cycle several times progressively reduces residual moisture more effectively than one long evacuation at the same total pump time. Triple evacuation does not replace pressure or leak testing, which must still be performed, and it is not inherently faster than a single pull, though it is more thorough.
  74. The correct answer is D (The depressor holds the Schrader core open, so air enters through a poorly purged hose): because a depressor-style fitting physically pushes the Schrader core open the moment it threads onto the service port, any air and moisture still sitting in an unpurged hose flows straight into the system with nothing blocking it, unlike a standard fitting where the core stays closed until the hose is connected and the valve deliberately opened. A depressor does not inherently damage a properly rated Schrader core through normal use, it does allow refrigerant flow once connected, and it does not perform recovery automatically without the technician operating the manifold valves.
  75. The correct answer is B (It delays compressor restart for a set number of minutes after any stop): It delays compressor restart for a set number of minutes after any stop. This dedicated anti-short-cycle timer enforces a fixed off period, typically around five minutes, every time the compressor stops, regardless of whether the stop was from a normal thermostat cycle or a pressure trip. This prevents the equalization delay problem entirely rather than relying only on pressure switches to catch high head pressure after the fact. It reduces mechanical and electrical stress from frequent short cycling that pressure switches alone would not prevent.
  76. The correct answer is C (Glide, which describes the temperature range between a blend's bubble point and dew point): Glide is the term for the temperature spread that a zeotropic blend passes through while changing state at a constant pressure, from its bubble point where the first vapour forms to its dew point where the last liquid disappears. Fractionation is a related but different concept, describing the components of a blend actually separating when the blend is removed unevenly as vapour rather than liquid. Superheat and subcooling are separate measurements taken after the refrigerant has already fully changed state in the evaporator or condenser.
  77. The correct answer is B (At least 22.2 kilonewtons, or 5,000 pounds, for each attached worker): Recognized fall protection standards require an anchor point to withstand at least 22.2 kilonewtons per worker attached to account for the dynamic forces generated during a fall arrest. Option A understates the true rating needed and would allow an anchor to be approved that cannot safely arrest a fall. Option C incorrectly suggests the rating can be divided among multiple workers rather than applied per person. Option D invents a lower figure that does not match any recognized fall protection anchor standard.
  78. The correct answer is A (A tracked list of outstanding items that must be corrected before final acceptance): A deficiency or punch list is a tracked record of outstanding items found during commissioning that must be corrected before the client can grant final acceptance of the completed work. It is not a general equipment inventory covering the whole site's installed equipment. It is not a marketing document meant to promote the system to future tenants. It is not a billing or invoicing document tracking labour hours.
  79. The correct answer is C (Three-phase power uses three hot conductors that are electrically out of phase): Three-phase power uses three hot conductors that are electrically out of phase. Each conductor is offset by 120 electrical degrees from the others, producing a rotating magnetic field that three-phase motors use directly. Single-phase power has only one alternating voltage waveform, which is why single-phase motors need starting components like capacitors. This phase relationship is the core reason three-phase motors run smoother and more efficiently under load.
  80. The correct answer is A (Motor speed is directly proportional to the frequency of the supplied power): Motor speed is directly proportional to the frequency of the supplied power. An induction motor's synchronous speed is determined largely by the frequency of the AC supply and the number of motor poles, so raising or lowering the output frequency from the VFD directly raises or lowers motor speed. The VFD also adjusts voltage along with frequency to maintain proper motor flux and torque across the speed range. This frequency-based control allows the compressor to match capacity to building load far more precisely than simple on and off cycling.
  81. The correct answer is B (Repair the leak within the required timeframe and document the repair and retest.): Once a leak rate exceeding the applicable threshold is confirmed, regulation requires the leak to be repaired within a defined timeframe, with the repair and a subsequent verification retest documented. Continuing operation until the next scheduled inspection ignores the confirmed exceedance. Immediate decommissioning is not required if repair is feasible, and the reporting obligation is not limited to charges above ten thousand kilograms.
  82. The correct answer is A (The pressure-temperature relationship, which is why a P-T chart works for any given refrigerant): For any pure refrigerant, or an azeotropic blend that behaves like one, a fixed saturation temperature corresponds to each saturation pressure whenever liquid and vapour coexist in equilibrium. This is exactly why a P-T chart lets a technician read a saturation temperature directly from a measured pressure. A true zeotropic blend behaves slightly differently because it exhibits glide, meaning its bubble point and dew point temperatures differ at a given pressure.
  83. The correct answer is D (Downstream of the condenser outlet, with the king valve at its outlet): A liquid receiver stores the extra refrigerant charge a system does not need at any given operating condition, so it sits after the condenser where liquid refrigerant collects before continuing to the metering device. The king valve at the receiver outlet lets a mechanic isolate and pump down the low side of the system for service without releasing the full charge.
  84. The correct answer is B (Sensible heat changes the dry-bulb temperature of air, latent heat changes its moisture content): Sensible heat changes the dry-bulb temperature of air, latent heat changes its moisture content. Sensible heat is the energy that raises or lowers dry-bulb temperature without changing moisture content, while latent heat is the energy absorbed or released when water vapour condenses or evaporates. Both components must be calculated separately and summed to size cooling equipment correctly, since two spaces with the same sensible load but different occupancy or ventilation can require very different total capacity. Ignoring latent load in a high-occupancy or humid application leads to a system that cools air but fails to control humidity.
  85. The correct answer is D (The oversized valve passes more refrigerant than the evaporator load can fully evaporate): A metering device sized for more tonnage than the system needs allows excess refrigerant flow into the evaporator, more than the coil's load can boil off into vapor, so liquid carries over toward the compressor and suction pressure rises with flooding, matching the classic flooding pattern of high suction pressure and low superheat. An oversized valve flows more, not less, refrigerant than correctly sized. The physical flow capacity of a larger valve is genuinely greater. Flooding from an oversized metering device is a cooling mode issue in this scenario, not exclusively a heating mode issue.
  86. The correct answer is D (It prevents the metal casing from becoming energized during an internal fault): It prevents the metal casing from becoming energized during an internal fault. Bonding connects all normally non-current-carrying metal parts of the equipment to a common ground reference, so if an internal fault causes a live conductor to contact the casing, the bonding path gives fault current a low resistance route back to the source. This causes the branch circuit overcurrent device to open quickly rather than leaving the casing energized at a dangerous touch voltage. Without proper bonding, a technician or building occupant touching the casing during a fault could receive a serious electrical shock.
  87. The correct answer is D (a 45 degree flare formed with a flaring tool after reaming and cleaning the tube end): refrigeration tubing connections in this trade use the SAE 45 degree flare, produced with a dedicated flaring tool that clamps the tube and presses a cone into the reamed, deburred end to form the sealing surface. Reaming and cleaning the tube first ensures the flare forms evenly without a crack starting at a burr. A pipe threader has no role in forming a flare, and heating the tube to soften it is not how a mechanical flare connection is made. A 90 degree angle is not the standard used for refrigerant flare joints.
  88. The correct answer is A (A hidden feature, such as ductwork or piping, located behind another object): dashed lines on a mechanical drawing conventionally represent a hidden edge or component that exists behind or beneath another object in the view, such as ductwork routed above a ceiling. A property boundary as in option B is typically shown with a distinct, different line type on a site plan rather than a mechanical drawing. Dimension lines as in option C usually appear as thin solid lines with arrows and numeric values, and a centerline as in option D is typically drawn as a long dash, short dash pattern, distinct from a simple hidden line.
  89. The correct answer is B (8.9 kW, the sum of all sensible line items listed in the exhibit): the exhibit lists solar, lighting, equipment, and occupant sensible gains that sum to the total sensible gain row shown as 8.9 kW, which is the figure equipment sizing for sensible cooling capacity must be based on. Option A isolates only one line item rather than the full sensible total. Option C wrongly combines sensible and latent gains into one figure, and option D again isolates a single line item rather than the required total.
  90. The correct answer is A (The Kigali Amendment to the Montreal Protocol on Substances that Deplete the Ozone Layer.): The Kigali Amendment adds HFC refrigerants to the Montreal Protocol framework and establishes a phase-down schedule for their production and import, even though HFCs themselves do not deplete the ozone layer. Canada is a party to this amendment. The original Montreal Protocol text, the Kyoto Protocol, and the Paris Agreement each address different substances or targets and are not the specific instrument driving HFC phase-down.
  91. The correct answer is D (Tare or zero the scale with the empty cylinder in place before opening any valve): taring the scale with the cylinder already resting on it resets the display to zero, so any weight lost from that point forward reflects only the refrigerant actually leaving the cylinder and entering the system. Relying on mental subtraction of a stamped tare weight introduces unnecessary arithmetic error during a live charging process. Ignoring the displayed weight defeats the purpose of using a scale at all, and a household bathroom scale lacks the resolution needed for an accurate refrigerant charge measurement.
  92. The correct answer is C (Infinite resistance, shown as OL, between one terminal pair only): An open winding breaks its internal conductor path completely, so the multimeter shows an over limit or infinite (OL) reading on the terminal pair connected to that winding. Because the run and start windings share the common terminal internally, a second pair that includes the same failed terminal will typically also read OL, while only the one pair not involving that terminal still shows the intact winding's normal, finite resistance. Option A describes a short circuit condition, not an open winding, since near zero resistance means current is flowing too freely. Option B would suggest all windings are intact and roughly balanced, which does not indicate a failure. Option D describes an unstable or fluctuating connection rather than a clean open circuit failure.
  93. The correct answer is C (Whether the charge is a liquid, gas, or cross liquid charge determines valve response behaviour): liquid, gas, and cross liquid charges each follow a different pressure-temperature relationship inside the bulb, which changes how aggressively the valve opens or closes as evaporator load and outlet temperature shift, and how stable superheat remains across a wide operating range. Bulb casing colour has no bearing on thermodynamic response, and while capillary tube length can affect response lag slightly, it does not solely determine superheat behaviour. Mounting screw torque only affects how securely the bulb contacts the suction line, not the fundamental charge response.
  94. The correct answer is C (The refrigerant charge will gradually drift away from its original rated composition): Each time vapour is drawn off a zeotropic blend, the lighter component leaves the cylinder disproportionately, so repeated vapour charging over multiple visits gradually shifts the system's overall refrigerant composition away from its designed ratio, degrading capacity and pressure-temperature behaviour. Charging as vapour does not itself cause liquid slugging, since no liquid enters the compressor in this scenario. Ozone depletion potential is fixed by a refrigerant's chemistry and is unaffected by charging method, and the blend does not lose its latent heat capability entirely.
  95. The correct answer is B (Section 8, Exposure Controls and Personal Protective Equipment): under WHMIS 2015, every Safety Data Sheet follows the same 16 section GHS format, and Section 8 is specifically where a manufacturer lists recommended gloves, eyewear, respirators, and ventilation controls for safe handling. Section 3 only lists the chemical ingredients and their concentrations, not protective gear. Section 14 covers shipping classification and Section 11 covers longer term toxicological data, neither of which tells a technician what PPE to put on before opening the system.
  96. The correct answer is B (Test the freeze point of the solution with a refractometer or hydrometer): A refractometer or hydrometer directly measures the concentration of antifreeze in the loop fluid, which determines the actual freeze protection temperature of the solution circulating through the ground loop. Electrical conductivity and refrigerant compatibility ratings do not indicate whether the fluid concentration is still adequate to prevent freezing at expected ground temperatures. Fluid color alone is not a reliable indicator of freeze point and can remain unchanged even as concentration drops from dilution or degradation. Confirming freeze protection before the heating season prevents loop piping damage from a frozen antifreeze solution.
  97. The correct answer is A (R-1234yf has a dramatically lower global warming potential than the high GWP value of R-404A.): R-404A is a blend with a global warming potential of roughly 3,922, one of the highest among common refrigerants, which has made it a priority target for phase-down regulations in many jurisdictions. R-1234yf is a hydrofluoroolefin with a global warming potential of approximately 4, making it thousands of times lower in atmospheric impact per kilogram released. Both refrigerants are rated for low toxicity, but GWP measures a completely separate property related to heat trapping in the atmosphere over time. This large GWP gap is the primary reason R-1234yf and similar low-GWP refrigerants are replacing high-GWP blends like R-404A in new equipment.
  98. The correct answer is C (The low pressure switch itself, which may be miscalibrated or reading falsely low): With charge, airflow, and coil condition all confirmed normal, the pressure switch itself becomes the prime suspect, since a miscalibrated or drifting switch can trip on a pressure reading that does not reflect the actual system condition, a genuine nuisance trip rather than a real low pressure fault. Low pressure trips are commonly caused by undercharge, but that cause has already been ruled out here by direct confirmation. The condenser fan affects head pressure, not the suction side low pressure switch. An outdoor ambient thermostat is unrelated to a suction pressure safety control.
  99. The correct answer is C (The unit rated at EER 13.5 is more efficient at the tested condition.): EER, or Energy Efficiency Ratio, is calculated as the cooling output in BTU per hour divided by the electrical power input in watts at one specific standardized outdoor temperature and load condition. A higher EER value means the unit produces more cooling for the same amount of electrical input at that test point, so the unit rated at 13.5 outperforms the one rated at 11.2 under identical conditions. SEER differs from EER by averaging performance across an entire cooling season rather than one fixed condition, but the same higher-number-is-better principle applies to both ratings. Contractors compare EER specifically when a customer cares about peak-condition performance rather than seasonal average efficiency.
  100. The correct answer is B (The crankcase heater has been energized for the required warm-up period.): Before any initial startup, the crankcase heater must warm the compressor shell long enough to drive refrigerant that migrated into the oil back out as vapor. Starting a cold compressor with refrigerant dissolved in the oil causes liquid slugging and can fracture reed valves. Fan rotation and thermostat setpoints matter for operation but do not protect the compressor on first start. This step is checked before the disconnect is even closed.
  101. The correct answer is B (A normally open contact that closes when its associated coil energizes): A normally open contact that closes when its associated coil energizes. In standard ladder diagram symbols, two parallel lines with a gap and no diagonal slash represent a normally open contact, which stays open in the resting state and closes once its coil is energized. A normally closed contact is drawn the same way but with a diagonal slash through the gap. Recognizing this distinction is essential for correctly tracing which conditions must be true for a rung to complete.
  102. The correct answer is A (Charge as liquid, since drawing vapor first shifts the blend's component ratio.): In a zeotropic blend like R-407C, the individual refrigerant components have different boiling points, so pulling vapor from the cylinder removes the more volatile component first and leaves the remaining liquid enriched in the higher-boiling component. This fractionation changes the operating characteristics of the refrigerant left in the cylinder and can be introduced into the system. Charging as a liquid, using a metering valve or restrictor to control flow rate, keeps the mixture proportional as it enters the system.
  103. The correct answer is D (Maintain continuous communication with the entrant and monitor for hazards outside): The attendant's core responsibility is to remain stationed outside the confined space, maintain continuous communication with the entrant, and monitor conditions so they can initiate rescue procedures immediately if something goes wrong. Option A is incorrect because entering the space to help with repairs would leave no one outside to monitor and respond to an emergency. Option B describes a distracted attendant who is not maintaining the continuous vigilance the role requires. Option C describes abandoning the post, which defeats the entire purpose of having a dedicated attendant present.
  104. The correct answer is B (Restricted condenser airflow from the matted coil surface.): A physically confirmed matted coil directly restricts airflow across the condenser, impairing heat rejection and raising discharge pressure while suction pressure stays close to normal because the metering device and evaporator side are unaffected. Undercharge and a closed TXV would both drive suction pressure down, which contradicts the near-normal reading given. Non-condensables would raise head pressure too, but the visibly dirty coil is the confirmed and directly observable cause here.
  105. The correct answer is D (Leave that diffuser fully open and adjust the remaining diffusers to match its ratio): Proportional balancing uses the diffuser with the lowest actual-to-design ratio as the reference point and opens it fully, then throttles every other diffuser on that branch down to match the same ratio. Closing the lowest diffuser further would only worsen its already low airflow relative to the rest of the branch. Balancing only the highest ratio diffuser ignores the branch relationship the method depends on. A low ratio is normally caused by damper position or duct restriction, not automatically a defective diffuser.
  106. The correct answer is A (Developing mechanical wear such as bearing degradation or fan imbalance requiring further investigation): A significant increase in vibration velocity at the same point over time, with fan speed unchanged, is a classic trend indicating developing mechanical wear such as bearing degradation, blade imbalance, or coupling misalignment that should be investigated before failure occurs. Seasonal temperature variation does not typically produce this magnitude of change in vibration velocity readings. An instrument calibration error would not explain a consistent trend across two separate measurement events months apart. Higher vibration is never a sign of improved fan performance and instead signals increasing mechanical stress on the equipment.
  107. The correct answer is D (Closed cell elastomeric is preferable because its structure resists moisture absorption on its own.): closed cell elastomeric foam has a cellular structure that inherently resists water vapour absorption, which is important on a suction line that stays below the dew point continuously, since trapped moisture in the insulation would reduce its performance and promote condensation and corrosion on the pipe. Standard fiberglass without a vapour barrier jacket readily absorbs moisture over time in this application. Higher temperature rating and weight are not the deciding factors for a cold suction line.
  108. The correct answer is C (How and how often to replace or clean the air filter, and normal thermostat use): Practical handover training for building staff focuses on routine tasks they are actually expected to perform, such as filter care and correct thermostat operation, so the system continues to perform as documented. Disassembling the compressor, adjusting factory refrigerant charge, or reprogramming control board firmware are technician-level tasks that should never be assigned to building maintenance staff. Keeping the training scope appropriate protects both the equipment and the warranty.
  109. The correct answer is B (The exact identity of the refrigerant in the system, since P-T charts are refrigerant specific): Every refrigerant has its own unique pressure-temperature relationship, so a pressure of 950 kPa corresponds to a completely different saturation temperature depending on whether the system contains R-410A, R-404A, or another refrigerant entirely. Reading the wrong chart for an unknown refrigerant would produce a saturation temperature that has no real connection to what is actually happening inside the system. Ambient temperature, amperage, and airflow rate all affect system performance but are not needed to convert a known pressure into a saturation temperature for a known refrigerant.
  110. The correct answer is B (Restricted condenser airflow is raising head pressure above the normal operating range): A dirty, airflow-restricted condenser coil cannot reject heat efficiently, so refrigerant backs up in the condenser and the discharge pressure shown on the gauge climbs above normal. Undercharge and a stuck-open metering device both tend to lower rather than raise head pressure. A frozen evaporator affects suction pressure directly and does not by itself explain an elevated high side reading. The visibly dirty coil and the elevated gauge reading point to the same restricted-airflow fault.
  111. The correct answer is C (The TXV automatically adjusts refrigerant flow to maintain a stable evaporator superheat.): Because the thermostatic expansion valve continuously modulates its opening to hold evaporator superheat roughly constant as load and airflow change, the amount of liquid refrigerant backed up in the condenser, and therefore the subcooling, mainly reflects how much total refrigerant charge is present rather than the current operating conditions. That is why manufacturers can publish one fixed target subcooling value for correct total charge instead of a chart that varies with wet bulb temperature. TXV systems still meter refrigerant using the standard vapor compression principle, not a different one.
  112. The correct answer is C (Subcooling is 9°C, which falls within the target range, so the charge is correct): subcooling equals the saturated condensing temperature minus the liquid line temperature, which here is 42°C minus 33°C, giving exactly 9°C. Since the manufacturer's target range for this chiller is 8°C to 10°C, a 9°C reading sits comfortably within range and confirms the charge does not need further adjustment at this point. Adding the two temperatures instead of subtracting produces a meaningless 75°C figure, and running amperage is a separate diagnostic value not required to calculate subcooling.
  113. The correct answer is D (CSA B52, the Canadian Mechanical Refrigeration Code adopted across Canada): CSA B52, the Canadian Mechanical Refrigeration Code adopted across Canada. CSA B52 is the national standard adopted by provincial regulators that governs the design, construction, installation, operation and inspection of mechanical refrigeration systems, including refrigerant safety group classification, machine room construction, and quantity limits per occupancy. The National Building Code addresses general building construction rather than refrigeration system specifics, ASHRAE 90.1 is an American energy standard, and the Canadian Electrical Code covers wiring but not refrigerant piping or safety classification. Provincial authorities such as TSSA in Ontario enforce compliance with B52 through permits and inspection.
  114. The correct answer is A (Approximately 500 microns or lower, depending on the manufacturer specification): Deep vacuum evacuation lowers the boiling point of any residual moisture in the system far enough that it boils off as vapor and gets pulled out by the pump. A target around 500 microns absolute is common trade practice, though some manufacturers specify a lower number, and a true absolute zero vacuum is not practically achievable with field equipment.
  115. The correct answer is D (Infiltration and product loads, driven by door traffic and incoming warm goods): Infiltration and product loads, driven by door traffic and incoming warm goods. In a heavily used walk-in cooler, frequent door openings bring in warm humid air that must be cooled and dehumidified, and incoming product arriving above storage temperature adds a significant pull-down load, together often exceeding the steady transmission gain through well-insulated panels. Transmission load through the envelope remains present but is comparatively small once modern insulated panel construction is used. Internal loads and defrost heat are real contributors but are typically smaller than infiltration and product load in a busy commercial application.
  116. The correct answer is A (Federal regulations under CEPA 1999 apply in addition to provincial trade certification): Provincial trade certification governs who may legally perform refrigeration and air conditioning work, and it does not replace Canada's separate federal obligations under CEPA 1999, which authorizes the Ozone-depleting Substances and Halocarbon Alternatives Regulations governing manufacture, import and export nationally, plus the Federal Halocarbon Regulations governing systems on federal and Indigenous lands. For most private-sector jobs like this one, the day-to-day duty to recover refrigerant and never vent it actually comes from the applicable province's own halocarbon or environmental protection regulation, which operates alongside provincial trade certification and the narrower federal rules above rather than being replaced by either. Option B invents a geographic limitation that does not exist. Option C incorrectly claims provincial certification substitutes for environmental regulation entirely. Option D invents an equipment-scale exemption with no basis in any of these regulations.
  117. The correct answer is A (The connector should be installed in the plane parallel to the crankshaft.): a flexible vibration eliminator absorbs vibration best when it flexes in the plane parallel to the compressor crankshaft, since that is the direction the compressor primarily vibrates during operation. Installing it in an unrelated or perpendicular orientation reduces its ability to absorb that motion and can lead to premature fatigue cracking of the connector or nearby brazed joints. Simply placing it near the compressor without regard to orientation does not achieve proper vibration isolation.
  118. The correct answer is B (Self-contained recovery, using the recovery machine's own dedicated compressor): Self-contained recovery equipment has its own independent compressor separate from the appliance being serviced, allowing recovery even if the appliance's compressor is inoperative. Option A describes the opposite approach, which depends on the appliance's own compressor. Option C describes a liquid-transfer technique rather than describing the equipment configuration itself. Option D is not a recognized recovery method, since some form of compression is always involved.
  119. Relying on memory alone from one demonstration risks staff forgetting details or using the wrong filter size later. Promising to personally return every time defeats the purpose of training staff to be self sufficient. Providing documentation for an unrelated piece of equipment gives no useful information for this specific filter change task.
  120. The correct answer is B (The refrigerant must be fully recovered from the system before dismantling begins): End-of-life decommissioning requires full recovery of any remaining refrigerant before equipment is dismantled or scrapped, regardless of the equipment's destination. Option A wrongly assumes scrapyards are equipped or permitted to handle refrigerant. Option C describes prohibited deliberate venting, which the destruction of the equipment does not excuse. Option D is not a recognized substitute for actually recovering refrigerant from the decommissioned system.
  121. The correct answer is A (Preventing the compressor from restarting immediately against high head pressure): Preventing the compressor from restarting immediately against high head pressure. This time delay relay creates a short pause before allowing the contactor coil to energize, giving refrigerant pressures time to equalize after a power interruption. Without this delay, the compressor could attempt to restart while discharge pressure is still elevated, straining the motor and increasing locked rotor current. This anti-short-cycle timing protects the compressor from repeated hard restarts during brief power blips.
  122.
