The correct answer is C (A mechanical installation permit, based on drawings meeting CSA B52 requirements): CSA B52 requires that refrigeration system installations above the exempt quantity for the occupancy be reviewed and permitted by the authority having jurisdiction before work begins, based on drawings and specifications that meet the code's documentation requirements. A boiler and pressure vessel registration relates to separate pressure equipment regulation, not the refrigeration installation permit itself. A refrigerant handling certificate applies to individual technicians, not the building owner, and a fire department occupancy letter is unrelated to the mechanical permit process.
  123. The correct answer is B (It is a minor incidental release, distinct from the intentional venting the regulation targets.): The regulation targets deliberate, avoidable releases of refrigerant into the atmosphere, and using low loss fittings and proper technique to minimize residual hose losses demonstrates due diligence rather than a violation. This differs sharply from intentionally opening a system or purging a full charge to atmosphere to avoid recovery. Routine incidental losses of this kind are not treated the same as, or reported the same way as, deliberate venting.
  124. The correct answer is C (A dirty filter or coil is adding unplanned system resistance across the whole unit): Elevated static pressure at every measurement point combined with system wide low airflow, despite correct fan speed, points to added resistance somewhere common to the whole airstream, most commonly a loaded filter or fouled coil. A single closed damper would only reduce airflow on its own branch, not across the entire system. A slipping belt would show up as reduced fan RPM on the tachometer, which was already confirmed correct. A miscalibrated thermostat affects one zone's damper position, not overall static pressure at every point in the system.
  125. The correct answer is D (Mineral scale or debris has fouled the water coil and restricted water flow): A water-source heat pump rejects heat to the water loop the same way an air-cooled unit rejects heat to outdoor air, so restricted water flow through a fouled coaxial coil limits heat rejection and raises discharge pressure just like a dirty air-cooled condenser would. Since refrigerant charge and the air-side components are both confirmed clean and correct, the fault is isolated to the water side of the exchanger. A stuck reversing valve would produce heating instead of cooling operation rather than simply high pressure within cooling mode. Descaling or flushing the water coil restores proper flow and normal discharge pressure.

Practice Test 2 Answer Key

  1. The correct answer is D (Reduced fan speed and airflow, leading to elevated condensing pressure.): Excessive belt slippage transmits less rotational energy to the fan, lowering its speed and the volume of air moved across the condenser coil. Reduced condenser airflow is a well established cause of high head pressure since heat rejection is impaired. A loose belt does not increase fan speed or amperage, and it has no direct relationship to compressor winding failure or a shaft seal leak.
  2. The correct answer is C (The system is undercharged with refrigerant and needs a leak check.): Low suction pressure with a correspondingly low head pressure, normal airflow on both coils, and bubbles in the sight glass together point to insufficient refrigerant charge rather than an airflow or mechanical fault. Bubbles in the sight glass specifically indicate flash gas from a charge that is too low to keep the liquid line fully liquid. Since airflow was already ruled out on both coils, a stopped blower or failed condenser fan capacitor would show up as an airflow related symptom instead.
  3. The correct answer is D (The sight glass is installed after the filter drier and before the expansion valve.): a moisture indicating sight glass is normally installed in the liquid line downstream of the filter drier and upstream of the expansion valve, which allows the mechanic to observe filtered, subcooled liquid flow and read the moisture indicator color after the drier has had a chance to remove any residual moisture. Placing it before the drier or before the receiver would not give an accurate final reading of system dryness, and a sight glass in this application belongs on the liquid line rather than inside the compressor or on the discharge line.
  4. The correct answer is B (Phasing out substances that deplete the stratospheric ozone layer, such as CFCs): The Montreal Protocol was originally designed to phase out ozone-depleting substances like CFCs and HCFCs, and the later Kigali Amendment extended its framework to also address high-global-warming-potential HFCs. Option A describes a separate area of climate policy not covered by the Protocol's original text. Option C and option D describe technical cylinder standards, not the treaty's original environmental purpose.
  5. The correct answer is C (The actual pipe run measures 2 metres in real length on site): A scale of 1 to 50 means every unit on the drawing represents 50 of the same unit in real life, so a 40 millimetre measurement multiplied by 50 equals 2,000 millimetres, or 2 metres, of actual pipe run. Option A applies an incorrect multiplier that does not match the stated scale. Option B also uses the wrong multiplication factor and overstates the true distance. Option D drastically overstates the length by applying a multiplier far larger than the drawing's actual scale calls for.
  6. The correct answer is D (Whether the isolators are correctly installed and the unit is level on its mounting curb): Normal cooling performance does not rule out a mechanical installation problem, and excess vibration is commonly traced to missing, damaged, or incorrectly installed isolators, or a unit that is not sitting level on its curb. Refrigerant overcharge and thermostat programming are not typical causes of elevated vibration readings. The technician should investigate the mounting and isolation before simply recording the out of range number and closing out the report.
  7. The correct answer is B (Joints inside a concealed space should be brazed rather than mechanical fittings.): mechanical joints are designed to be periodically inspected and are more prone to slow seepage from vibration, thermal cycling, or gasket wear compared to a properly made brazed joint, so codes and good practice call for brazed connections in locations that will be permanently concealed and inaccessible for future inspection. A single pressure test at installation does not guarantee a mechanical joint stays tight for the life of the building. Mechanical fittings remain appropriate at accessible locations where future service access is genuinely needed.
  8. The correct answer is A (About 1 volt, found by multiplying the current by the wiring resistance): About 1 volt, found by multiplying the current by the wiring resistance. Ohm's Law states voltage equals current multiplied by resistance, so 0.5 amperes multiplied by 2 ohms equals 1 volt dropped across the wiring run itself. This leaves approximately 23 volts actually reaching the contactor coil terminals, which is usually still within the coil's acceptable operating range. On a much longer or smaller gauge wire run, this voltage drop calculation becomes critical, since a coil receiving significantly less than its rated voltage may chatter or fail to pull in reliably.
  9. The correct answer is D (HFC refrigerants require polyol ester oil, which is not miscible with mineral oil.): R-410A and other HFC refrigerants are not chemically compatible with the mineral oil used in older R-22 systems, so they require polyol ester, or POE, oil to stay properly mixed and return to the compressor with the refrigerant. Introducing mineral oil into an HFC system causes the oils to separate, leading to poor oil return and eventual compressor starvation. R-22 systems, by contrast, traditionally use mineral oil or alkylbenzene oil. Compressors always require lubrication and cannot run dry.
  10. The correct answer is A (the pad is level and the unit sits on vibration-isolating pads at each anchor point): a condensing unit mounted on an unlevel pad can develop oil migration problems inside the compressor and uneven stress on refrigerant line connections over time, so confirming the pad is level is a standard pre-installation check. Vibration isolating pads or mounts between the unit's base and the anchors reduce vibration and noise transmitted into the structure and connected piping. Sloping the pad toward the unit would direct water into it rather than away, and bare rigid anchoring without isolation invites the vibration problem the isolators are meant to prevent. Pad sizing with some overhang is normal and is not restricted to an exact footprint match.
  11. The correct answer is B (Approximately 208 volts, since three-phase power is balanced across pairs): a properly balanced three-phase supply delivers essentially equal line-to-line voltage across all three conductor pairs. Confirming this balance across all three leg combinations is a standard troubleshooting step before condemning a compressor or contactor. A large deviation between pairs points toward a voltage imbalance problem upstream. Ignoring this check can lead a technician to misdiagnose a healthy compressor as faulty.
  12. The correct answer is B (To confirm the system holds vacuum steadily, ruling out a leak or residual moisture): if the micron reading climbs sharply and keeps climbing, a leak is present and must be found and repaired before charging. A slower rise that levels off usually indicates residual moisture still evaporating rather than a true leak, and the system needs further evacuation. This test has nothing to do with oil viscosity, gauge calibration, or calculating the charge mass, all of which are separate steps in the commissioning sequence.
  13. The correct answer is B (Turn the stem counterclockwise, which opens the valve further and lowers superheat.): On a standard externally adjustable TXV, turning the adjustment stem counterclockwise backs the internal spring pressure off, allowing the valve to open further and admit more refrigerant into the evaporator. More refrigerant flow absorbs more heat before it fully vaporizes, which lowers the superheat reading toward the target. Turning the stem clockwise does the opposite, restricting flow and raising superheat. Adjustments should always be made in small increments with time allowed for the system to stabilize between checks.
  14. The correct answer is C (The three phases are naturally offset, creating a rotating magnetic field): the three separate voltage waveforms in a three-phase supply are already 120 degrees apart, which naturally creates a smoothly rotating magnetic field inside the motor without needing an external phase-shifting capacitor. Single-phase motors lack this natural offset and rely on a capacitor or a start winding to artificially create a similar rotating effect. This is why three-phase motors generally start and run with more consistent torque.
  15. The correct answer is A (The authority having jurisdiction, the provincial regulator responsible for permits and inspections): the authority having jurisdiction is the provincial regulatory body assigned enforcement responsibility under the regulatory framework that CSA B52 operates within, and it is the body responsible for reviewing design drawings, issuing installation permits, and conducting inspections of refrigeration systems in that province. Equipment manufacturers, building owners, and refrigerant suppliers all play roles in a project, but none of them hold the legal authority to approve drawings or issue permits in place of the authority having jurisdiction. A mechanical contractor must know which specific provincial body serves this role in the jurisdiction where the work is taking place before beginning the permit process.
  16. The correct answer is C (A check valve or isolation valve controlling flow into the compressor): A check valve or isolation valve controlling flow into the compressor. A rectangle or similar shape with a diagonal internal line and a directional flow triangle is a common convention for a valve symbol, and its position immediately before the compressor suction inlet identifies it as an isolation or check valve controlling flow into that compressor. A liquid receiver is drawn as a larger vessel shape on the high-pressure liquid side, not the suction line, and a discharge muffler or oil separator would be located on the discharge or oil management portion of the circuit rather than at the suction inlet. Correctly reading valve placement is essential to understanding how a system can be isolated for service.
  17. The correct answer is D (Superheat is 11°C, within the acceptable range, so charging is complete.): The gauge reads 580 kPa, corresponding to a saturated evaporating temperature of 10°C on the R-22 pressure-temperature chart. Subtracting that from the measured suction line temperature of 21°C gives a superheat of 11°C, which sits comfortably within the 8 to 12°C acceptable band around the 10°C target. Since the reading falls inside the acceptable range, no further adjustment to the refrigerant charge is needed. Option C simply restates the saturation temperature rather than completing the actual superheat calculation.
  18. The correct answer is C (Compound gauges cannot resolve pressures low enough to confirm moisture removal.): A standard compound gauge reads in relatively coarse increments and effectively bottoms out near 0 kPa, which can appear identical whether the system is at 5000 microns or 300 microns. Moisture only boils off in the deep vacuum range measured in microns, well below what a compound gauge can distinguish. A dedicated electronic micron gauge is required to verify the system has actually reached the target vacuum level. The other options describe unrelated or incorrect limitations.
  19. The correct answer is A (Gas velocity in the oversized riser drops too low to carry oil upward): Oil return up a vertical suction riser depends on the refrigerant gas moving fast enough to entrain and carry oil droplets along the tube wall. A riser sized for full-load velocity becomes oversized the moment the compressor unloads, and the resulting low velocity lets oil fall out of suspension and collect in the riser instead of returning to the compressor crankcase.
  20. The correct answer is D (A failed start capacitor or start relay preventing full startup): a humming compressor that never actually starts and then trips on overload is a classic symptom of insufficient starting torque, most often traced to a weak or failed start capacitor or a start relay that fails to engage properly. Without adequate starting assistance, the motor draws high locked-rotor current without rotating, quickly heating the windings until the internal protector opens. A faulty high pressure switch or miswired fan terminal would not produce this specific humming and stalling symptom.
  21. The correct answer is B (These HFC refrigerants have an ozone depletion potential of zero because they contain no chlorine.): Ozone depletion is caused by chlorine and bromine atoms reaching the upper atmosphere and breaking down ozone molecules, which is why chlorine-containing CFC and HCFC refrigerants like R-22 were targeted for phase-out under the Montreal Protocol. HFC refrigerants such as R-410A, R-134a, and R-32 contain only hydrogen, fluorine, and carbon, with no chlorine atom present in their molecular structure. Because there is no chlorine to react with ozone, their ozone depletion potential is rated at zero. These refrigerants are instead regulated for their global warming potential rather than for any ozone impact.
  22. The correct answer is D (The compressor is locked out while the interior light stays lit): the door switch's normally closed contact opens when the door is propped open, breaking the compressor contactor rung and preventing the compressor from running while warm air enters the box. That same physical switch closes its normally open contact under this condition, completing the separate light rung so the interior light turns on for anyone working inside. This design prevents wasted compressor operation while still providing visibility during an open door condition.
  23. The correct answer is A (Ahead of the metering device, in an accessible straight run for a clear view): Placing the sight glass in the liquid line before the metering device lets a technician observe solid liquid flow versus bubbles, which indicates undercharge, a restriction, or a plugged drier upstream. An accessible straight section of tubing away from fittings gives an unobstructed view during future service calls, and the moisture indicator disk changes color to flag water contamination in the refrigerant.
  24. The correct answer is B (The system has likely lost refrigerant charge since it was commissioned.): Because the original startup report establishes a known-good baseline of 10°C subcooling at the time the charge was verified correct, a drop to 3°C on a later visit strongly suggests refrigerant has since left the system, most likely through a slow leak. Target subcooling for a given TXV system does not decrease simply due to age, and there is no reason to assume the original baseline reading itself was wrong just because a later reading differs. This kind of comparison is exactly the diagnostic value baseline data is meant to provide.
  25. The correct answer is C (Sum the liquid-holding component volumes, then multiply the total by the refrigerant liquid density): estimating design charge starts by identifying which components in the exhibit hold liquid refrigerant under normal operation, such as the liquid line and receiver, and summing those volumes together with the appropriate portion of coil volume as applicable to the system type. That combined liquid-equivalent volume is then multiplied by the refrigerant's liquid density to convert litres into an estimated mass in kilograms. Using only the receiver or only the evaporator ignores the meaningful volume held in the lines and other components, understating the true charge estimate. Simply adding litre values together also produces a volume figure, not a mass, and skips the density conversion entirely.
  26. The correct answer is A (Approximately 2 metres out from the base of the wall): The 4 to 1 rule means the ladder's working height should be four times the horizontal distance from the wall to the base, so an 8 metre height requires the base to sit about 2 metres out. Option B places the base too close, creating a steep angle that increases the risk of the ladder tipping backward. Option C places the base too far out, which flattens the angle and risks the base sliding away from the wall. Option D is far too close for any safe extension ladder setup at that height.
  27. The correct answer is A (Check the piston's stamped size or colour code against the manufacturer's published matching chart): fixed orifice pistons are sized in small increments and stamped or colour coded, and installing the wrong size for a given condenser and coil pairing will prevent the system from ever reaching a correct superheat no matter how the charge is adjusted. Measuring superheat first without confirming the orifice size risks chasing a charge target that can never be met. A fixed-orifice system has no TXV to compensate, and weighing the tiny piston on a refrigerant scale reveals nothing about its metering size.
  28. The correct answer is B (Diffuser 2, since its measured airflow is roughly 28 percent below its design CFM value): 150 minus 108 divided by 150 equals about 28 percent, which is well outside the 10 percent tolerance and requires the damper to be opened further. Diffuser 1 is within 2 CFM of design and needs no adjustment. Diffuser 3 is only 3 CFM over design, which is inside the acceptable tolerance band. Diffuser 4 is 2 CFM over design, a smaller deviation than diffuser 2 both in absolute and percentage terms.
  29. The correct answer is C (compare the riser's rated minimum load velocity from sizing tables against the actual minimum operating capacity): vertical suction riser sizing tables give the minimum refrigerant flow, tied to system capacity, needed to maintain gas velocity high enough to entrain and lift oil at a given tube size. Checking the actual minimum operating capacity of the installed system against that rating confirms whether the chosen riser size will still return oil when the system unloads to its lowest stage. Ambient room temperature, surface temperature of the pipe, and supply voltage do not indicate whether gas velocity in the riser is adequate for oil return.
  30. The correct answer is D (Formation of black copper oxide scale flaking off the internal tube walls): Heating copper tubing in the presence of trapped air oxidizes the interior surface, and that scale later breaks loose, circulates with the refrigerant, and can plug filter driers, screens, or metering devices. A slow nitrogen purge displaces the oxygen inside the tube during heating, so the copper stays bright and scale-free without needing a chemical cleanup afterward.
  31. The correct answer is B (A normally open contact, closed only when its associated coil energizes): the standard ladder diagram symbol for a normally open contact is two short parallel lines with a visible gap, showing an open circuit at rest. When the coil that controls this contact energizes, the contact closes and allows current to flow through that portion of the rung. A normally closed contact uses a similar symbol with a diagonal line drawn through it to show it is closed at rest. Recognizing this distinction is essential to correctly tracing current paths on any ladder diagram.
  32. The correct answer is C (It uses one run capacitor permanently wired in the start winding circuit): a PSC motor keeps a single run capacitor in series with the start winding at all times, during both starting and running operation. This gives moderate starting torque and quiet, reliable running performance, which is why it is common on fan and blower applications. Unlike a capacitor-start design, a PSC motor has no start relay or capacitor that drops out of the circuit.
  33. The correct answer is C (Swap the connections of any two of the three line leads): reversing the connection order of any two of the three incoming line conductors reverses the rotating magnetic field direction inside the motor, which reverses its rotation. Three-phase motors have no neutral conductor at the motor terminals, so that option does not apply here. This procedure is standard practice after confirming rotation is wrong on any newly installed or rewired three-phase motor.
  34. The correct answer is A (Cooling load temperature difference, which accounts for thermal lag through opaque building surfaces): CLTD is a tabulated equivalent temperature difference used in the CLTD/CLF method to reflect the time delay between the outdoor temperature peak and the resulting heat gain reaching the indoor space through walls and roofs. It exists because massive construction stores and releases heat over several hours rather than transmitting it instantly. Sensible heat ratio and bypass factor describe coil and airflow behaviour, not envelope thermal lag, and diversity factor addresses simultaneous equipment operation rather than construction mass effects.
  35. The correct answer is C (The coil has three zones, desuperheating, condensing, and subcooling, each behaving differently.): Refrigerant enters the condenser as a superheated vapour and first passes through a desuperheating zone where its temperature drops relatively quickly toward the saturation temperature without yet condensing. It then enters the condensing zone, where the refrigerant changes phase from vapour to liquid at a nearly constant saturation temperature while releasing large amounts of latent heat. Finally, in the subcooling zone near the outlet, the now liquid refrigerant continues losing sensible heat and drops below its saturation temperature before entering the liquid line. Because each zone involves a different heat transfer process, sensible cooling of vapour, latent heat rejection, and sensible cooling of liquid, the overall temperature profile along the coil is not a simple straight line.
  36. The correct answer is D (The refrigerant type, quantity added, and system pressures before and after service): Proper refrigerant service records must capture the specific type and quantity of refrigerant added along with the pressures observed before and after the work, since this data is essential for tracking whether the system has a recurring leak that needs further investigation. An invoice total says nothing about the technical work actually performed on the refrigerant circuit. A personal opinion about replacement is not objective service documentation. A general statement that the system is cooling properly again provides no data a future technician could use to spot a pattern of repeated recharges.
  37. The correct answer is B (A pressure relief valve that opens automatically once system pressure exceeds its set point): a spring loaded valve symbol with a discharge routed to atmosphere or a relief header is the standard representation of a pressure relief valve on a refrigeration piping and instrumentation diagram. The spring holds the valve closed under normal conditions and only allows it to open once system pressure exceeds the calibrated set point, protecting components from overpressure. A solenoid valve symbol instead shows an electrical actuator, a thermostatic expansion valve symbol shows a sensing bulb connection, and a check valve symbol shows a simple directional arrow without a spring or relief discharge routing. Correctly identifying the relief valve symbol is important since it identifies a critical safety device location on the drawing.
  38. The correct answer is C (The system charge and operation are verified correct, and baseline data should now be recorded): when subcooling, superheat, discharge pressure relative to ambient conditions, and oil level all independently agree with their expected targets, this convergence of multiple separate checks is the strongest confirmation available that the system is charged and operating correctly. At that point the correct next step is recording these values as the baseline for future service reference, not discarding a working charge or second-guessing consistent data. Dismissing agreement across several independent checks as coincidence, or trusting only one reading while ignoring the rest, both go against sound commissioning practice.
  39. The correct answer is C (Approximately 9.7 kilowatts of sensible heat is added to the passing airstream.): Sensible heat is calculated using Q equals mass flow rate multiplied by specific heat multiplied by the temperature change. Multiplying 1.2 kilograms per second by 1.006 kilojoules per kilogram per degree Celsius by an 8°C rise gives approximately 9.66 kilowatts. This value represents heat that raises the air temperature without changing its state, which is the definition of sensible heat. No moisture is added or removed in this calculation, so latent heat is not involved.
  40. The correct answer is D (10°C of superheat, found by subtracting negative 6°C from the suction line temperature of 4°C): Applying the superheat formula, 4°C minus negative 6°C equals 10°C once the subtraction of a negative number is handled correctly. This is a common style of calculation on low temperature and medium temperature commercial refrigeration systems, where saturated evaporating temperatures are frequently below zero. A technician who mishandles the negative sign and simply adds the absolute values together, as in a rushed mental calculation, risks recording a completely wrong superheat value.
  41. The correct answer is B (The larger unit above the threshold triggers the formal repair obligation the small cooler may not.): The formal leak repair obligation under the regulation covering these systems, whether provincial for most private-sector equipment or the Federal Halocarbon Regulations for federal and Indigenous land equipment, generally applies to stationary systems with a full refrigerant charge above a defined threshold, which the rooftop unit exceeds, while a small display cooler well under that threshold may fall outside that specific formal requirement. This does not mean small system leaks are acceptable, since deliberate venting rules still apply regardless of size. The charge size threshold specifically determines which systems fall under the formal leak repair obligation itself.
  42. The correct answer is B (The coil surface temperature drops below the entering air's dew point): Once the coil surface falls below the dew point temperature of the air passing over it, the air can no longer hold all of its moisture at that lower temperature, so water vapour condenses out onto the coil, which is the normal dehumidification process. A coil surface warmer than the wet bulb temperature would not cause condensation, it describes the opposite direction of heat transfer. Condensation actually becomes more likely at higher relative humidity, not below 20 percent, and it is an expected sign of proper operation, not a refrigerant leak.
  43. The correct answer is D (Estimate the receiver's operating liquid volume at design conditions and add it to the piping charge estimate): a receiver holds a variable liquid volume depending on system design and operating conditions, so an accurate design-stage charge estimate requires calculating the receiver's expected operating liquid volume and adding that figure to the piping and coil charge allowances rather than ignoring or guessing at its contribution. Assuming the receiver stays empty ignores its actual function of holding reserve liquid. A fixed 50 percent assumption is not a reliable design basis, and shell weight has no direct relationship to the liquid volume held inside.
  44. The correct answer is C (Closed-cell elastomeric foam insulation with an integral vapor barrier): The closed-cell structure of elastomeric foam has very low water vapor permeability built into the material itself, so it resists moisture migration into the insulation without needing a taped-on vapor barrier jacket. This matters most on cold suction lines, where any moisture reaching the tube surface can condense, freeze, and eventually saturate and destroy the insulating value of an open-cell material.
  45. The correct answer is A (Clearances ensure adequate airflow for heat rejection and space for service access): Clearances ensure adequate airflow for heat rejection and space for service access. Manufacturer clearance requirements are engineered distances intended to guarantee sufficient unobstructed airflow to and from the condenser coil for proper heat rejection, as well as physical space for technicians to safely access panels, coils and controls for maintenance and repair. These are functional requirements, not aesthetic preferences, and manufacturers commonly tie warranty coverage to installation according to their published clearance requirements. Outdoor condensing units are exactly the type of equipment these clearance requirements are most commonly published for, since airflow obstruction directly affects their performance.
  46. The correct answer is C (Cleaning both surfaces to bright, oxide-free copper and confirming full insertion depth): Capillary action only pulls molten filler metal into a joint when the clearance between tube and fitting is small and consistent, and when both surfaces are free of oxide, oil, or dirt that would block wetting. Full insertion, typically to the socket depth stamped by the fitting manufacturer, ensures adequate overlap for a leak-tight, mechanically sound joint once the alloy solidifies.
  47. The correct answer is A (The colour scheme identifies the cylinder as a recovery vessel, not a specific refrigerant.): Grey with a yellow shoulder is the accepted industry convention for recovery and recycling cylinders regardless of what refrigerant or refrigerant mixture is placed inside them. The technician must rely on the attached tag or label recording the actual contents, since virgin refrigerant colour codes do not apply to recovery cylinders. Assuming contents from cylinder colour alone risks cross contaminating a reclaimer's stock.
  48. The correct answer is C (Approximately eighty percent of its rated gross weight capacity, leaving vapour headspace.): Recovery cylinders must never be filled with liquid refrigerant beyond roughly eighty percent of their rated capacity, leaving vapour headspace so that liquid expansion from rising ambient temperature does not create a dangerous hydrostatic overfill condition. Filling to ninety five percent or completely full removes that safety margin and risks cylinder rupture. Fifty percent is overly conservative and not the standard industry fill limit used in practice.
  49. The correct answer is A (Digital gauges can calculate superheat and subcooling directly from built-in temperature clamps.): Many digital manifold gauges pair with clamp-on temperature probes on the suction and liquid lines and, once the refrigerant type is selected, automatically look up the saturation temperature and display live superheat and subcooling values. This removes the manual step of cross-referencing a printed pressure-temperature chart for every reading during commissioning. Digital gauges still require periodic calibration and full evacuation practice, and connecting the wrong refrigerant can still damage seals and internal components.
  50. The correct answer is D (the smaller riser is sized for minimum load velocity, and the larger riser adds capacity later): in a dual riser design, the smaller pipe is deliberately undersized relative to full load so that even at the lowest expected operating capacity, gas velocity through it stays high enough to carry oil upward. The larger riser has its own oil trap that stays sealed until capacity and velocity rise enough at higher load to lift that trapped oil and open flow, adding the extra flow area needed at maximum capacity. The two risers are intentionally different sizes rather than identical, and the smaller riser carries suction gas, not liquid, at all times it is active.
  51. The correct answer is B (Conduction is slowed because the ice layer adds resistance to heat flow through the coil wall.): Conduction is the transfer of heat through a solid material, and ice is a relatively poor conductor of heat compared to the bare metal of the coil tubing and fins. As the ice layer thickens, it adds an insulating barrier that heat from the air must pass through by conduction before reaching the refrigerant inside the tube. This reduces the overall rate of heat transfer into the refrigerant, lowering the coil's cooling capacity even though the refrigerant circuit itself may be operating normally. This is why scheduled defrost cycles are essential on any low-temperature coil that operates below the frost point.
  52. The correct answer is C (About 1.3 kg remains before the maximum gross weight, so stop well before that point.): Subtracting the current gross weight from the maximum gross weight listed in the exhibit leaves roughly 1.3 kg of usable capacity, and a technician should stop well short of the rated maximum to account for liquid expansion and gauge error. Continuing to add refrigerant close to or past the stamped maximum gross weight creates a serious overpressure and rupture hazard. The listed hydrostatic test date does not change the fill limit for a given filling event.
  53. The correct answer is A (Scroll compressors have fewer moving parts and no suction or discharge valves to fail): Scroll compressors have fewer moving parts and no suction or discharge valves to fail. The orbiting and fixed scroll form a continuously shrinking pocket that compresses gas without reed or plate valves, which reduces wear points, noise and the failure modes common to reciprocating designs. This construction also gives scroll compressors better tolerance of occasional liquid slugging than reciprocating designs, since the scrolls can separate slightly under a liquid load. These advantages, combined with high full and part load efficiency, make scroll the common choice in small to mid-size commercial and residential air conditioning.
  54. The correct answer is B (Lowering absolute pressure lowers water's boiling point, letting it vaporize at room temperature.): Water's boiling point depends directly on the surrounding absolute pressure, and at standard atmospheric pressure it will not boil until 100°C. As a vacuum pump drives the system pressure down into the deep vacuum range measured in microns, water's boiling point drops along with it until it is below the ambient system temperature, allowing trapped moisture to vaporize and be pulled out by the pump. This is a physical phase change, not a chemical reaction or a freezing process.
  55. The correct answer is D (A non-contact infrared thermometer aimed at the exposed portion of the line): A non-contact infrared thermometer can quickly capture a surface temperature reading from a short distance away without needing to attach a probe or disturb the insulation, making it practical for a fast check on a hard to access line. Option A is impractical and slow compared to an instant infrared reading and provides poor surface contact through insulation. Option B measures ambient air temperature, not the surface temperature of the refrigerant line itself. Option C requires physically damaging the insulation to insert a probe, which is more invasive than necessary for a quick check.
  56. The correct answer is D (Confirming the area is well ventilated and free of ignition sources before opening the system): Because R-454B is classified A2L, meaning it is mildly flammable, a technician must take additional care to ensure adequate ventilation and eliminate ignition sources such as open flame or sparks before releasing or working near any refrigerant that may have leaked into the surrounding air. Options A, B, and C all describe good general practice that applies regardless of a refrigerant's flammability classification, since they are not specifically tied to the flammability hazard itself. Manufacturers and codes increasingly require A2L specific handling procedures as these lower GWP refrigerants become more common in the trade.
  57. The correct answer is B (A vane anemometer suits larger openings, while hot wire suits low velocities in tight spots): A vane anemometer measures velocity through a rotating rotor and works well at open grille faces with reasonable airflow, while a hot wire anemometer's fine sensing element can accurately read the lower velocities and fit into tighter spaces such as small return openings. Neither instrument measures temperature as its primary function, and neither requires a wired connection to a building automation system to operate. Selecting the right instrument for the location improves the accuracy of the balancing readings.
  58. The correct answer is B (Radiant heat transfer occurs mainly between the compressor's hot shell and the surrounding machine room air.): Radiation is heat transfer through electromagnetic waves without requiring direct contact or a moving fluid, and a hot compressor shell running at elevated discharge temperatures can radiate a measurable amount of heat to cooler surrounding surfaces and equipment. This radiant component is usually small compared to the conduction and convection happening inside the coils and refrigerant lines, but it is not zero and is why machine rooms can feel warm near an operating compressor. Refrigerant itself does not meaningfully transfer heat by radiation, since heat exchange with refrigerant relies on direct contact through conduction and convection instead. Recognizing radiation as a minor but real factor helps explain small discrepancies between calculated and measured heat loads in a mechanical room.
  59. The correct answer is C (Safely discharge the capacitor using an insulated resistor or approved tool): a capacitor can retain a significant stored charge well after power is removed, and touching the terminals directly or with ungrounded test leads can deliver a serious shock. Discharging the capacitor safely, typically through an insulated resistor or a purpose-built discharge tool, removes this stored charge before any meter is connected. Only after discharging is it safe to test capacitance or check for shorts and opens with the meter. Setting an amperage function, leaving wiring connected, or warming the case do nothing to address this stored-charge hazard.
  60. The correct answer is C (Compressor capacity and efficiency both drop because of the artificially lower suction pressure at the compressor): excessive pressure drop over a long suction line lowers the refrigerant pressure, and therefore the saturation temperature, seen at the compressor inlet compared to the evaporator, which reduces the density of gas entering the compressor and directly lowers both compressor capacity and overall system efficiency. Lower suction pressure at the compressor does not favorably raise capacity through compression ratio, since the opposite effect dominates. The pressure drop affects both oil return and capacity together, not oil return alone, and suction line pressure drop is a well established factor in compressor performance.
  61. The correct answer is B (The calculated superheat at the compressor inlet is approximately 12 degrees Celsius): Superheat equals the actual suction temperature minus the saturated evaporating temperature, so 4 C at the suction state minus negative 8 C at the metered state equals 12 C of superheat. This simplified method treats the metering device exit as approximately the same low side pressure and saturation temperature as the compressor inlet, which is a common assumption in basic cycle analysis. The other values come from subtracting the wrong pair of temperatures shown on the diagram.
  62. The correct answer is B (Polyolester oil, commonly abbreviated POE, formulated for hydrofluorocarbon refrigerant compatibility): POE oil is miscible with hydrofluorocarbon refrigerants like R-410A and R-134a and carries properly through the refrigerant circuit for reliable oil return to the compressor. Mineral oil is not miscible enough with these refrigerants and was used mainly with older R-22 and other CFC or HCFC systems. Alkylbenzene oil is sometimes blended in for HCFC applications but is not the standard choice for hydrofluorocarbon systems, and automotive engine oil is never an acceptable substitute in refrigeration equipment.
  63. The correct answer is A (Commissioning formally verifies and documents that the system meets design intent, beyond just starting it): Startup simply gets the equipment running, with refrigerant flowing and the compressor operating, while commissioning is the more thorough process of testing, measuring, and documenting that the system actually performs to the design specifications. Commissioning applies to systems of any size, not just larger installations, and it always happens after or alongside startup rather than before it. This distinction is why a commissioning report is a separate and more detailed document than a basic startup checklist.
  64. The correct answer is A (Isolate the tube from the framing with a cushioned, non-rigid grommet): A refrigerant line vibrating in direct contact with wood framing or drywall transmits compressor noise throughout the structure, sometimes described as a droning or buzzing sound far from the equipment. A rubber grommet, sleeve, or isolation pad at the penetration point breaks that rigid contact so vibration energy is absorbed instead of carried into the building.
  65. The correct answer is D (A2L indicates mild flammability, requiring flammable-rated tools and ignition source control.): In the ASHRAE 34 classification, the number and letter together describe toxicity and flammability, and the 2L designation specifically identifies a lower flammability refrigerant with a measurable burning velocity, unlike the nonflammable A1 class. This requires the technician to use recovery and leak detection equipment rated for flammable refrigerants and to control ignition sources during service. The classification is not related to manufacturing date and does not by itself prohibit standard cylinder use.
  66. The correct answer is C (Residual moisture or trapped gas is still outgassing inside the system): A rising micron reading after the pump is isolated typically means trapped moisture or non-condensable gas is still evaporating and releasing pressure inside the system, which is exactly the condition a triple evacuation with dry nitrogen breaks is designed to address by diluting and removing that residue over repeated cycles. Option A is a possible but far less common explanation and is not the first conclusion drawn from a slow steady rise. Option B is incorrect since no refrigerant charging has occurred at this stage of the process. Option D jumps to a hardware failure without first considering the much more common moisture explanation.
  67. The correct answer is D (If minimum modulated velocity still stays above the oil carrying threshold, one riser can work.): the deciding factor for whether a double riser is needed is not simply whether the system modulates at all, but whether vapour velocity at the lowest expected operating capacity still stays above the minimum threshold needed to entrain and carry oil upward through a single, appropriately sized riser. If the minimum modulated load still produces adequate velocity in one correctly sized pipe, a double riser arrangement is not necessary. This depends on the specific capacity range and riser diameter chosen, so neither a single riser nor a double riser is automatically mandatory for every variable capacity system without checking actual velocities.
  68. The correct answer is D (500 microns or lower is generally accepted as a sufficiently deep vacuum level): Reaching 500 microns or lower removes the vast majority of moisture and non-condensable gases from a sealed refrigeration system, which is why it is the widely accepted target for a completed deep vacuum evacuation. Option A at 5,000 microns is far too shallow and would leave significant moisture behind. Option B at 2,000 microns still leaves more residual moisture than an acceptable evacuation allows. Option C at 1,000 microns is closer but still not deep enough to be considered a properly finished evacuation by most manufacturers.
  69. The correct answer is D (Enthalpy remains constant while pressure drops sharply across the valve): A vertical line on a P-h diagram moves straight down along a single enthalpy value while pressure drops sharply, which correctly represents the throttling process across a metering device where no heat or work crosses the boundary and enthalpy is conserved. Pressure clearly does not stay constant, since the vertical line spans a large pressure range. Temperature actually drops during expansion rather than staying constant, and entropy increases during throttling rather than remaining fixed, so neither of those descriptions matches a vertical line.
  70. The correct answer is C (Filter replacement schedule, thermostat operation, and who to contact for warranty service issues): Practical client training focuses on the day to day tasks and information the owner's staff actually need, such as changing filters on schedule, operating the thermostat correctly, and knowing who to call for warranty service. Internal wiring diagrams, gauge calibration procedures, and labour billing rates are not appropriate topics for a maintenance staff training session. Covering the right material at handover helps the system continue performing as documented in the commissioning report.
  71. The correct answer is A (At least 30 minutes after the hot work has finished): a standard hot work permit requires a fire watch to remain in the area for a minimum period, commonly 30 minutes, after the torch is shut off, because smoldering material near the work area can ignite into open flame well after the visible heat source is removed. Option B ignores this real delayed ignition risk entirely. Option C is far too short a window to catch a delayed ignition, and option D describes an unnecessarily long period that is not the standard practice for this type of hot work.
  72. The correct answer is B (Identifying workplace hazards and recommending corrective actions through inspections): A joint health and safety committee is made up of worker and management representatives who conduct regular workplace inspections, identify hazards, and recommend corrective measures to improve health and safety conditions. Option A describes a labour relations function that falls outside the committee's health and safety mandate. Option C describes a purchasing decision that is not part of the committee's defined role. Option D describes customer service handling, which has no connection to the committee's purpose under the Act.
  73. The correct answer is C (Open Zone 2's balancing valve further, since its measured flow is well below design): The exhibit shows Zone 2's measured flow at 0.75 L/s against a design target of 1.2 L/s, a significant shortfall that will leave that zone underheated. Zones 1 and 3 are both already close to their design values and do not need adjustment. Opening Zone 2's balancing valve, then rechecking flow at all three zones, is the correct next step to bring the loop into balance.
  74. The correct answer is A (500 microns absolute, confirmed stable for at least ten to fifteen minutes with the pump isolated): a deep vacuum of 500 microns or lower removes residual moisture and non-condensable gases well below what any compound gauge can resolve. The vacuum pump must be valved off from the system so the micron gauge reading reflects the system holding vacuum, not the pump still running. A single momentary reading of 1500 microns does not confirm dryness, and a compound gauge reading near 29 inches of mercury or 0 kPa is far too coarse to verify an acceptable evacuation level.
  75. The correct answer is B (Once every five years, per the standard requalification interval for these cylinders): Refillable refrigerant recovery cylinders regulated under Transport Canada standards must be hydrostatically retested on a five-year interval to remain legal for continued transport and use. Option A states an interval that is too short for this cylinder type. Option C ignores that retesting is a recurring requirement, not a one-time event. Option D confuses the interval with a different class of pressure vessel.
  76. The correct answer is D (The system is overcharged, backing up excess liquid refrigerant in the condenser coil): On a fixed-orifice system, extra refrigerant that cannot be metered through the fixed restriction backs up in the condenser, submerging more coil surface in subcooled liquid and driving subcooling above target while superheat stays close to normal. Undercharging instead tends to reduce subcooling because there is less liquid refrigerant available to back up in the condenser. A severely iced evaporator or a weak compressor would each disturb superheat readings well beyond the normal range described here.
  77. The correct answer is D (It provides written proof that the installed system was tested and performs to the design specifications): A commissioning report is the formal record showing that measured readings, such as airflow, pressures, and temperatures, were checked against the design documents. It protects both the contractor and the client by documenting the system's actual performance at handover. Without it, there is no evidence the installation was verified rather than simply assumed to work.
  78. The correct answer is A (Restricted airflow through the dirty coil is raising condensing pressure): a condenser coil that cannot reject heat properly because of a dirty surface forces the refrigerant to condense at a higher pressure and temperature, and this effect worsens as ambient temperature rises through the afternoon. Once discharge pressure exceeds the high pressure switch setpoint, the switch opens and shuts the compressor down. Cleaning the condenser coil restores normal heat transfer and typically resolves the trips. A weak capacitor could reduce fan speed and contribute, but the described dirty coil is the more direct and likely cause here.
  79. The correct answer is B (It eliminates one brazed joint, reducing potential leak points in the run): Swaging expands the bore of one tube end just enough to accept the outside diameter of a matching tube, so the two pieces telescope together and only need a single brazed joint instead of a coupling with two joints. Fewer joints means fewer places for a future leak to develop over the life of the system.
  80. The correct answer is D (A separate airflow restriction elsewhere in the duct system is worsening independent of the filter): Because the filter itself is replaced fresh at every visit yet the baseline pressure drop still climbs visit over visit, the rising trend points to a separate and worsening restriction elsewhere in the duct system, such as a closing damper or a developing coil restriction, rather than the filter. A defective filter brand would not explain a rising trend measured immediately after each fresh replacement. Trending pressure drop over time is exactly the kind of predictive maintenance data a PM contract is designed to capture, not unreliable noise. Dismissing a consistent multi-visit upward trend as normal seasonal variation would miss a real developing problem.
  81. The correct answer is B (Whether the zone's actual peak cooling load has increased beyond original design assumptions): Since airflow and coil discharge temperature both verified correctly at commissioning, a seasonal complaint on the hottest afternoons points toward the zone's actual peak load exceeding what the original design assumed, such as added equipment, occupancy, or solar gain, rather than a system installation defect. Whether the report was formally signed does not affect the physical performance of the equipment in the field. Attendance at client training does not change the zone's actual cooling capacity or load. Diffuser finish colour is a cosmetic detail with zero effect on cooling performance.
  82. The correct answer is D (Each nitrogen break dilutes and helps carry out trapped non-condensable gases): A single deep evacuation can struggle to remove moisture and air trapped in pockets, oil films, or porous areas of the system. Breaking the vacuum with dry nitrogen dilutes whatever contamination remains, and each subsequent evacuation removes a further share of it, so the triple evacuation sequence achieves a cleaner, drier system than one long pull alone.
  83. The correct answer is C (A technician must physically press the manual reset relay to restart): A technician must physically press the manual reset relay to restart. Because the relay coil in the safety chain is a manual reset type, its contact stays open even after the high pressure switch itself closes again, which is a deliberate design so that a technician investigates the cause of the trip. Without pressing the reset button, the run rung remains open and the contactor coil cannot energize. This differs from an automatic reset switch, which would allow the compressor to restart on its own once pressure drops.
  84. The correct answer is C (Apply moderate thumb pressure at the belt's midpoint and check deflection distance): The standard field method for checking belt tension without a dedicated gauge is to press the belt at its midpoint between the two sheaves and compare the resulting deflection against the manufacturer's specification. A belt that squeals on startup usually indicates it is too loose rather than confirming correct tension, so that is not a reliable check. Amperage draw alone does not isolate belt tension from other electrical or mechanical variables. Replacing a belt on every visit regardless of condition wastes parts and labor when the existing belt may still be within spec.
  85. The correct answer is B (CAT III, rated for fixed installation equipment like distribution panels): CAT III meters are built with insulation and internal protection rated for the higher potential fault energy present at distribution panels and fixed wiring, which is exactly the environment described. CAT I meters in option A are intended for low energy electronic circuits, and CAT II meters in option C are rated for plug in appliances and receptacle circuits, both of which are lower energy environments than a distribution panel. Option D is incorrect because category rating is a real safety requirement tied to fault energy, not an optional detail.
  86. The correct answer is C (To confirm equipment and controls respond correctly together, in the order the design intended): Sequence of operation testing goes beyond confirming that individual pieces of equipment simply turn on, verifying instead that fans, dampers, valves, and controls respond to each other in the correct order and under the correct conditions the design intended. This kind of testing catches interlock and programming faults that a basic equipment startup would never reveal. It has no connection to electrical demand charges, resale value, or wire nut colour coding.
  87. The correct answer is B (The mechanical equipment schedule, which lists design flow rates for each piece of equipment): The mechanical equipment schedule is the standard drawing sheet listing each piece of HVAC equipment along with its design airflow, water flow, and pressure drop values used during balancing. Architectural floor plans show layout and dimensions but carry no mechanical performance data. Electrical single line diagrams document power distribution, not water or air flow rates. Structural drawings address building loading and framing, unrelated to mechanical flow rate documentation.
  88. The correct answer is A (A physical leak is present in the system and must be located and repaired): when the micron reading rises continuously and never levels off or plateaus, air is actively entering the system through a leak rather than residual moisture simply evaporating and stabilizing. Moisture boiling off typically produces a rise that slows and levels within a reasonable time as the moisture is depleted. A gauge calibration issue would generally show as a stuck or clearly implausible reading rather than a steady non-leveling climb, and no refrigerant charge has been added yet at this stage of the evacuation process.
  89. The correct answer is A (It must be readily accessible and within sight of the equipment): CSA C22.1 requires a disconnecting means that is readily accessible and located within sight of the air conditioning or refrigeration equipment it serves, allowing a technician to de-energize the unit safely before servicing. This requirement applies broadly to this class of equipment, not only to three-phase installations. Locating the disconnect out of sight or behind a locked door does not satisfy this code requirement.
  90. The correct answer is D (R-404A has an unusually high global warming potential compared to most other common refrigerants.): R-404A carries a global warming potential of roughly 3,922, making it one of the highest GWP refrigerants still found in existing low-temperature commercial refrigeration equipment. This high GWP has led many jurisdictions to restrict or phase down its use in new equipment, pushing the industry toward lower-GWP alternatives for similar low-temperature applications. R-404A contains no chlorine and has zero ozone depletion potential, and it is rated A1 nonflammable, so neither of those factors is the concern driving its phase-down. Technicians specifying new equipment today are increasingly expected to recommend lower-GWP options in place of R-404A wherever a suitable alternative exists.
  91. The correct answer is D (Reject or fully separate the mixture, since mixed refrigerant cannot meet purity specification): A batch containing two mixed refrigerant types cannot meet the AHRI purity specification required for reclaimed product, so a licensed reclaimer must either reject the batch or fully separate the mixture before it can be certified and resold. Option A is false because reclamation does not automatically correct contamination from mixed refrigerants. Option B and option C both describe unsafe and noncompliant shortcuts that do not resolve the underlying contamination.
  92. The correct answer is D (verify the riser still meets minimum velocity requirements at the lowest expected operating capacity): a riser sized purely for full-load flow can easily be oversized relative to the reduced flow the system sees during light loads, and oversizing lowers gas velocity exactly when oil return becomes hardest to maintain. Before signing off, the technician should check the riser against minimum load velocity requirements, not just the full-load condition, and recommend a dual riser or resized pipe if the minimum load case fails. Increasing the diameter further would worsen a potential minimum load velocity problem rather than fix it, and removing the trap is unrelated to and unsupported by a sizing review.
  93. The correct answer is C (Leaving it connected would overheat and eventually destroy the start capacitor): Leaving it connected would overheat and eventually destroy the start capacitor. Start capacitors are designed for brief, high-current duty during the starting cycle only, not continuous operation. If the potential relay failed to drop out and remove the start capacitor, the sustained current would generate excessive heat and cause the capacitor to fail, often rupturing. The potential relay senses back EMF from the start winding to time this disconnection accurately.
  94. The correct answer is B (The VFD lets the compressor match output to actual load, reducing cycling losses): The VFD lets the compressor match output to actual load, reducing cycling losses. A variable frequency drive allows the compressor to slow down and continuously match its output to the actual part-load demand rather than repeatedly starting and stopping at full speed, which reduces the efficiency losses and mechanical wear associated with frequent cycling and improves comfort or process stability. This is particularly valuable when a system spends most of its operating hours well below the peak design condition, which is common in many real applications. A VFD does not remove the need for correct piping or condenser sizing, and efficiency still varies with load and design even on a variable-speed machine.
  95. The correct answer is D (A grounded winding, where insulation breakdown allows current to leak to the shell): A healthy compressor winding should show very high insulation resistance, typically many megohms, between any terminal and the grounded shell, so a low kilohm reading means winding insulation has broken down and current is leaking to the case, a grounded winding fault. A shorted winding is a separate test performed terminal to terminal, not terminal to shell. An open winding produces infinite resistance, the opposite pattern. A low kilohm reading to ground is well outside acceptable insulation tolerance and signals a real fault.
  96. The correct answer is C (A2L, meaning mildly flammable refrigerant with low overall toxicity rating): Both R-32 and R-454B carry the A2L classification, meaning they have low toxicity but are mildly flammable, which is why installers must follow specific charge-size and leak-detection precautions that older A1 refrigerants did not require. A1 describes nonflammable, low-toxicity refrigerants such as R-410A instead. B1 and A3 describe higher toxicity and much greater flammability respectively, neither of which applies to these two refrigerants.
  97. The correct answer is C (Basic thermostat and BAS graphic navigation for routine setpoint adjustments by staff): Client training at handover focuses on what building staff actually need to operate the systems day to day, such as basic thermostat use and simple BAS graphic navigation for routine adjustments. Detailed pitot tube traverse calculations are technical balancing procedures irrelevant to daily building operation by staff. Instrument calibration procedures are specialist test and balance tasks, not something building operating staff perform. Purchase pricing details are commercial information unrelated to operating the mechanical systems.
  98. The correct answer is D (The installation must pass inspection and receive approval from the provincial authority): The installation must pass inspection and receive approval from the provincial authority. Under CSA B52 as adopted by provincial regulators, a refrigeration system above the applicable threshold requires a permit before installation and a field inspection by the provincial authority, such as TSSA in Ontario, before it can be legally operated. Manufacturer conformance certificates and the contractor's internal quality checks support the inspection process but do not replace the regulatory sign-off itself. Electrical utility approval addresses the electrical service connection and is a separate requirement from the mechanical refrigeration inspection.
  99. The correct answer is D (Unit B, since its rated capacity is the smallest unit that still exceeds the design load): the exhibit shows a design load of 42.0 kW after the safety factor is applied. Unit A at 35.0 kW falls below the design load and cannot meet peak demand. Unit B at 44.5 kW is the smallest listed unit that still exceeds 42.0 kW, meeting the load with a reasonable margin. Unit C at 52.0 kW is oversized well beyond the requirement and would run for shorter periods, increasing short cycling and reducing dehumidification.
  100. The correct answer is D (The compressor motor is mechanically locked and cannot physically turn the rotor): Normal winding resistance readings rule out an electrical fault like a grounded or open winding as the cause of the failure to start. A very high momentary amperage draw combined with an immediate overload trip is the classic signature of a locked rotor, where the motor is energized and trying to turn but the compressor mechanism itself is physically seized. A failed open capacitor would reduce starting torque and current rather than spike it to a very high momentary value. A locked rotor condition means the compressor must be replaced since the mechanical seizure cannot be repaired.
  101. The correct answer is A (Whether the site can meet CSA B52 ventilation, detection, and structural requirements for each location): an indoor machine room location must satisfy CSA B52 ventilation, refrigerant detection, and construction requirements specific to enclosed spaces, while a rooftop location must satisfy structural loading, clearance, and access requirements appropriate to an exterior installation. The placement decision should be driven by whether the specific site can practically and code compliantly meet the applicable set of requirements for each option under consideration. Equipment paint colour and manufacturer branding have no bearing on this code driven decision, and CSA B52 does not reference or restrict specific equipment brands. Construction scheduling is a project management consideration and does not determine which location satisfies the applicable code requirements.
  102. The correct answer is C (Measure suction line temperature and saturation temperature at the same point, then find the difference): Superheat is found by measuring the actual suction line temperature at the evaporator outlet and comparing it to the saturation temperature corresponding to the measured suction pressure at that same point. The difference between these two values, when compared against the manufacturer's rated range, confirms whether the system is performing to design intent. Liquid line temperature, amperage draw, and discharge line temperature do not by themselves establish superheat.
  103. The correct answer is A (A1, meaning both refrigerants are nonflammable with low toxicity): R-410A and R-134a are both classified A1, meaning they are nonflammable and have low toxicity, which is why older equipment using them did not require the flammable-refrigerant handling precautions newer A2L refrigerants demand. The A2L, A2, and B2L classifications each describe some degree of flammability or elevated toxicity that does not apply to either of these two refrigerants. Knowing a refrigerant's classification affects storage, charging, and leak response procedures on the job site.
  104. The correct answer is C (Recover the refrigerant charge into an approved cylinder before opening the circuit): Recovering the refrigerant before opening any circuit is required by the halocarbon or environmental protection regulation of the province where the work is performed, which prohibits deliberate venting for private-sector systems such as this rooftop unit. The federal Ozone-depleting Substances and Halocarbon Alternatives Regulations made under CEPA 1999 instead control the manufacture, import and export of these substances nationally, and a separate federal regulation, the Federal Halocarbon Regulations, imposes a recovery and no-venting duty only on systems located on federal or Indigenous lands or owned by federal departments and Crown corporations. Option A describes the exact conduct every jurisdiction's venting prohibition exists to stop. Option B invents a fire department notification duty that does not exist. Option D is not a recognized substitute for proper recovery into a cylinder.
  105. The correct answer is A (The compressor contactor never energizes, so the compressor cannot start): the contactor coil rung depends entirely on the time delay relay's normally open contact closing to complete the path to C. If that contact fails to close, no current can reach the contactor coil regardless of how long the thermostat calls for cooling. This failure mode protects the compressor from short cycling but leaves the space uncooled until the relay is replaced.
  106. The correct answer is A (A steeper sling angle reduces the tension force on each sling leg): as the angle between a sling leg and the horizontal plane decreases, the tension in that leg increases dramatically for the same load, so a steeper angle closer to vertical keeps tension lower and well within the sling's rated capacity. Option B is factually wrong since angle has a very direct and significant effect on tension. Option C states the opposite of reality, since a shallower angle reduces, not increases, effective capacity, and option D is incorrect because this same trigonometric relationship applies to chain slings just as it does to wire rope.
  107. The correct answer is B (Checking belt tension, electrical connections, and refrigerant pressures in addition to the filter): A standard quarterly PM scope typically expands beyond a simple filter check to include belt tension, tightening electrical connections, and verifying refrigerant pressures are within normal range, giving a broader picture of system health than a monthly visual filter inspection alone. Replacing a compressor is a major repair triggered by a diagnosed failure, not a routine quarterly task. Rewiring an entire control circuit is not part of preventive maintenance unless a specific fault requires it. Relocating equipment is a capital project, not a maintenance contract task.
  108. The correct answer is C (Specify standby or redundant capacity so a single equipment failure does not cause total loss of cooling): for a critical hospital refrigeration load, sound design practice builds in standby or redundant equipment capacity so that a single compressor or component failure does not result in complete loss of cooling to a life-safety-relevant load, which a single unit design cannot guarantee regardless of its size. A single oversized unit remains a single point of failure no matter its capacity. Relying on general building HVAC as an informal backup does not provide the dedicated reliability a critical refrigeration application requires, and CSA B52 addressing critical facilities is not a reason to omit sound redundancy planning.
  109. The correct answer is A (The Internal Responsibility System, where everyone shares safety accountability): the Internal Responsibility System is the core principle underlying the Ontario Occupational Health and Safety Act, holding that everyone in a workplace, from workers to supervisors to the employer, shares responsibility for identifying and controlling hazards rather than relying on a single party. Option B misrepresents the role of government inspectors, who enforce the Act but are not the internal mechanism itself. Option C incorrectly places all liability on the employer alone, and option D is false because the Act's requirements are legally binding, not voluntary.
  110. The correct answer is C (This unit achieves an energy efficiency ratio of approximately 10): EER is calculated by dividing the cooling capacity in BTU per hour by the electrical input power in watts, so 30,000 divided by 3,000 equals an EER of 10. The other values would result from dividing by an incorrect power figure or rounding the capacity value incorrectly before dividing. A higher EER indicates the unit produces more cooling output for each watt of electricity consumed.
  111. The correct answer is B (Superheat method, since fixed-orifice devices are charged and verified using superheat, not subcooling): a fixed orifice, whether a piston or a capillary tube, has no active feedback mechanism to regulate flow with changing conditions, so superheat is the trade-standard method used to verify and fine-tune the charge on this device type. Subcooling is instead the standard method for TXV-equipped systems, which actively regulate superheat themselves. Claiming both devices are always charged identically or that fixed orifice systems need no verification at all both contradict standard commissioning practice and would leave the documentation inaccurate.
  112. The correct answer is A (The resistance to airflow created by all ductwork, filters, and coils outside the cabinet): Total external static pressure represents everything the blower must overcome that sits outside its own housing, including filters, coils, and duct runs. It is a key figure because exceeding the manufacturer's rated maximum reduces airflow and can shorten blower motor life. It has nothing to do with refrigerant pressure, motor voltage, or static electricity buildup on duct materials.
  113. The correct answer is A (The system suction pressure and saturated temperature drop, reducing compressor capacity and efficiency): The system suction pressure and saturated temperature drop, reducing compressor capacity and efficiency. Excess pressure drop in the suction line lowers the pressure and corresponding saturation temperature seen at the compressor inlet compared to the evaporator, which reduces both compressor capacity and efficiency for a given evaporator condition. This is a separate effect from velocity, which governs oil entrainment rather than pressure loss, so a line can have adequate velocity and still be oversized on pressure drop. Designers typically limit suction line pressure drop to an equivalent of about 1°C of saturation temperature loss.
  114. The correct answer is A (New import of virgin R-22 for use in Canada is no longer permitted): Canada's phase-out prohibits new production and import of virgin R-22 for use in the country, and a manufacture date preceding the phase-out does not create an exception for an import happening afterward. Option B incorrectly treats manufacture date as controlling rather than the import itself. Option C invents a geographic exemption that does not exist. Option D invents a quantity threshold that has no basis in the actual restriction.
  115. The correct answer is B (Obtaining inspection sign-off from the authority having jurisdiction before initial charging): once pressure testing and evacuation are complete, CSA B52 and provincial regulation typically require the authority having jurisdiction to inspect the installed piping and equipment and provide sign-off before the mechanic proceeds to the initial refrigerant charge. This sequence ensures the piping and safety devices are verified compliant before refrigerant, which introduces its own hazards, is introduced into the system. Submitting as-built drawings a full year later does not satisfy this pre-charge inspection requirement, and pressure relief devices must remain installed throughout, not removed before charging. Charging first and requesting inspection afterward reverses the required sequence and would not meet regulatory requirements.
  116. The correct answer is C (Confirm the refrigerant has been fully recovered and the area is ventilated and leak free.): R-32 is an A2L mildly flammable refrigerant, so before introducing an open flame the technician must confirm recovery is complete, the workspace is ventilated, and no residual flammable vapour remains. Raising the torch temperature does nothing to reduce flammability risk and increases hazard instead. A2L refrigerants do not self-extinguish, so full recovery before hot work remains mandatory regardless of refrigerant type.
  117. The correct answer is B (Lower toxicity with mild flammability, meaning it requires a spark and burns slowly): in the ASHRAE 34 classification referenced by CSA B52, the letter A indicates lower toxicity and the number 2L indicates mild flammability with a lower burning velocity than class 2 or 3 refrigerants. This means A2L refrigerants can ignite under specific conditions but burn much more slowly than a highly flammable refrigerant such as propane, which is classified A3. This is distinct from B classifications, which indicate higher toxicity, and from class 1, which indicates no flame propagation at all. Recognizing A2L correctly is essential for applying the right machine room, ventilation, and clearance requirements.
  118. The correct answer is C (A deficiency section documenting each out of tolerance reading along with a proposed corrective action): A complete commissioning report must explain any reading that falls outside design tolerance, typically in a deficiency or exceptions section listing the specific reading, the tolerance it missed, and the recommended corrective action so the consultant and client understand what remains outstanding. Listing personal certifications does not explain individual out of tolerance readings. Purchase invoices belong with procurement records, not a performance verification report. Unrelated safety incidents belong in separate site safety documentation, not the technical commissioning report.
  119.
