The correct answer is B (Flux is required on the copper to brass joint but not the copper to copper joint.): phosphorus copper brazing alloy is self fluxing on copper to copper joints because the phosphorus content promotes flow without additional flux, but that self fluxing property does not apply once a dissimilar metal such as brass or steel is introduced. A copper to brass joint therefore needs a separate flux applied to the joint surfaces to allow the alloy to wet and flow properly. Skipping flux on a dissimilar metal joint typically produces a poor, weak, or leaking braze.
  120. The correct answer is D (Approximately 6 amperes, based on dividing 240 volts by 40 ohms): Approximately 6 amperes, based on dividing 240 volts by 40 ohms. In a series circuit, total resistance equals the sum of the individual resistances, so 15 ohms plus 25 ohms equals 40 ohms combined resistance. Applying Ohm's Law, current equals voltage divided by resistance, so 240 volts divided by 40 ohms equals 6 amperes flowing through the entire series circuit. Using only one element's resistance instead of the combined series total is a common calculation mistake that produces an incorrect current value.
  121. The correct answer is D (Repair the leak first, verify it, then recharge the system to specification): Correct procedure repairs and verifies the leak before recharging, since adding refrigerant to an unrepaired leak wastes product and allows continued emissions. Option A reverses this sequence and is not an acceptable practice. Option B falsely makes leak repair optional and dependent on a customer request. Option C wrongly treats residual pressure as a reason to skip a needed repair.
  122. The correct answer is D (Size the line as large as possible while still maintaining minimum velocity for oil return): suction line sizing is a balance between two opposing effects of pipe diameter. A larger diameter reduces pressure drop, which lowers compressor work and improves capacity, but it also lowers refrigerant velocity, and velocity below the minimum threshold fails to carry entrained oil back to the compressor. The correct design approach selects the largest diameter that still keeps velocity above the minimum needed for oil return at the lowest expected load condition. Choosing either extreme diameter ignores one of the two requirements and risks either excess compressor work or oil starvation at the compressor.
  123. The correct answer is B (A compound gauge cannot resolve the fine vacuum depth needed to confirm moisture removal): a mechanical compound gauge's scale is coarse near its high-vacuum end, so it appears to read a deep vacuum long before moisture has actually finished boiling off, making it unsuitable for confirming a target such as 500 microns. An electronic micron gauge measures far more precisely in this deep vacuum range, which is exactly why it is required for evacuation verification. A compound gauge does display vacuum, just imprecisely, its usefulness has nothing to do with whether the refrigerant is a blend, and the two gauge types never read numerically identical values in this range.
  124. The correct answer is A (inspect the flare face for a crack or an uneven seat rather than simply overtightening it): a leaking flare joint at proper torque is often caused by a cracked flare, debris on the seat, or an out-of-round flare rather than insufficient clamping force. Overtightening beyond the recommended torque can crack a sound flare or deform the seat further, making the leak worse instead of solving it. A larger flare nut is not a compatible fix once the tube has already been flared to a specific size, and flux is not a sealant for a mechanical flare connection. Re-forming the same tube end without first identifying the defect risks repeating the same failure.
  125. The correct answer is A (On the compressor housing itself, at a point close to the mounting feet or base): Vibration readings must be taken directly on the vibrating component, close to its mounting points, to accurately capture the compressor's actual running condition. Readings taken on an unrelated panel, the line set, or an indoor thermostat plate would not reflect the compressor's true vibration level. This placement is essential for comparing the reading against the manufacturer's acceptable vibration limit during commissioning.

Practice Test 3 Answer Key

  1. The correct answer is A (Isolating pads reduce vibration and noise transmission from the unit into the structure.): vibration isolating pads placed between a condensing unit's base rails and a rigid concrete pad absorb and dampen operational vibration from the compressor and fan motors, reducing the amount of vibration and associated noise transmitted into the pad and surrounding structure. This is especially important where the equipment pad connects to or sits near occupied building areas. Isolating pads have no relationship to electrical code connection requirements or refrigerant capacity, and the unit must still be properly levelled during installation regardless of whether isolators are used.
  2. The correct answer is D (Maintaining adequate oil-carrying velocity across the full range of load conditions): Maintaining adequate oil-carrying velocity across the full range of load conditions. A single riser sized for full load velocity would run too slowly to entrain oil at minimum load, while a riser sized for minimum load would create excessive pressure drop at full load, so a double riser splits the flow into a smaller always-active riser and a larger riser that only carries gas at higher loads. This keeps velocity high enough in whichever riser is active to lift oil back to the compressor across the whole turndown range. A trapped base and periodic traps on a tall riser further help collect and lift oil in stages.
  3. The correct answer is B (It prevents warm moist air from reaching the cold pipe and condensing there.): sealing every seam in the vapour barrier prevents warm, moisture laden ambient air from migrating through gaps to the cold pipe surface, where it would condense, saturate the insulation, and eventually cause corrosion, mold growth, or dripping. A gap in the vapour barrier defeats the insulation's purpose even if the insulation thickness itself is otherwise adequate. This sealing step is about moisture control rather than thermal expansion or heat gain, and it has no relationship to protecting the tubing from scratches.
  4. The correct answer is D (It is the temperature at which liquid and vapour refrigerant coexist at that pressure.): For any given refrigerant, there is a fixed relationship between pressure and the temperature at which it boils or condenses, known as the saturation temperature. At -18°C and the corresponding low side pressure, liquid refrigerant inside the evaporator is boiling into vapour while both phases exist together in the coil. This saturation temperature is not the same as the actual suction line temperature measured further downstream, because the vapour continues absorbing superheat as it travels toward the compressor. Reading the P-T chart correctly is the first step in calculating superheat and subcooling accurately.
  5. The correct answer is D (the micron gauge is positioned at the system, not the pump, and holds steady once isolated): a micron gauge mounted directly on the vacuum pump can show an artificially deep reading caused by pressure drop across the hoses and fittings rather than the true condition inside the system. Placing the gauge at the far end of the system and confirming the reading holds steady once the pump is valved off shows the vacuum level actually achieved in the piping itself. The pump's own gauge matching the remote gauge does not rule out that both are reading the pump side inaccurately. A fixed time duration alone, without reference to the actual micron reading, does not confirm dehydration is complete.
  6. The correct answer is C (The rating of the overcurrent device protecting that specific branch circuit): The rating of the overcurrent device protecting that specific branch circuit. CSA C22.1 sizes equipment grounding conductors based on the rating of the branch circuit overcurrent device, using a table that pairs conductor size to that rating rather than to the connected load's actual horsepower or the wiring distance. This ensures the grounding conductor can safely carry enough fault current to operate the overcurrent device quickly during a ground fault. Using a conductor smaller than the code table requires is a common installation deficiency found during inspection.
  7. The correct answer is A (Use dedicated equipment specifically listed and rated for use with A2L refrigerants.): Recovery machines, gauges, and leak detectors intended for A1 nonflammable refrigerants are not automatically safe or approved for use with mildly flammable A2L refrigerants, since ignition risk changes the required equipment listing. A shop working on both refrigerant classes needs dedicated equipment specifically rated for A2L service to avoid an ignition hazard. Simply increasing ventilation or restricting A1 equipment to only one step does not address the underlying equipment rating requirement.
  8. The correct answer is A (Screw compressors provide continuous capacity modulation and high reliability at large capacities): Screw compressors provide continuous capacity modulation and high reliability at large capacities. Twin-screw compressors use a sliding valve mechanism to continuously vary displaced volume rather than cycling on and off, which gives smooth capacity modulation and high reliability with fewer wearing parts than a bank of reciprocating cylinders at the same total capacity. They rely on oil injection for both lubrication and cooling of the compression process, so they are not oil-free, and they scale up to very large capacities, which is a large part of why they dominate industrial process refrigeration. Screw compressors are in fact one of the most common compressor types used with ammonia in large industrial systems.
  9. The correct answer is D (Heating COP is higher because rejected heat plus compressor work both count as useful output.): In cooling mode, the useful output is only the heat absorbed at the evaporator, while the heat rejected at the condenser is treated as waste sent outdoors. In heating mode, the indoor coil becomes the condenser, so both the heat originally absorbed from outdoors and the heat equivalent of the compressor's own work output are delivered as useful heat indoors. Because the numerator of the COP formula effectively includes an extra term equal to the compressor work, heating COP is mathematically always at least one full unit higher than the equivalent cooling COP. This relationship holds true even though the physical refrigerant circuit and compressor are unchanged between the two operating modes.
  10. The correct answer is B (The refrigerant rejects heat, first desuperheating and then condensing into a liquid): The hot discharge vapour first cools down to its saturation temperature in the desuperheating portion of the condenser, then rejects latent heat as it condenses at a constant temperature into a high pressure liquid. Any additional cooling beyond that point produces subcooled liquid before the refrigerant leaves the condenser. This entire process depends on the condensing medium, air or water, being cooler than the refrigerant's saturation temperature.
  11. The correct answer is D (A thermostatic expansion valve, metering refrigerant based on evaporator outlet superheat): the triangle pointed into a diaphragm body with a capillary bulb strapped to the downstream suction line is the standard symbol and physical arrangement for a thermostatic expansion valve, which senses evaporator outlet superheat through the bulb and modulates liquid flow into the coil accordingly. A solenoid valve is shown with an electrical coil rather than a diaphragm and sensing bulb. A relief valve protects against overpressure rather than metering flow, and a filter drier is a straight-through body without a modulating diaphragm or sensing bulb.
  12. The correct answer is C (Convert each velocity pressure to velocity, then average the resulting velocity values together): Because the relationship between velocity pressure and velocity is not linear, each individual velocity pressure reading from the traverse must first be converted to a velocity value before those velocities are averaged together. Averaging the raw velocity pressure readings first, or using only the highest or wall-adjacent reading, produces an inaccurate final airflow figure. This correct sequence is essential before the average velocity is used to calculate total duct airflow in CFM.
  13. The correct answer is C (It keeps the internal spring under tension, causing calibration drift over time): leaving a click type torque wrench set at a torque value keeps its internal spring compressed, and over time that constant tension can cause the spring to weaken and the wrench to drift out of calibration. The correct habit is to back the wrench off to its lowest setting before storage. Rust risk in option A is unrelated to the torque setting itself, warranty terms in option B vary by manufacturer and are not the core technical issue, and option D is false since click type wrenches specifically do require periodic recalibration.
  14. The correct answer is A (24 volts, matching the standard control transformer secondary voltage rating): Ohm's Law states V = I × R, so multiplying 0.2 amps by 120 ohms gives 24 volts. This confirms the transformer secondary is producing the standard 24 volt control voltage used for thermostat and contactor coil circuits. A reading significantly different from 24 volts would point to a failing transformer or a shorted control circuit. Technicians rely on this calculation to verify transformer output without needing a direct voltage reading.
  15. The correct answer is C (500 microns or lower, confirmed on a calibrated electronic micron gauge.): Industry-accepted practice for commissioning most air conditioning and refrigeration systems targets 500 microns absolute pressure as the point at which the system is considered dry and free of enough non-condensable gas to charge safely. Reaching this level requires a calibrated electronic micron gauge, not a compound gauge or a positive pressure hold. Deeper vacuums do not collapse properly rated refrigeration-grade copper tubing. This target is verified before any refrigerant is introduced.
  16. The correct answer is C (Q = m x c x delta-T, using mass, specific heat, and temperature change): Sensible heat changes a substance's temperature without changing its state, so the formula multiplies mass by specific heat capacity by the temperature difference. The latent heat formula instead uses the heat of vaporization and applies during a phase change at constant temperature. The other two formulas do not represent any recognized heat transfer relationship used in refrigeration calculations.
  17. The correct answer is B (It removes only superheat from the discharge gas to preheat water before full condensation): a desuperheater is designed to extract the sensible superheat energy from the discharge gas, which is at its highest temperature immediately after compression, transferring that heat to preheat water, without condensing the refrigerant itself, which still proceeds to the main condenser afterward. Subcooling liquid before the expansion valve is a separate function performed elsewhere in the circuit. A desuperheater does not replace the main condenser at any time of year, and it does not regulate suction pressure.
  18. The correct answer is C (Take a velocity reading at the centroid of each equal area and average all readings): The equal area method divides the duct into a grid of equal sized zones so each reading represents the same proportion of total airflow. Averaging the centroid readings from every zone accounts for the velocity profile across the duct rather than assuming uniform flow. A single center point reading misses the slower velocity near the duct walls caused by friction. Static pressure alone cannot substitute for a direct velocity traverse when verifying actual delivered airflow.
  19. The correct answer is C (A hot wire thermal anemometer, since its small sensing tip reads low velocities in confined openings): Hot wire anemometers use a heated element whose cooling rate is proportional to air velocity, allowing accurate readings even at low speeds and in small diffuser necks. Rotating vane anemometers need enough open area for the blade to spin freely and lose accuracy below a certain velocity threshold. Pitot tubes are better suited to higher velocity duct traverses, not low velocity diffuser faces. Cup anemometers are designed for outdoor meteorological wind measurement, not indoor HVAC balancing work.
  20. The correct answer is C (A restricted metering device is starving the evaporator of refrigerant flow): A restriction at the metering device limits how much refrigerant can enter the evaporator, so suction pressure drops, the limited refrigerant fully boils off early and picks up excess superheat, and the compressor works harder compressing this low-density, high-superheat vapour, raising discharge temperature. An overcharged or flooded evaporator would instead produce low superheat, not excessive superheat. Neither strong condenser airflow nor a leaking discharge valve produces this specific combination of low suction pressure with high superheat.
  21. The correct answer is A (Lock out and verify isolation of both the electrical and gas energy sources): A combination unit with more than one energy source, such as electrical power and fuel gas, requires every source capable of causing harm to be locked out and verified isolated, not just the one directly related to the planned task. Option B wrongly assumes gas valves cannot be locked, when lockable gas valve devices are commonly available and required here. Option C ignores the electrical hazard entirely, even though the cabinet remains energized and dangerous. Option D substitutes a sign for physical isolation, which does not meet lockout requirements for multiple energy sources.
  22. The correct answer is C (The system should be pulled down to approximately 500 microns or lower.): standard practice for most refrigerant systems is to evacuate to roughly 500 microns or lower, a level fine enough that a manifold compound gauge, which only reads accurately to a few thousand microns at best, cannot reliably measure it, which is exactly why a dedicated micron gauge is connected directly to the system. A reading of 5000 microns still indicates significant residual moisture or air remaining in the system. Relying on the compound gauge reading zero or 29 inches of mercury does not confirm the deep vacuum level actually needed.
  23. The correct answer is A (Compressor overheating and motor damage caused by severely inadequate refrigerant flow rate): Compressor overheating and motor damage caused by severely inadequate refrigerant flow rate. When refrigerant charge is critically low, suction pressure drops far below normal, reducing the mass flow of refrigerant returning to the compressor for motor cooling and lubrication. The low pressure switch senses this abnormally low suction pressure and opens the control circuit before the compressor can overheat and suffer permanent damage. On a fixed orifice system, this loss of charge protection is especially important since there is no electronic metering device to compensate.
  24. The correct answer is B (Webbing fibres are cut, frayed, or show visible burn damage): Cut, frayed, or burned webbing fibres directly weaken the load bearing capacity of a harness and represent an immediate failure risk, which is why any such damage requires the harness to be pulled from service right away. Option A describes cosmetic fading from sun exposure, which alone does not confirm structural weakening without further inspection. Option C describes a tag that is still legible, which does not by itself condemn the harness. Option D describes age alone, which matters for inspection frequency but is not automatically disqualifying without other physical evidence of damage.
  25. The correct answer is C (Very low suction pressure with abnormally high superheat readings.): A TXV stuck fully closed blocks nearly all liquid refrigerant from reaching the evaporator, starving the coil of refrigerant and causing suction pressure to fall well below normal while superheat climbs abnormally high because the small amount of refrigerant that does pass boils off almost immediately. High suction pressure with near-zero superheat instead describes a flooding condition from an overly open valve, the opposite fault. Normal suction pressure and elevated discharge pressure are not consistent with a valve that is almost completely blocked.
  26. The correct answer is A (Eliminating ignition sources and confirming ventilation before connecting recovery equipment): A2L refrigerants such as R-32 are mildly flammable, so technicians must control ignition sources and confirm adequate ventilation before and during recovery, a precaution not required for nonflammable A1 refrigerants. Option B describes protection against toxic exposure, which is not the primary concern for a mildly flammable low-toxicity refrigerant. Option C invents a pressure requirement unrelated to flammability safety. Option D is false since A2L refrigerants are recovered using appropriately rated equipment, not skipped.
  27. The correct answer is D (It represents a normally closed contact that allows current until opened): a contact symbol drawn touching in its resting state represents a normally closed contact, meaning current flows through it until something, such as rising pressure or temperature, causes it to open and interrupt the circuit. This is the standard symbol convention used for safety switches like high pressure or high temperature limits. A normally open contact, by contrast, is drawn with a visible gap between the two lines.
  28. The correct answer is D (Charge the refrigerant as liquid by inverting the cylinder before charging): Without a dip tube, the cylinder must be inverted so liquid, not vapour, is drawn out, because pulling vapour off a blend removes the lighter, more volatile component faster and leaves the remaining charge with a shifted, incorrect composition. Charging entirely as vapour or mixing liquid and vapour partway through both risk this same fractionation problem. Fractionation risk exists throughout the entire charging process, not only near the end, which is why liquid charging is required from the start.
  29. The correct answer is C (It creates a pressure drop that reduces the refrigerant's saturation temperature): A metering device, whether a fixed orifice, capillary tube, or thermostatic expansion valve, restricts refrigerant flow enough to create a significant pressure drop between the high side and the low side of the system. That pressure drop lowers the refrigerant's saturation temperature so it can boil at a temperature cold enough to absorb heat from the conditioned space in the evaporator. Filtering contaminants is instead the job of a liquid line filter drier, not the metering device itself.
  30. The correct answer is D (Test and record the atmosphere, complete the permit, then station an attendant): A permit required confined space entry demands that the atmosphere be tested and the results recorded before the permit is finalized, and a qualified attendant must be stationed outside before anyone enters. Option A has workers entering before any testing occurs, which defeats the purpose of the permit system entirely. Option B allows entry before the atmosphere is confirmed safe. Option C delays atmospheric testing until after entry has already begun, which exposes the worker to an unassessed hazard.
  31. The correct answer is A (Fully recover the remaining refrigerant charge before the equipment is disposed of or scrapped.): Before any equipment is disposed of or scrapped, all remaining refrigerant must be fully recovered, since deliberate venting is prohibited regardless of whether the equipment is being decommissioned. Scrap processors are not equipped or authorized to vent refrigerant themselves. Refrigerant does not simply dissipate once a compressor stops, and this obligation is not waived for smaller charge sizes.
  32. The correct answer is A (Filter condition, belt tension, electrical readings, and coil cleanliness observations): A thorough preventive maintenance checklist documents the overall mechanical and electrical condition of the unit, not just refrigerant pressures, so future technicians can track trends like gradually loosening belts or rising electrical readings over successive visits. Recording only outdoor temperature or travel mileage provides no useful diagnostic information about the unit's actual condition. A single checkmark confirming the unit powered on tells a future technician nothing about component wear or developing problems. Comprehensive documentation at each visit supports both better troubleshooting and a stronger record for the client's maintenance contract.
  33. The correct answer is A (Retain the records for the period required by regulation in case of inspection or audit.): Repair documentation, retest results, and refrigerant transfer records must be retained for the period required by regulation, since these records are what demonstrate compliance if an inspection or audit occurs. Discarding them once an invoice is paid removes evidence the company may later need. This retention expectation is not limited to systems above an arbitrary one thousand kilogram threshold, and it is not merely optional paperwork.
  34. The correct answer is D (The oversized unit satisfies the load quickly and short cycles, reducing run time and dehumidification): an oversized condensing unit removes heat from the space much faster than the actual calculated load requires, satisfying the thermostat setpoint quickly and shutting off before it has run long enough to also remove adequate moisture from the air, since dehumidification depends heavily on sustained run time across the coil. This produces the described pattern of frequent short cycling and poor humidity control even though total cooling capacity is more than adequate. The unit is not operating below a minimum threshold that prevents cycling, it is cycling excessively because it satisfies demand too quickly. While wiring problems can cause short cycling in some cases, the described symptoms directly match the known behaviour of an oversized cooling system, and equipment sizing is the more likely explanation given the two size increase described.
  35. The correct answer is D (Discharge the capacitor safely using an insulated resistor across its terminals): a run or start capacitor can hold a dangerous stored charge even after power is removed from the unit, so discharging it safely is always the required first step before handling or testing. Only after discharging is it safe to connect meter leads and read the capacitance value against the nameplate rating. Skipping this step risks a painful shock and can also damage the meter.
  36. The correct answer is A (Have the cylinder hydrostatically retested and requalified before it is filled again.): A refillable recovery cylinder whose Transport Canada requalification date has expired must be hydrostatically retested and requalified before it can legally be filled again, since the outdated stamp no longer confirms the cylinder's structural integrity under pressure. Requalification requirements do apply to recovery cylinders, not only virgin refrigerant cylinders. Reducing the fill weight or simply repainting the cylinder does not restore a valid requalification status.
  37. The correct answer is A (Location, specific equipment identification, description of the issue, and the trade responsible for correction): A usable deficiency entry needs enough specificity for anyone reading it later to find and fix the item, which means location, the specific equipment tag or identifier, a clear description of what is wrong, and which trade is responsible for the correction. A general room name alone is not specific enough to locate one deficiency among many pieces of equipment. A personal opinion about workmanship quality is not an actionable, specific description of a defect. A photo without any written description leaves ambiguity about exactly what issue is being flagged and where.
  38. The correct answer is A (7°C, found by subtracting the liquid line temperature from the saturation temperature): subcooling is always the saturated condensing temperature minus the actual liquid line temperature, measured at the liquid line near the condenser outlet or the receiver. Here that is 45°C minus 38°C, which equals 7°C of subcooling. Option D reverses the subtraction and would produce a negative number, which is not how subcooling is reported. Adding the two temperatures or ignoring the liquid line reading entirely gives a value with no diagnostic meaning for this charging method.
  39. The correct answer is D (Purge the system with nitrogen and confirm the refrigerant has been fully recovered.): Because R-32 is rated A2L for mild flammability, any residual refrigerant vapour near an open flame during brazing presents a real ignition risk that does not exist with nonflammable A1 refrigerants. Recovering the charge and purging the lines with dry nitrogen displaces any remaining flammable vapour before heat is applied. This step is standard practice for A2L systems and is emphasized specifically because training built around older A1 refrigerants like R-410A did not require it. Skipping this precaution on a flammable refrigerant system is a genuine safety hazard rather than a minor procedural shortcut.
  40. The correct answer is D (The exposed high voltage terminal, since it presents an immediate personal safety hazard): Deficiency prioritization always places life safety hazards, such as an exposed energized high voltage terminal, above every other category because it presents an immediate risk of injury to anyone working nearby. A scuffed nameplate is a cosmetic issue with no functional or safety impact. A missing access panel screw is a minor item that may allow slight air leakage but poses no immediate danger. A slightly misaligned diffuser affects comfort, not safety, and ranks well below an active electrical hazard.
  41. The correct answer is A (The high pressure switch is opening from elevated head pressure.): Voltage present before the switch but absent after it during running confirms the open circuit point is the switch itself, and a heavily fouled condenser coil is a directly observed cause of restricted airflow that drives discharge pressure high enough to trip a high pressure safety switch. This protects the system from operating at damaging head pressures until the coil is cleaned and airflow is restored. A failed contactor coil, transformer, or a shorted winding would each produce a different fault location or symptom than the pattern measured here.
  42. The correct answer is A (Office 201 and boardroom, since both measured readings sit above their stated limit): Office 201 reads 48 dBA against a 40 dBA limit and the boardroom reads 36 dBA against a 35 dBA limit, so both exceed their applicable criteria and require follow up action. The mechanical room reads 68 against a 75 limit and passes comfortably. The corridor reads 44 against a 50 limit and also passes with margin to spare. Neither the mechanical room nor the corridor exceeds its limit, so pairing either of them with another space is incorrect.
  43. The correct answer is A (First compression, then condensation, then expansion, then finally evaporation occurs): Refrigerant vapour is first compressed, then rejects heat and condenses into liquid, then drops in pressure across the metering device during expansion, and finally absorbs heat and evaporates back into vapour before returning to the compressor. The other sequences scramble this order in ways that would place expansion before compression or condensation before compression, which does not match how the cycle actually operates. Memorizing this fixed order helps a technician trace where a fault is occurring.
  44. The correct answer is C (All six refrigerants have an ozone depletion potential of zero): Ozone depletion potential depends on a refrigerant's chlorine or bromine content, and since none of these six HFC or HFO refrigerants contain chlorine or bromine, every one of them has an ODP of zero. This is a key difference from older CFC and HCFC refrigerants, which did contain chlorine and did deplete stratospheric ozone. Flammability classification, which separates the A2L refrigerants from the A1 ones, has no bearing on ozone depletion potential.
  45. The correct answer is B (A thermostatic expansion valve, which actively adjusts refrigerant flow to hold a stable superheat): A thermostatic expansion valve continuously senses suction line temperature and pressure and adjusts its opening to keep superheat within a narrow target range even as the cooling load changes, which matches the steady 8°C reading in system one. A fixed orifice or capillary tube has no moving parts to respond to load, so superheat on those systems tends to swing more widely as conditions change, matching system two's behaviour instead. A solenoid valve only starts and stops flow and plays no role in maintaining a target superheat value.
  46. The correct answer is A (A twin-screw compressor with slide valve capacity modulation and oil injection cooling): A twin-screw compressor with slide valve capacity modulation and oil injection cooling. For a large cold storage warehouse needing large total capacity, continuously variable output across a wide range, and long reliable service intervals, a twin-screw compressor with slide valve modulation and oil injection cooling is the strongest match, since screw compressors are purpose-built for exactly this combination of scale, smooth capacity control and industrial-duty reliability. Rotary compressors are designed for very small fractional capacities and are not a practical way to reach large industrial totals, a single fixed-speed reciprocating compressor with no capacity control cannot meet the stated variable-load requirement, and a scroll compressor sized only to average load would be undersized during peak demand periods.
  47. The correct answer is B (Scroll compressor, widely used across manufacturers for this exact capacity range): scroll compressors dominate the 10 to 15 ton commercial rooftop range across most manufacturers because they deliver smooth, low-vibration compression with good efficiency and moderate cost, matching this design scenario directly. Reciprocating compressors have been largely superseded by scroll designs in this range for new equipment. Centrifugal compressors are reserved for much larger chilled water plant capacities, and screw compressors are more typical of larger industrial applications than this mid-size rooftop range.
  48. The correct answer is A (Swaging forms a socket in one tube, removing the need for a separate coupling.): a swaging tool expands one tube end into a socket sized to receive the mating tube, creating a joint using only the two tube ends rather than requiring a separate coupling fitting, which can simplify tight or hard to reach installations. The joint still needs to be brazed to be leak tight and structurally sound, so swaging does not remove the brazing step. Swaging is a general technique and is not restricted to liquid lines only.
  49. The correct answer is B (At ear height in the occupied zone, away from walls and reflective hard surfaces): Sound readings taken at ear height in the actual occupied space, away from surfaces that can reflect or amplify noise, most accurately represent what a building occupant will hear day to day. Readings taken right at the diffuser or outdoors on the roof measure the source noise rather than the experienced noise level in the room. Positioning the meter correctly is essential for the reading to be meaningful in the commissioning documentation.
  50. The correct answer is A (Non-condensable gases, such as trapped air, remain in the system.): Non-condensable gases like air do not condense at the system's operating temperatures, so they occupy space in the condenser and add their own partial pressure on top of the refrigerant's actual saturation pressure. This makes the gauge read higher than a pure refrigerant pressure-temperature chart predicts for the measured condenser conditions, even while other calculations like subcooling can still appear reasonable. This points back to an evacuation that did not fully remove air before charging began. Fan amperage, superheat setting, and oil charge do not produce this specific symptom.
  51. The correct answer is A (A UV lamp and protective glasses to reveal dye residue at the leak.): UV fluorescent dye circulates with the refrigerant and oil, and it leaves a visible glowing residue at the leak point only when illuminated with an ultraviolet lamp, with UV-blocking glasses worn to protect the technician's eyes and make the fluorescence easier to see. A standard flashlight does not excite the dye's fluorescent properties and will not reveal any residue. A stethoscope and a manometer are useful for other diagnostic purposes but cannot detect fluorescent dye residue at all.
  52. The correct answer is A (Cryogenic gloves rated for refrigerant contact and a full face shield): Liquid refrigerant escaping under pressure flashes to vapour instantly and can freeze exposed skin or eyes within seconds, so gloves specifically rated for cryogenic contact and a full face shield are the accepted minimum protection. Option B leaves skin exposed with open safety glasses and does not shield the face from a sudden spray. Option C uses gloves far too thin to resist extreme cold and offers no eye protection. Option D protects against neither cold nor liquid splash and the tinted lens serves no purpose here.
  53. The correct answer is B (A worn or leaking internal valve is allowing compressed gas to blow back internally): Normal amp draw with reduced cooling capacity and pressures that have moved toward each other, suction up and discharge down, points to a mechanical valve problem inside the compressor allowing already-compressed gas to leak backward instead of moving fully to the discharge line, which reduces pumping capacity without necessarily tripping any electrical protection. An open winding would stop the compressor from running entirely, contradicting the normal amp draw. A grounded winding trips overload protection rather than producing this pressure pattern. The described pressures and reduced capacity are inconsistent with normal operation.
  54. The correct answer is C (Reclamation, performed off site by a licensed reclaimer using lab analysis): Reprocessing refrigerant to the purity level of newly manufactured product for resale is reclamation, which is performed off site by a licensed reclaimer and verified through laboratory analysis against the AHRI specification. Option A describes recycling, which only reduces contaminants to a lesser standard on site. Option B and option D describe recovery alone, which involves no purification and cannot legally be resold as reclaimed product.
  55. The correct answer is C (Air-cooled condenser with a coil cleaning schedule to manage the dust loading): an air-cooled unit avoids the need for a water supply connection, which is unavailable at this site, and fits within the limited roof footprint compared to a water-cooled or evaporative system requiring tanks, pumps, and water lines. The dust loading concern is addressed through a maintenance cleaning schedule rather than by switching condenser type. Water-cooled and evaporative options both require water infrastructure this site cannot support, and a cascade condenser addresses staged refrigeration circuits, not dust or space constraints.
  56. The correct answer is B (From existing supplies of recovered, recycled, or reclaimed R-22 already in the market.): Because new production and import of R-22 for use in Canada is no longer permitted, technicians must source any needed R-22 from existing supplies of recovered, recycled, or reclaimed refrigerant already circulating in the market. Importing a newly manufactured batch for a single repair is not a permitted option under current regulation. Blending in an A2L substitute directly with existing R-22 is not an appropriate topping up method, and topping up existing R-22 systems from legitimate recovered sources remains permitted, not prohibited outright.
  57. The correct answer is B (An accurate refrigerant scale, comparing dispensed mass against the calculated total charge.): The weigh-in method starts from the factory nameplate charge, adds or subtracts the manufacturer's stated adjustment per meter of line set length beyond the base amount, and arrives at a target total mass. A refrigerant scale placed under the cylinder tracks exactly how much mass has been dispensed into the system as charging proceeds. This gives a repeatable starting point that is then fine-tuned using superheat or subcooling readings. A micron gauge, psychrometer, and ammeter serve other commissioning steps.
  58. The correct answer is C (Water-cooled condensers generally allow lower condensing temperatures but require ongoing water consumption and treatment): water-cooled condensers typically achieve lower and more stable condensing temperatures than air-cooled units in hot, humid climates because they reject heat to water rather than to high ambient dry bulb air, but this benefit comes with continuous water consumption, blowdown, and water treatment requirements that conflict with strict municipal water use limits. Air-cooled condensers do not achieve lower condensing temperatures than water-cooled systems in this climate. Water restrictions are directly relevant to condenser selection rather than irrelevant, and air-cooled systems do not require more water treatment than water-cooled systems since they use no process water at all.
  59. The correct answer is C (Refrigerant handling regulations require tracking to limit releases to atmosphere): federal and provincial refrigerant management regulations require documented tracking of recovered and added refrigerant quantities specifically to identify losses, discourage venting, and support environmental reporting obligations tied to ozone depleting and high global warming potential substances. Option A understates a legal obligation as a mere customer courtesy. Option B is unrelated, since technician pay is a separate business matter from regulatory tracking. Option D is incorrect because this documentation carries real legal weight under refrigerant handling regulations, not just informal shop practice.
  60. The correct answer is B (18.6 kilograms, the difference between maximum gross weight and tare weight): The maximum net refrigerant weight a cylinder can legally hold equals its maximum gross weight minus its tare weight, which is 33.1 kg minus 14.5 kg, or 18.6 kg. Option A confuses the water capacity rating with the actual weight limit. Option C only restates the empty cylinder weight. Option D ignores that the tare weight of the cylinder itself must be subtracted from the gross figure.
  61. The correct answer is A (Intermediate traps break the rise into segments so oil doesn't accumulate over the full height.): on an unusually long vertical suction riser, spacing short intermediate traps along the run breaks the total rise into shorter segments, each collecting oil locally so vapour velocity can more reliably sweep it upward in stages rather than relying on velocity alone to carry oil the full 12 metres without any collection point. Without these traps, oil could accumulate somewhere along the long rise even if the pipe were correctly sized. The traps are a functional oil management feature, not simply a mounting convenience, and reducing charge is not their purpose.
  62. The correct answer is A (Recycling, because contaminants were reduced on site without full lab verification): Recycling describes on-site contaminant reduction, such as oil separation and moisture or acid removal, performed to a lesser standard than reclamation and without laboratory verification. Option B is wrong because reclamation requires off-site reprocessing and lab analysis confirming the AHRI purity specification, which did not happen here. Option C is wrong because the refrigerant clearly was removed into a cylinder. Option D misapplies venting, which means releasing refrigerant to atmosphere, not processing it for reuse.
  63. The correct answer is B (Liquid slugging the compressor, since liquid refrigerant cannot compress like a vapour can): a compressor is designed to compress refrigerant vapour, and liquid refrigerant entering the suction line while the compressor runs can be drawn directly into the cylinder or scroll set, where it cannot compress and instead causes mechanical damage to valves, pistons, or the scroll set itself. This is why liquid charging into the low side is normally only done with the compressor off, or metered slowly into the high side liquid line while running. The remaining options describe effects unrelated to the actual mechanical risk of liquid entering a running compressor's suction path.
  64. The correct answer is B (Evaporator flooding with liquid and high suction pressure at the compressor.): A TXV stuck fully open removes the metering restriction entirely, allowing far more liquid refrigerant into the evaporator than can fully evaporate under normal load, producing flooding conditions with high suction pressure and near-zero superheat at the compressor inlet. This excess liquid can also return to the compressor and dilute the oil, risking mechanical damage. A starved evaporator with high superheat is the opposite failure, produced by a restricted rather than open valve, and a stuck open valve does not by itself cause a charge to be lost from the system.
  65. The correct answer is C (It extracts superheated discharge gas heat before the refrigerant reaches the main condenser): a desuperheater is installed in the discharge line between the compressor and the main condenser, where it captures a portion of the high temperature superheated discharge gas heat by transferring it to a separate fluid, typically water intended for domestic use. This recovered heat would otherwise simply be rejected at the main condenser with no useful benefit. A desuperheater does not subcool liquid refrigerant, since it acts on discharge gas before condensation occurs, and it does not affect suction pressure at the compressor inlet. It also does not replace the main condenser, which still handles the remaining condensing duty after the desuperheater.
  66. The correct answer is D (Discharge the capacitor terminals using an insulated resistor before any contact): Discharge the capacitor terminals using an insulated resistor before any contact. Capacitors can retain a significant stored charge well after power is disconnected, and touching the terminals directly or with an uninsulated tool can cause a painful or dangerous shock. Using an insulated discharge resistor across the terminals safely bleeds off the stored voltage before any testing or handling begins. Skipping this step, or assuming the capacitor is safe simply because the disconnect switch is open, is a common cause of preventable shock injuries in the trade.
  67. The correct answer is B (It combines heat rejection and water evaporation in one unit, using less water): It combines heat rejection and water evaporation in one unit, using less water. An evaporative condenser sprays water directly over the refrigerant coil while air is drawn across it, combining the functions of a cooling tower and a separate water-cooled condenser into a single piece of equipment, which reduces overall water consumption and eliminates an intermediate water loop and its pump. It still requires makeup water and water treatment to control scaling and biological growth, and its condensing temperature depends on ambient wet-bulb conditions similar to a tower system rather than being automatically lower. Refrigerant piping from the compressor to the condenser coil is still required in either arrangement.
  68. The correct answer is B (Shiny bare metal has low emissivity, which skews infrared readings low): infrared thermometers calculate temperature based on the infrared energy a surface emits, and shiny, reflective bare metal has low emissivity, meaning it emits less energy than its actual temperature would suggest, causing the reading to appear lower than reality. Option A is false because infrared accuracy actually depends heavily on the surface being measured, and a contact thermocouple is often more reliable on reflective metal. Option C is not physically realistic for a small measured spot, and option D is incorrect since thermocouples work perfectly well when properly clamped to copper tubing.
  69. The correct answer is A (The start relay, which opens its contacts based on current or voltage): a potential relay senses rising back-EMF voltage while a current relay senses falling start winding current, and either type opens its contacts to remove the start capacitor once the motor is close to running speed. The run capacitor stays in the circuit continuously and plays no role in disconnecting the start capacitor. Leaving the start capacitor connected too long can overheat it and cause premature failure.
  70. The correct answer is D (About 80 ohms of resistance for the motor circuit): Ohm's Law states R = V ÷ I, so dividing 208 volts by 2.6 amps gives 80 ohms. This value represents the effective circuit impedance at that operating point rather than a fixed winding resistance, since an ECM motor's electronic controls actively vary current draw as it adjusts speed and torque. Technicians use this kind of calculation as a sanity check against a multimeter reading rather than as a precise winding specification. A result far outside expected ranges would point toward a wiring or control board problem.
  71. The correct answer is D (It removes sharp burrs that could restrict flow or damage seals): a tubing cutter leaves a raised burr on both the inside and outside edges of the cut, and if left in place that burr can restrict refrigerant flow, create turbulence, or cut into flare and O ring seals during assembly. Deburring does not meaningfully affect the copper's shine as in option A, has no effect on weight as in option B, and does not change how the tubing expands under heat as in option C, since that behavior is a property of the metal itself.
  72. The correct answer is D (Compressor, condenser, metering device, then evaporator, repeating continuously in that order): Refrigerant vapour leaves the evaporator and enters the compressor, which raises its pressure and temperature, then travels to the condenser where it rejects heat and becomes a high pressure liquid. That liquid passes through a metering device that drops its pressure before it enters the evaporator to absorb heat again. Understanding this fixed order is essential for correctly interpreting gauge readings and temperatures at each stage of the cycle.
  73. The correct answer is A (On-site recycling to reduce moisture and particulate contamination before reinstalling it locally.): Because the refrigerant is uncontaminated apart from routine moisture and particulate concerns and will be reused at the same site, on-site recycling to reduce those contaminants is sufficient without the added cost and delay of off-site reclamation. Full reclamation is reserved for refrigerant being resold or reused elsewhere. Reusing recovered refrigerant with no treatment at all, or treating it as automatic hazardous waste, ignores the intermediate recycling option available for this scenario.
  74. The correct answer is A (Apply a solar heat gain factor specific to west orientation and glazing type for that exposure): west-facing glazing receives its peak solar load during afternoon hours when outdoor temperatures are also typically at their highest, so the load calculation must apply an orientation-specific solar heat gain factor matched to that exposure and glazing type to capture this compounding effect accurately. Reusing the north-facing factor would significantly understate the actual solar contribution. Solar gain through glazing is a sensible load component, not latent, and it must be applied to the glazing area itself, not only the roof.
  75. The correct answer is B (Preventing oil or liquid refrigerant from draining back into the compressor head during shutdown): Preventing oil or liquid refrigerant from draining back into the compressor head during shutdown. On a discharge line with a significant vertical drop, gravity can allow oil or condensed liquid refrigerant sitting in the line to drain back down into the compressor discharge valve area during an off cycle, which can cause valve damage or liquid slugging on the next start if not addressed with a properly located check valve and correct pipe routing. This concern is specific to vertical drops and is not significant on a short horizontal run where there is little elevation change to drive backflow. Pipe diameter on a discharge drop should still be selected from velocity and pressure drop criteria, not oversized arbitrarily, and insulation needs are determined by heat loss and safety concerns independent of line orientation.
  76. The correct answer is A (The total design load of 31.6 kW, which already includes the applied safety factor): The total design load of 31.6 kW, which already includes the applied safety factor. Equipment selection should be based on the final total design load, since that figure already combines room and ventilation sensible and latent components and includes the safety factor the designer intentionally applied for load calculation uncertainty. Using only the room sensible load would ignore ventilation and latent contributions and undersize the equipment, while using the pre-factor total discards a margin the designer deliberately built in. The sensible heat ratio is a useful diagnostic for equipment selection and dehumidification performance, but it is not itself a capacity figure to size tonnage from.
  77. The correct answer is C (The king valve, located at the receiver's liquid line outlet connection.): The king valve sits at the outlet of the liquid receiver on systems designed to store excess liquid refrigerant, and closing it while running the compressor pumps refrigerant out of the low side components and traps it in the receiver for isolation. This pump-down procedure allows components like evaporators, driers, or valves downstream of the receiver to be serviced without releasing the full system charge to atmosphere. The suction, discharge, and oil equalization valves serve different isolation and service purposes elsewhere in the circuit.
  78. The correct answer is C (Maintaining continuous communication with the entrant and monitoring for hazards): the attendant's core job is to stay in constant contact with the entrant, watch for signs of distress or a changing atmosphere, and call for rescue immediately if something goes wrong, without leaving their post. Operating equipment controls as in A is not the attendant's assigned safety role. Doing paperwork as in B would distract from the monitoring duty, and entering the space as in D is specifically prohibited for an attendant unless a properly trained rescue team takes over that function.
  79. The correct answer is D (Reduced airflow across the condenser coil, gradually pushing head pressure above normal): A worn, glazed belt slips under load and turns the condenser fan slower than its rated speed, reducing airflow across the coil and progressively pushing head pressure above normal as heat rejection falls behind, the same pattern as a dirty coil producing restricted airflow. Reduced condenser airflow affects head pressure primarily, and its influence on evaporator superheat is indirect at best, not a direct effect. Slower fan speed reduces airflow and heat rejection, which raises rather than lowers discharge pressure. Belt slippage has a real, measurable performance effect well beyond noise and vibration alone.
  80. The correct answer is D (Essential skills such as document use, numeracy, and communication): the Red Seal program recognizes that trades work requires more than technical theory alone, so exams also assess essential skills such as reading documents like work orders, performing workplace numeracy calculations, and communicating clearly, all of which are integrated throughout the exam rather than tested separately. Option A incorrectly narrows these skills to electrical theory alone. Option B mischaracterizes them as purely legal or regulatory content, and option C limits them to a single narrow technical skill, when essential skills questions actually span a much broader range of practical workplace tasks.
  81. The correct answer is D (Gas velocity falls below the minimum needed to carry oil upward, causing oil logging): an oversized riser increases the flow area, which lowers refrigerant velocity at reduced load conditions below the minimum threshold needed to entrain and carry oil droplets upward against gravity, allowing oil to accumulate and log in the vertical run. This starves the compressor of lubricant over time rather than affecting discharge pressure directly. Excess subcooling and short cycling at startup are not the direct consequence of an oversized suction riser.
  82. The correct answer is C (The rise likely indicates residual moisture outgassing rather than an active leak.): when the micron reading rises and then levels off at a steady value rather than continuing to climb all the way back toward atmospheric pressure, this pattern is generally associated with residual moisture within the system outgassing under vacuum rather than an ongoing leak, since a true leak typically causes the pressure to continue rising steadily toward atmospheric over time. This calls for additional evacuation time or another evacuation cycle rather than assuming a leak or a faulty gauge. Concluding the system needs to be rebrazed entirely is an overreaction not supported by this particular pattern.
  83. The correct answer is D (It reduces oil entrainment, risking oil accumulation in the discharge piping): It reduces oil entrainment, risking oil accumulation in the discharge piping. Just as with suction lines, discharge line gas velocity must stay high enough to entrain oil droplets and carry them toward the condenser and onward through the system rather than letting oil pool in low points or traps in the discharge run. This is a separate concern from pressure drop, which affects discharge pressure and compressor work rather than oil movement, so a discharge line can be oversized on diameter to control pressure drop yet still be undersized on velocity for oil return. Expansion valve hunting and suction pressure changes are driven by other parts of the circuit, not by discharge line velocity itself.
  84. The correct answer is A (No, A1 refrigerant carries no flammability rating, so flammability clearance rules do not apply): the A1 classification indicates lower toxicity and no flame propagation, meaning the refrigerant does not carry a flammability hazard that would trigger the ignition source clearance requirements CSA B52 applies to A2L or more flammable classifications. The mechanic should explain that flammability related clearance rules exist specifically because of a flammability hazard that A1 refrigerant does not present. This distinction does not depend on whether the installation is indoors or on an exterior rooftop, since the flammability classification itself, not the location type, drives whether those clearances apply. A1 also does not carry a higher toxicity rating than A2L under this classification system, so that reasoning is incorrect as well.
  85. The correct answer is D (Adjust the calibration screw on the gauge face to zero it properly): Most analog manifold gauges include a small calibration screw on the gauge face that allows the needle to be reset to zero at atmospheric pressure, and this adjustment should be made before the gauge is trusted for accurate diagnostic readings. Option A risks every subsequent pressure reading being skewed by the same uncorrected offset. Option B is an unnecessarily costly step when a simple calibration adjustment usually resolves the issue. Option C is a dangerous workaround that would introduce an incorrect refrigerant charge based on a faulty gauge reading rather than fixing the instrument itself.
  86. The correct answer is A (Trapped air can cause flow readings and pressure differentials to be inaccurate or unstable): Trapped air in a hydronic system can create unstable or inaccurate flow and differential pressure readings, so a complete purge must be verified before balancing readings are taken to make sure the recorded values are reliable and repeatable. Purging affects far more than water appearance, since air pockets directly disrupt hydraulic performance. Air purging is required on both chilled water and heating hot water systems, not chilled water alone. It is a functional hydraulic requirement, not a cosmetic step for a client walkthrough.
  87. The correct answer is A (A swaging tool to expand one tube end to accept the other directly): a swaging tool expands one tube end just enough that the other tube slides inside it, creating a joint that can be brazed without adding a separate coupling fitting, which reduces the total number of joints in the line. A tubing cutter as in option B is used to cut tubing to length, not to shape a joint. A flaring tool as in option C creates a cone shaped end for a mechanical flare fitting, not a sleeve joint, and a reamer as in option D only removes burrs from inside a cut tube.
  88. The correct answer is B (3.517 kW, equivalent to 12,000 BTU per hour): One ton of refrigeration is defined as 3.517 kW, which is equivalent to 12,000 BTU per hour, based on the historic rate of heat needed to melt one ton of ice in 24 hours. The 1.0 kW figure corresponds to about 3,412 BTU per hour, which is a different unit conversion entirely, not a ton. The other two values do not correspond to the standard ton of refrigeration rating used across the industry.
  89. The correct answer is B (A water-cooled shell and tube condenser, since it rejects heat to the tower loop quietly indoors): a shell and tube condenser rejects refrigerant heat into circulating tower water inside the tube bundle, allowing the condenser itself to sit quietly inside the mechanical room with no fan noise near the building exterior. The cooling tower on the roof still produces some sound, but it can be positioned and specified to meet the acoustic limit, which is far easier than eliminating fan noise from a large air-cooled unit near balconies. An air-cooled condenser would place noisy fans exactly where the acoustic limit forbids them. Since tower water is already available, the water-cooled option is both quieter and practical.
  90. The correct answer is A (Hunting, which describes a control loop repeatedly oscillating around its setpoint without settling): Hunting is the standard control theory term for a loop that repeatedly overshoots and undershoots its setpoint in an oscillating pattern instead of stabilizing, usually caused by improper tuning gains. Deadband refers to an intentional range around setpoint where no corrective action occurs, a different concept entirely. Setback refers to a deliberately lowered setpoint during unoccupied periods to save energy, unrelated to oscillating behaviour. Staging describes bringing multiple pieces of equipment online sequentially to match load, not a single valve's unstable positioning behaviour.
  91. The correct answer is D (Work backward through the control circuit, checking voltage at each switch toward the transformer): With zero voltage measured at the coil itself, the fault lies somewhere upstream in the control circuit rather than in the coil, so working backward and checking voltage at each switch or connection point toward the transformer isolates exactly where voltage is being lost. Replacing a contactor based on zero coil voltage skips diagnosing the actual upstream cause and risks not fixing the real problem. The compressor is downstream of the contactor and unrelated to why the coil itself has no voltage. Thermostat setpoint differential affects when a call occurs, not whether voltage reaches an already-calling coil.
  92. The correct answer is D (Back the wrench off to its lowest setting before placing it in storage): Releasing tension on the internal spring by backing a click type torque wrench down to its lowest setting before storage prevents the spring from taking a permanent set, which would throw off future torque readings. Option A keeps the spring under constant load, which is the exact condition that degrades calibration over time. Option B does not address spring tension at all and leaves the setting wherever it was last used. Option C risks physical damage from contact with heavier tools, which can also affect accuracy.
  93. The correct answer is A (A contactor is built with heavier contacts to switch higher current loads): contactors are constructed with larger, more robust contacts specifically to handle the higher amperage of compressor and fan motor loads. Control relays are generally used for lighter switching duties, such as energizing indicator lights or pilot circuits. Both devices work on the same electromagnetic coil principle, and either can technically operate on line or low voltage depending on the coil rating selected.
  94. The correct answer is D (Self-contained machines have their own internal compressor and do not need the system compressor to run.): A self-contained recovery machine carries its own internal compressor, so it does not depend on the serviced system's compressor being operational, which is exactly what makes it usable on a system whose compressor cannot assist recovery. This capability is not restricted to A2L flammable refrigerant systems. A system-dependent machine, by contrast, does require the system's own compressor to function, which is the opposite of the self-contained design, and the distinction is not tied to a fifty kilogram charge threshold.
  95. The correct answer is C (The relay's normally open contacts, to see if they physically close and pass power): Since the relay coil is confirmed energized with correct voltage and resistance, the fault must lie downstream at the relay's switching contacts, which may have failed to physically close or may be burned open despite a healthy coil, so testing continuity or voltage across those contacts is the logical next step. The thermostat's fan call has already been effectively confirmed by the energized coil, not left unverified. The compressor contactor is a separate circuit unrelated to blower motor operation. The outdoor disconnect supplies the condensing unit and has no bearing on indoor blower control.
  96. The correct answer is B (It safely reveals gross leaks before pressure is raised to the full test level.): starting at a low pressure allows the mechanic to catch a gross leak, such as a joint that was never brazed or a loose fitting, before continuing to raise pressure toward the final test level. If a large leak were present and the system were charged straight to full test pressure, the sudden pressure loss or associated hazard would be more severe. This staged approach applies to the whole system, not just the discharge side, and the low pressure step does not replace the required final higher pressure hold.
  97. The correct answer is A (Note and subtract out any significant background noise sources unrelated to the HVAC system): A valid sound test must account for background noise from sources unrelated to the HVAC equipment, since a loud unrelated activity nearby can inflate the reading and misrepresent the system's actual noise contribution. Deliberately testing during an unusually loud event, such as a fire alarm test, would produce a meaningless result for commissioning purposes. Documenting and controlling for background conditions keeps the recorded sound level accurate and defensible.
  98. The correct answer is C (A dashed line typically represents piping that is hidden or below grade): Standard piping drawing conventions commonly use a dashed or broken line style to show piping that runs behind a wall, above a ceiling, below grade, or otherwise out of direct view, distinguishing it visually from piping shown with a solid continuous line. Option A confuses line style with pipe sizing, which is normally indicated by a numeric callout rather than dash pattern. Option B is incorrect since refrigerant type is typically labelled with text or a legend, not line style. Option D is not a real drawing convention and drawings do not indicate failed piping this way.
  99. The correct answer is C (The metering device is overfeeding, or the system has been overcharged with refrigerant): A large drop toward very low superheat under otherwise unchanged conditions points to too much refrigerant reaching the evaporator, either from an overfeeding metering device or an overcharged system, both of which push liquid refrigerant further through the coil before it fully boils off. Undercharging instead tends to raise superheat, not lower it, since less refrigerant reaches the coil. Restricted condenser airflow and a leaking discharge valve produce different symptom patterns than a sudden superheat collapse.
  100. Option C mistakenly uses the tare weight itself as the refrigerant weight.
  101. The correct answer is D (Its simple mechanical design holds calibration without frequent adjustment when idle): a beam type torque wrench has no internal spring under constant tension, so it can sit unused for long periods without drifting out of calibration the way a click type wrench can. It does not produce an audible click like a click type wrench, which rules out option A, and it is not typically lighter or more compact than a click type tool, ruling out option B. Option C is also incorrect since a beam wrench reads torque visually on a scale rather than resetting automatically.
  102. The correct answer is B (Restricted or reduced water flow rate through the refrigerant coil.): Even with a circulator pump confirmed running and entering water temperature in range, an actual restriction inside the water side of the refrigerant coil, such as scale buildup or a partially closed valve, reduces the water flow volume moving heat into or out of the refrigerant, degrading both heat rejection and heat absorption at once. This produces the simultaneous high head and low suction pressure pattern described, since the coil cannot transfer heat efficiently in either direction. An oversized circulator or a clean airflow path would not by themselves create this combined pressure pattern on the refrigerant side.
  103. The correct answer is D (oil and refrigerant collect in the low pocket until velocity is high enough to clear it): an unintended dip in a suction line layout acts much like an oversized, unplanned trap, where oil and any liquid refrigerant can settle at the low point. At reduced system load, gas velocity may not be sufficient to clear that pocket every cycle, which can lead to oil starvation at the compressor over time or a slug of liquid and oil reaching the compressor when velocity does pick up. This is not a beneficial feature and is not the same as a properly sized and intentional oil trap engineered into the riser design.
  104. The correct answer is D (An ABC rated extinguisher, effective on combustibles and flammable gas adjacent fires): a multi purpose ABC rated extinguisher covers ordinary combustibles like the burning rag as well as being generally suitable near a flammable gas fed fire situation, making it the practical first response tool to keep near hot work. A Class A only extinguisher as in option A is limited strictly to ordinary combustibles and may not be labeled safe near the broader hazards present. Class C in option B applies specifically to energized electrical equipment, which is not the hazard described, and Class K in option C is designed for cooking oil fires, unrelated to this scenario.
  105. The correct answer is B (Fill the cylinder to no more than eighty percent of its rated liquid capacity by weight.): Recovery cylinders must not be filled beyond eighty percent of their rated liquid capacity by weight, leaving adequate vapour space to accommodate thermal expansion and prevent hydrostatic overpressure. Filling to full rated water capacity removes this necessary margin. Fill limits apply to liquid recovery specifically, not only to vapour recovery, and pressure gauge readings are not the basis for this specific fill limit rule.
  106. The correct answer is C (Apply a soap bubble solution to the fitting and watch for forming bubbles): The soap bubble test is the traditional, tool-free method for confirming a suspected leak, since escaping refrigerant creates visible, growing bubbles at the exact point of the leak. Listening for hissing with the system powered off would not reveal a leak that only becomes apparent under system pressure. Comparing surface temperatures is not a reliable way to isolate a small leak at a single fitting. Taping the fitting and checking for overnight frost is neither a standard nor a practical field test for this situation.

Option D is incorrect because obtaining a signature is standard practice on every completed call, not only when a paper copy is specifically requested.
  108.
