Option D describes prohibited deliberate venting rather than a legitimate recovery method.
  109. The correct answer is B (Bend the tubing slowly and smoothly while keeping it seated in the tool groove): Keeping the tubing properly seated in the bender's groove and applying slow, even pressure allows the tool's radius to support the tube wall throughout the bend, which prevents the kinking or flattening that occurs when the tube is allowed to shift or bend unevenly. Option A applies force too quickly, increasing the risk of an uneven or kinked bend. Option C describes overbending intentionally, which stresses the tubing unnecessarily and increases kink risk. Option D describes deliberately damaging the tube before bending, which would only worsen the final result.
  110. The correct answer is B (Have the instrument recalibrated or verified against a known standard before relying on its readings): An expired calibration sticker means the instrument's accuracy can no longer be trusted, even if the displayed reading happens to look reasonable, since drift is not always obvious from the number alone. The correct step is to have the manometer recalibrated or checked against a known reference standard before using it for official commissioning data. Manually extending the sticker date or switching to estimating by feel are not acceptable substitutes for verified instrument accuracy.
  111. The correct answer is A (Head pressure will drop closer to normal for the outdoor ambient temperature.): Restricted airflow across a dirty condenser coil is a classic cause of elevated head pressure because heat rejection is impaired. Once the coil is cleaned and airflow is restored, the condenser can reject heat efficiently and discharge pressure falls back toward the value expected for that ambient temperature. Coil cleanliness has no meaningful effect on trapped oil, and claiming no change ignores that airflow directly governs condensing pressure.
  112. The correct answer is D (Lower achievable condensing temperature and a smaller footprint suit the site constraints): Lower achievable condensing temperature and a smaller footprint suit the site constraints. Water-cooled condensers typically achieve a lower condensing temperature than air-cooled equipment because water is a more effective heat transfer medium than outdoor air, which directly improves compressor efficiency, and shell-and-tube condensers occupy much less physical space than an equivalent air-cooled coil and fan array, which matches the limited roof space described. Refrigerant piping is still required regardless of condenser type, and water-cooled systems need ongoing water treatment to control scale and biological growth, not none at all. Air-cooled condensers are generally less efficient than water-cooled equipment at a given ambient condition, the opposite of what option C claims.
  113. The correct answer is D (Recover the refrigerant charge into an appropriate cylinder before opening the system.): Before any refrigerant-containing line or fitting is opened for a compressor replacement, the halocarbon or environmental protection regulation covering this system requires the technician to recover the refrigerant present into a properly rated recovery cylinder to prevent it from escaping to atmosphere. For a private-sector system like this one, that duty comes from the province's own halocarbon regulation rather than directly from the federal Ozone-depleting Substances and Halocarbon Alternatives Regulations, which instead control manufacture, import and export nationally; a separate federal regulation applies only on federal and Indigenous lands and to federally owned equipment. Venting even a portion of the charge, such as the low side pressure, still violates the applicable deliberate release prohibition. Isolating the compressor with service valves does not remove the need for full recovery once a fitting is actually disconnected.
  114. The correct answer is B (Oil will fail to return up the riser and can accumulate, starving the compressor of lubrication): oil circulating with the refrigerant must be carried upward against gravity in a vertical suction riser, and this only happens reliably when refrigerant velocity stays above the minimum threshold for that pipe size. If velocity drops too low at reduced capacity, oil falls out of suspension and pools at the base of the riser or in the evaporator instead of returning to the compressor. Over time this starves the compressor of lubricating oil, leading to bearing and cylinder wear or eventual failure. This is a mechanical piping design issue, not something superheat settings or condenser fan cycling correct on their own.
  115. The correct answer is B (Reciprocating compressors handle a wide range of pressure ratios and capacities reliably): Reciprocating compressors handle a wide range of pressure ratios and capacities reliably. Reciprocating compressors are well suited to the high pressure ratios typical of low-temperature industrial refrigeration, and their design has been proven over decades across a wide range of capacities from small commercial units to large multi-cylinder industrial machines. They do have significant internal moving parts including pistons, valves and connecting rods, and they rely on oil for lubrication just as most other positive displacement compressor types do. Their robustness and serviceability at demanding operating conditions is what keeps them common in that segment despite scroll and screw compressors taking over lighter duty applications.
  116. The correct answer is D (Approximately 1920 watts, based on 240 volts multiplied by 8 amperes): Approximately 1920 watts, based on 240 volts multiplied by 8 amperes. Using the power formula, power equals voltage multiplied by current, so 240 volts multiplied by 8 amperes equals 1920 watts. This simplified calculation assumes a purely resistive load and does not account for power factor, which is common when comparing nameplate ratings to field readings. Comparing this calculated value to the compressor's rated nameplate wattage helps confirm the motor is operating within its expected range.
  117. The correct answer is D (The compressor is mechanically bound or overloaded internally, preventing it from reaching running speed): A brief inrush at or somewhat above locked rotor amperage is expected on every start, but a compressor that never transitions down to normal running amperage and instead keeps drawing high current until the overload trips indicates the motor is mechanically bound internally and cannot spin up to speed, forcing sustained high current draw. A brief LRA-level spike alone is normal, but failing to drop to running amps afterward is not. The overload tripping in response to genuinely sustained high current is doing its job correctly, not necessarily miscalibrated. A properly rated clamp meter capturing inrush current is a standard and reliable diagnostic method.
  118. The correct answer is D (Type K, because it has the thickest wall of the three classifications): copper water tube is classified K, L and M in order of decreasing wall thickness for a given nominal size, with Type K the thickest and therefore rated for the highest working pressure and typically used for underground or demanding pressure applications. Type L falls in the middle and is the classification most commonly chosen for refrigerant piping because it balances pressure rating with workability. Type M has the thinnest wall and the lowest pressure rating of the three. Refrigerant service is not restricted to a single lettered type, so the claim that only Type L is approved for refrigerant use does not hold up.
  119. The correct answer is C (Apply a small, justified margin above the calculated load rather than an arbitrary large factor): sound design practice applies a modest, documented safety margin, often in the range of a few percent, above a carefully completed load calculation to account for reasonable uncertainty, rather than doubling the load without justification, which leads to the short cycling and humidity control problems associated with gross oversizing. Never applying any margin ignores legitimate calculation uncertainty. Basing the margin on budget rather than calculation confidence disconnects the safety factor from its actual engineering purpose.
  120. The correct answer is B (Submitting design drawings and obtaining a permit from the authority having jurisdiction): CSA B52 and the provincial regulatory framework require that design drawings be submitted and reviewed, and that a permit be obtained from the authority having jurisdiction, before pressure piping installation work begins on site. This review confirms the design meets code requirements for piping, machine room construction, and safety devices before any physical installation locks in a non-compliant layout. Charging refrigerant and commissioning both occur only after installation and inspection are substantially complete. Installing piping before drawings are reviewed risks costly rework if the authority having jurisdiction identifies a deficiency after the fact.
  121. The correct answer is D (Apply the manufacturer's recommended grease amount at the scheduled lubrication interval): Bearings fitted with grease fittings are specifically designed to be serviced periodically, unlike sealed bearings which have no fittings at all. Following the manufacturer's grease type and quantity at the correct interval prevents the dry running and wear that produces grinding noise and eventual bearing seizure. Replacing the entire motor is an unnecessary expense when routine lubrication can resolve early-stage bearing noise. Refrigerant oil is not a substitute for proper bearing grease and can damage the bearing seals.
  122. The correct answer is C (The maximum permitted refrigerant charge for the occupied space, since A2L is mildly flammable): CSA B52 sets a maximum allowable refrigerant charge based on the occupied space's volume and occupancy classification when a mildly flammable A2L refrigerant is used, to keep a worst case leak below a hazardous concentration. This charge limit is what most directly constrains where and how large an A2L system can be installed relative to an occupied space below it. Compressor brand, cylinder colour, and supply voltage have no bearing on the flammability based charge limit set by the code. A contractor must calculate the space volume and applicable charge limit before finalizing the equipment selection.
  123. The correct answer is A (The system is operating within its normal low side pressure range): The pressure shown falls comfortably between the stated normal range of 200 kPa and 280 kPa, so it indicates the system is operating within its expected low side pressure band for this load condition. An abnormally low or high pressure reading would fall clearly outside that stated range rather than within it. A failed suction valve typically produces very low, unstable suction pressure rather than a steady mid-range reading like this one.
  124. The correct answer is B (Investigate the low subcooling value, since it falls outside the manufacturer's design range): The exhibit shows a subcooling reading of 4.6 C against a manufacturer's design range of 8 to 10 C, indicating the charge or metering device performance does not yet match design intent. Suction pressure and superheat readings alone do not confirm proper subcooling, so this discrepancy cannot simply be accepted. The technician must investigate before the readings log can be signed as meeting the commissioning target.
  125. The correct answer is B (Confirming freeze protection is adequate to prevent loop fluid from freezing and expanding): Ground loop antifreeze solution must stay at the correct concentration to protect the loop fluid from freezing at the lowest expected loop temperatures, since freezing fluid can expand and damage piping, so checking concentration with a refractometer is a standard PM task on geothermal systems. Refrigerant charge is a separate sealed circuit unrelated to the ground loop fluid. Compressor winding resistance and blower capacitor condition are unrelated electrical checks, not part of a loop fluid concentration test.

Practice Test 4 Answer Key

  1. The correct answer is C (The total superheat present in this circuit is approximately 10°C.): Superheat is found by subtracting the saturated evaporating temperature indicated by the low side pressure from the actual measured suction line temperature at the compressor. Subtracting -12°C from -2°C gives a superheat value of 10°C, showing the vapour has warmed by that amount after fully evaporating inside the coil. This confirms no liquid refrigerant should be reaching the compressor under these conditions, which protects it from slugging damage. Comparing this measured superheat against the manufacturer's specified target range is the next step in deciding whether the system charge or metering device needs adjustment.
  2. The correct answer is B (The capacitor has degraded internally and should be replaced with a matching one): The capacitor has degraded internally and should be replaced with a matching one. Capacitors typically have a manufacturing tolerance printed on the label, usually a small percentage range, so a reading well below that range indicates the capacitor has lost capacitance over time and can no longer provide adequate phase shift for starting torque. This matches the symptom of a motor that hums but fails to start, since the capacitor is no longer helping the motor develop enough starting torque. Replacing it with a capacitor of the correct microfarad rating and voltage restores proper motor operation.
  3. The correct answer is A (Refrigerant tracking regulations require recording additions to monitor potential leak rates over time): Refrigerant tracking and reporting regulations require documenting the quantity of refrigerant added during service so that repeated additions to the same equipment can be tracked, helping identify systems with ongoing leaks that need repair rather than repeated top-ups. Service records serve a genuine regulatory compliance function, not only internal billing. Refrigerant addition tracking is directly tied to environmental leak reporting requirements in many jurisdictions. Recording the amount added, not just the total charge, is specifically what allows leak rate calculations over time.
  4. The correct answer is B (One tap upstream of the unit inlet and one downstream past the last coil or filter): Total external static pressure is measured from just before the unit's entering airstream to just after the last component the fan must push air through, capturing the full external system resistance. Taps placed inside the fan housing only measure the fan's own pressure rise, not the external ductwork and coil resistance. Reading only the return side ignores supply duct, coil and filter resistance the fan must also overcome. Measuring at the louvers instead of at the unit connections would include unrelated building envelope losses.
  5. The correct answer is B (The measured airflow matches design closely enough and no further damper adjustment is needed): The exhibit shows a design airflow of 250 CFM against a measured value of 244 CFM, a difference well within the normal tolerance accepted during air balancing. Because the reading is close to target and the damper is already partially open, no further throttling or opening is warranted. Flagging this diffuser as under or over target would be an unnecessary and incorrect adjustment.
  6. The correct answer is B (A brazed joint, since it forms a permanent, leak-tight metallurgical bond): Mechanical fittings like flares and compression joints are valuable where future serviceability is needed, since they can be disassembled without heat, but every mechanical joint carries a small ongoing risk of loosening from vibration or thermal cycling. Concealed piping that cannot be easily reached for future tightening or inspection is best joined by brazing for a permanent seal.
  7. The correct answer is A (Every five years, stamped with the new test date after requalification.): Refillable steel recovery cylinders regulated under Transport Canada and DOT rules must undergo periodic hydrostatic requalification, typically on a five year interval, with the new test date stamped on the cylinder afterward. Relying on manufacture date alone or visible damage alone ignores the fixed periodic testing requirement that applies regardless of appearance. A twelve month interval is not the standard requalification period for these cylinders.
  8. The correct answer is D (Correcting the undercharge, since a properly charged system will not trip the switch repeatedly): An undercharged system runs suction pressure low enough to repeatedly trip a low pressure switch that is set close to normal operating pressure, and restoring the correct charge raises suction pressure clear of that cut-out point, stopping the cycling. Short cycling from low suction pressure does not itself indicate internal compressor damage. Adjusting thermostat differential alone does not fix the underlying pressure problem causing the trips. Disabling a safety control removes protection and does not correct the actual fault.
  9. The correct answer is B (Condensate may pool in the pan instead of draining fully to the outlet.): most air handler drain pans rely on a slight slope toward the drain connection to fully drain condensate by gravity, so a unit installed perfectly level, or worse tilted slightly away from the drain, can leave standing water pooling in the pan instead of draining completely, which can lead to overflow, microbial growth, or nuisance dripping. Sound output and airflow efficiency are not meaningfully affected by this small slope, and drain pan slope has no bearing on refrigerant charge warranty terms.
  10. The correct answer is A (Monitor suction and discharge pressures closely while listening for abnormal compressor noise.): The first moments after a new compressor starts are when problems like liquid slugging, incorrect rotation, or a stuck valve are most likely to reveal themselves. Watching the suction and discharge pressures stabilize into a normal pattern, combined with listening for knocking or unusual noise, lets the technician shut the unit down quickly if something is wrong. Front-seating a valve during startup would dangerously deadhead the compressor, and both the crankcase heater and the gauges are still needed at this stage.
  11. The correct answer is A (The trap collects returning oil so it can be swept upward by vapour velocity.): a short trap at the base of a suction riser collects a small amount of oil at the low point, which vapour flowing up the riser then picks up and carries along by velocity, helping ensure oil is continuously returned to the compressor rather than pooling at the bottom of the vertical run. The trap does not reduce refrigerant charge or equalize pressure between separate lines, and it has no role in blocking vapour flow during a defrost cycle.
  12. The correct answer is A (A mixture of liquid and vapour coexisting together): Inside the saturation dome, refrigerant exists as a two-phase mixture of liquid and vapour in equilibrium, which is exactly the condition found during most of the evaporation and condensation processes plotted on the diagram. Fully superheated vapour lies to the right of the dome, past the saturated vapour line, while fully subcooled liquid lies to the left of it, past the saturated liquid line. A supercritical fluid is a separate condition entirely, occurring only above the critical point at the top of the dome.
  13. The correct answer is C (Comparing the thermostat's displayed temperature against the reference thermometer's stabilized reading): Placing a known-accurate reference thermometer beside the thermostat sensor and letting both stabilize in the same air isolates whether the thermostat's own temperature reading is off, which is the direct definition of calibration drift. Comparing setpoint to factory default only shows what the user selected, not whether the sensor reads accurately. Supply air temperature reflects equipment output, not the thermostat sensor's own accuracy. Battery voltage relates to power supply health, not temperature sensing calibration.
  14. The correct answer is D (Isolate the vacuum pump with a valve, then watch the micron reading for ten minutes.): Closing the valve between the pump and the system isolates the vacuum so any pressure change reflects only the system itself, not the pump. Watching the micron gauge over roughly ten to fifteen minutes shows whether the reading holds steady, rises slowly and levels off, or climbs continuously. A steady or slightly rising reading that plateaus indicates a tight, dry system, while a continuous rise points to a leak. Disconnecting the gauge or opening to atmosphere would defeat the purpose of the test.
  15. The correct answer is D (Non-condensable gas such as air is present, contaminating the refrigerant charge.): A measured static pressure noticeably higher than the expected saturation pressure for the labelled refrigerant at the same stabilized temperature is a classic indicator of non-condensable gas, most commonly air, trapped in the cylinder. This contamination must be addressed before the refrigerant can be considered suitable for recycling or sent for reclamation processing. A defective gauge is possible but should only be considered after the more likely contamination explanation, and the discrepancy is not a normal reading.
  16. The correct answer is D (Type K tube has the thickest wall and suits underground or high pressure runs.): Type K copper tube has the heaviest wall thickness of the common types, which is why it is specified for underground, high pressure, or physically demanding installations. Type L has a medium wall thickness and is common for interior refrigerant piping, while ACR tubing wall thickness is comparable to Type L but cleaned for refrigeration use. Because wall thickness varies by designation at the same outside diameter, a mechanic must check the stamped marking rather than assume by appearance.
  17. The correct answer is B (Obtain confirmation from a structural engineer that the roof can support the unit's total operating load): confirming that an existing roof structure can support a new condensing unit's total operating weight, including any applicable snow load, is a structural engineering determination that should be obtained from a qualified structural engineer before the unit is installed. Manufacturers publish operating weight specifications, not only shipping weight, and this figure along with local snow load data is what the structural review must be based on. Rubber isolation pads address vibration transmission, not the roof's underlying load bearing capacity, so their use does not eliminate the need for structural confirmation. Electrical contractors are not qualified to assess structural capacity as part of an unrelated wiring permit process, so this step cannot substitute for a proper structural review.
  18. The correct answer is B (The refrigerant exhibits mild flammability while retaining lower toxicity classification.): The A2L designation specifically reflects mild flammability combined with lower toxicity, distinguishing it from the nonflammable A1 category. Ozone depletion potential and global warming potential relate to environmental classification, not the A1 or A2L safety classification distinction. End of service life recovery obligations apply to both A1 and A2L refrigerants equally and are not the basis for this classification difference.
  19. The correct answer is D (Every 1.5 to 2 metres, with insulated or cushioned support points): Copper tubing sags and work-hardens under its own weight if supports are spaced too far apart, which stresses brazed joints and can eventually crack them. Trade practice calls for supports roughly every 1.5 to 2 metres on horizontal copper runs, using cushioned clamps or insulated saddles so the support itself does not become a vibration or abrasion point.
  20. The correct answer is D (The letter indicates toxicity class and the number indicates flammability class): The letter indicates toxicity class and the number indicates flammability class. In the ASHRAE and CSA B52 safety group system, the letter A or B indicates toxicity, with A meaning lower toxicity and B meaning higher toxicity, while the number 1, 2L, 2 or 3 indicates flammability, ranging from no flame propagation at 1 up to higher flammability at 3, with 2L identifying lower flammability refrigerants like some newer low-GWP blends. This combined rating, such as A1 for a lower toxicity non-flammable refrigerant or A2L for a lower toxicity lower flammability refrigerant, directly drives machine room, ventilation and quantity limit requirements under CSA B52. It has no relationship to compressor type, capacity, or operating pressure range.
  21. The correct answer is C (3 tons of refrigeration, which is equal to about 10.55 kW of cooling): Dividing 36,000 BTU/h by 12,000 BTU/h per ton gives exactly 3 tons of refrigeration capacity. Multiplying 3 tons by 3.517 kW per ton gives approximately 10.55 kW, which is the metric equivalent of the same cooling effect. Option D repeats the correct tonnage but pairs it with an incorrect kilowatt figure that ignores the actual 3.517 kW conversion factor.
  22. The correct answer is B (The pressure differential across the balancing valve, converted using the valve's flow coefficient chart.): A calibrated balancing valve is designed with pressure ports specifically so a technician can measure the pressure differential across it and convert that reading into an actual flow rate using the manufacturer's published chart for that valve size and setting. Outdoor air temperature, pump amperage, and refrigerant pressure have no direct relationship to water flow through this valve. This method allows accurate hydronic balancing without needing a separate flow meter.
  23. The correct answer is D (Gas velocity has fallen below the minimum needed to carry oil back to the compressor): Gas velocity has fallen below the minimum needed to carry oil back to the compressor. When actual load and refrigerant mass flow drop well below the value a suction line was originally sized for, velocity through that same pipe diameter falls, and once it drops below roughly 2.5 m/s in horizontal runs the gas can no longer entrain and carry liquid oil droplets along the pipe wall. Oil then collects in low points and traps instead of returning to the compressor crankcase, which explains the steady drop in compressor oil level. An oversized line for reduced flow typically lowers pressure drop rather than raising it, so pressure drop and flash gas are not the operating issue here.
  24. The correct answer is B (Flow dry nitrogen through the tubing while brazing the joint.): Flowing dry nitrogen through the tubing during brazing displaces oxygen so copper oxide scale cannot form on the inside of the tube walls at brazing temperature. That scale, if allowed to form, later flakes off and travels through the system where it can clog the fine passages of a metering device or filter drier. Using compressed air instead of nitrogen introduces oxygen and moisture that promote oxidation, and skipping evacuation after a repair leaves air and moisture trapped in the system regardless of the successful braze.
  25. The correct answer is B (Positive subcooling ensures solid liquid, not flash gas, reaches the metering device): Keeping the liquid below its saturation temperature guarantees it stays fully liquid, without any flash gas bubbles forming before it reaches the metering device, which would otherwise reduce cooling capacity and cause erratic valve feeding. The metering device is designed to receive liquid, not vapour, so the opposite claim is incorrect. Subcooling is a functional design margin as well as a diagnostic reading, not merely a number on a gauge.
  26. The correct answer is B (The weak run capacitor is unable to provide adequate starting torque for the motor): A run capacitor that measures well below its rated microfarad value can no longer provide the phase shift needed to generate sufficient starting torque, so the motor hums under load without actually turning over. This starved condition draws high locked rotor current and trips the internal overload within seconds, exactly matching the described symptoms. A shorted winding would typically show as low resistance or continuity to ground on a meter, not as a weak capacitance reading. Replacing the run capacitor with one matching the rated value should restore normal starting.
  27. The correct answer is D (The wet bulb reading accounts for evaporative cooling and helps determine the air's moisture content.): As air passes over the wetted wick on the wet bulb thermometer, moisture evaporates from the wick and cools the bulb below the surrounding dry bulb temperature, with the amount of cooling depending on how much moisture the air can still absorb. Drier air causes more evaporation and a lower wet bulb reading, while saturated air produces almost no difference between the two readings. Comparing the wet bulb and dry bulb temperatures together on a psychrometric chart allows a technician to determine relative humidity, dew point, and total moisture content of the air sample. This combined reading is essential for accurately sizing cooling equipment where both sensible and latent heat loads must be addressed.
  28. The correct answer is D (Alkylbenzene oil is commonly specified since it offers improved miscibility with certain HCFC blends.): Alkylbenzene oil was developed as an alternative to straight mineral oil for use with certain HCFC blend refrigerants where mineral oil alone showed limited or marginal miscibility at low evaporator temperatures. It is sometimes used on its own or blended with mineral oil to improve oil return performance in these systems. This is distinct from polyol ester oil, which is reserved specifically for HFC refrigerants that are not compatible with mineral or alkylbenzene oils at all. Selecting the correct oil family for the refrigerant actually in the system remains one of the most basic but critical decisions a technician makes during any service or conversion job.
  29. The correct answer is C (Attempt to start the equipment and test for zero voltage present): A lock and tag only prove that a device is locked, not that the energy source is actually isolated, so the technician must attempt a start and use a meter to verify zero energy remains before beginning work. Option A confirms the lock is visible but says nothing about whether the disconnect actually cut power. Option B checks paperwork accuracy but does not test the equipment itself for stored or residual energy. Option D confirms the correct device was located but skips the verification test entirely.
  30. The correct answer is D (The low outdoor temperature and light load are dropping suction pressure below freezing): With airflow, filter condition, and refrigerant charge all confirmed correct, the icing and short cycling must stem from operating conditions rather than a mechanical or charge fault. A mild day with a light load reduces the heat load on the evaporator, which can drop suction pressure and coil temperature below freezing even on a properly charged, properly airflowing system, causing frost to build and the low pressure switch to trip repeatedly. Overcharge and grounded windings would each produce very different symptom patterns than this specific icing and cycling combination. Addressing this typically involves a low-ambient or low-load control strategy rather than adding refrigerant.
  31. The correct answer is D (The equipment schedule table listing model numbers and specifications): equipment schedules are dedicated tables within a drawing set that list every piece of equipment by tag number along with its capacity, electrical characteristics, and model information, making them the fastest way to find this exact data. General notes on the cover sheet, as in option A, usually cover broad project requirements rather than specific equipment data. The revision block in option B only tracks drawing changes over time, and the north arrow and scale indicator in option C are orientation and measurement tools, not sources of equipment specifications.
  32. The correct answer is A (brazed joints, because a concealed location removes any chance to tighten a loosened mechanical fitting later): mechanical fittings can loosen slightly over years of thermal cycling and vibration, and that is manageable where a mechanic can reach the joint to retorque or replace it. Inside a permanently concealed cavity with no access panel, that same slow loosening would go undetected and become a hidden leak that is expensive to locate and repair. A properly made brazed joint does not rely on ongoing mechanical clamping force and is the appropriate choice for a location that will never be opened again. Brazed joints can absolutely be formed inside a wall cavity before it is closed, so a rule limiting concealed work to mechanical fittings only does not hold up.
  33. The correct answer is B (The compressor raises the pressure and temperature of low pressure vapour from the evaporator.): The compressor is the mechanical heart of the cycle, drawing in low pressure vapour from the suction line and compressing it into a high pressure, high temperature superheated vapour. This pressure increase is what allows the refrigerant to reject heat at a temperature above the surrounding air in the condenser. Without this stage, the refrigerant would never reach a high enough pressure and temperature to condense against ambient conditions. The other three stages each move heat or change pressure but do not add mechanical work to the refrigerant the way the compressor does.
  34. The correct answer is C (An open leg on the incoming three-phase supply, commonly called single-phasing): when one of the three legs shows no voltage relative to the other two, the motor is only receiving power on two of its three phases. A three-phase induction motor cannot develop a proper rotating magnetic field on two legs alone, so it hums and draws high current without turning, quickly tripping the overload. Common causes include a blown fuse, a failed disconnect blade, or a loose terminal upstream of the compressor. This fault has nothing to do with capacitors or thermal protectors, which apply to single-phase motor designs.
  35. The correct answer is A (That the roof structure has adequate capacity to support the unit's total weight): Rooftop units add substantial concentrated dead load plus dynamic wind and, in many regions, snow load to a roof structure that was not necessarily designed with that equipment in mind. Confirming structural capacity, often through a review by a structural engineer or the building owner's documentation, is essential before the unit is permanently mounted, since correcting an inadequate structure after installation is far more costly.
  36. The correct answer is C (The metering device is overfeeding, flooding the evaporator with excess liquid refrigerant): The diagram shows only a 1°C difference between the suction temperature and the saturation temperature at the metered and suction pressure, which is an abnormally low superheat that indicates liquid refrigerant is not fully boiling off before leaving the evaporator coil. A restricted metering device would instead produce the opposite pattern, a low suction pressure paired with high superheat as too little refrigerant reaches the coil. The relatively low 60°C discharge temperature also fits an overfeeding fault, since returning liquid or wet vapour helps cool the compressor below its normal discharge temperature.
  37. The correct answer is A (Pascals, the SI unit for pressure typically expressed as kilopascals for larger values): Pascals, and kilopascals for larger magnitudes, are the SI pressure units used on Canadian mechanical drawings and commissioning documentation. Pounds per square inch is an imperial unit tied to refrigerant charge pressure, not duct static pressure. Degrees Celsius measures temperature, an entirely different physical quantity from pressure. British thermal units per hour measure a rate of heat transfer, not a pressure differential.
  38. The correct answer is C (A small riser sized for minimum load pairs with a large riser trapped for full load): The small riser is sized to maintain adequate oil-carrying velocity even at minimum unloaded capacity, while the larger riser includes its own trap and only carries refrigerant once velocity and pressure rise enough at higher loads to lift the oil trapped at its base. Together the pair maintains oil return across the compressor's full modulation range, which a single fixed-diameter riser cannot do.
  39. The correct answer is B (The refrigerant type, quantity recovered, date, and how it was ultimately handled.): Compliance records must capture enough detail to trace a given refrigerant charge from recovery through its eventual disposition, meaning the refrigerant type, quantity, date of the service call, and whether it was recycled, sent for reclamation, or otherwise disposed of. Billing information and equipment make and model alone do not satisfy this tracking purpose. A technician's certification number is useful but insufficient without the accompanying refrigerant handling details.
  40. The correct answer is C (It prevents refrigerant vapor from condensing and migrating into the crankcase oil.): During any off cycle, the coldest part of the system attracts refrigerant vapor, and the compressor shell is often the coldest point. The crankcase heater keeps the oil slightly warmer than the surrounding refrigerant so vapor cannot condense and dissolve into it. Without this, the next startup pumps foaming, refrigerant-laden oil through the cylinders and risks liquid slugging. Oil freezing and demand charges are not the reason this heater is required.
  41. The correct answer is B (A restricted metering device is starving the evaporator of liquid.): Low suction pressure paired with high superheat at the compressor inlet is the defining signature of a starved evaporator, meaning too little liquid refrigerant is reaching the coil to fully load it. Because subcooling ahead of the metering device tests normal, the restriction is isolated to the metering device itself rather than a charge or condenser problem upstream. Flooding produces the opposite pattern of high suction pressure and low superheat, and overcharge or a dirty condenser would primarily affect head pressure rather than this suction side reading.
  42. The correct answer is C (3,600 watts of power, found by multiplying voltage by current): the power formula P = V × I gives 240 volts multiplied by 15 amps, equal to 3,600 watts, or 3.6 kilowatts. This calculation assumes a purely resistive load and gives an approximation of real power draw for a single-phase circuit. Actual compressor power factor can shift this value slightly, but the basic formula remains the starting point for estimating electrical load. Adding or dividing the two values instead of multiplying them would give a meaningless result with no electrical significance.
  43. The correct answer is D (The 15 kg unaccounted for and 9 undocumented jobs indicate incomplete recovery record keeping.): The exhibit shows 15 kg of recovered refrigerant unaccounted for and 9 of 112 jobs missing recovery entries entirely, both of which point to gaps in the shop's recovery record keeping that should be raised before a compliance review. Total purchases exceeding recovered quantities does not by itself demonstrate compliance, since it does not explain the specific unaccounted amount. Unaccounted refrigerant is not something compliance recordkeeping is expected to leave unexplained, and cylinder inventory count alone does not substitute for complete recovery documentation.
  44. The correct answer is C (At least 24 hours, allowing dissolved liquid refrigerant to migrate out of the oil): a crankcase heater must run long enough, typically at least 24 hours before the first startup, to drive absorbed liquid refrigerant out of the compressor oil and prevent foaming and liquid slugging when the compressor first starts. Starting after only a brief warm-up, regardless of ambient temperature, risks refrigerant flashing violently out of the oil and damaging the compressor's mechanical components. A quick hand check of shell temperature is not a reliable or measurable substitute for the required warm-up time.
  45. The correct answer is C (It establishes a trend history that helps diagnose developing problems early.): Detailed records from multiple visits over time let a technician compare current readings against past readings for the same equipment, revealing gradual trends such as a slowly rising head pressure or falling suction pressure that would be invisible from a single visit alone. This trend history often allows a developing problem to be caught and corrected before it causes a failure or a costly breakdown. Records remain valuable well beyond the day of the visit and are useful for both warranty and non-warranty equipment alike, not just one category.
  46. The correct answer is C (Approximately 80 ohms, calculated by dividing 24 volts by 0.3 amps): Ohm's Law states resistance equals voltage divided by current, so R = 24V ÷ 0.3A = 80Ω. This value represents the coil winding resistance under normal energized conditions. A significantly lower reading would suggest a shorted coil, while an open winding would show infinite resistance on a meter.
  47. The correct answer is B (Wind is dispersing the escaping refrigerant gas before it reaches the sensor): Outdoor wind can scatter escaping refrigerant gas away from the joint before the sensor tip passes close enough to detect it, producing a false negative even though a real leak is present. Option A describes proper preparation that would actually improve detector sensitivity rather than cause a missed leak. Option C is the correct slow scanning technique recommended for accurate detection and would not itself cause a false negative. Option D removing contaminants also improves detection accuracy rather than hindering it, since oil and dirt can otherwise mask a leak.
  48. The correct answer is D (Nitrogen displaces oxygen inside the pipe to prevent internal scale formation): Flowing dry nitrogen through the piping during brazing displaces the oxygen inside the tube, which prevents the internal copper surface from oxidizing into black scale that would otherwise break loose and contaminate the system later. Option A is incorrect because nitrogen purging does not meaningfully cool the flame or the joint being heated externally. Option B is false since nitrogen has no effect on the brazing alloy's melting characteristics. Option C is also false because nitrogen purging affects internal oxidation, not the mechanical strength or cooling rate of the joint itself.
  49. The correct answer is A (The system is overcharged, backing excess liquid refrigerant up into the condenser): An overcharged system has more liquid refrigerant than the condenser needs to fully condense at normal load, so the extra liquid backs up and floods part of the condenser coil, which both raises the discharge pressure and increases subcooling. A restricted filter drier would instead produce low subcooling downstream of the restriction along with a noticeable pressure drop and often a frosted or cold spot at the restriction point. High subcooling combined with high head pressure is a classic overcharge signature.
  50. The correct answer is C (Regulatory recordkeeping expects a documented quantity to demonstrate no refrigerant was released improperly.): Even when refrigerant is properly recovered, regulatory recordkeeping expects the recovered quantity to be documented so that the paper trail itself demonstrates none of the charge was released improperly during decommissioning. Relying solely on the reclaimer's separate paperwork leaves a gap in the shop's own compliance file. This expectation is not limited to A2L refrigerants and is not merely cosmetic.
  51. The correct answer is D (It would raise the effective entering air temperature and increase condensing pressure): It would raise the effective entering air temperature and increase condensing pressure. When warm discharge air recirculates back into a condenser's intake side, the effective entering air temperature at the coil rises above true outdoor ambient, which reduces the achievable temperature difference for heat rejection and forces the condensing pressure and temperature upward to reject the same heat load, reducing efficiency and potentially triggering high-pressure safety trips. This is a real and well documented siting problem, not a negligible effect, and it works in the opposite direction from lowering suction pressure or reducing charge requirement, since higher condensing pressure generally increases the pressure difference the compressor works against. Proper siting and orientation relative to prevailing wind is part of avoiding this recirculation problem at the design stage.
  52. The correct answer is C (The system is likely overcharged, and refrigerant should be recovered in small amounts): on a TXV-equipped system, elevated subcooling above the target range while superheat still reads normally is a classic sign of excess liquid refrigerant backing up in the condenser, effectively an overcharge condition. Because the TXV actively regulates superheat, it can mask an overcharge on the superheat reading alone, which is exactly why subcooling is checked as the primary charging indicator on TXV systems. A single high subcooling reading does not mean the thermometer is unreliable, and a functioning TXV holding normal superheat argues against outright valve failure.
  53. The correct answer is D (Excessive pressure drop raises discharge pressure and reduces compressor efficiency): an undersized discharge line forces refrigerant through a smaller flow area at the design mass flow rate, creating higher friction losses that raise the effective discharge pressure the compressor must work against, reducing capacity and efficiency. Oil dilution relates to liquid refrigerant mixing with oil in the crankcase, not discharge line sizing. Minimum velocity for oil return is a suction and hot gas riser concern rather than a straight horizontal discharge run, and flood back during the off cycle is unrelated to discharge line diameter.
  54. The correct answer is B (The three offset supply phases already create a rotating magnetic field): The three offset supply phases already create a rotating magnetic field. Because the three phases are 120 electrical degrees apart, their combined magnetic fields naturally rotate around the stator without any switching devices. This rotating field induces current in the rotor and produces continuous starting torque from the moment power is applied. Single-phase motors lack this natural rotation, which is why they need extra components to create an artificial phase shift for starting.
  55. The correct answer is C (Superheat is 16°C, above target, suggesting the system is undercharged and needs refrigerant added): superheat equals the suction line temperature minus the saturated evaporating temperature, which here is 14°C minus negative 2°C, giving a positive 16°C. That value sits well above the 6°C to 10°C target, a pattern typical of a fixed-orifice system running short on refrigerant. A negative saturation temperature does not prevent the calculation, it simply means subtracting a negative number, which increases the result rather than blocking it. Reading 16°C as within range or as evidence of overcharge both misapply the target band given.
  56. The correct answer is B (The contactor coil itself is open and cannot generate a magnetic field): Measuring the correct 24 volts across the coil terminals confirms the low voltage circuit and the thermostat call are both working properly, ruling out the thermostat and the transformer as the cause. If a coil receiving its rated voltage still fails to pull the contactor in, the coil winding itself has failed open internally and can no longer generate the magnetic field needed to close the contacts. A high pressure switch fault would instead prevent voltage from ever reaching the coil in the first place. The contactor must be replaced to restore compressor and condenser fan operation.
  57. The correct answer is D (Cryogenic or thermal gloves rated for exposure to liquid refrigerant): liquid refrigerant flashing to vapor at a fitting drops in temperature extremely fast and can cause frostbite within seconds, so purpose built cryogenic or thermal gloves are the only option designed to resist that cold transfer. Cotton gloves in A offer no thermal barrier and can actually trap liquid against the skin. Nitrile gloves in B are meant for chemical splashes, not sub-zero cold, and electrical lineman gloves in C address voltage hazards, not temperature, so neither protects against frostbite.
  58. Recording the saturation temperature alone, or an unrelated intermediate value, does not represent the true superheat reading. This 7 C figure would then be compared against the manufacturer's target range for this system and outdoor condition.

Subtracting that from the measured suction line temperature of 20°C gives a superheat of exactly 10°C, matching the target used for this fixed-orifice system. Since the reading sits right at the target, no further refrigerant needs to be added or removed. The superheat method applies specifically to fixed-orifice systems, not TXV systems, so option D has that backwards.
  60. The correct answer is A (Gases under pressure, including compressed, liquefied, or dissolved gases): The gas cylinder pictogram specifically identifies gases under pressure, a category that includes the compressed and liquefied refrigerant gases technicians handle daily in cylinders and system charges. Option B describes the corrosion pictogram, which shows a different image involving a test tube pouring onto a surface and a hand. Option C describes the health hazard pictogram, shown as a human silhouette with a starburst on the chest. Option D describes the flame pictogram used for flammable materials, which is a visually distinct symbol from the gas cylinder icon.
  61. The correct answer is A (Conduction, heat transfer occurring directly through a solid material): Heat transfer directly through a solid material, such as the copper wall of a refrigerant tube, from a warmer region to a cooler region within that same solid, is conduction. Convection instead requires a moving fluid, such as air or water, carrying heat away from a surface. Radiation transfers heat as electromagnetic waves without requiring contact, and sublimation is a phase change from solid directly to vapour, not a heat transfer mode at all.
  62. The correct answer is D (The system is undercharged and likely has a refrigerant leak.): With both coil airflows confirmed normal, an abnormally low suction pressure well below the value expected for a 5°C evaporating temperature points directly to insufficient refrigerant mass in the system rather than an airflow restriction. Since charge does not disappear on its own, an undercharge found during a service call should prompt a leak search before simply adding refrigerant. Overcharge would raise pressures rather than lower them, and the airflow related options were already ruled out by the confirmed normal readings on both coils.
  63. The correct answer is B (Nitrogen displaces oxygen inside the tube, preventing internal copper oxide scale.): flowing dry nitrogen through the tubing while brazing displaces the oxygen inside the pipe, which prevents the high heat from forming black copper oxide scale on the interior tube walls. That scale, if allowed to form, can later flake off and contaminate filter driers, metering devices, and valves once refrigerant is circulating. Nitrogen purging does not raise flame temperature and is not used at brazing time to pressure test for leaks, since the tube is still open to atmosphere during the process.
  64. The correct answer is C (Both switches must be closed at the same time to complete the circuit): because the two safety switches are wired in series with each other and with the contactor coil, current can only reach the coil when every component on that rung is closed. If refrigerant pressure trips either switch open, the series path is broken and the contactor cannot energize regardless of the other switch's position. This series arrangement is intentional so that any single abnormal pressure condition, high or low, safely shuts the compressor down. A parallel arrangement would instead let either switch alone complete the circuit, which is not what this diagram shows.
  65. The correct answer is D (An external motor overload, sensing both current draw and shell heat): this bimetal disc device is strapped or bolted to the compressor shell so it responds to both the current flowing through it and the heat radiating from the compressor body. When either value exceeds its design limit, the disc opens the common circuit and stops the compressor before winding damage occurs. It resets automatically once the compressor cools. External overloads are common on smaller reciprocating compressors as an alternative to an internal winding protector.
  66. The correct answer is A (A gauge pressure reading at altitude corresponds to a different sea-level saturation temperature.): Gauge pressure is measured relative to the surrounding atmospheric pressure, and atmospheric pressure itself decreases with increasing elevation, so a fixed gauge reading actually represents a different absolute pressure at a higher elevation than it would at sea level. Since saturation temperature is a function of absolute pressure, not gauge pressure, the same gauge reading at altitude corresponds to a slightly different true saturation temperature than the standard sea-level pressure-temperature chart would indicate. Most trade pressure-temperature charts are built around standard sea-level atmospheric pressure, so significant elevation differences can introduce a small but real error if not accounted for. Technicians working at high elevation sites need to be aware of this shift when precise superheat and subcooling calculations matter.
  67. The correct answer is A (An economizer port, used to inject intermediate pressure vapor and improve compressor efficiency): an economizer port on a screw compressor allows partially expanded refrigerant vapor from an intermediate pressure stage, often from a flash tank or subcooling circuit, to be introduced directly into the compression process partway through the compression cycle. This reduces the work required to compress that portion of refrigerant from suction pressure all the way to discharge pressure, improving overall system efficiency. A relief port, crankcase oil port, and purge port each serve entirely different functions and are not related to intermediate pressure vapor injection for efficiency improvement. Recognizing the economizer port helps a mechanic correctly identify piping connections on an industrial screw compressor installation.
  68. The correct answer is C (The suction line velocity is too low to carry oil droplets back to the compressor): Refrigerant vapour must move fast enough through the suction line to carry small droplets of oil along with it back to the compressor, and a line that is oversized for the actual load produces velocities too low to accomplish this, allowing oil to pool at low points or vertical risers. This is a common problem on long line sets with rooftop or remote equipment where the line was sized for a larger future load rather than the current one. Correcting it typically requires resizing the suction line or adding a properly designed oil trap and double riser.
  69. The correct answer is A (The smaller riser stays active at part load when the larger riser alone runs too slow): a double riser arrangement uses a trap and interconnecting piping so that at full load, refrigerant flows through both risers together, since combined velocity in the larger riser is adequate. As the compressor unloads and total flow decreases, the trap causes refrigerant to route preferentially through the smaller diameter riser, keeping velocity high enough in that smaller pipe to carry oil upward even though total system flow has dropped. This maintains oil return across the full range of compressor loading rather than only at one operating condition. The smaller riser works alongside the larger one and the trap, rather than replacing either.
  70. The correct answer is D (Vertical axis is pressure, horizontal axis is enthalpy): A pressure-enthalpy diagram plots pressure, usually on a logarithmic scale, along the vertical axis and specific enthalpy along the horizontal axis, which lets a technician trace all four cycle stages as a closed loop. The other axis pairings describe different chart types, such as a temperature-entropy diagram, that are not the standard P-h chart used for refrigeration cycle analysis. Reading the correct axes correctly is essential before interpreting any line on the diagram.
  71. The correct answer is C (No, the phosphorus content is self-fluxing on copper to copper joints): Phosphorus-bearing alloys like Sil-Fos react with copper oxide at brazing temperature and clean the joint surface as the alloy flows, which is why manufacturers rate them for copper-to-copper work without added flux. Flux becomes mandatory the moment either side of the joint is brass, bronze, or steel, since phosphorus alone cannot clean those base metals.
  72. The correct answer is D (Millimetres per second, the SI unit of vibration velocity used in severity charts): Vibration severity standards like ISO 10816 classify equipment condition using vibration velocity expressed in millimetres per second, which is the standard SI unit for this purpose. Newtons measure force, a different physical quantity unrelated to vibration velocity readings. Kilograms measure mass, describing how heavy the compressor is rather than how it vibrates. Lumens measure light output and have no connection to mechanical vibration measurement.
  73. The correct answer is D (10°C, found by subtracting the saturation temperature from the suction line temperature): superheat is always the actual suction line temperature minus the saturated evaporating temperature taken at that same suction pressure. Here that is 15°C minus 5°C, which equals 10°C of superheat at the evaporator outlet. Adding the two values instead of subtracting, or dividing one by the other, produces a number with no physical meaning in refrigeration charging. Ignoring the saturation temperature entirely defeats the purpose of the superheat method on a fixed-orifice system.
  74. The correct answer is B (The system has been overcharged with refrigerant beyond its rated nameplate amount): With a clean condenser and normal airflow already confirmed, mechanical restriction is ruled out as the cause of the elevated gauge reading. The service history showing extra refrigerant added without a proper weigh-in points directly to overcharge, which floods extra liquid into the condenser and reduces the surface area available for the vapor to condense, raising head pressure. A starved evaporator or a closed expansion valve bulb would instead produce low suction pressure, not this high side symptom. The correct fix is to recover refrigerant down to the manufacturer's specified charge weight.
  75. The correct answer is B (Diagnose and relieve the restriction causing elevated head pressure before continuing recovery.): A restricted filter drier raises head pressure during recovery and repeatedly trips the machine's high pressure safety switch, so the technician must diagnose and relieve that restriction before continuing rather than forcing the machine to keep operating. Bypassing the safety switch defeats a protective device and risks equipment damage or injury. Increasing charge or venting pressure to atmosphere does not address the underlying restriction, and the latter is also prohibited.
  76. The correct answer is A (The ECM module needs low voltage signal wires programmed for airflow settings): The ECM module needs low voltage signal wires programmed for airflow settings. Unlike a PSC motor that simply receives line voltage and a capacitor connection, an ECM's internal control module reads low voltage signals to select speed taps or CFM profiles for heating, cooling, and fan-only operation. Skipping this programming step can leave the blower running at a default speed that does not match the equipment's design airflow. The technician must confirm the correct signal wires are landed according to the manufacturer's wiring chart.
  77. The correct answer is C (R-1234yf was adopted mainly because its global warming potential is far lower than that of R-134a.): R-134a carries a global warming potential of approximately 1,430, while R-1234yf, a hydrofluoroolefin refrigerant, has a global warming potential of only about 4, making it thousands of times less impactful per kilogram released into the atmosphere. Regulatory pressure in multiple markets, including European Union mobile air conditioning directives, pushed automakers to adopt lower-GWP refrigerants for new vehicle production. R-1234yf does carry an A2L mild flammability rating, unlike the nonflammable R-134a, which required updated service equipment and precautions rather than eliminating the need for specialized tools altogether. The dominant driver of the industry-wide switch remains the dramatic difference in environmental impact between the two refrigerants.
  78. The correct answer is C (Reciprocating compressor, valued here for simplicity and field repairability): reciprocating compressors remain a practical choice for small commercial reach-in coolers because their piston and valve design is straightforward to service in the field, tolerates occasional liquid slugging better than some designs, and carries a lower first cost than more complex alternatives. Screw compressors are reserved for much larger industrial capacities and are not practical here. Centrifugal compressors suit large chilled water plants, and rotary vane compressors are rarely applied outside very small window unit style applications.
  79. The correct answer is C (The compressor's internal discharge valve reeds have failed mechanically inside the cylinder): When discharge reeds fracture or fail to seal, compressed gas can leak back past the valve during the compression stroke, which explains both the abnormally low discharge pressure and the abnormally high suction pressure closer together than normal. This internal bypass also reduces the compressor's effective pumping capacity, showing up as lower than rated amperage even though the motor itself runs. A refrigerant leak or a fouled condenser would produce a very different pressure pattern than pressures converging toward each other. The rattling valve noise combined with this pressure signature points specifically to mechanical valve failure requiring compressor replacement.
  80. The correct answer is B (The system is undercharged and needs additional refrigerant added to specification): Low suction pressure, low subcooling, and high superheat together are the classic three-reading signature of an undercharged system, since insufficient liquid refrigerant in the condenser and evaporator produces exactly this combination. An overcharged system would instead show high subcooling and often elevated discharge pressure, the opposite of what was measured here. Confirmed correct airflow and no restrictions in the liquid line or metering device rule out those as the underlying cause. The technician should add refrigerant to bring the charge up to the manufacturer's specified subcooling or superheat target.
  81. The correct answer is D (Report the concern to the supervisor or employer before refusing the work): The right to refuse process under the Act requires the worker to first report the perceived hazard to their supervisor or employer, who must then investigate before any further steps such as involving a joint health and safety committee member occur. Option A skips the required internal reporting step and jumps straight to an external authority too early in the process. Option B fails to formally invoke the refusal process at all and leaves the worker exposed to the hazard. Option C substitutes a coworker opinion for the mandatory employer notification the process requires.
  82. The correct answer is A (The coefficient of performance for this system is approximately 3.5.): COP is calculated as the useful cooling output divided by the energy input required to produce it, so dividing 10.5 kW of cooling effect by 3.0 kW of compressor power input gives a COP of exactly 3.5. This unitless value shows the system delivers 3.5 units of cooling for every unit of electrical energy consumed, which is why COP is such a useful efficiency comparison across differently sized equipment. A higher COP always indicates a more efficient system for the same operating conditions. This same formula structure is later adapted for heat pump heating COP calculations, though the useful output measured changes from cooling to heating.
  83. The correct answer is A (Attempt to start the equipment using its normal controls to verify isolation): trying the start button or switch after locking out confirms the energy source is truly isolated before any hands on work begins, which is the actual verification step required by lockout procedure. Simply having a coworker look at the lock, as in A, or photographing it, as in C, confirms the lock is present but not that isolation actually worked. Removing the lock to inspect the disconnect, as in D, defeats the entire purpose of the lockout and reintroduces the hazard.
  84. The correct answer is A (Draw refrigerant off as liquid so the full blend composition enters the system together.): Zeotropic blends are made of components with different boiling points, so pulling refrigerant from the cylinder as vapour lets the more volatile component boil off first, shifting the ratio of the remaining liquid in the cylinder. Charging as liquid moves the entire mixed composition into the system together, preserving the intended blend ratio and its designed performance characteristics. This is why manufacturers of blends such as R-410A and R-454B specify liquid charging on the cylinder instructions. Charging a zeotropic blend incorrectly as vapour can leave a system with refrigerant that no longer matches its rated pressure-temperature behavior.
  85. The correct answer is C (This is not acceptable, since operating before inspection approval violates the permit process): This is not acceptable, since operating before inspection approval violates the permit process. CSA B52 as enforced by provincial regulatory authorities requires that a permitted refrigeration system receive inspection and approval before it is placed into operation, and proceeding to charge and commission the system beforehand bypasses that safety check regardless of how confident the contractor is that approval will follow. Thorough documentation, refrigerant classification, or the building owner's written permission do not substitute for the regulatory approval itself, since the permit and inspection process exists precisely to have an independent authority verify the installation before it operates. Anticipating approval is not the same as receiving it, and starting early exposes the contractor and owner to real compliance and safety risk.
  86. The correct answer is B (A variable frequency drive providing modulating capacity, since it matches output closely to varying load): a variable frequency drive allows the compressor motor speed, and therefore refrigerant flow and capacity, to be adjusted continuously to closely track the building's actual cooling demand at any moment. This avoids the inefficiency of staged on-off control, where a full-capacity compressor cycles on and off and often overshoots the actual part load requirement before shutting down. Compressor efficiency is not constant across load, and matching output more precisely to demand generally reduces both energy use and mechanical wear from frequent cycling. Modern variable frequency drives used in refrigeration applications typically modulate well below fifty percent of rated speed.
  87. The correct answer is A (Recover the refrigerant charge and purge with nitrogen before any open flame work.): Because R-32 is mildly flammable, refrigerant must be fully recovered and the line set purged with nitrogen to remove any flammable vapour before an open flame brazing operation begins. Compressed air must never replace nitrogen for purging, since it introduces oxygen and moisture rather than an inert atmosphere. Skipping the leak test is unsafe and unrelated to the flammability precaution required here.
  88. The correct answer is B (It provides significantly higher starting torque for hard starting loads): the start capacitor briefly adds extra capacitance during starting, which boosts starting torque well beyond what the run capacitor alone can provide. This makes capacitor-start capacitor-run motors well suited to compressors that must start against high head pressure. Once the motor reaches roughly 75 percent of running speed, a start relay disconnects the start capacitor and the run capacitor continues supporting normal operation. The design does not remove the need for overload protection or change the motor from single-phase to three-phase.

Dividing 8.9 kW by 3.517 kW per ton gives approximately 2.53 tons, so a 2.5 ton nominal unit is the closest standard size to the calculated load without significant oversizing. A 1.5 ton unit would leave the room undercooled during peak equipment load, while 4 or 6 ton options assume future capacity or redundancy that was not part of the stated design load and would cause the system to short-cycle at the actual load. Redundancy and expansion allowances are separate design decisions made explicitly, not built in through oversizing alone.
  90. Options A and C both dramatically understate the true force involved in arresting a fall. Option D is incorrect because an anchor must meet this specific engineered rating, not just appear structurally sound by eye.
  91. The correct answer is A (Relocate the unit or revise the layout to meet the manufacturer's minimum clearance): Relocate the unit or revise the layout to meet the manufacturer's minimum clearance. Manufacturer clearance requirements exist to guarantee adequate condenser airflow and physical access for coil cleaning and service, and installing below the specified minimum risks reduced heat rejection, nuisance high-pressure trips and voided warranty coverage. Electrical clearance rules address a different hazard and do not substitute for the mechanical airflow clearance stated in the installation manual. The correct design response is to revise the layout, not to add compensating equipment or simply accept the shortfall.
  92. The correct answer is C (Forced convection, heat carried away by fan-driven moving air): Forced convection describes heat transfer to a fluid that is being actively moved by an external device, in this case a fan pushing air across the coil surface, which is far more effective at removing heat than air moving on its own. Conduction through still air is a much slower and less significant path here since the air is being actively circulated. Radiation plays only a minor role compared to convection at typical coil surface temperatures, and natural convection describes airflow driven only by buoyancy, not by a fan.
  93. The correct answer is A (It dilutes and flushes out non-condensable gases and moisture with each cycle.): Each time the vacuum is broken with dry nitrogen and pulled down again, the remaining contaminated atmosphere inside the system is diluted and a portion is removed with the next evacuation. Repeating this three times progressively lowers the concentration of air, moisture vapor, and other non-condensables far more effectively than one long single pull on a heavily contaminated system. A final micron gauge reading and standing vacuum test are still required afterward. Nitrogen breaks are not a leak check method.
  94. The correct answer is D (Open the circuit to stop the compressor before internal damage occurs): Open the circuit to stop the compressor before internal damage occurs. An external line break overload monitors both current draw and shell temperature, and it opens its internal contacts to interrupt power to the compressor when either value exceeds its calibrated limit. This protects the motor windings from overheating due to excessive current or poor heat rejection from the shell. Once conditions cool and current drops, most external overloads reset automatically and allow the compressor to restart.
  95. The correct answer is C (Conduct a verification retest confirming the leak rate is now below the threshold.): After completing the physical repair, the technician must conduct a verification retest confirming the leak rate has actually dropped below the applicable threshold before the equipment can be considered compliant again. A completed repair alone does not confirm the leak was actually resolved. Waiting a full calendar year before retesting ignores the retest obligation that follows directly from the repair, and replacing the entire system is not required when repair has addressed the leak.
  96. The correct answer is D (A per-person sensible and latent heat allowance is multiplied by expected occupancy count): A per-person sensible and latent heat allowance is multiplied by expected occupancy count. Standard load calculation practice applies published per-person sensible and latent heat gain values, which vary with activity level, and multiplies them by the expected occupant count for the space to arrive at the total internal occupant load, adding both components separately to the space's overall sensible and latent totals. Occupant heat gain is a real and often significant contributor in densely occupied spaces and should never be ignored or tied arbitrarily to lighting load or a fixed occupant count threshold. Accurately estimating occupancy is important because underestimating it directly understates both the sensible and, particularly, the latent portion of the load.
  97. Subtracting the measured liquid line temperature of 28°C gives a subcooling of 18°C, well above the 8 to 12°C acceptable band around this system's 10°C target. A subcooling reading that has climbed well above target since original commissioning points toward refrigerant having been added since then, backing extra liquid up in the condenser, rather than a leak, which would instead lower subcooling.
  98. The correct answer is A (Brush a generous coat of solution completely around the joint and watch for growing bubbles): A generous, complete coating of soap solution around the entire joint, held in place while watching for bubbles that grow and persist, is the standard technique because a leak can occur anywhere around the joint's circumference, not just at one spot. Wiping the solution away immediately removes the film needed for bubbles to form and grow. Leak location around a brazed joint is not limited to the top surface. Nitrogen pressurization is a standard and effective way to perform a soap bubble test without needing refrigerant in the system.
  99. The correct answer is B (The mismatch will likely cause poor oil return, risking compressor damage from oil starvation.): Mineral oil does not mix well with HFC refrigerants such as R-134a the way it did with the older CFC refrigerant R-12 the system was originally designed around. Poor miscibility means the mineral oil can separate from the circulating refrigerant and collect in the evaporator, condenser, or suction line instead of returning to the compressor crankcase as intended. Over time, this oil starvation increases friction and wear inside the compressor and can lead to a complete compressor failure. This is exactly why refrigerant conversions and service work require using the oil type specified for the actual refrigerant in the system, not the oil associated with a previous refrigerant.
  100. The correct answer is D (Residual moisture vaporizing and stabilizing, which is generally acceptable): When isolated vacuum pressure rises and then levels off at a stable reading, it usually means trace moisture or trapped vapor is outgassing and reaching equilibrium rather than a continuous inflow from outside the system. A true leak instead shows a steady, continuing rise toward atmospheric pressure with no leveling off, since outside air keeps entering at a constant rate.
