Personal home addresses have no place in a technical report and raise privacy concerns. Blank space for unrelated future notes serves no purpose within a specific project's report. Decorative artwork has no functional role in a technical commissioning document.
  102. The correct answer is D (It gives future service calls a known-good reference point for comparison.): Recording the exact operating conditions at commissioning, when the system is known to be correctly charged and functioning, creates a documented baseline that any future technician can compare against during a service call. A later reading that differs significantly from this baseline points quickly toward a developing problem such as a leak, a fouled coil, or a failing component. While often referenced during warranty claims, its practical value goes far beyond paperwork, and it remains useful regardless of refrigerant type.
  103. The correct answer is D (The defrost sensor or defrost control board has failed to trigger the cycle): Because manually forcing defrost proves the reversing valve and compressor both operate correctly, the mechanical refrigerant-side components of the defrost system are ruled out as the fault. The problem must instead be upstream in the control logic that decides when to initiate defrost, meaning the sensor reading coil temperature or the control board itself has failed to recognize the frost buildup and issue the call. A weak start capacitor would affect compressor starting, not the automatic defrost initiation logic. The defrost sensor and control board should be tested and replaced as needed to restore automatic defrost cycling.
  104. The correct answer is A (Lower toxicity and no flame propagation, meaning the refrigerant is classified as nonflammable.): The A1 classification specifically indicates lower toxicity combined with no flame propagation, meaning the refrigerant is nonflammable under the applicable safety classification system. It does not describe a refrigerant that is readily ignitable or one requiring flammable gas storage precautions. A1 is unrelated to whether a refrigerant has been phased out, and it does not carry the higher toxicity or mild flammability associated with other classifications.
  105. The correct answer is B (Six randomly distributed broken wires within a single rope lay length): Wire rope sling standards generally condemn a sling once it reaches a threshold number of broken wires within one lay length, since this indicates significant loss of the rope's original strength and a real risk of sudden failure under load. Option A describes cosmetic weathering that does not affect the rope's structural integrity. Option C is simply residual grease and has no bearing on strength. Option D describes light surface rust that can often be cleaned and inspected further rather than an automatic condemning condition on its own.
  106. The correct answer is B (The difference between the dew point and bubble point temperatures at a given pressure): Because a zeotropic blend is made of components with different boiling points, it does not boil or condense at one single fixed temperature the way a pure refrigerant does, instead spanning a range between the bubble point and dew point at a given pressure, which is the glide. Refrigerant does not change colour as pressure changes, and glide is unrelated to flow rate through a metering device or to a coil's pressure drop. Technicians must account for glide when reading pressure-temperature charts for blended refrigerants.
  107. The correct answer is A (Polyol ester oil, commonly called POE oil, which is miscible with HFC refrigerants): POE oil was developed specifically because mineral oil does not mix well, or is not miscible, with HFC refrigerants such as R-410A, R-134a, and R-404A. Mineral oil remains the traditional lubricant for older HCFC refrigerants like R-22, while alkylbenzene oil is sometimes used as a partial solution or additive rather than the primary lubricant for HFCs. Using the wrong oil type can prevent proper oil return to the compressor, risking bearing damage from oil starvation.
  108. The correct answer is C (Excessively high discharge pressure that could damage the compressor or piping): a high pressure switch monitors discharge line pressure and opens its contacts if that pressure climbs past a preset safety limit, breaking the control circuit and stopping the compressor. This protects against conditions like a dirty or blocked condenser, condenser fan failure, or refrigerant overcharge, all of which can drive discharge pressure dangerously high. Low suction pressure is instead monitored by a low pressure switch, and oil pressure is monitored by a separate oil safety control on applicable systems.
  109. The correct answer is A (Detection and ventilation strategies addressing potential flammable concentration in the mechanical room.): Because R-32 is an A2L mildly flammable refrigerant, commissioning must specifically address detection and ventilation strategies to manage potential flammable concentration in the mechanical room, a consideration that does not apply the same way to nonflammable refrigerants. Standard leak checking for charge accuracy and recording nameplate charge are general practices for any refrigerant, not driven specifically by flammability classification. Compressor oil type confirmation is likewise a general commissioning step unrelated to the A2L classification.
  110. The correct answer is B (The refrigerant's pressure rises sharply while enthalpy increases only moderately, consistent with an efficient compression): An ideal compression process is modeled as isentropic, meaning it follows a constant entropy line on the P-h diagram, which appears as a steep, nearly vertical path where pressure climbs quickly while enthalpy increases by a comparatively smaller amount. This shape reflects mechanical work being converted mostly into a pressure rise rather than the refrigerant absorbing or rejecting heat during the process itself. A real compressor deviates somewhat from this ideal line due to friction and other inefficiencies, but the isentropic line remains the standard reference for evaluating compressor performance.
  111. The correct answer is A (The expected saturated condensing pressure for the current outdoor ambient temperature and refrigerant): a normally functioning condenser should produce a discharge pressure that corresponds, through the pressure-temperature chart, to a condensing temperature roughly 10°C to 20°C above the outdoor ambient temperature depending on the design. Comparing the actual reading against this expected range quickly flags a dirty condenser coil, low airflow, or overcharge. Compressor voltage rating, charge weight already recorded, and suction line insulation thickness are all separate commissioning checks unrelated to interpreting this specific gauge reading.
  112. The correct answer is B (The cylinder may not be refilled or reused for recovery because it is a non-refillable type.): A DOT-39 cylinder is a non-refillable type intended for a single factory fill of virgin refrigerant, and refilling it for recovery is both illegal and unsafe regardless of its external condition or rated pressure. Recovery requires an approved refillable cylinder, such as a DOT or Transport Canada 4BA or 4BW type, that carries a current hydrostatic requalification. Good external condition or adequate pressure rating does not change the fact that this cylinder type cannot legally be refilled.
  113. The correct answer is D (The line along which liquid and vapour phases coexist together in equilibrium): The saturation curve plots every temperature-pressure pair at which liquid and vapour refrigerant exist together in equilibrium, such as inside the evaporator during boiling or the condenser during condensing. It has nothing to do with a solid-to-liquid boundary, which refrigerants rarely encounter in normal service. It is also not a compressor rating or a generic manufacturer recommendation, it is a physical property of the refrigerant itself.
  114. The correct answer is C (3.0 tons, since 9.1 kW divided by 3.517 kW gives about 2.59 tons, which rounds up to the next standard nominal size): dividing 9.1 kW by 3.517 kW per ton gives approximately 2.59 tons, and standard catalogue sizing always rounds up to the next available nominal size to fully cover the calculated load, which here is 3.0 tons. Selecting 2.0, 1.5, or 1.0 tons would all leave the unit undersized, since each of those nominal sizes is below the calculated 2.59 ton requirement and the unit would be unable to maintain space temperature on design days. Jumping unnecessarily far beyond the closest standard size above the calculated load would add needless oversizing and short cycling risk, but 3.0 tons is the correct closest standard size that still fully covers the load.
  115. The correct answer is C (Radiation, since a dark matte surface emits more infrared energy than bare metal): Radiant heat transfer depends heavily on surface emissivity, and a dark matte finish has a much higher emissivity than a bright, reflective bare metal surface. This means the painted duct radiates infrared energy toward cooler surroundings far more effectively than the shiny duct does. Conduction and convection are largely unaffected by surface colour, since they depend on material contact and fluid movement rather than surface finish.
  116. The correct answer is D (A travel restraint system sized to prevent reaching the roof edge at all): when a worker's lanyard length is set so they physically cannot get close enough to fall off the edge, travel restraint eliminates the fall hazard entirely and is preferred over fall arrest whenever it is feasible. Option A is wrong because proximity to an unprotected edge always requires some form of protection regardless of nearby equipment. Option B overstates the case, since guardrails or restraint can both be valid alternatives to arrest systems, and option C describes a rarely used method that is not the standard first choice for roof edge work.
  117. The correct answer is C (A zeotropic blend exhibits glide, so it has different bubble and dew point temperatures at one pressure): Because a zeotropic blend is made of components with different boiling points, it does not condense or evaporate at one single fixed temperature for a given pressure, instead showing a range called glide between its bubble point and its dew point. A P-T chart for this kind of blend must list both temperatures, or the technician must know which one applies to the measurement being taken. Treating the blend like a pure refrigerant with only one saturation temperature can lead to an incorrect superheat or subcooling calculation.
  118. The correct answer is B (Residual refrigerant oil or vapor on the fitting surface triggered a false alarm): Residual refrigerant vapor or oily film left behind from disconnecting and reconnecting a fitting can trigger a sensitive electronic detector even without an active leak, which is why confirming with a soap bubble test at the same location is standard practice before condemning a fitting. The soap test is a valid and commonly used confirmation method, not something to dismiss outright. A confirmed leak would typically also show bubbles forming under sustained system pressure. Electronic detectors are specifically designed to sense refrigerant vapor at fittings, so an alarm there is expected behavior, not a detection limitation.
  119. The correct answer is A (R-32 and R-454B, since both carry the A2L classification): The exhibit shows R-32 and R-454B classified as A2L, the mildly flammable category requiring ignition-source control and A2L-rated recovery equipment, while the other three refrigerants are classified A1 and nonflammable. Option B incorrectly bases the answer on GWP value rather than the safety classification. Option C incorrectly groups two A1 refrigerants together. Option D incorrectly pairs one A1 and one A2L refrigerant rather than the two true A2L entries.
  120. The correct answer is B (Both the contactor and the run indicator lamp immediately de-energize together): the high pressure switch is wired in series directly ahead of the contactor coil, so opening it breaks the entire control circuit path to C. With the coil de-energized, the auxiliary contact feeding the run indicator lamp also opens, since that contact only closes when the contactor is energized. This series safety design ensures a lockout condition cannot be missed by the operator.
  121. The correct answer is B (Soap bubble testing may not reveal very small or slow pinhole leaks): A very small or slow leak may not release enough refrigerant volume to form a visible bubble within a reasonable inspection time, which is why soap bubble solution is considered less sensitive than an electronic detector for tiny leaks. Option A is false since soap solution is formulated to be inert and safe on fittings without causing damage. Option C is incorrect because the system must actually be pressurized for a bubble test to work at all. Option D is also false since soap testing works on threaded, flared, and brazed joints alike.
  122. The correct answer is D (A properly sized trap is still needed, but a double riser is unnecessary here): Even when a single riser maintains adequate velocity across the full range of unloading, gravity still drains any liquid and oil sitting in the vertical run back toward the evaporator whenever the compressor stops. A trap at the base of the riser is a standard requirement for controlling that off-cycle drainage regardless of whether velocity concerns force a double riser design.
  123. The correct answer is A (The system is undercharged, and refrigerant should be added until superheat falls into range): superheat here is 20°C minus 2°C, which equals 18°C, well above the 8°C to 12°C target range for this system. High superheat on a fixed-orifice system generally points to insufficient refrigerant reaching the evaporator, so the correct response is to add refrigerant in small increments while rechecking superheat until it settles into the target band. This reading is clearly out of range rather than acceptable, and a crankcase heater fault would not produce this specific pressure and temperature relationship.
  124. The correct answer is C (Recovery, since the refrigerant was only removed with no further processing): Recovery describes the simple removal of refrigerant from a system into a cylinder, without any contaminant reduction or purity testing, which matches exactly what occurred here. Option A is wrong because reclamation requires off-site lab-verified reprocessing that did not happen. Option B is wrong because the cylinder performs no filtration function on its own. Option D is wrong because transferring refrigerant into an approved cylinder is the opposite of venting.
  125. The correct answer is D (The outdoor air temperature sensor or its wiring to the controller may be faulty): If the economizer sequence is supposed to trigger free cooling below a programmed outdoor air temperature and the damper never responds even though real outdoor conditions justify it, the most likely cause is a faulty sensor or a wiring problem preventing the controller from seeing the correct value. This is not normal or expected behaviour and should never be assumed to be a compressor sizing issue. The technician must investigate and correct the sensor or wiring fault before the sequence test can be considered passed.

Practice Test 5 Answer Key

  1. The correct answer is B (The reversing valve body itself is mechanically stuck in position.): A solenoid coil that tests with correct voltage and resistance confirms the electrical signal to shift the valve is present and functioning, so if the valve still fails to actually switch modes the fault must be mechanical, meaning the valve body's internal slide is physically stuck in its current position, often from debris or a worn seal. An open compressor winding would prevent the compressor from running at all, which contradicts the confirmed running compressor. A blower capacitor or outdoor sensor issue would not explain the refrigerant circuit staying in the wrong mode.
  2. The correct answer is A (Replace or clean filters every 30 days given the heavy dust loading.): Heavy dust loading accelerates filter loading far faster than a typical light duty space, so a monthly interval prevents restricted airflow across the evaporator. Restricted airflow from a dirty filter is a primary cause of low suction pressure and coil icing. Waiting for visible frost or a fixed annual schedule ignores actual site conditions and risks compressor damage from liquid flooding.
  3. The correct answer is A (The valve is stuck closed and cannot open to feed the evaporator): Low suction pressure and high superheat already point toward a starved evaporator, and warming the sensing bulb is the standard field test for a thermostatic expansion valve, since heat should cause the valve to open further and raise suction pressure. No response at all to a warmed bulb means the valve mechanism itself is not moving, consistent with the valve being stuck closed rather than simply set too conservatively. A disconnected external equalizer would usually cause erratic superheat rather than a completely unresponsive valve. The expansion valve should be replaced since it cannot be repaired in the field.
  4. The correct answer is C (COP exceeds one because refrigeration systems move existing heat rather than create new energy.): A simple efficiency percentage compares energy output to energy input for a device that converts one form of energy into another, and such a device can never output more energy than it consumes. A refrigeration or heat pump system, however, does not create heat energy, it simply moves existing heat from one location to another using a relatively small amount of compressor work as the driving force. Because the heat moved can be several times larger than the compressor work required to move it, COP values well above one are both normal and expected for a properly operating system. This is a fundamentally different concept from combustion or electrical conversion efficiency, which is why COP uses its own distinct terminology rather than a capped percentage scale.
  5. The correct answer is D (The voltage rating, since start capacitors are typically rated lower than run capacitors): Run capacitors are typically rated around 370V or 440V AC for continuous duty, while start capacitors for the same voltage class are commonly rated lower, often 125V to 330V AC, in a different design intended only for brief energization during startup, and comparing the printed voltage rating alongside the shape and microfarad value confirms which type is in hand. A manufacturer's logo appears on both capacitor types and does not distinguish their function. Capacitors have no relationship to refrigerant type, and internal lead wire gauge is not a specification used to identify capacitor type in the field.
  6. The correct answer is B (A loaded filter gradually restricts airflow, reducing evaporator capacity before it looks dirty): Airflow resistance rises well before a filter looks obviously dirty to the eye, so evaporator airflow and capacity decline gradually and invisibly on a walk-by inspection. Scheduled replacement prevents this creeping restriction from causing low suction pressure and coil icing. Filters have no effect on compressor winding insulation or refrigerant charge. Waiting for a visibly dirty filter allows performance loss to go undetected for weeks.
  7. The correct answer is A (Flag the vibration reading for investigation, since it slightly exceeds the manufacturer's stated limit): The exhibit shows a vibration velocity of 4.8 mm/s against a manufacturer's maximum of 4.5 mm/s, which exceeds the acceptable limit even though the excess is small. The sound level of 59 dBA is below the 62 dBA limit and does not need to be flagged. The report cannot be signed off as fully within specification until the vibration issue is investigated and corrected.
  8. The correct answer is C (Confirming the repaired joint and surrounding system hold pressure with no measurable drop): A standing nitrogen pressure test holds the system at a set pressure over time and checks the gauge for any drop, confirming the brazed repair and the rest of the system are leak tight before committing to evacuation and recharge. Nitrogen itself is odorless and is not used as a scent tracer without an added refrigerant trace gas, which is a different specific method. The test has nothing to do with warming refrigerant oil. A pressure test does not replace the separate deep vacuum evacuation step needed to remove moisture and air.
  9. The correct answer is C (ream the inside edge with a deburring tool, then wipe out any loose copper shavings): a tubing cutter leaves a burr that constricts the bore and disturbs refrigerant flow if it is not removed. Reaming restores the full inside diameter, and wiping the cut end afterward prevents loose copper shavings from being carried into the system where they can score compressor bearings or plug a metering device. The burr will not melt away during brazing and can instead become a permanent restriction. A hacksaw cut is not the correction for a burr and typically leaves a rougher edge than a proper tubing cutter.
  10. The correct answer is A (About 0.6 amperes, matching the expected current for this coil resistance): About 0.6 amperes, matching the expected current for this coil resistance. Ohm's Law states current equals voltage divided by resistance, so 24 volts divided by 40 ohms equals 0.6 amperes. This value should match the coil's nameplate rating within a small tolerance for accurate diagnosis. A significantly different reading would point to a shorted or open coil winding.
  11. The correct answer is C (Low pressure coil cleaner and rinse applied against the direction of normal airflow): Low pressure coil cleaner combined with a reverse-direction rinse pushes debris out the way it entered without flattening the delicate aluminum fins. A wire brush and a close-range pressure washer both crush and bend fins, which itself restricts airflow permanently. Open flame near a coil is unsafe and does nothing useful for cleaning. Gentle chemical cleaning preserves fin spacing while still removing embedded dust and lint.
  12. The correct answer is D (The compressor winding is grounded and shorted internally to the metal casing): Continuity between any winding terminal and the compressor shell means the winding insulation has broken down and is making electrical contact with the grounded case. This grounded winding condition draws excess current and trips the internal overload almost immediately on every start attempt, exactly as described. Normal winding resistance readings between terminals with no continuity to the shell would indicate a healthy compressor instead. A grounded compressor must be replaced, since this insulation failure cannot be repaired in the field.
  13. The correct answer is A (The evaporator is starved because too little refrigerant is reaching the coil): Low suction pressure paired with superheat above the target range is the defining signature of a starved evaporator, since not enough liquid refrigerant is reaching the coil to fully absorb the available heat load. Flooding would instead produce the opposite pattern of high suction pressure with superheat near zero. Confirmed normal airflow and a clean filter rule out an airflow restriction as the underlying cause of the low pressure reading. The technician should check for undercharge or a restricted metering device as the likely source of the starvation.
  14. The correct answer is C (The outer insulation surface temperature falls below the dew point, causing condensation): Insulation thickness on a cold suction line must be selected so the outer surface temperature stays above the dew point of the surrounding air, based on the ambient temperature and relative humidity of that specific space. Undersized insulation in a hot, humid mechanical room lets the outer surface run cold enough that moisture condenses on it continuously, leading to dripping, wet insulation, and eventual corrosion.
  15. The correct answer is B (The internal protector will not reset until the windings cool sufficiently): The internal protector will not reset until the windings cool sufficiently. Because the protector is embedded within the motor windings, it responds directly to actual winding temperature rather than external casing temperature, and it needs enough cooling time before its bimetallic contacts close again. Cycling the disconnect switch does not speed up this cooling process and will not force an immediate reset. The technician should investigate the root cause of the overheating, such as a running overload or restricted airflow, while the compressor cools.
  16. The correct answer is A (Compressor 2, rated closest to but slightly above the calculated load): Compressor 2 at 4.6 kW is the smallest listed capacity that still exceeds the calculated 4.2 kW load, providing coverage without significant oversizing. Compressor 1 at 2.8 kW is undersized and would fail to maintain freezer temperature. Compressor 3 at 9.1 kW is more than double the load and would cause excessive short cycling, and Compressor 4, despite a similar capacity number, is rated for a different refrigerant than the system specifies and cannot be selected.
  17. The correct answer is D (The undercharged system lacks sufficient liquid refrigerant to maintain adequate subcooling and stable control): omitting the receiver operating volume from the design stage charge estimate directly understates the total refrigerant quantity ordered, and an undercharged system commonly shows insufficient subcooling at the condenser outlet along with unstable expansion valve control, since the expansion device needs a consistent supply of subcooled liquid to modulate properly. Since piping and equipment sizing were confirmed correct, an equipment selection error such as compressor or coil oversizing is not the explanation for these particular symptoms. A reversed expansion valve installation would typically produce a very different and more severe failure pattern rather than a subtle subcooling and control instability issue. The commissioning symptoms point directly back to the omitted receiver volume in the original charge calculation.
  18. The correct answer is B (The combined resistance of everything downstream and upstream of the blower, including the coil cabinet interior): Total external static pressure is measured across everything the blower must push or pull air through outside its own housing, which is why an added evaporator coil's own internal resistance is included in the reading taken across the whole assembly. It has nothing to do with refrigerant pressure drop across a metering device or the voltage supplied to the blower motor. Understanding this distinction is essential for correctly interpreting a static pressure reading during commissioning.
  19. The correct answer is A (Refrigerant remained dissolved in the cold oil and slugged the compressor on start.): A two-hour warm-up is far short of the roughly twenty-four-hour period typically needed to fully drive migrated refrigerant out of a cold compressor's oil charge. When the compressor started with refrigerant still dissolved in the oil, that refrigerant flashed to vapor rapidly inside the cylinder, carrying liquid oil and refrigerant into the compression chamber and bending the connecting rods on the very first strokes. A deep evacuation, high superheat setting, and overfilled oil level do not produce this specific slugging failure pattern.
  20. The correct answer is A (Manufacturer factory charge plus a line-set charge allowance based on pipe length and diameter): manufacturers publish a base factory charge for the condensing unit along with a per-metre charge allowance for field-installed line sets, allowing a reliable early estimate before piping is physically installed and weighed. Compressor nameplate amperage relates to electrical draw, not refrigerant volume. Deriving charge from cooling load alone ignores actual piping geometry, and reusing another unrelated project's charge figure ignores this system's specific line lengths and equipment.
  21. The correct answer is B (Enthalpy remains essentially constant while pressure and temperature both drop sharply): A metering device throttles refrigerant without adding or removing heat and without doing external work, so enthalpy stays essentially unchanged across the valve, while the sudden pressure drop causes the temperature to fall sharply as some liquid flashes to vapour. The other options describe enthalpy or temperature behaving in ways that contradict this throttling process. This constant-enthalpy expansion is why the process plots as a vertical line on a pressure-enthalpy diagram.
  22. The correct answer is C (The system likely has a restricted or undersized metering device limiting refrigerant flow into the evaporator.): Normal subcooling indicates the condenser side of the system has an adequate refrigerant charge and is functioning correctly up to the metering device, ruling out a simple undercharge or overcharge as the primary explanation. High superheat combined with normal subcooling points toward a restriction after the point where subcooling is measured, such as a partially blocked or undersized metering device, which starves the evaporator of refrigerant flow relative to the heat load it needs to handle. This mismatch causes the limited refrigerant entering the coil to fully evaporate very early, leaving the remaining coil length to only add excess superheat to the vapour. A completely plugged filter drier or a fully failed metering device would typically produce a much more severe symptom, such as a starved, frosted, or completely non-functional evaporator, rather than a moderately elevated superheat reading with otherwise normal subcooling.
  23. The correct answer is D (An evaporative condenser, since it typically achieves lower condensing temperatures with reasonable water use): An evaporative condenser, since it typically achieves lower condensing temperatures with reasonable water use. Evaporative condensers are widely used on large ammonia industrial systems specifically because they achieve condensing temperatures close to the outdoor wet-bulb temperature, which is typically lower than an air-cooled unit can achieve and improves compressor efficiency over the equipment's operating life, while using far less water than a once-through potable water arrangement. A once-through water-cooled system wastes large volumes of water and is rarely permitted for continuous discharge to a sewer system. Ammonia system efficiency, like any refrigerant, is directly affected by condensing temperature, so condenser choice is not a neutral decision here.
  24. The correct answer is B (The accumulator temporarily holds liquid refrigerant, releasing only vapour toward the compressor.): a suction line accumulator is designed to catch liquid refrigerant that may leave the evaporator unevaporated during a rapid load change, holding that liquid temporarily inside its shell while metering only vapour, and a controlled amount of oil bearing liquid through a bleed, back toward the compressor suction inlet. This prevents liquid slugging, which can damage compressor valves or cause hydraulic lock. It has no heater element to actively boil off liquid, does not raise suction pressure, and performs a different function from a filter drier's particulate and moisture filtration role.
  25. The correct answer is B (at least equal to the outside diameter of the tube being joined): a swaged joint relies on enough straight overlap between the two tube ends to give the braze alloy a proper capillary gap over a sufficient length, which is why the swage depth is set to at least the tube's own outside diameter. A shallow swage that barely allows the mating tube to enter does not give enough engagement length for a strong brazed joint and increases the risk of the joint separating under stress. Swaging deeper than necessary wastes tube length and can distort the tube wall unnecessarily. A flare depth reference does not apply to a swaged and brazed joint.
  26. The correct answer is A (Bubbles continuously form and grow at the leaking point.): Escaping gas under pressure pushes through the soap solution film and forms visible, growing bubbles exactly at the point where it is leaking out of the joint, giving a precise physical location for the repair. A solution that simply dries with no reaction indicates no escaping gas at that spot. A color change is not a property of a standard soap bubble leak test, and pressure would fall rather than rise as gas escapes from a genuine leak.
  27. The correct answer is A (ACR tubing, cleaned and dehydrated with sealed ends for refrigerant work): ACR stands for air conditioning and refrigeration, and the manufacturing process leaves the bore free of oil and oxide film. The sealed, nitrogen-charged ends keep moisture and air out until the installer is ready to cut and braze the line. Type K and Type L are plumbing grades not processed or certified for refrigerant service.
  28. The correct answer is D (Report the hazard immediately to the supervisor or coworker involved): Every worker has a responsibility to report observed hazards promptly regardless of whether the equipment or task belongs to them, since an immediate report allows the hazard to be corrected before someone is injured. Option A ignores a genuine safety responsibility that applies to all workers on a site, not just the person using the equipment. Option B removes the hazard without communicating it, which could create confusion when the ladder later goes missing. Option C delays reporting a hazard that could cause an injury at any point before the end of the shift.
  29. The correct answer is C (A burr can break loose and travel through the system, clogging valves and driers.): a burr left on the inside of a cut tube can eventually break free from vibration or refrigerant flow and travel downstream, where it can lodge in a metering device, valve seat, or filter drier and cause a restriction or malfunction. The burr also disrupts smooth flow and can be a nucleation point for turbulence. Reaming removes this sharp edge before the joint is assembled and brazed. The outside diameter and nitrogen purge flow are not meaningfully affected by an internal burr.
  30. The correct answer is B (A2L refrigerants carry a flammability hazard, so CSA B52 requires additional detection and ventilation measures): the A2L classification indicates lower toxicity but mild flammability, and CSA B52 sets machine room requirements based partly on the refrigerant's safety group classification, not only on charge quantity. A room containing an A2L refrigerant needs detection and ventilation measures suited to a flammability hazard that a non-flammable A1 room does not need, since a leak could reach a flammable concentration near an ignition source. Treating the two classifications as equivalent overlooks this real hazard difference and could leave the room under protected. The contractor's assumption ignores how CSA B52 actually differentiates requirements by refrigerant classification.
  31. The correct answer is B (Liquid refrigerant is slugging into the compressor cylinders from an oversized load mismatch): Liquid refrigerant reaching the compressor unvaporized produces a distinctive metallic knocking sound as the incompressible liquid strikes the pistons or scroll mechanism during compression. Near-zero superheat and frost extending back to the shell confirm liquid is present at the compressor rather than fully vaporized refrigerant, and an unusually light evaporator load explains why not enough heat is available to boil off the liquid before it returns. A worn condenser fan bearing or arcing contactor points would produce a very different type of noise unrelated to refrigerant state. Correcting the load mismatch or metering device issue is needed to stop the slugging before it damages the compressor valves.
  32. The correct answer is A (At reduced load, velocity in the single riser may drop below the level oil return needs): a single riser sized for adequate velocity at full load carries less total refrigerant flow once the compressor unloads to part capacity, and since the pipe diameter cannot change, reduced flow at the same diameter produces lower velocity. If that reduced velocity falls below the minimum needed for oil return, oil can begin to accumulate at the base of the riser or in the evaporator during the frequent part load operation this system expects. Rise height directly affects the static pressure the riser must overcome and is a real factor in overall system design, not something to ignore. A double riser design specifically exists to address this exact weakness of a single riser on a system with a highly variable load profile, and a trap is still typically required at the base of a single riser to prevent liquid or oil pooling.
  33. The correct answer is C (Leave the charge as is, since 13C superheat already falls comfortably within the target range and no charge change is needed): superheat here is 21C minus 8C, which equals 13C, comfortably inside the stated 10C to 14C target range for this unit combination. Because the reading already falls within range, the correct action is to leave the charge alone rather than add or remove refrigerant. Describing the result as 29C misreads the calculation by adding instead of subtracting, and a correctly targeted superheat reading gives no reason to suspect the orifice itself is faulty.
  34. The correct answer is B (All three temperatures, dry bulb, wet bulb, and dew point, are equal to one another): At 100 percent relative humidity the air is fully saturated, meaning it already holds all the moisture it can at that temperature, so there is no evaporative cooling effect left to separate wet bulb from dry bulb, and the dew point equals both as well. Dew point cannot exceed dry bulb temperature under normal atmospheric conditions. Wet bulb is only lower than dry bulb when evaporation is actively occurring, which stops happening once the air is fully saturated.
  35. The correct answer is A (The leak rate exceeds the threshold, so a repair and verification retest are required.): The exhibit's calculated rate is well above an acceptable level for this equipment, so the technician must repair the leak and conduct a verification retest confirming it has been resolved. Canadian halocarbon regulation does not actually use an annualized percentage leak-rate formula; the real Canadian standard, set out for federally regulated systems in the Federal Halocarbon Regulations and mirrored in provincial halocarbon regulations, requires that any detected leak be repaired as soon as possible in the circumstances and in any case within seven days of detection, followed by a retest confirming the repair worked, regardless of what percentage the leak represents. The compressor serial number and an arbitrary one thousand kilogram charge size remain irrelevant to the repair-and-retest obligation.
  36. The correct answer is A (A2L, mildly flammable, requiring flammable-rated equipment and ignition source control.): R-454B shares the A2L classification with R-32, meaning it is mildly flammable with a low burning velocity, which requires technicians to use flammable-rated recovery and leak detection equipment and to control ignition sources during service. It is not classified A1 nonflammable, so treating it with standard A1 precautions is unsafe. It is also far less hazardous than the A3 highly flammable class used for hydrocarbon refrigerants, which requires stricter explosion-proof handling.
  37. The correct answer is A (About 23,000 BTU/h, found by multiplying the EER rating by the wattage): EER is defined as cooling output in BTU/h divided by electrical input in watts, so multiplying the EER of 11.5 by the 2000 watt input gives approximately 23,000 BTU/h of cooling capacity. This is the reverse of the EER formula and is a useful way to estimate output when only wattage and EER are known. SEER differs from EER because it averages performance across a full cooling season rather than at one fixed test condition.
  38. The correct answer is D (Switching higher current line voltage loads such as compressors and fan motors): a contactor is built with heavier contacts rated to switch the higher current line voltage supplying motors like compressors and larger fan motors. A control relay is generally built for lighter duty switching, often at the low voltage side of the circuit. Pressure sensing belongs to pressure switches, and timed switching belongs to time delay relays, both separate control components. Understanding this distinction helps a technician select the correct replacement part rather than substituting a lighter-duty relay for a contactor.
  39. The correct answer is B (Water requires roughly four times as much heat as air per degree of temperature change): Dividing water's specific heat of 4.19 by air's specific heat of 1.005 gives a ratio of roughly four, meaning it takes about four times as much heat energy to raise one kilogram of water by one degree Celsius as it does to raise one kilogram of air by the same amount. The comparison is reversed in the first distractor, and the other two options wrongly claim the two substances behave identically or that specific heat has no bearing on the calculation at all. This large difference is why water-based systems can carry much more heat per kilogram than air-based systems.
  40. The correct answer is D (Reduced airflow from the dirty filter and blower is starving heat absorption): With refrigerant charge confirmed correct by superheat and subcooling, the cause of the icing must be mechanical rather than a charge problem. A loaded filter and a debris-coated blower wheel both reduce the volume of warm return air passing across the evaporator coil, so the coil surface drops below freezing and ice forms even though refrigerant flow is normal. Overcharge and non-condensable gases would instead raise discharge pressure and are unrelated to this specific airflow restriction. Cleaning the filter and blower wheel restores normal airflow and clears the icing.
  41. The correct answer is D (Repair the identified leak before recharging, and log the leak and repair): Proper servicing practice requires repairing an identified leak and documenting it in the service record before adding refrigerant, since recharging a leaking system without repair simply allows the new charge to escape and violates recordkeeping expectations. Option A reverses the correct order and wastes refrigerant. Option B delays a known repair and risks continued emissions. Option C is an unnecessary and disproportionate response to a repairable fitting leak.
  42. The correct answer is A (Air velocity varies across the duct cross section, so a grid gives a more accurate average): Air velocity is naturally higher near the centre of a duct and lower near the walls due to friction, so a single point reading does not represent the true average velocity across the whole cross section. A pitot tube traverse takes multiple readings across a grid of equal areas and averages them to produce an accurate total airflow figure. This method applies to both round and rectangular ducts, not only one shape.
  43. The correct answer is C (Approximately 6.8 kg, matching the factory charge listed on the nameplate.): The nameplate factory charge value represents the refrigerant quantity originally installed in the system, so an intact unit with no prior leaks should yield close to that amount during recovery. The rated high side pressure figure describes operating pressure, not refrigerant mass, so it cannot be used to estimate charge weight. The compressor's tonnage rating is a capacity figure, not a direct measure of the actual refrigerant charge.
  44. The correct answer is A (Label the cylinder clearly identifying it as mixed or unknown refrigerant contents.): A cylinder holding mixed or contaminated refrigerant from multiple unrelated jobs must be clearly labeled as mixed or unknown contents so the reclaimer can handle and test it appropriately. Leaving it unlabeled shifts an identification burden that belongs to the technician. Labeling it as a single refrigerant type, or only with customer information, would misrepresent the actual mixed contents of the cylinder.
  45. The correct answer is A (Subcooling is 8°C, within the acceptable range, so charging can be considered complete.): The gauge reads 1629 kPa, corresponding to a saturated condensing temperature of 45°C on the R-22 pressure-temperature chart. Subtracting the measured liquid line temperature of 37°C gives a subcooling of 8°C, which sits right at the lower edge of the 8 to 12°C acceptable band around the 10°C target for this system. Since the reading still falls within the acceptable range, no further refrigerant needs to be added at this stage. Option B incorrectly treats the low end of the acceptable range as unacceptable.
  46. The correct answer is D (The A-weighting filter setting, which approximates human hearing sensitivity across the frequency spectrum): The A-weighting filter reduces the contribution of very low and very high frequencies to approximate how the human ear perceives loudness, which is why occupied space specifications are typically written in dBA. Linear or unweighted readings treat every frequency equally and do not represent perceived loudness. C-weighting is generally used for peak or impulse noise events like explosions or gunshots, not steady HVAC noise. Z-weighting is a flat unweighted response used mainly for detailed acoustic analysis, not standard occupied space compliance readings.
  47. The correct answer is D (A screw compressor, since its slide valve allows smooth capacity modulation at high continuous tonnage): screw compressors use twin rotating helical rotors and an internal slide valve that can continuously adjust the compression volume, giving smooth capacity control from a fraction of full load up to 100 percent. This makes them well suited to large industrial ammonia plants that run continuously at high tonnage with variable process demand. Scroll compressors are limited to much smaller capacities and are not typically found on large ammonia process systems. Reciprocating compressors modulate in discrete cylinder-unloading steps rather than the continuous adjustment a slide valve provides.
  48. The correct answer is D (The contactor coil de-energizes and the compressor stops running immediately): The contactor coil de-energizes and the compressor stops running immediately. Because the high pressure switch is wired in series with the thermostat contact and the coil, opening any one of these series components breaks the entire control circuit path. Without a complete path back to the C rail, the contactor coil loses power and its contacts open. This series safety wiring is exactly why the switch is placed in that rung rather than in a separate branch.
  49. The correct answer is C (The compressor's maximum operating pressure exceeds the existing piping's pressure rating): The compressor's maximum operating pressure exceeds the existing piping's pressure rating. The nameplate lists a maximum operating pressure of 3200 kPa for the new compressor and refrigerant combination, which is higher than the 2800 kPa rating of the existing piping and receiver being reused in the retrofit, creating a real overpressure risk unless the piping and vessels are upgraded or replaced to match the new working pressure. The oil type shown, POE 32, is a common polyol ester oil compatible with many HFC and HFO refrigerants and is not inherently incompatible with the market. Capacity and suction design pressure values shown are unremarkable and not the concern raised by the pressure rating mismatch described.
  50. The correct answer is C (Location, responsible trade, severity, and a target date for correcting the deficiency): A useful deficiency list gives the trade responsible for follow up repairs everything needed to locate and prioritize the work, including where the issue is, who needs to fix it, how serious it is, and by when it should be resolved. A vague general statement or a personal opinion without specifics does not give a follow up crew anything actionable. Including a retail price for equipment has no relevance to tracking and resolving a deficiency.
  51. The correct answer is B (A continuous slope of at least 1/4 inch per foot of horizontal run): Condensate drain lines rely entirely on gravity to move water away from the drain pan, so a continuous downward slope, commonly a minimum of 1/4 inch of fall per foot of horizontal run, is needed to prevent standing water. Any low spot or reverse slope allows water to pool, which can back up into the pan, overflow, and cause water damage to the ceiling below.
  52. The correct answer is D (The electronic detector needs a small amount of actual refrigerant gas to sense.): an electronic leak detector is designed to sense specific refrigerant gas molecules, so pure nitrogen alone will not trigger it even at a real leak point, which is why a small trace charge of refrigerant is added to the nitrogen already in the system before the final scan. This lets the mechanic pinpoint even a small leak that a pressure drop test alone might not have revealed over the test window. The trace charge is not meant to raise system pressure for the earlier nitrogen test, which was already completed, and it does not verify tank purity.
  53. The correct answer is D (The Ozone-depleting Substances and Halocarbon Alternatives Regulations made under CEPA 1999.): These regulations, made under the Canadian Environmental Protection Act 1999, are the federal instrument that controls the manufacture, import and export of ozone-depleting substances and HFCs across Canada, and CEPA 1999 also authorizes the separate Federal Halocarbon Regulations, which prohibit deliberate venting and require recovery specifically for systems on federal or Indigenous lands and equipment owned by federal departments and Crown corporations, all administered by Environment and Climate Change Canada. For the private-sector service, disposal and decommissioning work most technicians perform day to day, the venting prohibition and recovery duty instead comes from the halocarbon or environmental protection regulation of the province or territory where the work takes place, not from a voluntary trade association code or from provincial technical safety legislation, which governs equipment safety rather than environmental halocarbon handling.
  54. The correct answer is C (Approximately 419 kJ, based on the mass, specific heat, and temperature rise): The sensible heat formula is Q equals mass multiplied by specific heat multiplied by the temperature change, so 5 kg multiplied by 4.19 kJ/(kg·°C) multiplied by 20°C equals 419 kJ. This value represents heat added within a single phase, since the water never boils or freezes during the process. A technician who drops the mass or the full temperature difference from the formula will underestimate the true heating load.
  55. The correct answer is D (The saturated evaporating temperature corresponding to this measured suction pressure): the low side gauge reading is cross-referenced against a pressure-temperature chart for the specific refrigerant to find the saturated evaporating temperature at that exact pressure. That value is then subtracted from the actual suction line temperature to calculate superheat. The saturated condensing temperature comes from the high side gauge instead, and this reading alone reveals nothing about total charge mass or compressor volumetric efficiency without further calculation and additional instruments.
  56. The correct answer is C (Heat is transferred energy while temperature indicates the intensity level of that energy.): Heat is thermal energy in transit between two substances or systems due to a temperature difference, measured in units such as joules or BTUs. Temperature is simply an indication of how intense that thermal energy is at a given point, measured in degrees. A substance can absorb a large quantity of heat during a phase change without any change in temperature, which is why the two concepts must never be treated as the same thing.
  57. The correct answer is C (It protects the transformer winding from overheating due to a low voltage short): It protects the transformer winding from overheating due to a low voltage short. A secondary side fuse responds quickly to excessive current caused by a short in the field wiring or a shorted component, opening before that current can overheat and damage the transformer secondary winding. The primary side fuse or breaker protects the incoming line voltage supply and the transformer primary winding, but a low voltage short may not draw enough primary current to trip it quickly. Having both fuses gives more targeted protection at each voltage level.
  58. The correct answer is D (Refrigerant vapor is migrating to and condensing inside the coldest part, the crankcase.): During an extended off cycle, refrigerant vapor throughout the system naturally migrates toward and condenses in whichever component is coldest, since that location has the lowest local saturation pressure. If the compressor crankcase in a cool basement becomes colder than the coil, refrigerant will preferentially condense and collect there, dissolving into the oil. This is exactly the condition the crankcase heater is meant to reverse before the next startup. The other options describe unrelated or backwards mechanisms.
  59. The correct answer is D (Insufficient heat rejection from the loop is raising condensing pressure inside the unit): In cooling mode a water-source heat pump rejects heat into the loop water at its condenser, so entering water that is warmer than specified cannot absorb heat effectively, raising condensing pressure inside the refrigerant circuit until the high pressure switch trips. This points toward the loop or cooling tower system rather than an internal compressor mechanical fault. Entering water temperature is directly linked to condensing pressure in a water-cooled condenser design. High entering water temperature is a loop heat rejection problem, not a refrigerant charge indicator.
  60. The correct answer is B (A portion of the liquid refrigerant has flashed to vapour before reaching the expansion valve): A portion of the liquid refrigerant has flashed to vapour before reaching the expansion valve. When pressure drop across a restriction such as a partially blocked filter drier lowers the liquid line pressure enough that it approaches the refrigerant's saturation pressure at that temperature, a portion of the liquid flashes to vapour prematurely, and this mixed liquid-vapour flow is visible as bubbles in the sight glass. This reduces the effective liquid supply reaching the expansion valve and can cause reduced capacity and erratic valve operation. The bubbles are a real symptom of a restriction problem rather than an indication of correct charge or a faulty sight glass, and the underlying restriction should be diagnosed and corrected.
  61. The correct answer is B (About 240 ohms, found by dividing the voltage by the current): About 240 ohms, found by dividing the voltage by the current. Ohm's Law states resistance equals voltage divided by current, so 12 volts divided by 0.05 amperes equals 240 ohms for this bleed resistor. Comparing this calculated value to the resistor's expected rating, often printed on the capacitor or found in service literature, helps confirm whether the bleed resistor itself has failed open or is functioning correctly. A significantly different measured resistance would point the technician toward replacing the capacitor assembly rather than continuing to search elsewhere in the circuit.
  62. The correct answer is D (an intermediate trap partway up the run so oil does not accumulate over the full rise): on long vertical suction risers, oil can begin to fall out of suspension over an extended climb even when velocity at the bottom is adequate. Installing a trap at intervals along a tall run breaks the rise into shorter segments and collects oil so it can be re-entrained and carried the rest of the way up rather than draining back to the evaporator during an off cycle. Reducing tube size mid-run would create an unwanted restriction rather than aid oil return, and a vent fitting would release refrigerant and is never appropriate on a closed suction line. A second full-length parallel riser is the double riser technique used for capacity modulation, not for long single runs.
  63. The correct answer is C (Mineral oil is not miscible with HFC refrigerants and will not circulate properly with them): Mineral oil does not dissolve into or travel well with HFC refrigerants, so it tends to separate out and pool in the coils and piping rather than returning reliably to the compressor, which starves the compressor of lubrication over time. Cost is not the reason mineral oil is unsuitable here, and it does not react explosively with A2L refrigerants either. Mixing oils does not change a refrigerant's global warming potential, which is a fixed property of the refrigerant's own chemistry.
  64. The correct answer is A (Local exhaust ventilation or a properly fitted respirator rated for the fumes): Brazing fumes, particularly from alloys containing cadmium, pose a genuine respiratory hazard in poorly ventilated spaces, so local exhaust ventilation or a respirator specifically rated for metal fumes is required to protect the technician beyond basic eye and hand protection. Option B describes protection intended for nuisance dust, not the toxic metal fumes produced by heated brazing alloy. Option C is not a viable or reliable control method for ongoing fume exposure during an extended task. Option D reduces but does not eliminate exposure, and standing back alone does not substitute for proper ventilation or respiratory protection.
  65. The correct answer is C (45 degrees, the standard angle for refrigeration flare fittings): refrigeration and air conditioning flare connections are manufactured to a standard 45 degree flare angle so the cone shaped tube end seats correctly against the matching flare nut fitting. Automotive brake lines commonly use a different double flare or a 37 degree fitting depending on the standard, which is why option A's 30 degree reference does not apply here. Options B and D describe angles that are not used in standard refrigeration flare fittings and would not seal correctly against the mating part.
  66. The correct answer is B (Complete and leave a startup report documenting all readings and any adjustments made.): A finished commissioning visit is not complete until the technician documents every relevant reading, including pressures, superheat or subcooling, oil level, and any adjustments made to the metering device, on a startup report left with the equipment or customer. This report becomes the baseline data referenced on every future service call and can be required for manufacturer warranty coverage. The crankcase heater must remain wired and energized permanently during off cycles, reopening the vacuum pump after charging serves no purpose, and thermostat wiring should not be disconnected as part of closing out the job.
  67. The correct answer is C (Remove the manometer from service and send it for formal recalibration before further use): An instrument that fails a field verification check against a known reference beyond its stated tolerance can no longer be trusted for accurate readings and must be pulled from service and sent for formal recalibration. Continuing to use a suspect instrument risks recording inaccurate data throughout the entire balancing survey. Averaging a known reference reading with a suspect instrument's reading does not correct the suspect instrument's error. Estimating pressures visually abandons instrumentation entirely and cannot meet commissioning documentation standards.
  68. The correct answer is C (Published ASHRAE climatic design data for the nearest weather station location): Published ASHRAE climatic design data for the nearest weather station location. Industry practice uses published ASHRAE climatic design data, which provides statistically derived design temperatures such as the 0.4% or 1% annual exceedance values for a given weather station, giving a realistic peak condition rather than an extreme outlier. Using the all-time record high would grossly oversize equipment for a condition that occurs rarely if ever during normal operation, while an annual average temperature is far too mild to represent peak cooling conditions. An arbitrary temperature has no defensible technical basis and would produce an unpredictable and unjustifiable design load.
  69. The correct answer is C (Cracked, stretched, twisted, or worn links and damaged hooks): a proper pre-use inspection looks for actual physical damage such as cracks, elongated or twisted links, excessive wear at contact points, and any deformation or cracking in the hooks, since any of these defects can cause sudden failure under load. Checking only the rating tag as in option A ignores the actual physical condition of the chain itself. Paint or coating condition as in option B has no bearing on structural integrity, and simply confirming chain length as in option D does not identify any of the damage that would make the sling unsafe to use.
  70. The correct answer is D (Purging uses a very low flow pressure while testing uses system rated pressure): Purging during brazing only needs a gentle, low pressure flow of nitrogen moving through the pipe to displace oxygen, while a standing pressure test afterward requires raising the nitrogen pressure up to the system's rated test pressure to properly check for leaks under real operating conditions. Option A reverses the actual relationship between the two pressures needed for each task. Option B is factually wrong since nitrogen, not compressed air, is the standard gas used for refrigeration pressure testing. Option C is unnecessary since the same regulator can be adjusted to different outlet pressures for each task.
  71. The correct answer is D (R-454B is rated A2L for mild flammability, requiring ignition source precautions R-410A did not need.): Unlike R-410A, which carries a nonflammable A1 safety rating, R-454B is classified A2L under ASHRAE Standard 34 due to its mild flammability, meaning it can ignite under specific concentration and ignition energy conditions. This requires technicians to follow additional precautions during brazing, leak detection, and recovery work, such as ensuring adequate ventilation and avoiding open flame near a system that has not been properly recovered and purged. R-454B was chosen partly because it has a similar pressure-temperature profile to R-410A, allowing much of the existing equipment design to carry over with modest changes. The flammability classification, not the pressure behavior, is the primary safety difference technicians must adapt their procedures around.
  72. The correct answer is C (Modulating condenser fan speed with ambient temperature still reduces total energy use across the season): while the compressor is typically the largest single energy consumer, condenser fan energy and head pressure control still contribute meaningfully to total system energy use, especially in a cold climate with large seasonal ambient swings. A variable frequency drive on the condenser fan motor allows fan speed to reduce automatically as ambient temperature drops, cutting fan energy use during cold weather while still maintaining adequate head pressure for proper expansion valve and system operation. Ignoring fan energy because it is smaller than compressor energy overlooks a real seasonal savings opportunity available specifically because of the large temperature swing described. Fixed speed fans cannot capture this seasonal benefit and typically require additional head pressure control devices instead.
  73. The correct answer is C (New production and import of R-22 for use in Canada is no longer permitted.): R-22 no longer has any new production or import allowed for use in Canada, so existing systems must be serviced using recovered, recycled, or reclaimed R-22 already circulating in the market. The claim that new R-22 remains freely manufactured is false, as is the claim restricting the ban to exports only or to residential systems only. This phase-out applies to R-22 broadly, not to a specific system type.
  74. The correct answer is D (The measured subcooling at the condenser outlet is approximately 8 degrees Celsius): Subcooling equals the saturated condensing temperature at the measured high side pressure minus the actual liquid line temperature, so 43 C minus 35 C equals 8 C of subcooling. This confirms the liquid leaving the condenser has been cooled below its saturation point before reaching the metering device. The other values result from subtracting the wrong pair of numbers or transposing the two readings.
  75. The correct answer is C (Estimated line lengths, internal volumes of major components, and refrigerant density): Estimated line lengths, internal volumes of major components, and refrigerant density. A design-stage charge estimate is built from the internal volumes of the evaporator, condenser, receiver and piping, combined with typical refrigerant density at expected operating conditions and preliminary line length estimates from the drawing layout. Nameplate amperage and voltage describe electrical loading, not refrigerant volume, and floor area or outdoor temperature alone give no information about the physical volume the refrigerant will occupy. This early estimate is later refined once actual field-measured line lengths are known.
  76. The correct answer is A (Polyol ester (POE) oil, formulated for miscibility with HFC refrigerants): POE oil was developed to remain miscible with HFC refrigerants such as R-410A, allowing oil to circulate properly with the refrigerant and return reliably to the compressor. Mineral oil does not mix adequately with HFC refrigerants and is intended instead for older CFC and HCFC systems. Alkylbenzene oil is sometimes used in specific retrofit or blending situations, but POE remains the standard correct choice, and oil miscibility strongly affects lubrication and compressor reliability in HFC systems.
  77. The correct answer is D (That the recovery equipment and fittings are rated for A2L flammable service): Because R-454B is a mildly flammable A2L refrigerant, the technician must confirm that recovery equipment and fittings are specifically rated for A2L service before the job, which is not required for a nonflammable A1 refrigerant like R-134a. Option A focuses on cylinder colour, which does not address flammability safety ratings. Option B invents a pressure requirement unrelated to flammability. Option C describes an administrative step with no basis in refrigerant safety regulation.
  78. The correct answer is B (Recover the remaining refrigerant from the line before any cutting begins): Deliberate venting is prohibited under the halocarbon or environmental protection regulation of the province where this walk-in cooler is located, and that prohibition applies regardless of the quantity involved or whether the release happens indoors or outdoors, so any remaining refrigerant must be recovered before the line is opened or cut. Option A wrongly assumes a small-quantity exemption that does not exist. Option C incorrectly claims outdoor venting is treated differently. Option D describes exactly the deliberate release the regulation is designed to prevent.
  79. The correct answer is C (Screw compressor, using meshing helical rotors with slide valve capacity control): screw compressors are the standard choice for large industrial ammonia plants because their meshing helical rotor design delivers high continuous capacity with efficient part-load turndown through slide valve control, without the vibration and maintenance burden of many reciprocating cylinders. Scroll compressors are limited in capacity and rarely used with ammonia at this scale. Reciprocating compressors would require an impractical number of cylinders to match this output, and rotary vane compressors are limited to small fractional applications.
  80. The correct answer is D (Replace the weak run capacitor with one matching the rated microfarad value): Normal line voltage at the compressor terminals rules out a supply voltage or wiring problem as the cause of the failure to start. A capacitance reading well below the rated microfarad value directly explains the humming and overload trip, since the capacitor can no longer provide adequate starting torque for the motor. Replacing the entire compressor is unnecessary when the capacitance test has already isolated the fault to the capacitor itself. Increasing breaker size or removing the capacitor entirely would not restore proper starting and could damage the motor.

  82. The correct answer is B (Advance the wheel gradually with light turns, tightening only slightly each pass): Making light, gradual passes while only slightly tightening the cutter wheel between rotations keeps the tubing round and minimizes the internal burr that would otherwise restrict refrigerant flow or damage a flare seat. Option A applies too much force in a single pass, which tends to deform the tubing and create a larger burr. Option C relies on brute force rather than controlled cutting and commonly ovals the tube. Option D describes snapping the tube by bending, which crushes the end and makes it unusable for flaring.
  83. The correct answer is A (Comparing total supply CFM against total return CFM plus any planned exhaust or ventilation offset): The space pressure relationship depends on the actual air volume balance, so total measured supply CFM must be compared against total return CFM plus any deliberate outdoor air or exhaust offset to confirm the space is neither over pressurized nor depressurized beyond design intent. Motor amp draw reflects electrical load, not actual delivered airflow volume. Duct insulation colour has no bearing on the airflow balance between supply and return. Duct physical dimensions influence potential capacity but do not confirm actual measured airflow balance.
  84. The correct answer is D (A locked rotor condition where the compressor cannot turn.): A loud hum with no rotation followed by a rapid, extreme amperage spike and a fast overload trip is the classic signature of a locked rotor, where the compressor motor is energized but the mechanical components are seized and cannot turn. This mechanical binding forces the motor to draw many times its rated running current almost instantly. A properly functioning compressor or a correctly sized capacitor would allow normal startup and running current, and refrigerant undercharge alone does not produce this locked, non-rotating symptom.
  85. The correct answer is B (The VFD enable input opens, preventing the drive from running the compressor): because the fault relay contact and the enable input are wired in series on their own rung, opening that normally closed contact removes the enable signal the drive needs to run, regardless of what the separate run command rung is doing. This safety interlock lets the drive shut itself down or block a run request whenever it detects an internal fault, even though the thermostat's cooling call and run relay coil circuit remain completely unaffected. The Y contact circuit is not welded or damaged by this event, it simply becomes unable to start the compressor until the fault clears.
  86. The correct answer is D (Shop air introduces moisture and, mixed with refrigerant oil, creates an explosion hazard.): compressed shop air contains moisture, oxygen, and often trace oil, and pressurizing a system that contains refrigerant oil residue with an oxygen bearing gas creates a genuine risk of combustion or explosion under certain conditions, in addition to introducing unwanted moisture into the system. Dry nitrogen is inert and moisture free, making it the correct choice for pressure testing. Shop air's pressure capability and detectability by leak detectors are not the reason it is prohibited; the safety and moisture concerns are.
  87. The correct answer is A (The contactor coil de-energizes, dropping the contactor and stopping the compressor): because the protector and the coil share the same series path on that rung, opening the protector breaks the only path completing the coil circuit. With no current through the coil, the contactor drops out and removes line voltage from the compressor motor. This is the intended fail-safe behavior, letting an internal overheat condition shut the compressor down through the low voltage control circuit rather than relying only on line-side protection. The fan relay rung operates independently and is not affected by this fault.
  88. The correct answer is C (Verify the refrigerant identity from the printed label and paperwork, not colour alone.): AHRI cylinder colour coding is a widely followed industry convention rather than a strictly enforced legal guarantee, and similar shoulder colours between different products can still occur, so a technician must confirm refrigerant identity from the printed label and accompanying paperwork before charging any cylinder into a system. Assuming interchangeability based on colour similarity risks charging the wrong refrigerant into a system. Discarding usable, correctly identified cylinders is unnecessary once identity is properly verified through documentation.
  89. Option B incorrectly claims an immediate shutdown requirement that does not exist. Option C invents a mandatory conversion timeline with no basis in the regulation.   90. The correct answer is C (The motor's own bearing is worn, since the sound persists independent of the belt and fan): Removing the belt isolates the motor from the fan mechanically, so a whine that continues when the motor runs alone, independent of the fan, points to the motor's own bearing as the source rather than the fan bearing or the belt itself. A loose belt would produce slipping noise tied to belt contact, which has already been eliminated by removing the belt entirely. A fan bearing fault would only appear when the fan itself is spinning under load, not from the motor running alone. Isolating components this way is a standard and effective diagnostic technique, not an inconclusive test.
  91. The correct answer is A (The other branches are likely carrying excess airflow that must be redistributed to the low branches): A matching total airflow at the fan can hide an uneven distribution among branches, since excess flow at some diffusers can offset a shortage at others. The technician must throttle the branches carrying too much air and open the ones reading low until each individual diffuser meets its own design target. Relying on the total alone would leave two spaces underventilated even though the system appears balanced overall.
  92. The correct answer is C (Refrigerant pressures and target superheat or subcooling ranges vary with ambient and return air conditions): a suction or discharge pressure reading only has meaning when compared against the outdoor ambient and return air conditions present at the time, since manufacturer target charts shift with these values. Recording ambient and return air temperature lets a future technician compare like conditions rather than misreading a baseline taken on a cool day against readings taken on a hot afternoon. These temperatures are not a blanket legal requirement in themselves, do not replace refrigerant pressure data, and have no bearing on compressor warranty terms.
  93. The correct answer is C (Removing the cores eliminates a flow restriction, allowing faster, more complete evacuation.): a Schrader valve core creates a significant flow restriction at the small port opening, which slows evacuation considerably, so removing the cores with a core removal tool before pulling vacuum allows air and moisture to be pumped out much faster and more completely through the full port opening. Once evacuation and charging work is complete, the cores are reinstalled to allow normal gauge access. Core removal does not change the target vacuum level itself, and it is a practical technique rather than a universal code requirement.
