This confirms the liquid refrigerant leaving the condenser is fully liquid and appropriately cooled below its saturation point before reaching the metering device. Comparing this calculated 7°C value against the manufacturer's specified subcooling target helps the technician decide whether the refrigerant charge is correct for this system.
  95. The correct answer is A (The SAE standard refrigeration flare angle is 45 degrees included angle.): refrigeration and air conditioning flared fittings in North America follow the SAE 45 degree flare standard, matching the mating flare nut and fitting cone angle so the two surfaces seal metal to metal when tightened. A 37 degree flare is used in aerospace and some hydraulic fittings, not standard refrigeration work, and using mismatched angles produces a joint that will not seal properly regardless of torque applied.
  96. The correct answer is D (The system is undercharged, starving the evaporator of enough liquid refrigerant): When a system does not have enough refrigerant, liquid boils off very early in the evaporator coil, leaving a long section of coil to superheat the vapour and producing an abnormally high superheat reading. The low suction pressure occurs because too little refrigerant mass is flowing through the coil to maintain normal evaporator pressure under the current load. An overcharged system would instead tend to show low superheat and a flooded, cold compressor rather than these symptoms.
  97. The correct answer is B (A 3.5 ton unit rated for 42,000 Btu/h of total capacity): A 3.5 ton unit rated for 42,000 Btu/h of total capacity. Dividing the 42,000 Btu/h load by 12,000 Btu/h per ton gives exactly 3.5 tons, so the nearest standard nominal size matching the load without meaningful oversizing is 3.5 tons. Selecting the 2 ton unit would leave the space significantly undercooled, while the 4.5 or 5 ton units would short-cycle on mild days and waste energy. Matching equipment tonnage closely to the calculated load also improves dehumidification performance because the coil runs longer at part load.
  98. The correct answer is C (An empty or near-empty system gives meaningless superheat and subcooling readings until charged.): Superheat and subcooling calculations only become meaningful once enough refrigerant is in the system to establish stable operating pressures and a real liquid seal in the condenser, so attempting to charge purely by gauge reading from an empty system provides no useful reference at the start. Weigh-in charging uses the manufacturer's calculated total charge and an accurate scale to get the bulk of refrigerant in first, creating conditions where superheat or subcooling readings finally become meaningful for fine-tuning. Evacuation is still required beforehand regardless of charging method chosen.
  99. The correct answer is B (Replace the contactor because damaged contacts cause unreliable electrical connection under load): Pitted, burned contact surfaces increase electrical resistance across the contactor and can cause intermittent or arcing connections, especially under the higher current draw that accompanies hot weather operation. Since the coil measures normal resistance and receives correct voltage on every call, the fault is isolated to the mechanical contact surfaces rather than the coil circuit. Sanding damaged contacts is a temporary measure at best and does not restore reliable long-term operation. Replacing the contactor is the correct repair once contact damage has progressed this far.
  100. The correct answer is C (Liquid phase into the high side, since vacuum pulls it in without a compressor running.): With the compressor off and the system under a deep vacuum, the pressure differential alone can draw liquid refrigerant in through the high side service valve without needing the compressor to move it. Introducing liquid on the high side during this stage also avoids the risk of liquid slugging that would occur if liquid were sent into the low side suction port. Once the bulk charge is in, the compressor is started and fine-tuning charge is added as vapor on the low side.
  101. The correct answer is C (it collects oil at the low point so refrigerant velocity can re-entrain it up the riser): the trap at the base of a vertical suction riser forms a low pocket where oil naturally settles before the gas stream carries it upward. Adequate suction gas velocity in the riser then picks that oil back up and continues moving it toward the compressor, which is essential since the compressor depends on a steady oil return to stay lubricated. It is not primarily an expansion loop, a refrigerant reservoir, or a strainer, even though it happens to sit at a bend in the line.
  102. The correct answer is D (The refrigerant must go to a licensed reclaimer off site before resale): Refrigerant intended for resale to another company must meet the reclamation standard, which requires off-site processing by a licensed reclaimer and laboratory verification against the AHRI purity specification, not just on-site recycling. Option A wrongly treats on-site recycling as equivalent to reclamation. Option B misstates that reclamation can happen without lab analysis. Option C ignores that buyer agreement does not substitute for meeting the legal reclamation standard.
  103. The correct answer is A (Fully recover the refrigerant charge into a labelled cylinder before any dismantling begins.): Before a decommissioned system is cut apart or scrapped, the technician must fully recover the remaining refrigerant into a properly labelled cylinder to prevent an uncontrolled release. Cutting lines open before recovery, or venting any portion of the charge, both violate the deliberate release prohibition. Sending an intact charged unit to a scrap yard without recovery risks the refrigerant being released during mechanical shredding.
  104. The correct answer is B (A silver brazing alloy along with an appropriate flux applied to both surfaces): Phosphorus-bearing alloys are self-fluxing on copper but cannot adequately wet or clean steel or brass surfaces, so a copper-to-steel joint requires a silver brazing alloy paired with a flux suited to the base metals involved. Skipping flux on this type of joint typically results in poor wetting, voids, and a joint that fails a pressure or leak test.
  105. The correct answer is B (The discharge state, since compression raises both pressure and temperature to their peak): The diagram shows the discharge state at 82°C and 2140 kPa, which is the highest temperature and pressure reached anywhere in the cycle because the compressor has just added mechanical work to the suction vapour. The liquid state is cooler than discharge because the condenser has already rejected heat by the time refrigerant reaches that point. The metered state actually drops in both pressure and temperature as the refrigerant passes through the metering device, the opposite of what option D describes.
  106. The correct answer is B (The filter drier core is restricted and dropping pressure across itself): A pressure drop severe enough to create a noticeable temperature difference across a component, with warm liquid entering and cold liquid leaving, is the classic signature of a restriction inside that component. Because the drier core is partially plugged, only a reduced flow of refrigerant reaches the expansion valve, which starves the evaporator and drops suction pressure system-wide. Normal evaporator airflow with no ice rules out a fan or coil airflow problem as the primary cause here. The technician should replace the filter drier to restore normal refrigerant flow and suction pressure.
  107. The correct answer is D (Measure differential pressure across the valve ports and calculate flow from its Cv chart): Balancing valves publish a Cv value that relates measured pressure drop across the valve to actual flow rate, so reading the differential pressure and applying the manufacturer's chart or formula gives the true flow without needing a separate meter. Fully opening the valve gives maximum flow for that circuit's resistance, not necessarily the design flow rate needed. Visual handle position alone does not account for actual system resistance and cannot confirm design flow. Pump discharge pressure reflects the whole system, not the flow through one individual balancing valve.
  108. The correct answer is A (The saturated condensing temperature is approximately 45°C at this high side pressure.): The pressure shown on the gauge in the diagram corresponds to roughly 45°C on a standard R-410A pressure-temperature chart, the point at which R-410A vapour condenses back into liquid at that pressure. A technician cross-references this value on a P-T chart or slide rule rather than assuming any fixed relationship, since every refrigerant has its own unique pressure-temperature curve. This saturated condensing temperature is then used together with the measured liquid line temperature to calculate subcooling. Misreading the gauge or using the wrong refrigerant's chart would produce an incorrect saturation temperature and a false subcooling calculation.
  109. The correct answer is B (Approximately 838 kilojoules of heat must be removed to reach the freezing point.): Sensible heat removed is calculated as mass multiplied by specific heat multiplied by the temperature change, so multiplying 10 kilograms by 4.19 kilojoules per kilogram per degree Celsius by a 20°C temperature drop gives 838 kilojoules. This value only accounts for cooling the liquid water down to its freezing point and does not yet include the additional latent heat of fusion needed to actually freeze the water into ice. Once freezing begins, the temperature stays constant at 0°C while a separate, much larger quantity of latent heat is removed during the phase change itself. Keeping these two heat quantities separate is essential for accurately sizing refrigeration equipment that both chills and freezes a product.
  110. The correct answer is B (It sets a schedule of stepped reductions to national HFC production and consumption baselines over years.): The Kigali Amendment works through a defined schedule of stepped reductions to national HFC production and consumption baselines rather than an immediate blanket ban taking effect the day it is signed. It extends the Montreal Protocol framework to cover HFCs, which were not addressed by the original ozone-focused text. It also addresses production and import directly, not only leak rates.
  111. The correct answer is A (The Kigali Amendment to the Montreal Protocol's HFC phase-down schedule): The Kigali Amendment establishes the scheduled phase-down of HFC production and import that is shrinking allocation quotas over time, and Canada is a party to this binding international commitment. Option B incorrectly describes it as a voluntary pledge without legal force. Option C wrongly attributes the reduction to provincial building codes. Option D is incorrect because the original 1987 Protocol text did not address HFCs at all.
  112. The correct answer is A (Investigate the staging control or pressure switch setpoint before completing the sequence verification): Condenser fans on a staged system are designed to energize progressively as head pressure rises, and both fans starting together instead of staging indicates a control or pressure switch setpoint problem that needs correction. This is not an efficiency improvement and should not be accepted as normal behaviour. The technician must investigate and resolve the staging fault before the sequence of operations test can be signed off as passed.
  113. The correct answer is B (The difference between actual suction line temperature and saturated evaporating temperature.): Superheat is calculated by taking the actual temperature measured on the suction line at a given point and subtracting the saturated evaporating temperature that corresponds to the suction pressure at that same point, read from a pressure-temperature chart. This value tells the technician how much sensible heat has been added to the refrigerant vapor after it fully boiled off in the evaporator. It is unrelated to discharge or liquid line temperatures, and it is not a pressure difference between ports.
  114. The correct answer is C (Without a base trap, oil can pool and fail to return during low load operation): a trap at the base of a vertical suction riser is a standard piping detail that helps prevent liquid refrigerant and oil from draining back down into the evaporator during off cycles and helps maintain the velocity conditions needed for oil to be swept upward once the compressor restarts. An isometric drawing showing a continuous rise with no trap indicated should be flagged, since omitting this detail before fabrication risks building a system that struggles with oil return, particularly at reduced load. This concern applies broadly across direct expansion suction piping, not only to flooded evaporator systems, and it is standard practice to show trap locations explicitly on drawings so fabrication and installation match the intended design. A continuously rising line without a trap does not improve oil return, it removes a feature specifically included to support it.
  115. The correct answer is D (Every one to three months depending on dust load and continuous run hours): Continuously operating commercial units accumulate dust faster than residential systems, so filters need checking on a short interval rather than an annual one. Waiting a full year or checking only twice a year lets a dirty filter restrict evaporator airflow long before anyone notices the loss of capacity. Restricted airflow across the evaporator drives suction pressure down and can eventually cause coil icing. Frequent filter checks protect both efficiency and the compressor from liquid slugging risk.
  116. The correct answer is D (Suction pressure should fall and discharge pressure should rise on the gauge manifold): a scroll compressor running in the correct direction compresses refrigerant normally, so suction pressure drops and discharge pressure climbs within seconds, along with a noticeable drop in running amperage and noise compared to reverse rotation. If pressures instead move toward each other or barely change, the compressor is very likely running backward on a miswired three-phase supply and must be shut down immediately. Continuous locked rotor amperage and automatic crankcase heater shutoff are unrelated checks and do not confirm correct rotation direction.
  117. The correct answer is B (Subcooling is 7°C, below target, so refrigerant should be added to the system.): The gauge reads 1433 kPa, which corresponds to a saturated condensing temperature of 40°C on the R-22 pressure-temperature chart. Subtracting the measured liquid line temperature of 33°C gives a subcooling of 7°C, which falls short of the 10°C target used for this system. Low subcooling means the condenser is not backing up enough liquid refrigerant, so small increments of refrigerant should be added while continuing to monitor the liquid line temperature. Option D correctly calculates the value but reverses the required action.
  118. The correct answer is A (Briefly crack the hose fitting to purge trapped air with refrigerant vapor.): Manifold hoses trap ordinary air whenever they are disconnected, and that air contains moisture and non-condensable gases that would enter the sealed system on connection. Cracking the fitting at the gauge manifold for an instant while refrigerant flows from the cylinder pushes that trapped air out through the small gap before the connection is tightened. Compressed shop air would introduce even more moisture and contamination rather than remove it. Soap bubble testing checks for leaks, not for purging air.
  119. The correct answer is A (The low pressure switch has drifted out of calibration and trips too early): Because gauge readings taken immediately after each trip show suction pressure still within the normal range, the system itself is not actually reaching a genuinely low pressure condition. This points to the switch's own internal setpoint drifting higher than its rated trip point rather than any refrigerant-side problem, since charge, airflow, and the filter have all already been ruled out. A grounded compressor would trip on electrical overload protection, not a pressure switch, and unpredictable condenser fan cycling would show up as changing discharge pressure rather than repeated low pressure faults. The switch should be recalibrated or replaced to stop the nuisance shutdowns.
  120. The correct answer is D (An air-cooled condenser, since it needs no water supply or treatment and suits rooftop weight limits): an air-cooled condenser rejects heat directly to ambient outdoor air using a coil and fans, requiring no water supply, water treatment chemicals, or drain connection. It is also generally lighter than a shell and tube or evaporative condenser of comparable capacity, which helps meet a strict structural roof loading limit. Water-cooled and evaporative condensers both depend on a water source and ongoing chemical treatment to control scale and biological growth, neither of which is available at this site. These constraints make an air-cooled condenser the practical choice here.
  121. The correct answer is D (Withhold the cylinder from service because its retest interval has already passed): The last hydrostatic test was recorded in 2019-02, and adding the five-year retest interval places the due date in 2024-02, which is now in the past relative to the 2026-08 inspection date, so the cylinder must be withheld from service until requalified. Option A wrongly treats a passing valve check as a substitute for a current hydrostatic test. Option B invents an exemption that does not exist for vapor-only use. Option C incorrectly allows one more fill cycle before compliance.
  122. The correct answer is D (Recovery cylinders use a standard grey body with yellow shoulder regardless of refrigerant type.): Recovery cylinders follow a standardized grey body with yellow shoulder convention regardless of which refrigerant they currently hold, unlike new virgin refrigerant cylinders which are colour coded by refrigerant type. The apprentice's belief that recovery cylinders must match their contents' virgin colour is therefore incorrect. This is a recognized convention, not an area with no standardization at all, and it is not tied to the cylinder manufacturer's country of origin.
  123. The correct answer is C (The crankcase heater never energizes, even while the compressor is off): the heater relay depends entirely on that normally closed auxiliary contact closing to complete its circuit path to C during the off cycle. If the auxiliary contact fails open, no current can reach the heater relay at any time, regardless of contactor status. Without heater operation, refrigerant can migrate into the compressor oil during long off cycles, risking a liquid slugging failure on the next start.
  124. The correct answer is B (A failed or weak run capacitor providing insufficient starting torque.): A single phase motor that hums and needs a manual nudge to start, but then runs normally once turning, is the textbook symptom of a weak or failed run capacitor that can no longer provide the phase shift needed for adequate starting torque. Normal running amperage afterward confirms the winding itself is intact. A shorted or open winding would prevent normal running current entirely, and a seized bearing would not allow the motor to spin freely even after a manual nudge.
  125. The correct answer is C (Standard manifold gauges lack the resolution to read accurately in that range): A typical compound gauge is graduated for the working pressure range of a refrigeration system and has no meaningful markings once pressure drops into the deep vacuum range. A micron gauge is built specifically to resolve very low absolute pressures, which is the only way to confirm the system has actually reached a dehydration-level vacuum rather than just a rough visual near zero.

Practice Test 6 Answer Key

  1. The correct answer is B (The next standard size above 11.94 tons, typically a 12.5 ton packaged unit): dividing the 42 kW design load by 3.517 kW per ton gives 11.94 tons of required capacity. Manufacturers build packaged equipment in fixed standard sizes rather than any arbitrary tonnage, so the mechanic must select the next standard size at or above the calculated value. A 10 ton unit is undersized and cannot meet the design load on peak days. Choosing 12.5 tons meets the load with a reasonable margin without the excess capacity and short cycling risk of jumping to 15 tons.
  2. The correct answer is A (The system has been overcharged with refrigerant during the recent service.): A clean coil with normal airflow rules out restricted heat rejection, so an abnormally high head pressure with those variables controlled points to excess refrigerant charge. Overcharge floods the condenser with liquid, reducing effective condensing surface and driving discharge pressure well above normal for the ambient temperature. Undercharge produces low pressures rather than high ones, and a failed discharge valve typically lowers head pressure rather than raising it.
  3. The correct answer is C (To physically show the owner key equipment locations, controls, and confirm outstanding items are understood): An in-person handover walkthrough lets the technician physically point out equipment locations, demonstrate basic controls, and confirm the owner understands any outstanding deficiency items, none of which is as effective through email alone. It is not an opportunity to renegotiate contract pricing or a substitute for providing complete written documentation. The walkthrough and the documentation package work together to give the owner a clear understanding of what was delivered.
  4. The correct answer is D (The 24 kg gap between nameplate charge and recovered weight is unexplained and needs documentation.): The exhibit shows a 65 kg nameplate charge against only 41 kg recovered, a 24 kg difference that the technician's notes leave completely unexplained despite a visible leak being noted at arrival. This gap needs documentation explaining whether it reflects prior leakage, an inaccurate nameplate, or an incomplete recovery before the file can be considered complete. Reclaimer receipts matching the recovered weight do not account for the missing portion of the original charge, and responsibility for explaining it belongs to the recovering technician, not the scrap metal recycler.
  5. The correct answer is C (Unit one frosts from restricted airflow, unit two frosts from insufficient refrigerant charge): Evaporator frost has more than one distinct root cause, and these two units illustrate different ones: unit one's dirty filter restricts airflow across the coil, dropping coil surface temperature below freezing even with correct charge, while unit two's low charge directly lowers suction pressure and coil temperature despite normal airflow. Treating both as the same restricted-airflow problem would leave unit two's undercharge unaddressed. Overcharge and defrost board failure are not supported by the facts given for either unit. Frost formation from both airflow restriction and low charge is a real and common field condition.
  6. The correct answer is C (The opposition a material offers to the flow of electric current): resistance describes how strongly a given material or component opposes current flow, and it is measured in ohms. Ohm's Law directly relates this property to voltage and current through the formula V equals I times R. Understanding resistance is fundamental to interpreting multimeter readings on windings, capacitors, and control components throughout a refrigeration or air conditioning system.
  7. The correct answer is A (It routes line voltage directly to the motor while skipping the failed drive): It routes line voltage directly to the motor while skipping the failed drive. A bypass contactor arrangement allows the technician to switch the motor from VFD-controlled operation to running directly across the line at fixed speed, keeping the equipment operational while a replacement drive is sourced. This is not full-speed control like the VFD provides, but it avoids a complete system shutdown for the customer. Once the new drive is installed and verified, the technician switches control back from bypass to normal VFD operation.
  8. The correct answer is A (The internal thermal protector is tripping from excess heat and resetting): a compressor that runs for several minutes, shuts down with no external control involved, and restarts after a cool-down period matches the automatic reset behavior of an internal thermal protector responding to elevated winding or shell temperature. Normal running amps rule out an electrical short as the trigger, pointing instead toward a heat-related cause like restricted airflow, low refrigerant charge, or high head pressure. A welded contactor would prevent the unit from shutting off at all, and single-phasing would trip the overload almost immediately rather than after several minutes.
  9. The correct answer is B (The low pressure switch itself has drifted out of calibration and trips early): With clean airflow, a clean filter, and correct refrigerant charge already confirmed, both airflow-related icing and a charge problem are ruled out as causes of the short cycling. A switch tripping just above its rated cut-out point, rather than well below it, indicates the switch's internal setpoint has shifted rather than the system actually reaching a genuinely low pressure. A fouled condenser would raise discharge pressure, not cause low pressure trips, and grounded windings would trip on overload rather than a pressure switch. The low pressure switch should be recalibrated or replaced to stop the nuisance cycling.
  10. The correct answer is D (As-built drawings and documentation showing conformance to the approved permit design): CSA B52 inspection requires as-built drawings and supporting documentation on site so the inspector can verify the completed installation matches the design that was originally permitted, including any authorized field changes. A shipping invoice showing purchase price provides no code conformance information. A verbal summary alone does not satisfy documentation requirements, and a fire insurance policy number is unrelated to mechanical code inspection.
  11. The correct answer is B (copper oxide scale formed inside the tubing during brazing and later migrated to the drier): without a nitrogen purge, every brazed joint heats the inside surface of the tube in open air, producing loose black copper oxide scale. That scale breaks free once refrigerant begins flowing and collects on the first fine screen it reaches, which is typically the filter drier. An undersized drier would show high pressure drop from normal flow rather than a screen coated in black flakes, and ice blockage from moisture looks like a wet, frozen mass rather than dry black particles. Oil fouling also has a different appearance and does not typically appear this early after a properly evacuated startup.
  12. The correct answer is D (combined flow area increases at low load, velocity drops, and oil return through the risers weakens): the entire reason for trapping the larger riser closed at low capacity is to keep all of the minimum load gas flow moving through the smaller, correctly sized riser at adequate velocity. If the trap seal fails and both risers stay open together, the same low volume of gas now spreads across a larger combined cross section, and velocity drops in both pipes. Lower velocity reduces the system's ability to entrain and lift oil, which matches a pattern of gradual compressor bearing wear from oil starvation. Flow splitting across two open paths does not improve oil carrying capacity, and refrigerant charge does not self-adjust for a failed trap.
  13. The correct answer is D (The remaining charge has fractionated, so its composition no longer matches virgin refrigerant.): When a zeotropic blend leaks, its more volatile components tend to escape preferentially, leaving the refrigerant still inside the system enriched in the less volatile components. This shifted composition, called fractionation, means the remaining charge no longer matches the original blend ratio printed on the cylinder or nameplate. Best practice is to recover what remains, evacuate fully, and charge an entirely fresh, correctly proportioned charge rather than topping up a fractionated mixture. There is no blanket ban or flammability change caused simply by a leak.
  14. The correct answer is D (The valve is mechanically stuck partially open, continuously overfeeding the evaporator coil): High suction pressure paired with low or near-zero superheat is the flooding pattern, and a TXV mechanically stuck partially open continuously overfeeds the evaporator regardless of actual load, producing exactly this result even though total system charge is correct by weight. A valve stuck fully closed would starve the coil, producing low suction pressure and high superheat, the opposite pattern. Correct total charge does not rule out a metering device that is distributing that charge incorrectly. Compressor valve failure produces a different pattern involving reduced capacity and pressures moving toward each other, not this flooding signature.
  15. The correct answer is B (a single riser sized for one operating capacity, since velocity stays adequate without a load range): a dual riser exists specifically to maintain adequate oil-carrying velocity across a wide range of operating capacities, from a lightly loaded stage up to full load. When a system runs at one steady capacity with no unloading or staging, a single riser can simply be sized for that one flow condition and will maintain adequate velocity at all times the system operates. A trap is still needed at the base of even a single riser to manage oil at startup and shutdown, so a claim that no trap is needed at all does not hold up, and a double riser configuration is not a blanket code requirement independent of load variation.
  16. The correct answer is D (Reduced evaporator airflow caused by the clogged return air filter.): Evaporator frost forms when the coil surface temperature drops below freezing because insufficient warm return air passes across it to keep the coil above 0°C, and a severely clogged filter is a confirmed and direct cause of that reduced airflow. The blower spinning at correct speed does not guarantee adequate airflow if the filter is choking the air path ahead of it. Overcharge and condenser side issues do not directly explain a frosted evaporator when airflow restriction has already been confirmed at the filter.
  17. The correct answer is A (Residual soap solution and moisture on the fittings triggered false detector readings): Electronic leak detectors are sensitive to trace hydrocarbon and moisture vapors, and soap solution left on a fitting can off-gas or trap refrigerant residue that sets off the sensor even without an active leak. Since the fittings tested leak-free once cleaned and dried, the alarms were not caused by a worn sensor cell, incompatible refrigerant, or ambient humidity, all of which would still trigger after cleaning. Best practice is to thoroughly wipe and dry any fitting before rechecking it electronically. This prevents wasted time chasing leaks that do not actually exist.
  18. The correct answer is D (The programming or wiring interlock is faulty and must be corrected before sign off.): If the design sequence calls for the supply fan to shut down when the exhaust fan starts, a failure to interlock properly indicates a programming or wiring fault in the building automation system. Leaving this uncorrected could allow the space to be depressurized or pressurized incorrectly during operation. This kind of finding must be documented as a deficiency and resolved before the sequence test can be marked as passed.
  19. The correct answer is A (Shipping documentation identifying the refrigerant type, quantity, and origin of each cylinder.): Transferring recovered refrigerant to a reclaimer should be accompanied by shipping documentation that identifies the refrigerant type, quantity, and origin for each cylinder, supporting both traceability and the reclaimer's own processing records. Cylinder tags alone are useful but do not replace formal shipment documentation covering the whole transfer. A verbal phone call or driver and vehicle details alone do not provide the material identification the transfer requires.
  20. The correct answer is A (How close the attic air is to reaching its dew point temperature): relative humidity readings from a hygrometer, combined with the air temperature, allow a technician to calculate the dew point, and if the duct surface temperature drops below that dew point, moisture in the air will condense on the duct exactly as observed. Refrigerant charge in option B is checked with pressure and temperature readings on the refrigerant circuit, not with a hygrometer. Compressor efficiency in option C and duct static pressure in option D are both measured with entirely different instruments unrelated to humidity.
  21. The correct answer is B (Follow the bearing manufacturer's specified grease type, interval, and quantity.): Bearings are engineered for a specific grease type and fill quantity, and following the manufacturer's published interval avoids both starving the bearing of lubricant and overpacking it, which can blow seals and force grease into the motor windings. Mixing incompatible greases can cause chemical breakdown and premature bearing failure. Skipping lubrication entirely on serviceable bearings accelerates wear and shortens motor and fan life.
  22. The correct answer is B (The contactor coil stays de-energized because the circuit path is incomplete): the diagram shows the Y contact and the contactor coil wired in series on the same rung, so both must be closed to complete the path from R to C. If the Y contact never closes, no current can reach the coil and the contactor cannot pull in. This is why a stuck-open thermostat contact or a broken Y wire is a common cause of a no-cooling call with 24 volts present at the board. The fan relay rung is a separate parallel path and cannot substitute for the cooling rung.
  23. The correct answer is B (The metering device drops the refrigerant's pressure and controls its flow rate into the evaporator.): The metering device, whether a thermostatic expansion valve, capillary tube, or electronic valve, creates a controlled pressure drop between the high pressure liquid line and the low pressure evaporator, which also lowers the refrigerant's saturation temperature enough to absorb heat effectively in the coil. It simultaneously regulates how much refrigerant flows into the evaporator, matching that flow to the current heat load so the coil is neither starved nor flooded with liquid. This is distinct from the compressor, which raises pressure rather than lowering it, and from the condenser and evaporator, which move heat rather than controlling pressure or flow directly. A properly sized and adjusted metering device is essential for maintaining correct superheat at the evaporator outlet.
  24. The correct answer is D (Record it on the deficiency list with a description and target completion date for correction): A sticking economizer actuator found during commissioning testing is exactly the kind of item that belongs on the deficiency list, documented clearly enough for a follow up trade to locate and repair it. Verbally mentioning the issue without written documentation risks it being forgotten, and signing the report as fully complete would misrepresent the true state of the system. Recording it properly keeps the project moving while ensuring the item is not lost.
  25. The correct answer is C (The calculated exhaust rate will likely be understated, leaving the room under ventilated during a leak): the CSA B52 machine room ventilation formula is based on the actual room volume, which includes ceiling height, not floor area alone. Omitting ceiling height from the calculation produces an understated volume, and since required exhaust rate scales with volume, this leads to an undersized fan selection. An undersized fan cannot dilute a potential refrigerant leak below the hazardous concentration threshold within the intended time, defeating the purpose of the ventilation requirement. There is no exemption in CSA B52 based on ceiling height alone, so the full volume must always be used in the calculation.
  26. The correct answer is D (10°C of superheat, found by subtracting the saturated evaporating temperature from the suction line temperature): Superheat is calculated as the actual suction line temperature at the compressor minus the saturated evaporating temperature corresponding to the low side pressure, so 15°C minus 5°C equals 10°C of superheat. This value confirms that the refrigerant has fully boiled off inside the evaporator and picked up additional heat before reaching the suction line. Reporting either raw temperature alone, as in options A and B, does not correctly answer what superheat actually measures.
  27. The correct answer is C (The Y terminal, since that terminal energizes the compressor contactor coil): The Y terminal, since that terminal energizes the compressor contactor coil. On the standard thermostat wiring convention, Y carries the signal that energizes the compressor contactor coil for a cooling call, while G controls the indoor fan and W controls heating. The C terminal simply provides the common return path back to the transformer and does not carry a call signal by itself. Landing R to Y at both the thermostat and the equipment board completes the cooling call circuit.
  28. The correct answer is B (R-404A ranks highest, then R-410A, then R-1234yf ranks lowest overall): R-404A carries one of the highest GWP values among common HFC blends, R-410A sits well below it but still far above the newer HFO refrigerant, and R-1234yf has an extremely low GWP close to that of carbon dioxide. This is a major reason R-404A has been phased down aggressively in commercial refrigeration applications. The other orderings incorrectly place either R-410A or R-1234yf above R-404A, which does not match published GWP data.
  29. The correct answer is B (Each intermediate trap catches oil draining back over that specific riser segment): On a very long vertical riser, oil draining down during the off cycle only falls as far as the next trap below it rather than the full height of the run, which limits how much liquid and oil must be re-entrained and lifted at once on start-up. Spacing traps at regular intervals, commonly around every 7 to 8 metres of rise, keeps each segment's oil load manageable for the available gas velocity.
  30. The correct answer is A (It protects against a loss of refrigerant charge or a restriction): a low pressure switch opens the compressor control circuit when suction pressure drops below a set point, which can indicate a refrigerant leak, a restricted metering device, or a blocked evaporator coil. Shutting the compressor down under these conditions prevents it from running with insufficient refrigerant flow for lubrication and cooling. This switch is distinct from the high pressure switch, defrost controls, and crankcase heater circuit.
  31. The correct answer is D (A minimum overlap of 1.5 metres between the two ladder sections): For an extended length between 11 and 16 metres, standard ladder guidelines call for a minimum 1.5 metre overlap between sections to maintain sufficient structural rigidity when the ladder is fully extended. Option A describes an overlap suited to a much shorter combined ladder length, not this range. Option B still falls short of the overlap required at this extended height. Option C describes an overlap larger than necessary, which would unnecessarily reduce the ladder's usable working length for this height range.
  32. The correct answer is B (A suction line accumulator that meters trapped liquid back slowly through a bleed hole): An accumulator is a storage vessel installed in the suction line before the compressor that catches any liquid refrigerant arriving during defrost, startup, or low-load conditions, holding it and metering it back slowly through a small bleed hole so only vapor and manageable amounts of oil-laden liquid reach the compressor. This is especially important on heat pumps, where reversing valve operation increases the risk of liquid floodback.
  33. The correct answer is A (As-built drawings, commissioning reports, warranty information, and operation and maintenance manuals): A complete handover documentation package gives the building owner everything needed to operate, maintain, and service the system going forward, including as-built drawings showing what was actually installed. Invoices alone, a blank service log, or a single summary page do not meet this requirement. This documentation is what the owner and future service technicians rely on for the life of the equipment.
  34. The correct answer is D (A hunting thermostatic expansion valve that is oscillating rather than holding a steady position): Fluctuating superheat combined with normal subcooling and audible hissing at the valve points toward the expansion valve itself repeatedly overshooting and undershooting its correct opening, a condition commonly called hunting, often caused by an oversized valve, a poorly located sensing bulb, or moisture in the system. A restricted filter drier would instead reduce subcooling downstream and produce a steady, not fluctuating, set of symptoms along with a measurable pressure drop across the drier. This distinction matters because correcting a hunting valve requires addressing bulb placement or valve sizing rather than replacing the drier or adjusting the charge.
  35. The correct answer is B (Compound gauges lack the resolution to read accurately below roughly 1000 microns): a standard compound gauge's scale is too coarse to distinguish meaningful differences once the system drops into the deep vacuum range, so it can show the needle sitting near zero long before moisture has actually been fully removed. Option A is incorrect since compound gauges are not refrigerant specific in this way. Option C is false because compound gauges are simple mechanical instruments needing no power source. Option D is also wrong, since compound gauges are specifically designed to read both pressure and vacuum on the same dial.
  36. The correct answer is A (At least 1 metre, or about 3 feet, above the landing surface): extending the ladder rails at least 1 metre past the landing gives the climber a stable handhold to grip while stepping off, reducing the risk of losing balance during the transition. A ladder that stops exactly level with the landing, as in option B, forces the climber to release their grip too early. Option C describes an excessive extension that offers no real safety benefit and can make the ladder harder to secure, and option D ignores an actual, specific requirement.
  37. The correct answer is C (flow dry nitrogen through the line at low pressure to displace air from inside the tube): heating copper in the presence of air oxidizes the inside surface of the tube, producing black flaky copper oxide scale that later breaks loose and can plug a filter drier, capillary tube or expansion valve. Purging the line with a small, steady flow of dry nitrogen before and during brazing displaces the oxygen inside the tube so scale cannot form. Packing the tube with wet rags does not address internal oxidation, and flux inside the bore contaminates the system rather than protecting it. Flame temperature and speed do not prevent internal oxide formation on their own.
  38. The correct answer is D (The microfarad rating, which should closely match the original 5 µF value): the microfarad, or µF, rating determines how much capacitance is added to the start winding circuit, which directly affects motor torque and running current. Using a capacitor with a significantly different microfarad value than the original can cause overheating, reduced torque, or premature motor failure. The voltage rating should be equal to or greater than the original, never lower, but matching microfarad rating is the more critical electrical specification. Case color and brand name have no bearing on electrical performance.
  39. The correct answer is C (6°C of subcooling, found by subtracting the liquid line reading from the saturated condensing temperature): Subcooling equals the saturated condensing temperature that corresponds to the high side pressure, minus the actual liquid line temperature measured at the condenser outlet, so 50°C minus 44°C equals 6°C. This tells the technician the liquid refrigerant has been cooled 6 degrees below the point where it finished condensing, which is normal for many residential and light commercial systems. Options A and B each report a single raw temperature rather than the actual difference the question asks for.
  40. The correct answer is D (Contactors switch higher current loads while relays switch smaller control loads): Contactors switch higher current loads while relays switch smaller control loads. Contactors are built with heavier contacts rated to handle the higher amperage of loads like compressor and fan motors directly connected to line voltage. Relays typically switch smaller loads, such as another coil, an indicator light, or a low current control signal. Both work on the same electromagnetic principle, but their contact ratings and typical applications differ significantly.
  41. The correct answer is D (Abnormally high superheat as refrigerant starves the far end of the evaporator coil.): An undersized piston restricts refrigerant mass flow below what the evaporator load requires, so the liquid refrigerant fully vaporizes early in the coil and the remaining coil surface only carries superheated vapor. This shows up as an abnormally high superheat reading at the outlet, and reduced cooling capacity as less of the coil is actively evaporating liquid. It does not cause flooding, since flooding is the oversized-orifice failure mode. Suction pressure drops somewhat but does not typically collapse to a deep vacuum on a properly loaded system.
  42. This data directly contradicts the claim that staged control produces lower energy use, and it shows the two peak demand figures are not identical between strategies. The building's average part load percentage is directly relevant here, since a variable frequency drive delivers its efficiency advantage specifically by matching output to varying part load conditions like the one described. Based on the values listed, modulating VFD control is the better performing strategy on both measures shown.
  43. The correct answer is C (1 metre from the wall, one quarter of the working height): the 4 to 1 ratio means the ladder base sits out 1 unit of distance for every 4 units of height reached, so a 4 metre climb requires the base positioned roughly 1 metre from the wall for a safe climbing angle. Option A places the ladder far too steep, increasing the risk of tipping backward, while options B and D place it far too shallow, risking the base sliding out from under the climber.
  44. The correct answer is A (A clamp on ammeter positioned around one individual conductor only): A clamp on ammeter uses the magnetic field surrounding a single current carrying conductor to measure amperage without requiring any wire to be cut or disconnected, making it the correct tool for this task. Option B measures voltage, which requires direct contact with two points in a circuit rather than measuring current through induction. Option C is used to test insulation integrity with a high test voltage, not to measure normal operating current. Option D only confirms whether a closed circuit path exists and cannot quantify the actual current flowing through it.
  45. The correct answer is A (The vapour's pressure and temperature both increase as mechanical work is added): The compressor performs mechanical work on the low pressure, low temperature suction vapour, which raises both its pressure and its temperature significantly by the time it reaches the discharge line. This hot, high pressure vapour must remain a superheated gas throughout compression, since a compressor is designed to pump vapour rather than liquid. The resulting discharge condition is what allows the refrigerant to reject heat at a higher temperature than the outdoor air in the next stage.
  46. The correct answer is A (Residual mineral oil can separate from the refrigerant and restrict oil return to the compressor): Leftover mineral oil is not miscible with the new HFC refrigerant, so it can separate out in the coils and traps rather than flowing back with the refrigerant, leading to poor oil return and eventual compressor lubrication problems even though POE oil was correctly added. Residual mineral oil does not improve cooling capacity, it is a lubrication contaminant, not a performance enhancer. It also does not remove the need for POE oil, and mixing oils has no ability to change a refrigerant's fundamental chemical composition into a blend.
  47. The correct answer is B (The evacuation was incomplete and non-condensable air remains trapped in the system): With the exact factory charge weighed in and a clean, properly airflowing condenser confirmed, overcharge and condenser fouling are both ruled out as explanations for the elevated pressure. Trapped air left behind from an incomplete vacuum occupies volume in the condenser and adds its own pressure on top of the refrigerant's normal condensing pressure, producing exactly this kind of unexplained high side reading. A restricted evaporator or filter drier would instead show up primarily as a low side symptom rather than an elevated high side reading. The system should be recovered, properly evacuated to a deep vacuum, and recharged.
  48. The correct answer is B (The owner must have the confirmed leak repaired promptly, not after a fixed waiting period.): Once a leak is confirmed on a system covered by the applicability threshold, the owner must have it repaired as soon as possible in the circumstances, and in any case within seven days after the leak is detected, rather than waiting for the next scheduled inspection or some other arbitrary countdown before starting the repair at all. This seven-day maximum is a real, fixed deadline set by the halocarbon regulation, distinct from the flat prohibition on deliberate venting, which applies with no charge threshold and allows no delay whatsoever. Continued operation of a leaking system does not remove or extend the owner's obligation to repair it within that window.
  49. The correct answer is B (Scan all fittings, joints, and coil surfaces with the UV lamp for dye traces): Fluorescent dye leak detection works by circulating dye through the system with the oil so it accumulates and glows visibly under ultraviolet light at the exact point where refrigerant is escaping. Running the system for several days before inspection gives the dye time to travel to and concentrate at the leak site, so the correct next step is a thorough UV scan of every joint and coil surface. Recovering the charge without inspecting first would remove the evidence the dye was added to create. A nitrogen-only pressure test does not make use of the dye that was just introduced into the system.
  50. The correct answer is C (Add refrigerant charge gradually while rechecking superheat until it approaches the target value): A measured superheat higher than the manufacturer's target, with airflow already confirmed normal, points toward an undercharged system, so the correct next step is to add small amounts of refrigerant while continuously rechecking superheat until it approaches the target value. Removing charge would push the actual superheat even further above target, worsening the mismatch rather than correcting it. Superheat readings above target do not by themselves indicate compressor failure, and simply accepting an out of specification reading risks long term compressor damage from repeated light loading.
  51. The correct answer is D (The cylinder should be labeled immediately at the job site upon completing recovery.): Labeling a recovery cylinder immediately at the point of recovery prevents misidentification, since relying on memory later in the day, or worse at the end of the week, introduces a real risk of mixing up refrigerant types across multiple job sites. Correct labeling at the time of recovery is standard proper practice and directly supports accurate compliance recordkeeping. Waiting until the cylinder reaches the shop leaves it unlabeled and vulnerable to misidentification in the meantime.
  52. The correct answer is A (Drawings and specifications prepared and stamped as required, showing system design details per CSA B52): CSA B52 requires permit applications to be supported by drawings and specifications prepared to the standard the code sets out, including any required professional stamp, showing sufficient system design detail for the authority having jurisdiction to review conformance. A rough hand sketch does not meet this documentation standard. A manufacturer's standard brochure lacks the project-specific design detail required, and submission is not optional based on the contractor's prior experience.
  53. The correct answer is C (Wet bulb reads lower due to evaporative cooling from a moistened wick, dry bulb does not): A wet bulb thermometer has a moistened wick around its bulb, and as air moves across it, evaporation cools the wick, pulling the reading below the ordinary dry bulb temperature unless the air is fully saturated. Dry bulb temperature is simply the standard air temperature measured with no evaporative cooling effect. The instrument type used and which single property each reading measures are both described incorrectly in the other two options.
  54. The correct answer is D (R-22 that has already been recovered, recycled, or reclaimed within Canada): Since new production and import of virgin R-22 for Canadian use has stopped, existing systems can only be serviced using recovered, recycled, or reclaimed supply already in the marketplace. Option A describes a source that is no longer legally permitted. Option B describes an unsafe and unapproved practice that changes the refrigerant identity. Option C ignores that the origin and processing of the refrigerant determines whether it is legally usable.
  55. The correct answer is A (The cooler with higher relative humidity will frost faster since more moisture condenses on the coil.): Frost forms when moisture in the air contacts a coil surface below freezing and changes directly from vapour to ice, so the total rate of frost accumulation depends heavily on how much moisture the entering air actually carries. Higher relative humidity air holds more water vapour at a given temperature, meaning more moisture is available to condense and freeze onto the coil surface per unit of time. Both coolers may share the same coil temperature and box temperature, but the humidity difference in the entering air is the dominant variable driving frost rate in this comparison. This is why coolers in humid climates or with frequent door openings typically require more aggressive defrost scheduling than equivalent units in drier environments.
  56. The correct answer is B (Environment and Climate Change Canada, which administers ozone and halocarbon regulations federally.): ECCC administers and enforces CEPA 1999 and the regulations made under it, including the Ozone-depleting Substances and Halocarbon Alternatives Regulations governing manufacture, import and export nationally, and the Federal Halocarbon Regulations governing venting and recovery specifically on federal and Indigenous lands and for federally owned equipment. Day-to-day venting prohibitions and recovery requirements for private-sector service and maintenance work, which is what most stationary refrigeration and air conditioning technicians actually perform, are instead set and enforced by each province and territory's own halocarbon or environmental protection regulation. Natural Resources Canada handles energy efficiency labelling, not halocarbon emissions. Provincial authorities such as TSSA regulate pressure vessel and technical equipment safety, a separate jurisdiction from both federal ozone protection law and provincial environmental halocarbon law.
  57. The correct answer is D (The larger capacitor, since start capacitors typically have a higher microfarad rating): The larger capacitor, since start capacitors typically have a higher microfarad rating. Start capacitors are designed to deliver a strong burst of phase-shifted current for only a brief moment during startup, which requires a significantly higher microfarad rating than a run capacitor needs for continuous duty. The bleed resistor is also a telltale sign of a start capacitor, since it safely discharges stored voltage once the potential relay removes the capacitor from the circuit. Run capacitors are built for continuous operation and are typically smaller with a lower microfarad rating.
  58. The correct answer is B (Investigate that zone's airflow and duct condition, since the difference suggests an imbalance.): A significantly larger supply to return temperature differential in one zone compared to the rest of the floor typically points to reduced airflow through that zone, whether from a closed damper, restricted duct, or blocked diffuser. Ignoring the outlier or adjusting the setpoint building wide would not address the actual cause of the imbalance. The technician should investigate that specific zone's airflow and ductwork before finalizing the commissioning readings.
  59. The correct answer is A (It phases down HFC production and consumption over time due to global warming potential.): The Kigali Amendment extends the Montreal Protocol framework to hydrofluorocarbons, which do not deplete ozone but carry significant global warming potential, requiring a scheduled step-down in production and consumption. Canada follows an established reduction schedule rather than an immediate ban, giving industry time to transition to lower GWP alternatives. The amendment does not restore CFC production and is not limited to mobile air conditioning equipment.
  60. The correct answer is C (The condensing process is drawn as a horizontal line on the diagram at constant pressure.): On a pressure-enthalpy diagram, pressure is plotted on the vertical axis and enthalpy on the horizontal axis, so any process occurring at constant pressure appears as a flat horizontal line. Condensation happens at essentially constant high side pressure as the refrigerant moves right to left, losing enthalpy as heat is rejected to the outdoor air. This is different from the compression process, which is drawn as a nearly vertical line where pressure rises sharply. Recognizing the horizontal condensing line helps a technician quickly identify desuperheating, condensing, and subcooling zones along that same pressure line on the diagram.
  61. The correct answer is A (The compressor winding is open and current cannot flow through that winding): A complete absence of continuity between two winding terminals means that specific winding path has broken internally, so no current can flow through it even though the contactor closes and full voltage reaches the terminals. This open winding explains why the compressor draws no amperage at all rather than a high locked rotor current, which would instead indicate a mechanical seizure. A grounded winding would show continuity to the compressor shell rather than a lack of continuity between terminals. An open winding cannot be repaired, so the compressor must be replaced.
  62. The correct answer is D (The letter represents toxicity class and the number represents flammability class): In this ASHRAE safety classification system, an A rating means lower toxicity while a B rating means higher toxicity, and the number that follows indicates flammability, with 1 meaning no flame propagation, 2L meaning mildly flammable with a lower burning velocity, 2 meaning flammable, and 3 meaning highly flammable. R-410A and R-134a both carry the A1 classification, meaning low toxicity and nonflammable, while R-32, R-454B, and R-1234yf all carry the A2L classification, meaning low toxicity but mildly flammable. This classification has nothing to do with ozone depletion potential or global warming potential, which are separate published ratings.
  63. The correct answer is C (Together they establish a complete operating baseline usable for future fault diagnosis on this unit): no single reading fully describes system health, but suction and discharge pressure, superheat, subcooling, and amperage together capture the refrigerant-side and electrical-side condition of the system at a point known to be correct. A future technician comparing new readings against this full baseline can pinpoint whether a fault is refrigerant related, airflow related, or electrical. Treating any one value as sufficient on its own, or dismissing the recording as a mere formality, ignores the diagnostic value multiple correlated baseline readings provide.
  64. The correct answer is C (clean both surfaces with sand cloth or a wire brush until bright, shiny copper shows): capillary action only draws molten filler alloy evenly into a joint gap when both mating surfaces are free of oxidation, oil and dirt. Cleaning to bright copper immediately before assembly gives the flux and alloy a sound base and reduces the chance of a cold joint or an unfilled void. Oil on the surfaces would contaminate the joint rather than protect it, and oxidation left in place on the tube works against the flux instead of helping it. Pre-heating the fitting to burn off residue is not the standard preparation step and can reintroduce oxide scale.
  65. Subtracting -6°C from 8°C gives a superheat value of 14°C, meaning the vapour has absorbed that much additional heat after fully boiling off inside the evaporator. This confirms that all the liquid refrigerant has vaporized before reaching the compressor, protecting it from liquid slugging. A superheat reading this high on a fixed metering device system could also indicate an undercharged system or a restricted evaporator, which would need further investigation.
  66. The correct answer is D (A very small pressure difference between two points, typically expressed in pascals or inches of water): A manometer is specifically designed to measure very small pressure differences, such as duct static pressure, that a standard mechanical pressure gauge is not sensitive enough to read accurately. It has no relationship to refrigerant charge weight, electrical resistance, or blower rotational speed. Understanding this makes clear why a manometer, rather than a general purpose gauge, is the correct tool for this part of commissioning.
  67. The correct answer is B (Ventilation must be adequate to dilute a worst-case refrigerant release for that room's actual charge): Ventilation must be adequate to dilute a worst-case refrigerant release for that room's actual charge. Because a larger refrigerant charge represents a larger potential release in a leak scenario, CSA B52 ties required ventilation capacity to the actual quantity of refrigerant present so the ventilation system can realistically dilute a worst-case release to a safe concentration rather than using an arbitrary rate that might be inadequate for a heavily charged room or wastefully oversized for a lightly charged one. This charge-based approach directly ties the safety measure to the actual hazard rather than to floor area or an assumed flat rate. It reflects the core purpose of machine room ventilation, which is refrigerant dilution and life safety in a release event.
  68. The correct answer is B (To maintain adequate oil-carrying velocity across the full range of possible load conditions): a double riser configuration uses a small diameter riser sized for adequate oil-carrying velocity at minimum load and a larger diameter riser that activates at higher loads, together covering a wide load range without ever dropping below the minimum velocity needed for reliable oil return. Reducing total refrigerant charge is not the primary purpose of this configuration. It does not eliminate the need for a trap at the base, and it does not allow different refrigerants to be used simultaneously in the same circuit.
  69. The correct answer is A (Correct orientation lets the internal plunger move freely so the valve seats fully.): many solenoid valves rely on gravity and a freely moving internal plunger or armature to fully open and close the valve seat, so mounting the valve in an orientation other than what the manufacturer specifies, such as inverted, can cause the plunger to bind, hang up, or fail to seat properly, resulting in a valve that leaks by or does not open fully. Orientation has nothing to do with the coil's voltage rating, and whether a valve is normally open or closed is a design feature of the valve itself, not a function of mounting angle.
  70. The correct answer is D (Discharge the capacitor safely, then measure its microfarad rating with a capacitance meter): After safely discharging the capacitor, measuring its actual microfarad value with a capacitance meter and comparing it to the rated value stamped on the capacitor confirms whether it has weakened or failed, which is more reliable than judging by a visible bulge alone since some capacitors fail electrically before bulging appears. Line voltage at a stopped disconnect tells nothing about the capacitor's condition. Compressor winding resistance is unrelated to a fan motor's run capacitor circuit. A worn contactor produces chattering or failure to energize, a different symptom pattern than a motor that hums and needs a manual push to start.
  71. The correct answer is B (One of the windings has an open circuit internally.): An infinite resistance reading with no continuity between winding terminals means the electrical path through that winding is broken somewhere inside the compressor, which is called an open winding. A shorted winding condition would instead produce an abnormally low resistance reading, not infinite. Because normal windings always show a measurable finite resistance value between terminals, this reading rules out a normal healthy compressor.
  72. The correct answer is B (It provides a low resistance path to clear a fault safely): bonding the equipment cabinet to the grounding system gives any fault current, such as a hot conductor contacting the metal case, a low resistance path back to the source. This allows the circuit protection device to trip quickly and removes dangerous voltage from the cabinet before a person can be shocked. Grounding and bonding have no functional relationship to capacitor performance or refrigerant containment.
  73. The correct answer is A (Verify correct incoming line voltage is present at the disconnect first): confirming that proper line voltage is actually reaching the unit is the logical starting point, since no component downstream can function correctly without adequate supply power. Working from the source of power toward the individual components prevents wasted time testing parts that were never the actual problem. Skipping straight to component replacement without this verification often leads to unnecessary parts changes and repeat service calls.
  74. The correct answer is B (Small window air conditioners, dehumidifiers, and household refrigerators under one ton of capacity): rotary compressors, whether rolling piston or rotary vane style, are compact, low cost, and efficient at the very small capacities typical of window units, dehumidifiers, and small household refrigerators. Their construction does not scale economically to the tonnage required by industrial ammonia plants, district cooling systems, or continuous duty marine chillers. Those larger applications instead rely on reciprocating, scroll, or screw compressors depending on the required capacity. Recognizing this typical application range helps a mechanic identify the compressor style expected on a given job.
  75. A shorted winding condition reads a low resistance, not infinite. A grounded winding is tested between a terminal and the shell, not between two winding terminals. An open winding is itself the electrical fault, so it cannot be dismissed as fine with the fault located elsewhere.
  76. The correct answer is C (The client or their representative reviews and signs off on the final commissioning documentation): Formal project closeout requires the client or their authorized representative to review and sign off on the completed commissioning documentation, which is what formally accepts the verified work and transfers responsibility for ongoing operation to the owner. Discarding the documentation package after only a verbal confirmation leaves no formal record of acceptance. Keeping the only copy in a personal filing cabinet denies the client access to their own required project records. Sending the documentation to an unrelated third party for unrelated review serves no purpose in the actual project closeout process.
  77. The correct answer is B (Calculating required subcooling to offset the pressure drop caused by the vertical liquid line rise): a vertical rise in the liquid line creates additional pressure drop from the static head of the refrigerant column, and if the liquid arriving at the expansion device is not subcooled enough to stay below its saturation pressure at that lower point, it will flash into vapor prematurely. The design step to prevent this is calculating the amount of subcooling needed at the condenser outlet to offset the pressure loss from the rise, ensuring liquid remains fully liquid until it reaches the expansion device. Reducing liquid line diameter does not create subcooling and can actually increase pressure drop further. Suction line diameter and the presence of a receiver do not directly control liquid line flashing on a vertical rise.
  78. The correct answer is B (Flag the layout, since A2L is mildly flammable and ignition sources need separation per code): A2L refrigerants carry a mild flammability classification under ASHRAE 34, meaning ignition sources such as open flames and certain electrical equipment must be evaluated and separated from potential refrigerant accumulation areas per code and manufacturer guidance. Treating A2L as non-flammable is incorrect and ignores its classification. A single detector alone does not resolve an ignition source proximity issue, and the concern applies regardless of whether the charge exceeds a specific mass threshold.
  79. The correct answer is C (The defrost termination sensor, which should signal coil temperature has risen enough to end defrost): Defrost mode is intended to end once the outdoor coil temperature sensor detects the coil has warmed enough to confirm the frost has melted, so a failed or stuck-open termination sensor never signals that condition and the reversing valve stays in defrost far past the normal cycle length, worsening frost buildup instead of clearing it. The indoor blower relay controls airflow inside and does not govern defrost termination logic. A weak run capacitor affects compressor starting, not how long defrost runs. The outdoor fan relay being de-energized during defrost is normal operation, not a fault explaining a stuck cycle.
  80. The correct answer is A (An air-cooled condenser rejecting heat directly to outdoor ambient air): An air-cooled condenser rejecting heat directly to outdoor ambient air. Air-cooled condensers need no process water at all, which makes them the practical choice where water access is limited or a blowdown discharge permit cannot be obtained. Water-cooled and evaporative condensers both offer lower condensing temperatures and better efficiency, but they depend on a continuous water supply and a permitted discharge or bleed-off path. Where that water infrastructure is not available, an air-cooled condenser trades some efficiency for a workable, low-maintenance installation.
  81. Since the reading is within range, no further refrigerant needs to be added or removed at this stage. Option D correctly notes the value but incorrectly suggests further action is still required.
  82. The thermostat cooling setpoint, the fan run capacitor, and the compressor overload are unrelated to the defrost initiation logic being tested here.
  83. The correct answer is C (The fault output energizes the relay and the alarm light turns on): The fault output energizes the relay and the alarm light turns on. When the VFD detects an internal fault, its fault output changes state and energizes the fault indicator relay coil on that rung, which then closes its contact on the alarm rung to light the alarm. This gives the technician or building operator immediate visual notice that the drive has tripped, separate from whatever run command the building automation system is still sending. The run command rung and the fault monitoring rung operate independently, since one commands the drive and the other simply reports its status.
  84. The correct answer is D (Reclamation reprocesses refrigerant off site to AHRI purity specification, verified by laboratory analysis.): Reclamation is the highest level of reprocessing, performed off site by a licensed reclaimer and confirmed against AHRI purity specification through laboratory analysis. Recovery is simply removing refrigerant from a system into a container, and recycling reduces contamination on site to a lesser standard without laboratory verification. Only reclamation carries the documented purity guarantee needed for refrigerant to be resold as reclaimed product.
  85.
