  86. The correct answer is D (The subcooling is approximately 8°C based on the readings taken by the technician.): Subcooling is calculated by subtracting the actual liquid line temperature from the saturated condensing temperature that corresponds to the high side pressure. Subtracting 36°C from 44°C gives a subcooling value of 8°C, confirming the liquid leaving the condenser is fully liquid with no flash gas present. Adequate subcooling protects the metering device from having to handle a vapour and liquid mixture, which would reduce its capacity to control refrigerant flow. A subcooling reading far outside the manufacturer's specified range would point toward either an overcharge or an undercharge condition needing correction.
  87. The correct answer is A (Adding a modest factor of roughly 5 to 10 percent to the calculated load): Adding a modest factor of roughly 5 to 10 percent to the calculated load. A modest safety factor in the range of about 5 to 10 percent is commonly applied to a well-supported load calculation to account for reasonable uncertainty in inputs such as occupancy or infiltration, without pushing the equipment into a significantly oversized range that would hurt part-load efficiency and humidity control. Doubling the load is excessive and leads to short-cycling and poor dehumidification performance, while applying no margin at all leaves no allowance for real-world variation from the calculated assumptions. Jumping to whatever the next available nominal size happens to be, without regard to how large that jump is, is not a controlled or defensible sizing method.
  88. The correct answer is C (It varies the output frequency supplied to the motor windings): a VFD converts incoming AC power to DC and then back to AC at a controllable frequency, and since motor speed is directly related to the supply frequency, varying that frequency varies compressor speed. This allows the system to modulate capacity smoothly rather than only cycling the compressor fully on or off. Voltage is also adjusted alongside frequency to maintain proper motor torque characteristics.
  89. The correct answer is A (The reversing valve is mechanically stuck and not shifting into the defrost position): Since the defrost control board is confirmed to be calling for defrost at the right time and energizing the reversing valve solenoid correctly, the electrical control side of the defrost sequence is working properly. If the outdoor coil temperature never responds despite the solenoid being energized, the valve body itself is mechanically stuck and failing to physically shift the refrigerant flow into the defrost position. An outdoor sensor reading falsely low would delay the defrost call itself, which is not what is happening here since the call is occurring correctly. A stuck reversing valve must be replaced to restore normal defrost operation.
  90. The correct answer is A (The rise is from residual moisture releasing vapor, and the plateau suggests an acceptable, tight system.): A small rise that levels off and holds steady, rather than climbing continuously, is a classic sign of trace residual moisture releasing a small amount of vapor pressure until it reaches equilibrium, not an active leak. A true leak typically shows a continuous, non-leveling rise that keeps climbing the longer the system is isolated. Since this system plateaued and held, it can generally be considered acceptably tight and dry, though the technician should still confirm the reading stays stable for a longer period if there is any doubt.
  91. The correct answer is B (moisture from the air is migrating through the tear and condensing inside the insulation over time): closed-cell elastomeric insulation resists vapour transmission through its intact surface, but a tear creates a direct path for warm, humid air to reach the cold tube surface underneath. Over time that moisture condenses inside the insulation at the cold surface and accumulates, which explains the added weight and dampness localized near the tear. Refrigerant does not leak through insulation material, so a refrigerant leak is not what is described here. Intact closed-cell foam does not absorb ambient humidity on its own, which is exactly why a breach in the jacket, rather than a defect, is the explanation here.
  92. The correct answer is B (One probe upstream of the filter and return duct, one probe downstream of the coil): Total external static pressure must capture the resistance of everything the blower has to push air through, from the return side filter and ductwork to the supply side coil and ductwork. Placing both probes inside the cabinet or on the wrong side of the assembly leaves out major resistance sources and produces a falsely low reading. This combined reading is then compared directly against the manufacturer's rated maximum for the blower.
  93. The correct answer is D (Thermostat calibration drift requiring recalibration or sensor replacement.): A consistent offset between the thermostat's displayed temperature and the actual measured room temperature, confirmed with a calibrated reference thermometer, is the definition of calibration drift, where the internal sensor or its programmed offset no longer matches reality. This is corrected by recalibrating the thermostat per the manufacturer's procedure or replacing the sensor if it cannot be brought back into tolerance. A meaningful offset of several degrees is well outside normal manufacturing tolerance and needs correction, and refrigerant flooding or a high pressure switch fault have no direct relationship to a room temperature sensing error.
  94. The correct answer is D (It must be readily accessible and within sight of the equipment): Canadian Electrical Code requirements referenced through CSA C22.1 call for a readily accessible disconnecting means located within sight of the air conditioning or refrigeration equipment it serves, allowing a technician to see the equipment while operating the disconnect. This ensures someone servicing the unit can isolate power without needing to locate a disconnect elsewhere in the building or rely on someone else confirming the panel position. Mounting the disconnect inside the compressor's own electrical compartment or only at the main panel would not satisfy this within-sight accessibility requirement.
  95. The correct answer is C (The refrigerant type contained and whether it is virgin, recycled, or reclaimed): Proper cylinder labeling must clearly identify the specific refrigerant contained and its processing status, since mixing refrigerant types or grades creates contamination and safety risks. Option A omits the refrigerant identity entirely, which is the essential information. Option B describes administrative information that does not belong on a refrigerant cylinder label. Option D is irrelevant to safe handling and identification of the cylinder's contents.
  96. The correct answer is D (Apply force at the belt midpoint until it deflects about one belt width, then read the gauge): Standard practice deflects the belt at its midpoint by roughly one belt width and reads the force required on the tension gauge, comparing that force to the manufacturer's specification for the belt and pulley size. Listening for squealing is a rough indicator only and gives no measured value. A fully slack belt is the opposite of what proper tensioning requires. Belt length alone says nothing about how tightly the belt is actually riding on the pulleys.
  97. The correct answer is B (to remove residual moisture and non-condensable gases from inside the piping and components): pulling a deep vacuum lowers the internal pressure enough to boil off residual moisture at room temperature and to pull out air and other non-condensable gases that were not purged during brazing or that entered during assembly. Leaving either moisture or non-condensables in the system leads to problems ranging from ice formation at metering devices to acid formation and abnormal high side pressure once the compressor runs. A vacuum is not a second pressure test, since it measures the opposite condition, and it has no role in verifying torque on mechanical fittings, which is a separate visual and mechanical check.
  98. The correct answer is B (Recalibrate or replace the thermostat so its reading matches the reference thermometer): A consistent offset between the thermostat's displayed temperature and an accurate reference thermometer over a full test cycle indicates the thermostat's internal sensor or calibration has drifted rather than a refrigerant or airflow problem. Replacing the contactor or cleaning the condenser coil addresses electrical or airflow issues unrelated to a sensing error at the thermostat itself. Adjusting refrigerant charge to compensate for a miscalibrated thermostat would only introduce a second problem without correcting the first. Recalibrating or replacing the thermostat restores accurate temperature control for the space.
  99. The correct answer is A (The defrost termination thermostat is failing to signal defrost has ended.): The defrost termination sensor is responsible for detecting when the outdoor coil has warmed enough to confirm the frost has melted and signaling the control board to end defrost and return to normal heating, so a failed termination sensor that never signals this condition leaves the unit stuck running the reversing valve and auxiliary heat in defrost mode indefinitely. A failed indoor blower capacitor or a normally operating outdoor fan and contactor would not by themselves prevent the control board from ending the defrost cycle on its own timer or sensor logic.
  100. The correct answer is D (The system is likely undercharged, and refrigerant should be added in small amounts): low subcooling on a TXV-equipped system typically means insufficient liquid refrigerant is backing up in the condenser, a sign of undercharge, even while the TXV itself continues to hold superheat within its normal range by throttling the reduced liquid supply it does receive. This is exactly why subcooling, not superheat, is the primary charging indicator trusted on TXV systems. An oversized condenser fan or reversed evaporator fan rotation would produce a very different combination of symptoms and are not supported by this specific pressure and temperature data alone.
  101. The correct answer is A (It shifts the current phase in the start winding to improve running torque): It shifts the current phase in the start winding to improve running torque. The run capacitor creates a phase difference between the start and run windings, which produces a more even rotating field and smoother running torque. Because the capacitor stays in the circuit continuously, PSC motors have simpler wiring than capacitor-start designs but lower starting torque. This makes PSC motors common on fan loads that do not need high breakaway torque.
  102. The correct answer is A (A COP of 4, calculated as heating output divided by electrical input): Coefficient of performance is defined as useful output divided by energy input, so dividing the 8 kW of heating output by the 2 kW of electrical input gives a COP of 4. COP is unitless because both the numerator and denominator are expressed in the same power units. A heat pump's heating COP is always higher than its equivalent cooling COP for the same equipment, since heating output includes both the absorbed heat and the compressor work.
  103. The correct answer is C (The discharge valve opens to release high pressure vapour from the cylinder into the discharge line.): As the piston moves upward on its compression stroke, the discharge valve opens once cylinder pressure exceeds the pressure in the discharge line, allowing the newly compressed high pressure vapour to leave the cylinder and enter the system's discharge piping toward the condenser. This valve remains closed during the intake stroke so that the cylinder can properly draw in low pressure vapour through the separate suction valve instead. Both valves are typically simple reed-type check valves that open and close purely from the pressure differences created by piston movement. A damaged or leaking discharge valve reduces compressor efficiency because some already-compressed vapour can leak backward into the cylinder on the next stroke.
  104. The correct answer is B (The winding is shorted to ground and the compressor must be replaced.): A near-zero resistance reading between any winding terminal and the grounded compressor shell indicates the winding insulation has broken down and is shorted to ground, which is an internal electrical fault that cannot be field repaired. This explains the repeated overload tripping since a grounded winding draws excessive and unbalanced current. An open winding would instead show infinite resistance rather than near-zero, and a healthy compressor should read very high resistance to ground, not close to zero.
  105. The correct answer is D (ACR tubing is cleaned, capped, and nitrogen charged internally before shipping from the mill.): ACR tubing is manufactured to be internally clean and dry, then capped and charged with a small nitrogen holding pressure so no moisture or debris enters before installation. Type L water tube is not treated this way and can contain oxides or contaminants unsuitable for a sealed refrigerant circuit. Both tube types share comparable wall thickness, so wall thickness is not the distinguishing factor. ACR tubing is sold in both soft coils and hard drawn lengths depending on size.
  106. The correct answer is C (10°C, based on the R-410A saturation pressure shown on the low side gauge): A standard R-410A pressure-temperature chart lists approximately 1025 kPa as the saturation pressure corresponding to a 10°C evaporating temperature. Because the refrigerant in the evaporator is a mixture of liquid and vapour at this point, its pressure and temperature are directly linked by that fixed relationship. Reading the correct saturation temperature from the gauge is the first step in later calculating superheat at the compressor.
  107. The correct answer is D (Violations can lead to federal enforcement action, including significant fines under environmental law.): Deliberate venting is an offence under environmental law and can result in significant fines and other enforcement action, independent of any separate provincial trade licensing consequences. For most private-sector work like this, that enforcement comes from the applicable province or territory's own halocarbon or environmental protection regulation and is carried out by the provincial environment ministry; Environment and Climate Change Canada directly enforces the federal Ozone-depleting Substances and Halocarbon Alternatives Regulations for manufacture, import and export violations, and the Federal Halocarbon Regulations for violations on federal or Indigenous lands or involving federally owned equipment. This is not limited to a private civil matter between the technician and the equipment owner, since a government regulator itself enforces the applicable regulation. Provincial trade boards may also act separately, but that does not eliminate the underlying regulatory enforcement exposure.
  108. The correct answer is D (Redesign to reduce charge, split the system, or reclassify occupancy with proper safeguards): Redesign to reduce charge, split the system, or reclassify occupancy with proper safeguards. When an estimated charge exceeds the CSA B52 quantity limit for a given occupancy classification, the designer must actually change the design, whether by selecting more efficient components to reduce required charge, splitting the load across multiple smaller systems, or applying additional safeguards that support a different occupancy classification. Increasing machine room size does not by itself raise the occupancy-based quantity limit, and the limit is not specific to ammonia alone but applies based on refrigerant class and occupancy. Adjusting the design ambient temperature would change the calculated load, not the actual physical charge the installed system requires.
  109. The correct answer is D (7°C of subcooling, found by subtracting the liquid line temperature from the saturated condensing temperature): Subcooling is calculated as the saturated condensing temperature corresponding to the high side pressure, minus the actual measured liquid line temperature, so 45°C minus 38°C equals 7°C. This value tells the technician how much additional heat has been removed from the liquid after it finished condensing. Reporting either raw temperature alone, as in options A and B, does not answer the actual subcooling question being asked.
  110. The correct answer is A (Refrigerant with the lowest listed saturation pressure at the common condensing temperature): the exhibit directly lists saturation pressure at 40 degC for both refrigerants, and Refrigerant Y at 980 kPa is lower than Refrigerant X at 1520 kPa, meaning a system using Refrigerant Y would operate at the lower discharge pressure at that same condensing temperature. Critical temperature relates to the point above which the refrigerant cannot be condensed by pressure alone, not directly to discharge pressure at a given condensing temperature. Glide and global warming potential are unrelated to operating discharge pressure at a fixed condensing temperature.
  111. The correct answer is C (Preheating domestic hot water before it reaches a conventional water heater): Preheating domestic hot water before it reaches a conventional water heater. A desuperheater recovers only the superheat portion of the discharge gas, which is a relatively modest and fairly steady quantity of heat well matched to preheating domestic hot water ahead of a conventional water heater, reducing the primary heater's energy use. It is not sized to meet a building's full space heating load on a cold design day, nor is it a realistic source of process steam or a full heating system replacement, since those loads are far larger and more variable than the recoverable desuperheat quantity. Preheating hot water remains the most common and cost-effective application of this recovered heat.
  112. The correct answer is A (The cylinder is appropriate for recovery since it is grey with a yellow shoulder and current.): The exhibit shows a grey body with a yellow shoulder, a refillable DOT/TC 4BA classification, and a current requalification status, all of which confirm this cylinder is properly configured and legally eligible for recovery service. Grey and yellow specifically indicates recovered or contaminated refrigerant use rather than virgin product only. A dual port valve does not disqualify a cylinder from recovery, and repainting it green would be inconsistent with the recovered refrigerant colour convention.
  113. The correct answer is B (A means of controlling water flow and preventing refrigerant migration into the potable water side): a desuperheater used for domestic water preheating must include controls for water flow, typically an aquastat or similar device, and must maintain a double wall or otherwise safe heat exchange separation to prevent any possibility of refrigerant migrating into the potable water side, since that water is intended for human use. A desuperheater is installed on the discharge line, between the compressor and the main condenser, not between the evaporator and expansion device. It also does not eliminate the need for the main condenser, which continues to handle the remaining condensing duty, and it is sized to recover only a portion of total system heat rather than the entire heat rejection load.
  114. The correct answer is D (ACR tube is cleaned, dehydrated and capped at the mill, and sized by actual outside diameter): ACR, meaning Air Conditioning and Refrigeration tube, is manufactured specifically for refrigerant service, internally cleaned, dehydrated and capped at both ends to keep out moisture and debris until installation. Type K, L and M water tube are instead sized by a nominal dimension that is close to but not equal to the true outside diameter, while ACR tube is sized directly by its actual OD. Mixing the two systems without accounting for this difference can cause a poor fit at braze cups and flare connections. Both ACR and Type L tube can be brazed, so the claim that ACR must be flared instead does not hold up.
  115. The correct answer is A (The heating COP calculates to approximately 4.0 for this heat pump): Heating COP equals the useful heat output at the condenser divided by the compressor's work input, so 9.6 kW divided by 2.4 kW equals 4.0. This means the heat pump delivers four units of heat to the space for every one unit of electrical energy it consumes. The other values result from dividing the wrong pair of numbers or misreading one of the two measured quantities.
  116. The correct answer is C (The name and address of the chemical's initial supplier or manufacturer): Supplier labels under WHMIS 2015 must identify the initial supplier or manufacturer so the product can be traced back to its source, a requirement that workplace labels are not held to. Workplace labels instead only need the product identifier, safe handling precautions, and a reference directing workers to the SDS. Option A is required on both label types since the product identifier must match across the label and SDS. Options B and D describe elements that workplace labels commonly include and are not unique to supplier labels.
  117. The correct answer is D (The moisture content of the air, since wet bulb reflects evaporative cooling effects): Wet bulb temperature is measured with a moistened thermometer bulb exposed to airflow, and the amount it drops below the dry bulb reading reflects how much moisture the surrounding air can still absorb through evaporation. Combining dry bulb and wet bulb readings on a psychrometric chart lets a technician determine relative humidity, dew point, and the total heat content of the air. Neither reading has any direct connection to refrigerant charge or discharge pressure, which must be measured separately with gauges.
  118. The correct answer is C (Take a new airflow reading at the diffuser to confirm the adjustment reached the design value): Balancing dampers requires an iterative process of adjusting, then rechecking the actual airflow reading, since the relationship between damper position and resulting CFM is not always predictable in advance. Moving on without rechecking, or assuming the reading changed without verification, risks leaving the diffuser out of balance and recording inaccurate data in the report. Only a fresh reading after adjustment confirms the diffuser has actually reached its design target.
  119. The correct answer is A (It is non-refillable and must never be refilled or requalified once emptied or disposed of.): DOT-39 cylinders are manufactured as non-refillable, single-use containers and are not authorized for hydrostatic requalification or refilling under any circumstance, unlike refillable steel recovery cylinders. Attempting to refill or requalify a DOT-39 cylinder is a serious safety and regulatory violation regardless of its outer condition. Once its contents are properly disposed of or transferred, the empty cylinder itself must be discarded according to disposal requirements, not reused.
  120. The correct answer is C (Unit One is flooding refrigerant back while Unit Two has a starved evaporator): High suction pressure combined with near-zero superheat is the signature of flooding, since excess liquid refrigerant is reaching the compressor faster than it can absorb heat and boil off. Low suction pressure combined with high superheat is the opposite pattern, showing too little refrigerant reaching the coil to satisfy the load, which is a starved evaporator. These two conditions are direct opposites of each other and must never be confused when reading gauges. Reversing the diagnosis on either unit would lead the technician to make exactly the wrong repair.
  121. The correct answer is C (Momentarily energize the unit and observe that suction pressure drops while discharge pressure rises): bumping the compressor briefly and watching for suction pressure falling and discharge pressure rising confirms the scroll or reciprocating compressor is pumping refrigerant in the correct direction, since reverse rotation produces little to no pressure differential. Equal voltage readings across all three phases only confirm balanced supply voltage, not correct phase sequence or rotation direction. Sound level alone is subjective and unreliable for confirming rotation, and crankcase heater warmth relates to oil conditioning, not compressor rotation direction.
  122. The correct answer is B (Subcooling is 3°C, below the typical 8°C to 12°C target, suggesting undercharge likely): subcooling equals the saturation temperature minus the liquid line temperature, which here is 44°C minus 41°C, giving 3°C. That value sits well below a typical 8°C to 12°C target range for a TXV-equipped R-410A system, a pattern consistent with an undercharged system that has not yet built up sufficient liquid in the condenser. Adding the two temperatures instead of subtracting produces an impossible 85°C figure, and while ambient temperature affects the target range chosen, it is not required to perform this specific subtraction.
  123. The correct answer is C (The fan relay coil never energizes automatically during a cooling call): the G terminal carries the signal that energizes the fan relay to run the indoor blower, and many thermostats also send a G signal automatically whenever Y is active, but that signal cannot reach the equipment if the wire is not landed. With the G circuit broken, the fan relay never pulls in during an automatic cooling call, even though the compressor circuit on the Y terminal remains completely unaffected. The homeowner still gets cooling because the compressor runs, but airflow across the evaporator can suffer since the indoor fan is not confirmed running with it in this scenario.
  124. The correct answer is A (A single riser sized for full load would run below oil-carrying velocity at part load): A single riser sized for full load would run below oil-carrying velocity at part load. On a system with wide capacity turndown, a single riser large enough to keep pressure drop reasonable at full load would be oversized relative to the reduced gas flow at part load, letting velocity fall below the minimum needed to lift oil the full nine metres back to the rooftop compressor. The double-riser design keeps a smaller leg permanently active so velocity stays high enough at minimum load, with the second leg only carrying flow once load rises. This is a velocity and oil-return design decision, not a charge limit or a fixed CSA B52 height trigger, since no such blanket three-metre rule exists in the code.
  125. The correct answer is A (Isometric drawings show three-dimensional routing, including rises, drops and offsets, in one view): Isometric drawings show three-dimensional routing, including rises, drops and offsets, in one view. An isometric drawing represents piping in a pseudo-three-dimensional projection, allowing a mechanic to see vertical rises, drops and horizontal offsets along the actual route in a single view, which a flat plan view alone cannot convey clearly, especially where the line crosses between floors or around structural obstructions. This is essential for accurately estimating pipe lengths, fitting counts and trap locations before ordering material. Isometric drawings do not replace pipe sizing calculations or a P&ID, which serve different purposes, and they are a piping documentation tool, not an electrical drawing type.

Practice Test 7 Answer Key

  1. The correct answer is C (Conducting regular workplace inspections and making safety recommendations): a joint health and safety committee's core functions include conducting scheduled workplace inspections, identifying hazards, and making recommendations to the employer for improving conditions, acting as an advisory body rather than an enforcement authority. Option A is incorrect because only Ministry of Labour inspectors have the legal authority to issue fines, not the committee itself. Option B and option D describe business and financial functions that fall outside the committee's health and safety mandate entirely.
  2. The correct answer is B (Normally closed, since continuity is present with the coil de-energized): Normally closed, since continuity is present with the coil de-energized. A normally closed contact set is closed and conducting in the relay's resting, unpowered state, and it opens once the coil is energized. The set showing an open circuit with the coil de-energized is the normally open set, which will close only when the coil is energized. Confirming this behavior before installation prevents wiring a load to the wrong contact set.
  3. The correct answer is B (Documented records showing what training was provided and when it occurred): Employers are generally expected to keep documented records of the health and safety training provided to workers, including what was covered and the date it occurred, since this documentation demonstrates compliance and supports future refresher scheduling. Option A is incorrect because verbal delivery of training does not remove the expectation of maintaining a written record afterward. Option C describes an incomplete record that would not show what training content was actually delivered. Option D is false since training documentation is a standard expectation, not something left to individual worker discretion.
  4. The correct answer is D (This transfer occurs through convection, since a moving fluid carries heat away from the surface.): Convection is the transfer of heat between a solid surface and a moving fluid, whether that fluid is air pushed by a condenser fan or water moved by a pump in a hydronic system. The condenser fan forces air across the hot coil surface, and that moving air carries absorbed heat away, allowing more heat to continue transferring from the coil. This is different from conduction, which describes heat moving through a solid material such as the coil tubing itself before it ever reaches the air. Fan speed and airflow volume both directly affect how efficiently convective heat transfer occurs at the condenser coil.
  5. The correct answer is C (To track refrigerant use and support leak reporting and recovery compliance.): Environmental regulations governing controlled refrigerants require accurate tracking of quantities added and recovered so that excessive refrigerant use on a given system can be flagged as a potential leak requiring repair, and so that recovered refrigerant is properly accounted for rather than vented to the atmosphere. This record keeping supports broader environmental compliance goals around refrigerant emissions. Billing rates, building electrical consumption, and automatic filter scheduling are unrelated administrative or maintenance matters, not the purpose of this specific regulatory requirement.
  6. The correct answer is A (The temperature stays essentially constant while the liquid changes into vapour): While a pure refrigerant is boiling at a constant pressure, it absorbs latent heat and changes state without any corresponding rise in temperature. This constant temperature region is why a P-T chart can list a single saturation temperature for a given evaporator pressure. Only after the refrigerant becomes 100 percent vapour does further heat addition begin to raise its temperature, which is when superheat starts to build.
  7. The correct answer is D (A continuity test across the winding terminals with the meter set to resistance): with the circuit deenergized, setting the multimeter to resistance and measuring across the run, start, and common terminals will show infinite resistance or an open circuit reading if a winding has actually failed open. Option A is dangerous and pointless here since testing voltage requires power to be applied, contradicting the lockout already performed. Option B also requires the compressor to attempt starting, which will not happen with a genuinely open winding, and option C checks for a grounded winding, a different fault than an open winding.
  8. The correct answer is C (Twenty-four hours, allowing sufficient time to drive dissolved refrigerant out of the oil.): Most commercial refrigeration and air conditioning manufacturers specify a minimum twenty-four hour crankcase heater warm-up period before an initial startup, giving the heater enough time to raise the oil temperature high enough to drive dissolved refrigerant vapor back out of solution. Shorter periods like ten minutes or two hours are not sufficient for this purpose, and brazing joint curing is unrelated to crankcase heater function entirely. This requirement applies specifically to protecting the compressor from liquid slugging on start, not to any electrical or mechanical curing process.
  9. The correct answer is B (It is based on the heat needed to melt one ton of ice in 24 hours.): Before mechanical refrigeration became common, cooling was often provided by blocks of ice, and a ton of refrigeration was originally defined as the rate of heat absorption needed to melt one short ton, 2,000 pounds, of ice over a 24-hour period. Using the latent heat of fusion of ice, this works out mathematically to exactly 12,000 BTU per hour, which became the standard definition still used today even though ice cooling has largely disappeared from modern practice. This historical basis is why the unit ties so cleanly to a round BTU per hour figure rather than an arbitrary number. The ton of refrigeration remains the standard capacity unit on most imperial-rated compressors, condensers, and cooling towers throughout the trade.
  10. The correct answer is C (Oxygen level first, then flammability, then toxic gas concentration last): Testing oxygen first is critical because both flammability and many toxic gas sensors depend on a normal oxygen level to read accurately, so confirming oxygen content establishes a reliable baseline before checking for flammable atmospheres and then toxic contaminants. Option A tests toxic gases before confirming oxygen levels, which can produce unreliable sensor readings. Option B similarly checks flammability before oxygen is confirmed, risking an inaccurate combustible gas reading. Option D ignores the accepted sequence entirely, even though most multi gas meters do display all readings together once oxygen is validated.
  11. The correct answer is B (Liquid floodback to the compressor from unvaporized refrigerant in the suction line.): A superheat reading well below target combined with suction line frosting beyond its normal extent both indicate that liquid refrigerant is not fully vaporizing in the evaporator and is instead traveling further down the suction line than intended. If this condition continues, that unvaporized liquid can reach and enter the compressor itself, washing lubricating oil off the cylinder walls and causing mechanical damage on each compression stroke. This is a floodback condition, distinct from the overheating, bearing, and electrical failure modes listed in the other options.
  12. The correct answer is A (Provide an emergency purge rate triggered by a refrigerant detector, sized per the code formula): CSA B52 requires a mechanical exhaust system for an ammonia machine room sized using a formula based on refrigerant charge and room volume, actuated automatically by a refrigerant detector to purge the room at a high rate during a release event, in addition to normal ventilation. A constant low comfort-level exhaust rate does not meet the emergency purge requirement. Natural infiltration alone is not an acceptable substitute, and exhaust rates must be calculated per the specific room and charge rather than copied from an unrelated boiler room.
  13. The correct answer is C (the unsupported section allowed the line to sag, concentrating bending stress at that joint): with the surrounding hangers still in place and correctly spaced, a missing hanger at one location creates an unsupported span on either side of it, and the tubing sags toward that gap under its own weight. That sag concentrates repeated bending stress at the nearest rigid point, which in this case is the brazed tee, until a hairline crack develops. Pressure in a liquid line does not vary by fitting location under normal steady operation, and velocity through a tee branch is not inherently higher than through the main run. A manufacturing defect is possible in isolation but is far less likely given the direct correlation with the missing support.
  14. The correct answer is C (Lower the output frequency sent to the compressor motor windings): reducing the VFD's output frequency slows the compressor's rotational speed, which directly reduces refrigerant mass flow and overall cooling capacity without shutting the compressor down completely. The VFD also proportionally adjusts output voltage alongside frequency to maintain proper motor torque and avoid overheating the windings at reduced speed. Disconnecting a leg or switching phase configurations are not valid or safe methods of capacity control on a VFD-driven compressor.
  15. The correct answer is C (A grey cylinder body with a yellow shoulder, marked for recovered or contaminated refrigerant.): Cylinders intended for recovered, mixed, or contaminated refrigerant use a standardized grey body with a yellow shoulder rather than matching the colour of any specific refrigerant. This convention distinguishes them clearly from virgin product cylinders regardless of what refrigerant they last contained. An unpainted or refrigerant-matched cylinder would not meet this recognized recovery cylinder marking convention.
  16. The correct answer is A (Relative humidity increases as air temperature falls toward the dew point): As air cools toward its dew point while holding the same amount of moisture, it becomes closer to saturated, so relative humidity rises even though the actual moisture content has not changed at all. Relative humidity is not independent of temperature, since it reflects how close the air is to its saturation point at that specific temperature. No additional instrument is needed to reason through this relationship once dry bulb and dew point are both known.
  17. The correct answer is D (Vapour phase, metered slowly into the low side suction line while monitoring superheat): because a single-component refrigerant like R-22 has no blend to fractionate, metering a small vapour trim into the low side while the compressor runs is an accepted way to fine-tune superheat without risking liquid slugging. Feeding liquid directly and rapidly into the low side risks the exact slugging hazard vapour trimming avoids. Refusing to charge at all once running is overly restrictive for a minor trim adjustment, and shutting the compressor off is unnecessary for this small single-component vapour trim.
  18. The correct answer is A (It relies on an open flame and can produce toxic decomposition gases): a halide torch burns propane or acetylene and changes flame color when refrigerant passes over a heated copper element, but that process can produce toxic decomposition byproducts, and the open flame itself is a hazard around flammable materials, both of which pushed the trade toward safer electronic detectors. Cost in option B is not the primary safety driven reason for the shift. Option C is inaccurate since the method can still react to many halogenated refrigerants, and option D describes a licensing requirement that does not reflect the actual historical reason for the method's decline.
  19. The correct answer is B (Any deliberate release to atmosphere is prohibited, so low loss fittings should be used instead.): The prohibition on deliberate venting covers any intentional release to atmosphere, regardless of the quantity involved, so even a small release while connecting gauges falls within its scope and low loss fittings should be used to avoid it. There is no exemption threshold based on a specific kilogram amount, and gauge connection releases are not specifically exempted. The prohibition also is not limited to high global warming potential refrigerants alone.
  20. The correct answer is B (Return airflow is well under design, driving the zone strongly positive relative to adjacent areas): Return CFM measures 210 against a design of 380, a large shortfall, while supply airflow is nearly at design, which pushes more air into the zone than is being removed and creates the strongly positive pressure, draft, and door resistance described in the complaint. Supply airflow in the exhibit is close to design, not above it, so an above design supply claim misreads the data. Nothing in the exhibit points to defective door hardware as the cause, and the pressure imbalance fully explains the symptoms. The pressure reading and door behaviour in the exhibit confirm a real physical condition, not merely a subjective perception.
  21. The correct answer is B (Briefly loosen the hose fitting at the manifold to purge trapped air with system refrigerant): cracking the valve to let a small burst of system refrigerant flow through the hose and out the loosened fitting pushes the trapped air and moisture out before a full connection is made. Connecting without purging introduces air and moisture directly into the refrigerant circuit, which raises head pressure and can form acids over time. Blowing shop air through the hose adds more contamination rather than removing it, and submerging the hose in water is not a recognized purging method.
  22. The correct answer is C (Compressor 2 runs continuously whenever the system has power, regardless of Y2): a welded stage 2 relay contact stays closed no matter what the thermostat's Y2 terminal is doing, so contactor 2 receives a continuous path to C and stays energized. Stage 1 operates on its own independent rung and is unaffected by this fault. This kind of failure typically shows up as excessive cooling capacity, higher power bills, or short cycling on temperature.
  23. The correct answer is A (4520 kJ, since latent heat is multiplied directly by the mass boiled): Latent heat of vaporization is expressed per kilogram, so the total heat absorbed equals the latent heat value multiplied by the mass being boiled, giving 2260 kJ/kg multiplied by 2 kg. No temperature change occurs during this calculation because latent heat only accounts for the phase change itself. This is the same principle that lets an evaporator absorb heat from air while the refrigerant stays at a constant saturation temperature.
  24. The correct answer is A (The measured superheat is approximately 7 degrees Celsius at this reading): Superheat equals the actual suction line temperature minus the saturated evaporating temperature at the measured low side pressure, so 11 C minus 4 C equals 7 C of superheat. Recording only the saturation temperature or only the line temperature, without subtracting one from the other, produces the incorrect distractor values. Superheat confirms the refrigerant leaving the evaporator is fully vaporized before it reaches the compressor.
  25. The correct answer is D (Self-contained recovery, generally preferred when the system's own compressor is inoperable or damaged.): Self-contained recovery equipment uses its own dedicated compressor to pull refrigerant from the system, making it the appropriate choice whenever the serviced unit's compressor cannot be relied upon, such as after a burnout or mechanical failure. System-dependent recovery instead uses the unit's own working compressor and is only viable when that compressor is confirmed operational. Push-pull recovery is a separate large-volume liquid transfer technique, not something limited only to small residential systems.
  26. The correct answer is C (Liquid phase, so the blend enters the system with its original mixed composition intact): zeotropic blends have components with different boiling points, so drawing vapour off the top of the cylinder pulls a disproportionate share of the more volatile component first, a problem called fractionation. Charging liquid from the dip tube keeps the mixture ratio consistent with what left the factory. Vapour composition does not match liquid composition for a blend with glide, and alternating phases or assuming composition never shifts both risk leaving the system with an off-ratio charge.
  27. The correct answer is D (On the suction line near the outdoor unit, close to the compressor): placing the probe on the suction line close to where it enters the outdoor unit, after insulating it against ambient air influence, gives a reading close to the actual gas temperature returning from the evaporator for an accurate superheat calculation. Option A measures discharge gas leaving the compressor, which is used for a different calculation entirely, not superheat. Option B measures the liquid line, which relates to subcooling rather than superheat, and option C would only capture return air temperature, not refrigerant temperature.
  28. The correct answer is B (Enthalpy, representing the heat content of the refrigerant at each point in the cycle): On a standard P-h diagram, enthalpy is plotted along the horizontal axis and pressure is plotted along the vertical axis, usually on a logarithmic scale, which allows a technician to visually trace each stage of the vapour compression cycle as a nearly rectangular shape. Reading horizontal distance across the diagram directly shows the change in heat content, which is useful for estimating capacity and work of compression. Temperature and entropy lines can also appear on a P-h diagram as reference curves, but neither one is the axis itself.
  29. The correct answer is C (Designated lugs are engineered to safely support the unit's actual weight): Manufacturer designated lifting lugs are engineered and positioned based on the unit's actual weight distribution and centre of gravity, ensuring the lift remains balanced and the attachment points can safely bear the load. Option A understates the real engineering purpose behind the lug placement, reducing it to mere convenience. Option B describes a cosmetic concern that is secondary to the actual structural safety reasoning behind using designated lift points. Option D incorrectly implies a weight threshold applies, when using designated lugs is good practice regardless of the unit's specific weight.
  30. The correct answer is C (Correct flow direction and a drier sized appropriately for the system tonnage): Filter driers are directional, and the arrow on the shell must point toward refrigerant flow or the drier will not filter or dry effectively. An undersized drier for the system's tonnage creates excessive pressure drop and restricts flow, while an oversized one adds unnecessary cost, so matching drier capacity to system size while respecting flow direction is essential at installation.
  31. The correct answer is B (Non-condensable gas, such as air, is present in the system and adding to the total pressure): With the compressor off overnight, refrigerant pressure should settle to the saturation pressure that matches ambient temperature for that refrigerant, so a static reading well above that expected value with confirmed correct charge points to non-condensable gas such as air occupying space and adding its own partial pressure to the total. A dirty condenser only affects pressure while the compressor is actively running and moving refrigerant. The compressor cannot be overworking while it is off. An elevated static pressure above the expected saturation value is a real anomaly, not a normal condition to ignore.
  32. The correct answer is D (Staged capacity control, cycling multiple compressors on and off to match load): piping multiple fixed-capacity compressors in parallel on common suction and discharge headers, with a controller bringing units online or offline based on demand, is the standard definition of staged capacity control in refrigeration system design. Hot gas bypass control diverts discharge gas to the suction line to artificially load a single compressor rather than staging multiple units. Floating head pressure control adjusts condenser fan speed with ambient conditions, and suction pressure regulation throttles flow at individual evaporators rather than staging whole compressors.
  33. The correct answer is A (Elevated entering water temperature is reducing the coil's heat rejection.): A water-source unit rejects heat to the entering water in the same way an air-source unit rejects heat to outdoor air, so water that arrives warmer than the design temperature reduces the available temperature difference for heat rejection and drives head pressure up. This mirrors how a dirty air-cooled condenser raises head pressure by impairing heat transfer, except here the limiting factor is water temperature rather than airflow. Excess flow rate would generally help rather than hurt heat rejection, and this scenario specifically states refrigerant charge is not the relevant factor.
  34. The correct answer is C (It keeps a slow outward flow that reliably excludes air from re-entering): a small regulated positive pressure ensures nitrogen continues flowing outward through the open end of the piping throughout the brazing process, which reliably keeps atmospheric air from diffusing back in and causing internal oxidation. Nitrogen purging has no effect on flame temperature, which rules out option A, and it plays no chemical role in activating flux, ruling out option B. Option D is also incorrect because a proper pressure or leak test is performed as a separate step after the joint has fully cooled, not during active brazing.
  35. The correct answer is C (The heater never energizes, so ice may not fully clear the coil): the heater coil rung requires both the defrost relay contact and the termination thermostat contact to be closed, so a permanently open termination thermostat breaks this path no matter what the defrost timer and relay are doing. Without heater operation during a called defrost cycle, frost and ice can build up on the evaporator coil over repeated cycles. This eventually restricts airflow and reduces system cooling capacity significantly.
  36. The correct answer is B (Send it for reclamation to verified purity, since it will be resold and reused off site.): Because the refrigerant will be reused at a different site for a different customer, it must go through reclamation to verified AHRI purity rather than simple on-site recycling, and this requirement applies regardless of the refrigerant's A2L flammability classification. A2L refrigerants are not exempt from reclamation requirements. Venting the recovered refrigerant and recharging with newly manufactured product is both prohibited and unnecessary when the recovered charge can be properly reclaimed and reused.
  37. The correct answer is D (Superheated discharge gas is used to transfer waste heat to water before final condensing): Superheated discharge gas is used to transfer waste heat to water before final condensing. A desuperheater is a small heat exchanger placed in the hot discharge line ahead of the main condenser, where the high-temperature superheated gas gives up recoverable heat to a domestic hot water loop before the refrigerant continues on to full condensing. This recovers heat that the main condenser would otherwise reject to outdoor air or a cooling tower, improving overall system efficiency at low added cost. Heat recovery can be paired with air-cooled, water-cooled or evaporative condensers, since it only intercepts a portion of the discharge line.
  38. The correct answer is A (The fire damper failure must block sign off, while the diffuser reading is acceptable as documented): A life safety device failing its functional test, such as a fire damper not closing, must be corrected and retested before sign off can be recommended, while the diffuser reading falls inside a tolerance band the consultant has already accepted and requires no further action. Treating both items as equally blocking ignores that one has already been approved within tolerance. Allowing both to pass would let a genuine life safety failure go undocumented before handover. Reversing the priority and blocking on the diffuser while ignoring the fire damper would leave an actual safety device unverified.
  39. The correct answer is D (R-32 has a substantially lower global warming potential than R-410A, which manufacturers want to reduce): R-32 carries a global warming potential of roughly one third that of R-410A, which makes it an attractive lower impact alternative as regulations increasingly restrict refrigerants with high GWP values. Both R-32 and R-410A have an ODP of essentially zero, since neither contains chlorine that would deplete stratospheric ozone. It is actually R-32 that carries the A2L mildly flammable safety classification, while R-410A is classified as A1, nonflammable, so option C has the classifications reversed.
  40. The correct answer is C (Auxiliary or emergency electric resistance heat strips inside the air handler): the W terminal energizes supplemental electric heat strips mounted in the air handler, used either as backup heat when the heat pump alone cannot maintain setpoint in cold weather or as full emergency heat if the outdoor unit is disabled. This is separate from the O or B terminal, which typically controls the reversing valve solenoid for switching between heating and cooling. The compressor contactor is instead controlled through the Y terminal circuit, and defrost bypass logic is handled by the equipment's own defrost control board.
  41. The correct answer is A (A core removal tool installed between the hose and the service port): a dedicated core removal tool threads onto the service port and allows the technician to remove or reinstall the Schrader core through the hose connection itself, without having to break the seal or let system pressure escape uncontrolled. A flat blade screwdriver as in option B or vice grips as in option C are not designed for this purpose and risk damaging the valve stem or the port threads. A tubing cutter as in option D has no application to removing a Schrader core.
  42. The correct answer is B (Purge the line with dry nitrogen while brazing to prevent internal oxide scale): Flowing dry nitrogen through the copper line during brazing displaces the oxygen inside the tubing, preventing the black oxide scale that forms when copper is heated in open air. This scale can later break loose and contaminate the filter drier, compressor, or metering device if it is allowed to form during the repair. Brazing without purging risks introducing exactly this kind of internal contamination into a freshly repaired system. Leaving refrigerant in the line during brazing is unsafe and is never an appropriate substitute for proper nitrogen purging.
  43. The correct answer is D (A licensed reclamation facility that verifies purity through laboratory analysis.): Only a licensed reclaimer, operating a facility equipped to test and reprocess refrigerant against AHRI Standard 700, is authorized to certify recovered refrigerant as reclaimed product for resale to another party. A field technician's handling certification permits recovery and service work but not reclamation certification. Wholesalers and original equipment manufacturers are not automatically authorized reclaimers simply by virtue of selling or producing the equipment.
  44. The correct answer is C (Within sight of the equipment it serves and readily accessible for use): Within sight of the equipment it serves and readily accessible for use. CSA C22.1 requires a disconnecting means to be located within sight of the equipment it supplies and to be readily accessible, so a technician can see that the equipment is de-energized and locked out before working on it. This visual confirmation requirement is a key safety principle behind disconnect placement rules, rather than simply requiring any weatherproof enclosure somewhere on the roof. Mounting the disconnect too far from the unit, or where it is obstructed from view, would not meet this code requirement.
  45. The correct answer is B (Cleaning removes oxidation and oils so molten alloy can properly wet the surfaces.): removing surface oxidation and oils with an abrasive cloth exposes clean base metal so the molten brazing alloy can wet and flow into the joint by capillary action, which is essential for a sound, leak free braze. Skin oils left by bare fingers can also contaminate the surface and interfere with proper alloy flow. The abrasive cloth does not enlarge the tube diameter, and while moisture on the surface is undesirable, oxidation and oil contamination are the primary reasons for this step.
  46. The correct answer is A (Approximately 9.6 kilowatts of sensible heat output from the coil): Sensible heat equals mass flow rate multiplied by specific heat multiplied by temperature change, so 1.2 kg/s times 1.005 kJ/(kg C) times 8 C equals 9.65 kW, which rounds to 9.6 kW. The other values result from using the wrong mass flow rate or an incomplete multiplication of the three terms. Always confirm all three quantities, mass flow, specific heat, and temperature change, are entered before multiplying.
  47. The correct answer is D (Adhesive on both surfaces creates a continuous, complete bond that resists vapour migration.): applying contact adhesive to both mating surfaces of a seam or butt joint creates a complete, continuous bond across the full seam rather than a partial bond that could leave gaps for moisture vapour to penetrate. A gap at even a small section of seam can compromise the vapour barrier for the whole insulated run. This practice applies to both liquid and suction line insulation, not suction lines exclusively, and it strengthens rather than weakens the finished bond despite taking a bit more time.
  48. A clean visual exterior does not substitute for a current hydrostatic test stamp, since internal integrity cannot be confirmed by appearance alone. The cylinder must be retested and restamped before it can legally be returned to recovery service.
  49. The correct answer is A (The regulation was expanded to also cover HFC alternatives adopted to replace ozone-depleting refrigerants.): The regulation's scope was expanded specifically to cover halocarbon alternatives, meaning HFC refrigerants that were adopted as replacements for ozone-depleting substances, even though HFCs themselves do not deplete ozone. HFCs do not actually deplete the ozone layer, so that is not the reason for their inclusion. This inclusion reflects a deliberate regulatory scope decision rather than a drafting error, and halocarbon alternatives are not limited to refrigerants never used in Canada.
  50. The correct answer is D (The line must be sized to limit pressure drop while still carrying oil at adequate velocity): a discharge line, like a suction line, must be sized to keep pressure drop within an acceptable limit so it does not unnecessarily raise compressor work and discharge temperature, while still maintaining enough velocity to carry oil through the horizontal run and up the vertical rise toward the condenser. Discharge and suction lines operate at different pressures and typically require different diameters, so matching them directly is not correct sizing practice. Oversizing the discharge line as much as possible reduces pressure drop but can drop velocity below the level needed for reliable oil transport, particularly on the vertical rise. Excessive discharge pressure drop does meaningfully raise discharge temperature and compressor energy use, so it cannot be dismissed as negligible.
  51. The correct answer is D (A higher-than-chart static pressure at that ambient temperature suggests non-condensables are present.): With the system off long enough to reach a stable temperature equal to the surrounding ambient air, the refrigerant's static pressure should match the pressure-temperature chart value for that exact ambient temperature if only pure refrigerant is present in the system. Non-condensable gases such as trapped air do not change phase with the refrigerant and instead add their own partial pressure on top of the refrigerant's expected saturation pressure. This causes the measured static pressure to read higher than the chart predicts for that temperature, which is a recognized diagnostic sign of contamination. Confirming this finding typically leads to recovering the refrigerant, evacuating the system properly with a vacuum pump, and recharging with a clean, verified refrigerant charge.
  52. The correct answer is A (Superheat calculates to 14 C, higher than typical target values, suggesting the system may be undercharged): Subtracting the metered state's 4 C from the suction state's 18 C gives 14 C of superheat, which sits above the typical 5 to 10 C target range most manufacturers specify, and elevated superheat like this commonly points toward an undercharged system or a restricted metering device limiting refrigerant flow. A properly charged system would normally show superheat closer to the target range rather than well above it. Both temperature values needed for the calculation are clearly provided on the diagram.
  53. The correct answer is C (The discharge and liquid state points share the same high pressure value shown.): The diagram shows both the discharge and liquid state points at approximately 1900 kPa, since the compressor raises the refrigerant to this pressure and the condenser only removes heat without significantly changing that high side pressure. The refrigerant temperature drops considerably between these two points as heat is rejected to the outdoor air, but the pressure stays essentially constant along that path. The metered and suction points do share a common low pressure value near 480 kPa, but that pair belongs to the low pressure side connected through the evaporator, not the high side condenser path asked about here. Recognizing which state points share pressure helps a technician quickly locate where a suspected restriction or pressure drop is actually occurring in a real system.
  54. The correct answer is A (VFD modulation typically improves part-load efficiency compared to simple on-off staging): a variable frequency drive allows a scroll compressor to continuously adjust its speed to match the actual load, avoiding the repeated start losses and less precise matching inherent in cycling fixed-speed units fully on and off, which generally improves part-load efficiency over a typical operating year. Staged fixed-speed compressors do not automatically outperform a VFD option. VFDs are used on Group A1 refrigerant circuits regularly, and the two approaches are not identical in part-load performance.
  55. The correct answer is B (The internal discharge or suction valve reeds have failed mechanically.): Normal amperage and electrically sound windings rule out an electrical fault, while pressures that collapse toward each other combined with an audible valve clatter point to a mechanical failure of the compressor's internal valve reeds. Failed reeds allow gas to leak back past the piston or scroll during compression, reducing capacity without affecting electrical draw. An electrical short would typically trip an overload rather than produce this specific pressure and noise pattern, and a stuck closed TXV would instead produce very low suction pressure with normal compression ratio.
  56. Subtracting this 4°C saturation temperature from the measured 12°C suction line temperature gives a superheat value of 8°C. This confirms the refrigerant has fully evaporated inside the coil and picked up additional sensible heat before reaching the compressor inlet. Because R-32 runs at higher pressures than refrigerants like R-410A at equivalent temperatures, using the correct refrigerant-specific chart is essential to avoid a miscalculated superheat value.
  57. Adequate clearance around the unit ensures the condenser fan can pull sufficient air across the coil without recirculating already-warmed discharge air.

  59. The correct answer is A (Oxygen level first, then flammability, then toxic gas concentrations): oxygen is checked first because both the flammability and toxicity readings on most multi gas meters depend on a normal oxygen level to be accurate, so an oxygen deficient or enriched atmosphere must be identified before the other sensors can be trusted. Testing flammability or toxicity first, as in options B and C, risks getting a false reading if the oxygen level is already abnormal. Option D is incorrect because a defined sequence, not a simultaneous or random check, is the accepted practice for confined space entry testing.
  60. The correct answer is C (A2L refrigerant is mildly flammable, so ignition sources and intake proximity still matter outdoors): A2L refrigerant is mildly flammable, so ignition sources and intake proximity still matter outdoors. Because A2L refrigerants carry a lower flammability rating rather than being non-flammable, CSA B52 and manufacturer guidance still require attention to ignition source separation and proximity to building openings such as fresh air intakes even for outdoor equipment, since a leak could still create a locally flammable concentration near an ignition source or draw into occupied space through the intake. Outdoor placement reduces some risks compared to an enclosed machine room but does not exempt the installation from these siting considerations. The classification and its associated requirements apply based on refrigerant properties, not simply on whether the equipment sits indoors or outdoors.
  61. The correct answer is C (Fully back-seated open, so refrigerant can flow freely through both service ports): back-seating both the liquid and suction valves fully opens the line to the system and closes off the gauge port, letting refrigerant circulate without restriction. Starting the compressor against a front-seated or partially seated valve deadheads the line and can spike discharge pressure to a damaging level within seconds. A quarter-turn crack or mid-seated position still restricts flow and is never correct for normal running. Every pre-startup checklist confirms both valves are fully back-seated before the contactor is ever energized.
  62. The correct answer is D (The contactor's load side power contacts, which can be pitted despite a healthy coil): A contactor coil can pull in normally on the 24 volt control side while its separate load side power contacts have become pitted or burned, intermittently failing to pass line voltage through to the compressor even though the coil circuit shows nothing wrong. The transformer secondary and the thermostat contact both belong to the control circuit already proven functional by the energized coil. A low pressure switch wired into the control circuit would prevent the coil itself from energizing if it were the fault, which contradicts the confirmed energized coil. The load side contacts are the one component in the power path not yet verified.
  63. The correct answer is A (Approximately 2880 watts, multiplying 240 volts by 12 amps): power equals voltage multiplied by current, so P = 240V × 12A = 2880W, which is the apparent power drawn by the circuit. This calculation assumes a purely resistive relationship for simplicity, while an inductive motor load technically also involves a power factor that slightly changes the true power figure. Comparing a measured current reading against the compressor's rated load amps is a routine troubleshooting check.
  64. The correct answer is D (45°C, read directly from the R-134a saturation table at that pressure): A standard R-134a pressure-temperature chart lists approximately 1059 kPa gauge as the saturation pressure corresponding to a 45°C condensing temperature. Because refrigerant condensing inside the coil exists as a liquid and vapour mixture, its pressure and temperature stay locked together at this fixed value until the refrigerant is either fully condensed or fully vaporized. This reading becomes the starting point for later calculating subcooling once the liquid line temperature is also measured.
  65. The correct answer is C (The actual liquid line temperature, measured with a clamp-on thermometer at that location): subcooling requires two values, the saturated condensing temperature derived from the high side pressure shown on the gauge, and the actual liquid line temperature measured directly with a clamp-on thermometer near the condenser outlet or receiver. Subtracting the liquid line temperature from the saturation temperature gives the subcooling value used to judge the charge. Compressor amperage, distant ambient air temperature, and evaporator fan speed are all useful commissioning data points but none of them are part of the subcooling calculation itself.
  66. The exhibit lists 0 kg for that line, consistent with proper recovery practices across the reporting period. Purchased, recovered, and systems serviced figures are expected to be positive operational numbers and do not need to equal zero.

Floor area has no bearing on hydronic flow rate through matched piping and coils. Closing an already fully open valve further would only reduce flow, worsening the shortfall. Thermostat setpoint affects the control signal to a valve, not the maximum hydraulic capacity of the branch.
  68.   69. The correct answer is D (Invert the cylinder or use its liquid valve so refrigerant leaves as a liquid): Blended refrigerants must always be charged into a system as a liquid, either by inverting the cylinder or using a dedicated liquid port, so that the full mixture of components leaves the cylinder together in its original ratio. Withdrawing a blend as vapour lets the more volatile component boil off first, leaving the remaining liquid in the cylinder with a different composition, a problem called fractionation. Applying heat directly to a refrigerant cylinder with a torch is also a serious safety hazard and should never be done.
  70. This written record is what allows the facility to demonstrate compliance if an enforcement officer later requests evidence of proper handling. A verbal confirmation or a photograph of the empty shell alone does not provide traceable, verifiable documentation.
