Estimating volumes without transfer documentation leaves that traceability broken. The fifty kilogram cylinder size and manufacturer status of the company are not the basis for this recordkeeping expectation.
  72. The correct answer is D (To provide a reference point for diagnosing future performance changes during service calls): baseline readings taken at commissioning, when the system is known to be running correctly, give a technician on a later service call a trustworthy comparison point to spot a developing fault such as a slow leak or a fouling condenser. Filing the report is not the goal in itself, since the recorded values are meant to be actively used later. The data does not retroactively determine the charge that should have been weighed in, and warranty periods are set contractually, not calculated from amperage readings.
  73. The correct answer is D (Approximately 20 amps, dividing 240 volts by 12 ohms): applying Ohm's Law, current equals voltage divided by resistance, so I = 240V ÷ 12Ω = 20A. This simplified calculation ignores the start capacitor's reactance and is only an approximation, but it illustrates why start windings briefly draw significant inrush current. In practice, the start capacitor and relay circuit shape this current, and the winding is only in circuit for a fraction of a second.
  74. The correct answer is B (The subcooling value to record on the ticket is approximately 9 degrees Celsius): Subcooling is the saturated condensing temperature minus the actual liquid line temperature, so 38 C minus 29 C equals 9 C. This positive subcooling value confirms the liquid leaving the receiver is safely below its boiling point at that pressure before reaching the metering device. Reversing the subtraction or using only one of the two readings produces each of the incorrect distractor values.
  75. The correct answer is C (Pull a trend log of the setpoint and actual value over a full 24 hour period): A trend log pulled over a full 24 hour period shows how the reset setpoint actually tracked against outdoor or load conditions throughout the day, confirming the sequence responds correctly across its full operating range rather than at a single moment. A single spot reading at noon cannot capture how the reset behaves during morning warm up or evening setback periods. Comfort complaints are indirect anecdotal evidence and do not confirm the actual programmed sequence logic. Reviewing only the design narrative confirms intent but not the installed system's real behaviour.
  76. The correct answer is C (Deliberate venting is prohibited regardless of job duration or system size): The prohibition on deliberate venting applies to every job regardless of how quick the repair is or how small the system's charge may be, which is why recovery is standard practice before any line is opened. Option A incorrectly dismisses this as mere shop preference rather than a real regulatory requirement. Option B and option D invent size and date thresholds that do not exist in the regulation.
  77. The correct answer is C (A trapped smaller riser stays oil sealed at low load, forcing velocity through it too.): a double riser arrangement uses a smaller diameter riser sized for minimum load velocity alongside a larger riser sized for full load velocity, with the smaller riser trapped so an oil seal forms and closes it off at low load, forcing all flow and velocity through the smaller pipe at that condition. At full load the oil seal in the small riser breaks and both risers carry flow together. This design keeps oil carrying velocity adequate across the whole staging range rather than at just one load point, which a single riser sized for one condition cannot do.
  78. The correct answer is D (Pump impeller condition and coupling alignment, since wear can reduce actual delivered head): A pump reading below its rated curve with air already ruled out commonly points to a worn or damaged impeller, slipping coupling, or misalignment reducing the mechanical energy actually transferred to the water. Breaker sizing affects whether the motor can run at all, not the hydraulic head it produces once running. Thermostat setpoint controls calls for heating or cooling, not the pump's hydraulic performance. Pipe insulation colour has no relationship to internal system pressure or pump output.
  79. The correct answer is C (Each saturation temperature corresponds to one specific saturation pressure for that refrigerant): For any pure refrigerant, saturation pressure and saturation temperature are fixed to each other, so measuring one on a gauge lets a technician find the other on a pressure-temperature chart. This relationship breaks down once the refrigerant leaves the two-phase saturated region and becomes fully superheated vapour or fully subcooled liquid. Saturation pressure rises, not falls, as saturation temperature increases for essentially every common refrigerant.
  80. The correct answer is A (Approximately 101 watts, multiplying 240 volts by 0.42 amps): power equals voltage multiplied by current, so P = 240V × 0.42A = 100.8W, which rounds to approximately 101 watts. This wattage is typical for a small crankcase heater designed to keep refrigerant from migrating into the oil during the off cycle. A reading far outside this range during troubleshooting would suggest a failing heater element or an incorrect replacement part.
  81. The correct answer is C (Flaring the tube end to the standard 45 degree SAE angle): A flaring tool clamps the tube and forms the end into a cone that matches the mating flare fitting, and the flare nut then draws the two cones together to make a metal-to-metal seal without solder or braze filler. Refrigeration flares are cut to 45 degrees, distinct from the 37 degree flare used in some hydraulic fittings.
  82. The correct answer is D (A loose sensing bulb senses warmer ambient air instead of the cold suction line, causing the valve to overfeed the evaporator): A TXV bulb that is not making tight, well-insulated contact with the suction line senses the surrounding air, which is warmer than the actual line temperature, so the valve reads this as high superheat and opens further than the load requires, flooding the evaporator with excess refrigerant and producing exactly this pattern of high suction pressure paired with low superheat. Charge and airflow being correct actually points investigation toward the metering device control itself rather than ruling it out. A leaking discharge valve reduces compression efficiency and typically pulls suction and discharge pressures toward each other rather than raising suction pressure this way. Condenser fan speed has no direct link to suction side pressure through the metering device.
  83. The correct answer is B (Permanent Split Capacitor, a motor using a run capacitor continuously): a PSC motor keeps its run capacitor wired in series with the start winding for the entire time the motor runs, which improves running efficiency and torque. Unlike a capacitor-start motor, a PSC motor has no start switch or relay to disconnect the capacitor once running speed is reached. This design is common on condenser fan motors and smaller PSC compressors because of its simplicity and reliability. The other terms do not correspond to any recognized motor nameplate designation.
  84. The correct answer is D (One quarter to one half of the sight glass visible, without excessive foaming present): most semi-hermetic compressors are designed to run with the oil level sitting roughly one quarter to one half up the sight glass, with only light, momentary foaming visible on startup. A completely full sight glass under running conditions can indicate refrigerant migrating into the crankcase and diluting the oil. An empty sight glass signals a serious lubrication problem, and constant heavy foaming throughout operation points to flooding or refrigerant contamination rather than a healthy oil level.
  85. The correct answer is D (Every single device on that rung must be closed at the same time): Every single device on that rung must be closed at the same time. In a series rung, current can only flow from one rail to the other if every device along that single path is closed, since an open contact anywhere breaks the entire circuit. This is why safety switches like high and low pressure switches are deliberately placed in series with the thermostat contact rather than on a separate parallel rung. If any one device opens, whether from a safety trip or a normal thermostat cycle end, the contactor coil immediately de-energizes.
  86. The correct answer is B (Stop using the instrument, verify it against a currently calibrated unit, and flag affected readings): An expired calibration means the instrument's accuracy can no longer be guaranteed, so the technician must stop using it, cross check it against a known good instrument, and flag any readings collected while it was out of calibration for review or retesting. Internal consistency among readings does not prove absolute accuracy, since an instrument can drift uniformly and still read consistently wrong. Permanently discarding the instrument model outright is an overreaction before it has even been verified against a reference. Calibration stickers document a real accuracy requirement, not an administrative formality that can be ignored.
  87. The correct answer is C (Check both gauge sets against a known reference to confirm which one is calibrated correctly): When two instruments disagree on the same reading at the same point, the first step is always to determine which instrument, if either, is actually accurate by comparing both against a known calibrated reference. Averaging the readings or adding refrigerant based on an unverified number risks making an incorrect adjustment to a system that may already be correctly charged. Only after confirming instrument accuracy should any troubleshooting of the actual refrigerant charge begin.
  88. The correct answer is D (Reject the smaller fan, since exhaust rate must meet or exceed the calculated CSA B52 value): the exhaust rate calculated from the CSA B52 formula based on refrigerant charge and room characteristics is a code requirement, not a suggestion, so the installed fan must be sized to meet or exceed that calculated value. Substituting a smaller fan already on hand to save cost would leave the machine room under ventilated in the event of a refrigerant leak, defeating the purpose of the requirement. A manually operable window is not an acceptable substitute for the required mechanical ventilation rate, and altering the reported refrigerant charge on paper to justify a smaller fan misrepresents the actual installation and is not an acceptable practice. The mechanic must require the contractor to install a fan that meets the calculated rate.
  89. The correct answer is C (It varies the output frequency supplied to the motor to change speed): a variable frequency drive converts incoming AC power to DC and then back to AC at a controllable frequency, and since an AC induction motor's synchronous speed is directly tied to the supply frequency, varying that frequency varies the motor's operating speed. Voltage is also adjusted along with frequency to maintain proper motor performance across the speed range, but frequency control is the core operating principle. The drive does not bypass the motor windings or convert the output to single-phase power.
  90. The correct answer is D (The proximity to the intake and operable window creates a recirculation or release exposure concern): placing a condensing unit directly beneath an operable window and next to the fresh air intake risks discharge air recirculation into the building and creates a pathway for any refrigerant release to enter occupied space or the ventilation system, which site assessment under CSA B52 requires the mechanic to evaluate before installation. Sound level compliance alone does not address this separate exposure concern. Rooftop units are not automatically exempt from placement review, and intake and release exposure concerns apply across refrigerant types, not only ammonia.
  91. The correct answer is B (Scroll compressor, using orbiting spirals with continuous smooth compression): a scroll compressor uses two interleaved spiral scrolls, one fixed and one orbiting, that trap and progressively compress gas pockets moving toward the center, producing smooth continuous compression with fewer moving parts and lower vibration than a piston design. Screw compressors use meshing helical rotors and serve larger capacities. Reciprocating compressors use pistons in cylinders, and rotary vane compressors are limited to small fractional loads, neither matching this description.
  92. The correct answer is A (To document verified system performance against design intent as a permanent project record): The commissioning report compiles and organizes the raw field readings into a formal record proving the systems were tested and perform as designed, which becomes part of the permanent project documentation for the owner. It does not replace manufacturer warranty paperwork, which is a separate legal document tied to the equipment itself. It is not a marketing piece describing equipment brands to the client. It is a required closeout deliverable on most commissioned projects, not an optional formality.
  93. The correct answer is D (Operation and maintenance manuals covering every major piece of installed equipment on the project): Operation and maintenance manuals, commonly called O&M manuals, are the standard document set handed over at project completion so maintenance staff can properly operate, service, and troubleshoot every major piece of equipment going forward. Payroll records are internal contractor business documents and have no place in an owner's operating documentation. A blank template with no project specific content provides no useful information to maintenance staff. Supplier marketing brochures describe products generally but do not substitute for actual equipment specific operation and maintenance instructions.
  94. The correct answer is D (The cylinder should not exceed 80 percent of its rated capacity by weight): Standard practice limits liquid refrigerant fill to 80 percent of a cylinder's rated capacity by weight, leaving vapor space to accommodate thermal expansion and prevent hydrostatic overpressure. Option A describes a completely full cylinder, which creates a serious overpressure hazard. Option B is overly conservative and understates the actual accepted limit. Option C exceeds the safe margin needed for thermal expansion.
  95. The correct answer is D (Turn the TXV adjustment stem counterclockwise in small increments to increase refrigerant feed to the coil): on most thermostatic expansion valves, turning the adjustment stem counterclockwise reduces spring tension, letting the valve open further at a lower superheat and feeding more refrigerant to bring superheat down toward the target range. Turning it clockwise increases spring tension and raises the superheat required before the valve opens, which would push superheat even higher rather than lowering it. Adding charge when subcooling already reads correctly risks overcharging the system, and a TXV-equipped system has no fixed orifice piston to replace.
  96. The correct answer is C (to prevent sagging and to keep stress off the fittings at each end of the run): copper tubing has enough flexibility that an unsupported horizontal run will sag between two widely spaced points, which puts continuous bending stress on the brazed or flared connections at each end and can eventually cause a leak. Hangers spaced along the run at intervals appropriate to the tube size keep the line straight and distribute the weight so no single joint bears the load. Hangers have no role in refrigerant velocity or turbulence, and insulation quantity is determined by tube length and diameter rather than by support spacing.
  97. The correct answer is D (scan each joint with an electronic leak detector or apply bubble solution and watch for bubbles): a stable overall gauge reading confirms there is no significant total leak rate, but a very small leak can sometimes be masked by ambient temperature changes affecting the test pressure slightly. Directly scanning each joint with an electronic sniffer or applying bubble solution and watching for bubbles pinpoints even a very small standing leak at its exact location. Raising pressure beyond the rated test pressure risks damaging a joint or a component, and reheating a completed brazed joint is not a leak test method and can itself cause new defects. Moving straight to evacuation skips confirming joint-by-joint integrity first.
  98. The correct answer is D (Existing recovered, recycled, or reclaimed R-22 already in the Canadian supply chain.): Since new production and import of R-22 for use in Canada has stopped, existing equipment can only be serviced using R-22 that has already been recovered, recycled, or reclaimed from other systems. Retrofitting an R-22 system to an A2L blend without engineering approval and component changes is unsafe and not a simple direct substitution. Unlabelled substitute blends of unknown composition and purity should never be charged into a system without proper identification.
  99. The correct answer is B (The tube should protrude a small amount, roughly the thickness of the flare.): the tube end should extend above the flaring block by an amount roughly equal to the thickness the finished flare needs, rather than a large or specific fixed dimension for every tube size. Too little protrusion produces an undersized flare that can leak, while too much protrusion produces an oversized or cracked flare. A flush or below flush setting will not allow the flaring tool to form a proper 45 degree flare at all.
  100. The correct answer is C (Conduction, because heat travels through a solid material by direct molecular contact): Conduction is the transfer of heat through a solid object as faster moving molecules pass energy to their neighbours without the material itself moving. The copper tubing wall does not change state or move, so neither latent transfer nor convection applies to that portion of the path. Convection instead describes the refrigerant vapour carrying heat to the inner wall, and the shop air carrying heat away from the outer wall.
  101. The correct answer is B (7°C of subcooling, found by subtracting the liquid line reading from the saturated condensing temperature): Subcooling equals the saturated condensing temperature corresponding to the high side pressure, minus the actual liquid line temperature, so 48°C minus 41°C equals 7°C. This calculation works the same way for R-1234yf as it does for any other refrigerant, since subcooling is a general concept based on the pressure-temperature relationship rather than a property unique to one refrigerant. Reporting a raw temperature alone, as in options A and D, fails to answer what subcooling actually represents.
  102. The correct answer is D (An oversized or failed metering device allowing excess liquid through.): High suction pressure combined with superheat near zero at the compressor inlet is the signature of refrigerant flooding, where liquid rather than fully vaporized gas is returning to the compressor. An oversized or stuck-open metering device feeds far more liquid to the evaporator than it can boil off, matching both readings exactly. A restricted metering device or undercharge would instead produce low suction pressure and high superheat, the opposite pattern known as starving. A dirty condenser primarily affects head pressure, not this suction side signature.
  103. The correct answer is B (Apply steady, smooth pressure and stop pulling immediately once it clicks): A click type torque wrench is designed to be pulled with smooth, steady pressure, and the technician should stop applying force the instant the click is felt to avoid exceeding the calibrated target torque. Option A describes a jerking motion that can overshoot the actual torque value before the mechanism has a chance to register it accurately. Option C describes continuing past the click, which overtightens the fastener beyond the intended specification. Option D describes striking the wrench, which is an unsafe and inaccurate way to apply torque and can damage the tool.
  104. The correct answer is B (Meter liquid slowly through a commercial charging device into the high side liquid line): a metering charging device throttles liquid refrigerant down to a controlled trickle, allowing it to be added safely into the high side liquid line of a running system without flooding the compressor, while still preserving the blend's liquid composition. Drawing vapour off the top of a zeotropic blend cylinder risks fractionation and shifts the mixture ratio left in the cylinder. Adding straight liquid into the low side suction port risks compressor slugging, and switching refrigerants mid-job is never an acceptable substitute for a correct trim procedure.
  105. The correct answer is A (A perimeter guardrail system, which protects all workers without individual gear): When workers move frequently and close to an unguarded edge throughout the day, a physical guardrail system provides passive protection for everyone on the roof without depending on each individual worker to correctly wear and anchor personal fall arrest equipment every time. Option B places fall arrest as the first choice, but hazard control hierarchy favours a passive engineering control like a guardrail over personal protective equipment whenever practical. Option C is unsafe since fall protection equipment must be individually fitted and cannot be shared simultaneously. Option D relies on behaviour alone, which is the least reliable control for a physical fall hazard.
  106. The correct answer is A (The difference between saturated condensing temperature and actual liquid line temperature.): Subcooling is found by reading the saturated condensing temperature that corresponds to the high side gauge pressure from a pressure-temperature chart, then subtracting the actual temperature measured on the liquid line near the condenser outlet or at the service valve. This tells the technician how much additional heat has been removed from the liquid refrigerant below its condensing point before it reaches the metering device. Ambient, suction, and discharge line temperatures are not part of this specific calculation.
  107. The correct answer is B (SEER averages seasonal part-load performance, while EER reflects efficiency at a single steady full-load condition): SEER is calculated from a seasonal model that averages efficiency across a range of part-load and outdoor conditions typical of residential cycling operation, while EER is measured at one fixed full-load condition, which better represents equipment that runs continuously near full load like a data centre cooling system. The first distractor reverses which rating is the seasonal average and which is the fixed-condition figure. SEER and EER use different test conditions and are not interchangeable, and both are cooling metrics rather than heating-only metrics.
  108. The correct answer is B (It allows certified tradespeople to work across Canada without re-examination): the Red Seal program exists specifically so a tradesperson who passes the interprovincial exam can have their certification recognized in other participating provinces and territories without writing another certification exam, supporting labour mobility across Canada. Option A is incorrect because the exam does not replace required apprenticeship hours, only supplements the certification process after they are completed. Option C confuses trade certification with a separate business licensing process, and option D describes the opposite of the Red Seal's actual interprovincial purpose.
  109. The correct answer is B (Scroll compressors have fewer moving parts and continuous compression, giving smoother operation and higher efficiency): a scroll compressor uses two interleaving spiral scrolls that compress refrigerant continuously as gas moves toward the center, rather than the intermittent stroke of a piston. This design has far fewer moving parts than a reciprocating compressor, which reduces vibration, noise, and mechanical wear points. The continuous compression cycle also improves volumetric efficiency at the capacities common in residential and light commercial equipment. Scroll compressors are not immune to liquid migration, but they tolerate occasional slugging somewhat better than a reciprocating design.
  110. The correct answer is A (Over-greasing forced excess grease past the seal and raised internal bearing operating temperature): Adding grease far more often than specified overfills the bearing cavity, and the churning excess grease generates heat that breaks down lubrication and pushes past the seal, which explains the hardened grease found at the seal. Following the correct interval, not shortening it, is the standard practice that prevents this. There is no indication moisture entered through the grease gun itself. Manufacturer intervals are set from testing and are not a universal underestimate.
  111. The correct answer is C (Essential skills such as reading comprehension, numeracy, and problem solving): Red Seal exams are built around realistic workplace scenarios that inherently draw on essential skills like reading comprehension, numeracy, and problem solving alongside the specific technical trade knowledge being tested. Option A describes a specialized skill set that is not a general component underlying Red Seal exams across trades. Option B describes a communication skill that is not directly assessed through the multiple choice exam format. Option D is not a requirement of the Red Seal program, which is conducted in English or French but does not mandate additional foreign language proficiency.
  112. The correct answer is B (No, the pressure corresponds to a condensing temperature far above the normal range for this ambient): The gauge reads 2658 kPa, which corresponds to approximately 45°C on an R-410A saturation chart, and 45°C minus 27°C ambient leaves an 18°C split that exceeds the expected 10 to 15°C range. A split like this points toward a restricted or dirty condenser coil, a failed condenser fan, or an overcharged system pushing extra liquid into the coil. Option C incorrectly applies this high side reading to the evaporating temperature, which is a low side concept.
  113. The correct answer is A (This unit's cooling capacity is approximately 26.4 kilowatts overall): Multiplying the rated tonnage by the standard conversion factor gives 7.5 tons times 3.517 kW per ton, which equals approximately 26.4 kW. The other values come from using an incorrect tonnage figure or an incorrect conversion factor during the multiplication. This conversion lets a technician compare a unit's rated tonnage directly against metric load calculations performed in kilowatts.
  114. The correct answer is C (Sufficient clearance must prevent discharge air recirculation back into the condenser inlet airflow): an air-cooled condenser draws in cooler outdoor air and discharges warmer air after passing across the coil, and inadequate clearance near an adjacent wall allows that discharge air to recirculate back into the intake. Recirculation raises the effective entering air temperature, increasing head pressure and reducing capacity and efficiency, so clearance is a real performance requirement, not a cosmetic one. This clearance need applies at the equipment's air inlet and discharge locations, not only the front service panel, and it applies regardless of unit tonnage. A site assessment must confirm the available clearance meets both manufacturer and CSA B52 requirements before the unit location is finalized.
  115. The correct answer is C (The existing 30 A breaker is acceptable since it exceeds the minimum circuit ampacity): The existing 30 A breaker is acceptable since it exceeds the minimum circuit ampacity. The nameplate lists a minimum circuit ampacity of 24.1 A, which is the smallest conductor and overcurrent protection the manufacturer allows, and the maximum fuse size of 40 A is only an upper limit, not a requirement. A 30 A breaker falls between those two values so it satisfies the manufacturer's requirement without needing to be upsized. Locked rotor amps describes momentary starting current, not the continuous branch circuit sizing basis, so it is not the deciding figure here.
  116. The correct answer is D (closed-cell elastomeric foam has low enough moisture permeability that it typically needs no separate vapour barrier): the closed-cell structure of elastomeric foam insulation resists water vapour transmission on its own, which is why it is widely used on cold suction lines without an additional vapour barrier layer, provided seams are properly sealed with the correct adhesive. Fiberglass insulation is open and absorbent by comparison, so on a cold line it needs a continuous vapour barrier jacket to keep from wicking moisture, and unfaced fiberglass alone performs poorly in that role. The two insulation types clearly do not behave the same way on a moisture-exposed cold line.
  117. The correct answer is B (Never refill the cylinder, since non-refillable cylinders cannot legally be reused): Disposable, non-refillable recovery cylinders are designed and rated for one-time use only and cannot legally be refilled or requalified under Transport Canada requirements. Option A incorrectly suggests refilling followed by testing, but this cylinder type is never eligible for retesting. Option C and option D both wrongly assume refilling is permitted under some condition, which is not the case for this cylinder class.
  118. The correct answer is C (Confirmation that the recovery cylinder fill amount was actually verified): The exhibit shows every field completed except the recovery cylinder fill verification, which is marked as not recorded, leaving a gap in the documentation trail. Option A is incorrect because the service date is clearly present in the record. Option B is incorrect because the refrigerant type, R-410A, appears in both the recovered and added fields. Option D is incorrect because the equipment ID, RTU-07, is clearly identified.
  119. The correct answer is A (Excessive work hardening and a distorted, out of round tube end): forcing a cutter wheel through copper too aggressively in one pass work hardens the metal at the cut and can squeeze the tube out of round, making it harder to flare or fit properly afterward. The correct light, multiple pass technique actually produces the smooth cut described in option B, which is why aggressive cutting is the wrong approach. Discoloration in option C comes from heat during brazing, not from cutting, and the tube's temper rating printed on the coil in option D is unaffected by how the cut itself is made.
  120. The correct answer is C (Hangers are typically spaced at intervals of about 1.8 metres for this size.): a commonly followed guideline for horizontal copper tubing in this size range is support intervals of roughly 1.8 metres, which limits sag enough to avoid creating unwanted low points that could trap oil or liquid refrigerant along an otherwise sloped or level run. Supporting only at the ends of a long run allows excessive deflection between supports. Spacing tightens for smaller diameter tube and can open up somewhat for larger, heavier wall tube, so a single fixed number is not applied to every size.
  121. The correct answer is D (It is a variable-speed DC motor controlled by an internal microprocessor): an ECM converts incoming AC power to DC internally and uses electronic commutation to drive the motor, allowing precise, programmable speed control that a standard induction motor cannot achieve on its own. This design improves efficiency and airflow consistency across a wider range of operating conditions. Unlike a PSC motor, an ECM does not rely on a run capacitor to create phase shift for starting torque.
  122. The correct answer is C (Three-phase motors start smoother and run more efficiently at larger horsepower loads): three-phase power delivers three overlapping AC waveforms that produce a rotating magnetic field directly, giving smoother starting torque and higher efficiency than single-phase at larger horsepower. This is why most commercial and industrial compressors above a few horsepower use three-phase supply. Three-phase motors still need overload protection and utility rates are not inherently lower. Single-phase motors need extra starting components like capacitors precisely because they lack this natural rotating field.
  123. The correct answer is A (replace the rigid clamp with a cushioned, isolated clamp or hanger at that support point): a discharge line vibrates with the compressor's operating frequency, and clamping it tightly to a rigid structural member transmits that vibration directly into the building, where it can be heard or felt as a buzz. A cushioned or vibration-isolating clamp absorbs that motion instead of passing it through the mount. Tightening the existing rigid clamp further makes the direct metal-to-metal contact worse rather than better, and simply removing the support leaves the line unsupported, which risks fatigue at the nearby fittings. Moving the clamp closer to the compressor without changing its type would not solve a rigid-mounting problem.
  124. The correct answer is B (Check continuity between the common and run terminals with power off): with the compressor disconnected from power, an open internal protector shows infinite resistance, or no continuity, between the common terminal and the other winding terminals it protects. Allowing the compressor to cool for a period and retesting can confirm whether the protector was simply open on a thermal trip rather than permanently failed. Testing for a ground fault to the shell is a separate, unrelated diagnostic step.
  125. The correct answer is D (A check valve permitting flow in one direction and blocking reverse flow): the circle with a single directional arrow through a straight line is the standard P&ID symbol for a check valve, commonly placed after the compressor discharge to prevent reverse gas flow into the compressor during shutdown or when parallel compressors are staged. A relief valve is typically shown as a spring-loaded symbol venting to a header. A solenoid valve is shown as a rectangle with a coil, and a thermostatic expansion valve is shown as a triangle into a diaphragm body.

Practice Test 8 Answer Key

  1. The correct answer is C (Confirm clearances against the manufacturer's installation manual and CSA B52 minimums together): proper clearance planning requires checking both the specific unit's manufacturer instructions, which vary by model and capacity, and the applicable CSA B52 minimums for the installation type, since the two sources can differ and the more restrictive requirement generally governs. Relying only on the framing layout ignores equipment-specific airflow needs. Assuming a fixed clearance regardless of unit size risks being inadequate for larger equipment, and deferring verification until after installation risks a costly rework.
  2. The correct answer is B (Documentation identifying any leak found and the repair performed): Proper recordkeeping practice documents any leak identified and the repair performed, since this information supports compliance review and demonstrates that refrigerant was not simply recharged into an unrepaired leak. Option A describes irrelevant personal equipment preferences. Option C and option D describe information with no bearing on refrigerant compliance recordkeeping.
  3. The correct answer is D (60 amps of current would flow through the winding): Ohm's Law states I = V ÷ R, so dividing 240 volts by 4 ohms gives 60 amps. This calculation approximates the kind of locked rotor amperage a compressor can briefly draw when the motor is not yet turning and generating counter electromotive force. Real running amperage is much lower once the motor is up to speed and developing back EMF. This is why compressors need starting components sized to handle a brief high inrush current safely.
  4. The correct answer is A (Modulating capacity avoids repeated start losses and better matches output to the varying part-load demand): a modulating unloader continuously adjusts compressor output to track the actual part-load demand, avoiding the electrical inrush and mechanical wear associated with repeated starts inherent in on-off cycling, and this closer output matching generally improves efficiency across the many hours a chiller spends at partial load. On-off cycling does not use less total energy simply because it runs at full efficiency briefly between cycles, since start losses and mismatch offset that benefit. The efficiency benefit of modulation applies broadly, not only to water-cooled systems, and the two approaches do not consume identical energy over a full year.
  5. The correct answer is C (At the low point of the line, immediately before the vertical rise begins): Placing the trap at the low point of the suction line, right where the piping turns upward, collects any oil and liquid that drains down the riser during the off cycle in the smallest, most effective pocket. From there, the next start-up sweeps the collected oil straight up the riser rather than leaving it to travel further along low horizontal runs first.
  6. The correct answer is C (Directly on a rigid bearing housing point on the compressor itself, firmly attached): For a reliable measurement of the compressor's own mechanical vibration, the accelerometer must be firmly attached to a rigid point close to a bearing housing on the compressor itself, minimizing signal loss and picking up the actual source vibration. Resting the sensor loosely against distant piping introduces damping and picks up transmitted vibration rather than the source. Mounting on roof decking measures building structure response, not the compressor's own condition. Sheet metal ductwork panels resonate independently and would distort the true vibration signature being measured.
  7. The correct answer is B (Latent heat changes state at constant temperature, sensible heat changes temperature without a state change): Latent heat is the energy absorbed or released as a substance changes phase, such as liquid refrigerant boiling into vapour, and the temperature stays constant during that change. Sensible heat, by contrast, raises or lowers temperature without any change in phase. The other options reverse this relationship or invent a rule with no basis in refrigeration theory.
  8. The correct answer is C (Zone 3, since its measured flow is roughly 35 percent below its design flow value): 2.0 minus 1.3 divided by 2.0 equals 35 percent, well past the 15 percent threshold, meaning zone 3 needs further investigation for a restriction or valve issue before sign off. Zone 1 is only 4 percent below design, comfortably inside tolerance. Zone 2 is only about 3 percent below design and does not need flagging. Zone 4 is within 3 percent of design flow and passes without further action.
  9. The correct answer is B (Both arms extended out to the sides with palms facing downward, swept back and forth horizontally): the standard Emergency Stop hand signal used in crane operations is the two-arm version of the routine single-arm Stop signal, with both arms extended, palms down, and swept back and forth horizontally, a large, unmistakable motion designed to be seen clearly and understood instantly by the operator. Option A describes the standard single-arm Stop signal used for a routine stop, not an emergency stop, so it does not provide the same urgent, unmistakable cue. A circular motion overhead as described in option C typically signals to hoist or raise the load, and thumbs up as in option D is not a recognized standard crane signal at all.
  10. The correct answer is C (Pilot duty switching, rated for a smaller low current control signal): a pilot duty relay is designed to switch a lighter control signal, such as energizing a contactor coil, rather than the full current of the load itself. The contactor then handles the actual line duty switching of the higher current compressor circuit. This staged arrangement lets a small, inexpensive relay control a much larger load safely. Confusing the two duty ratings and substituting a pilot duty relay directly into a line duty application would quickly damage the relay contacts.
  11. The correct answer is A (It steps down line voltage to a safe 24 volts for controls): the control transformer reduces the incoming line voltage, typically 120 or 240 volts, down to a nominal 24 volts used to safely power the thermostat, contactor coil, and other low voltage control components. Operating the control circuit at 24 volts reduces shock hazard and allows lighter gauge thermostat wiring to be used throughout the system. The transformer does not filter noise, convert current type, or increase voltage, and a shorted secondary is a common cause of a blown transformer fuse.
  12. The correct answer is A (A starved evaporator, consistent with low suction pressure paired with high superheat): Low suction pressure combined with high superheat is the defining pattern of a starved evaporator, meaning too little refrigerant is reaching the coil relative to the load, the opposite of a flooding evaporator which pairs high suction pressure with low or near-zero superheat. An overcharged system typically raises suction pressure rather than lowering it. A dirty condenser coil primarily drives head pressure up and does not directly explain this low suction, high superheat combination. The gauge reading and the superheat value are consistent with the same starved-evaporator fault.
  13. The correct answer is D (The low pressure switch has opened due to the low charge.): Voltage present ahead of the switch but absent after it means the open circuit is located at that switch, and a critically low refrigerant charge is exactly the condition that drops suction pressure enough to trip a low pressure safety switch open. This protects the compressor from operating without adequate refrigerant flow and lubrication return. A failed contactor coil or thermostat contact would show a different voltage pattern elsewhere on the rung, and a failed transformer would leave no voltage present anywhere on the circuit at all.
  14. The correct answer is A (to dilute and carry off residual moisture vapour the previous vacuum pull could not remove): a single evacuation cannot always remove every trace of moisture bound up in oil or trapped in tight spaces within the system. Breaking the vacuum with dry nitrogen introduces a moisture free gas that mixes with and dilutes any remaining moisture vapour, and pulling the vacuum again removes a further share of it, so repeating the cycle three times progressively reduces residual moisture to a very low level. This step is not a pressure test, is not primarily about pump cooling, and checking the gauge against atmospheric pressure is not its purpose.
  15. The correct answer is A (A reciprocating compressor, since it tolerates frequent cycling well across small to mid-range tonnage): reciprocating compressors use a piston and crankshaft arrangement that has been the standard choice for commercial refrigeration racks for decades, handling repeated starts and stops without excessive wear. They are readily available across a wide capacity range and are cost effective to service in the field. Centrifugal and screw compressors are built for large continuous industrial duty rather than frequent cycling at moderate tonnage. Rotary vane compressors are limited to very small fractional horsepower equipment, not a multi-case commercial rack.
  16. The correct answer is B (Electronically Commutated Motor, a DC brushless motor with a control module): an ECM motor uses an integrated electronic control module that converts incoming AC power to DC and electronically commutates the motor, rather than relying on a start or run capacitor like a traditional PSC motor. This design allows for variable speed operation and generally higher efficiency across a wider range of airflow demands. The control module itself is a common point of failure and should not be confused with a simple capacitor or contactor. The other three answer choices do not correspond to any real motor nameplate designation.
  17. The correct answer is B (The Red Seal endorsement, which is recognized across participating provinces and territories): The Red Seal is a nationally recognized standard added to a provincial certificate once a tradesperson passes the interprovincial exam, and it allows that certification to be recognized in other participating provinces and territories without additional testing. Option A describes a temporary permit system, which is not how Red Seal mobility works. Option C is incorrect because the Red Seal does not replace provincial certificates, it supplements them. Option D describes a private arrangement that has no bearing on the national trade certification system.
  18. The correct answer is A (it carries refrigerant gas only when capacity is high enough to lift trapped oil): in a dual riser arrangement the smaller riser is sized to maintain adequate gas velocity for oil entrainment at minimum system load. The larger riser has an oil trap at its base that stays sealed by trapped oil until capacity, and therefore gas velocity, rises enough to push that oil up and open flow through it. This lets the combined risers handle full load velocity without starving oil return at part load. It is not a defrost bypass and it does not open at the lowest compressor stage, which is the opposite of how the trap behaves.
  19. The correct answer is C (The receiver must hold the full system charge plus a vapour space margin): The receiver must hold the full system charge plus a vapour space margin. When a receiver is required to accept the entire system charge during a pump-down for service, its minimum internal volume must be large enough to hold that full liquid charge while still leaving a vapour space margin, typically not filling completely, so pressure does not build dangerously as remaining refrigerant condenses into it. Compressor horsepower and condenser face area influence system capacity and charge indirectly but are not themselves the direct sizing basis for receiver volume. CSA B52 does not mandate a single fixed receiver size for every system regardless of charge, since receiver sizing is charge and application specific.
  20. The correct answer is C (The refrigerant must be sent through reclamation to meet resale purity standards.): Recycled refrigerant is only accepted for continued use within the same system or by the same owner, since on-site recycling does not verify purity against the AHRI Standard 700 resale specification. Transferring refrigerant to an unrelated third party requires it first pass through reclamation, including laboratory analysis, at a licensed facility. Simply relabelling the cylinder or venting the refrigerant does nothing to satisfy this purity and ownership requirement.
  21. The correct answer is C (A written record of the quantity of refrigerant fully recovered from the system): Proper end-of-life decommissioning documentation should include a written record of the refrigerant quantity recovered, providing evidence that the system was emptied responsibly before disposal. Option A fails to create the durable record that compliance review requires. Option B and option D describe information unrelated to refrigerant handling during decommissioning.
  22. The correct answer is A (Terminals R and Y, since Y is the standard cooling call wire): the R terminal supplies 24 volt hot from the transformer, and the Y terminal is the industry standard wire that energizes the compressor contactor coil when the thermostat calls for cooling. The W terminal controls heating, and the G terminal controls the indoor fan independently of cooling. The C terminal simply provides the common return path needed to complete the low voltage circuit.
  23. The correct answer is B (Selecting equipment rated appropriately to avoid introducing an ignition source near residual vapour.): Because R-454B is mildly flammable, the vacuum pump selected for final evacuation must be rated appropriately to avoid introducing an ignition source near any residual refrigerant vapour remaining after recovery. Choosing the smallest available pump without regard to ignition source rating ignores this specific flammability consideration. Refrigerant flammability classification does affect equipment selection, and a pump rated only for A1 refrigerants does not provide equivalent protection for A2L work.
  24. The correct answer is D (Newer thermostats need a constant low voltage common to power their electronics): Newer thermostats need a constant low voltage common to power their electronics. Mechanical thermostats used a simple bimetallic switch that only needed momentary contact to signal a call, drawing no continuous power. Smart thermostats contain displays, wireless radios, and processors that require a steady return path to the transformer secondary through the C terminal to stay powered at all times. Without a C wire, some smart thermostats attempt to steal power through the call wires, which can cause erratic equipment operation.
  25. The correct answer is C (It must be chemically verified against AHRI Standard 700 by a licensed reclaimer.): Reclamation requires laboratory analysis confirming the refrigerant meets the purity specification set out in AHRI Standard 700 before it can be sold as reclaimed product. This reprocessing and verification must occur at a facility qualified to perform reclamation, not simply by any technician with a filter drier. A single on-site filtering pass only achieves recycling, which does not meet the resale purity standard.
  26. The correct answer is D (Recycling machines are not designed to separate different refrigerant types mixed together in one cylinder.): On-site recycling equipment reduces moisture, acid, and particulate contamination, but it is not designed to separate two different refrigerant types that have become mixed together, which is a distinct contamination problem recycling cannot correct. Claiming recycling machines separate mixed refrigerants as effectively as reclamation misstates their actual capability. Mixed refrigerant contamination is a recognized contamination type, and recycling machines operating on site do not themselves require laboratory certification to function.
  27. The correct answer is B (Rotary compressors are compact, low cost and efficient at the very small capacities involved): Rotary compressors are compact, low cost and efficient at the very small capacities involved. Rotary compressors, along with small reciprocating designs, are well suited to the very small fractional-ton capacities typical of window air conditioners and domestic refrigerators because they are compact, inexpensive to manufacture at that scale, and reasonably efficient for that size range. Scroll compressors are readily manufactured in small sizes as well and are used in many light commercial applications, so the claim that they cannot be made below ten tons is incorrect. Rotary compressors are not the only type compatible with any given refrigerant, and they do not inherently require more maintenance than a scroll compressor at a comparable size.
  28. The correct answer is D (The water-regulating valve has failed and needs to be replaced.): A water-regulating valve is designed to open further as head pressure rises in order to increase water flow and hold pressure at its set point, so a valve that stays partially closed despite high pressure is not responding correctly to that signal and has failed. Manually opening the valve and watching pressure immediately normalize confirms the valve itself, not the refrigerant charge, coil fouling, or compressor valves, was the restriction preventing adequate water flow. A charge or coil fouling issue would not resolve simply by manually repositioning this one valve.
  29. Option A wrongly assumes legality without evidence. Option B invents a self-certifying property that cylinders do not have. Option C describes an unsafe practice that does not resolve the documentation gap.
  30. The correct answer is A (The equipment must provide enough latent capacity, not just sensible cooling, given high occupant density): a busy dining room with close seating generates significant latent heat from occupant moisture, so the selected equipment must have adequate latent capacity in addition to sensible capacity, or humidity control will suffer even if the thermostat is satisfied. Ignoring latent load risks a clammy, uncomfortable space despite correct temperature. Refrigerant piping size is driven by total capacity and equipment selection, not sensible load alone, and sensible and latent loads are not generally equal in restaurant occupancies.
  31. The correct answer is D (Whether delivered supply airflow volume is sufficient to remove the actual peak zone load): Since discharge air temperature is correctly matching setpoint, the cooling coil and control loop are working, so the remaining variable is whether enough cool air volume is actually reaching the space to offset the real peak occupancy heat load, pointing toward an airflow or ductwork investigation. Sensor display font legibility has no bearing on actual thermal performance in the space. Decimal place formatting on a graphic is a cosmetic display detail, not a functional issue. Graphic background colour is purely cosmetic and irrelevant to actual system performance.
  32. The correct answer is B (Stay within the standard plus or minus six percent microfarad tolerance): run capacitors are manufactured to an industry accepted tolerance, typically within about six percent of the rated microfarad value, and a replacement should fall within that same range to maintain proper motor phase shift and running performance. Voltage rating should also be equal to or greater than the original, never lower. Using a capacitor with a significantly different microfarad rating can cause overheating or reduced motor torque.
  33. The correct answer is A (A thermostatic expansion valve metering liquid refrigerant into the evaporator): A thermostatic expansion valve metering liquid refrigerant into the evaporator. Expansion valves are commonly drawn as a circle or diamond with an angled restriction line to indicate the metering orifice, positioned in the liquid line immediately upstream of the evaporator where the pressure drop and flashing occur. Relief valves use a different spring-and-seat symbol typically shown venting to atmosphere or a relief header, and compressors are drawn as a larger shape with suction and discharge ports rather than a simple metering symbol. Recognizing this symbol and its correct location on the drawing is basic to reading any refrigeration P&ID.
  34. The correct answer is B (The specific tasks, visit frequency, and equipment covered under the agreement): A clear preventive maintenance contract protects both parties by spelling out exactly which units are covered, what tasks will be performed at each visit, and how often those visits occur, leaving no ambiguity about what falls inside or outside the agreement. An hourly rate alone says nothing about the actual scope of work being purchased. A vague promise to keep equipment running invites disputes the moment something unexpected fails outside the technician's control. Refrigerant brand has no bearing on defining the scope of a maintenance agreement.
  35. The correct answer is C (Rotary compressor, using an eccentric rolling piston within the cylinder housing): rotary compressors use a rolling piston or sliding vane rotating eccentrically within the cylinder to compress refrigerant, offering compact size and continuous rotation well suited to small capacity applications like window air conditioners. Screw compressors are reserved for much larger industrial ranges. Reciprocating compressors use a piston and connecting rod assembly rather than a rolling eccentric mechanism, and scroll compressors typically start at capacities above this small application range.
  36. The correct answer is B (R-454B has a much lower global warming potential than R-410A while performing similarly): R-454B was developed specifically to deliver a much lower global warming potential than R-410A while keeping similar cooling and heating performance, which is the main environmental driver behind the refrigerant transition. Calling R-454B nonflammable is incorrect since it carries the A2L mildly flammable classification. Refrigerant selection is driven mainly by regulation and environmental impact rather than manufacturing cost, and every refrigerant, including R-454B, still follows a pressure-temperature relationship.
  37. The correct answer is B (The small riser is sized to hold adequate oil-carrying velocity at minimum load only): The small riser in a double-riser arrangement is deliberately undersized relative to full-load flow so that even at the compressor's lowest unloaded step, gas velocity through it stays high enough to sweep oil upward. The large riser stays closed off by its own trapped oil seal at minimum load and only opens to carry additional flow once capacity rises enough to lift that oil column.
  38. The correct answer is D (Allow the room to ventilate and calibrate the detector before starting.): Mechanical rooms can accumulate background refrigerant or other volatile compounds from nearby equipment, and both can cause false positive readings on a sensitive electronic detector. Ventilating the space and calibrating the detector against a known reference before searching establishes a clean baseline so a genuine leak signal can be trusted. Running the detector near combustion sources can damage the sensor or produce misleading readings, and skipping calibration undermines the accuracy of every reading taken afterward.
  39. The correct answer is B (To let system pressures equalize before the compressor restarts under load): when a compressor shuts off, high side and low side pressures need time to equalize before the compressor tries to start against them again. Restarting too soon forces the motor to start against high head pressure, which sharply increases starting torque requirements and can trip overloads or damage the motor over time. The anti-short-cycle delay simply holds the restart command until enough time has passed for pressures to balance. It has no direct connection to capacitor discharge, fan speed, or thermostat display behavior.
  40. The correct answer is C (Cylinder unloading, which deactivates one or more cylinders to reduce compressor capacity in steps): cylinder unloading is a common capacity control feature on multi-cylinder reciprocating compressors, using unloader valves or similar mechanisms to hold specific cylinder suction valves open, effectively deactivating those cylinders so they contribute no compression while the remaining cylinders continue operating normally. This reduces overall compressor capacity in discrete steps that correspond to the number of cylinders unloaded, allowing the compressor to better match reduced load without cycling fully off. Superheat and subcooling adjustments are separate system parameters and are not the mechanism used for reciprocating compressor capacity staging. While suction throttling exists as a technique, it is not the sole method used, and cylinder unloading is the more common and more efficient approach on multi-cylinder equipment.
  41. The correct answer is C (It prevents refrigerant migration into the evaporator and floodback on restart): Without a liquid line solenoid, refrigerant vapor can migrate into a colder idle evaporator during the off cycle and condense there, then return to the compressor as a liquid slug when that circuit restarts. Closing the solenoid the moment the thermostat is satisfied isolates the evaporator from the liquid line, preventing this migration and protecting the compressor from a damaging liquid return.
  42. The correct answer is D (The trap connecting the two risers was sized or installed incorrectly, preventing flow diversion): a double riser arrangement relies on a properly sized and positioned trap at the base of the risers to divert refrigerant flow into the smaller riser once flow drops at reduced load, keeping velocity in that smaller pipe high enough for oil return. If the trap is sized or installed incorrectly, refrigerant may continue to favour the larger riser even at part load, leaving the smaller riser inactive and defeating the entire purpose of the double riser design. Condenser sizing and evaporator superheat setting do not control which riser carries flow at part load, since that behaviour depends on the trap and riser piping geometry itself. This points directly to a piping installation or design error at the trap rather than an equipment selection issue.
  43. The correct answer is D (Briefly crack the cylinder valve to purge air from the hose fittings): Cracking the cylinder valve briefly allows a small burst of refrigerant to push the air trapped inside the hose out through a loosened fitting before the connection is fully tightened, which prevents that air from entering the evacuated system. Option A introduces compressed shop air directly into the hoses, which is the opposite of what is needed before charging a clean system. Option B is a leak testing method, not a technique for purging air from hoses. Option C leaves the hose open to atmosphere, which does nothing to remove trapped air before connection.
  44. Option A encourages rushing through a hazard rather than addressing it, which is not a legal right and increases risk.   45. The correct answer is C (A1, lower toxicity with no flame propagation): under the ASHRAE 34 safety group system referenced by CSA B52, the A1 classification specifically means lower toxicity and no flame propagation observed under standard test conditions, making it the group with the fewest handling restrictions related to flammability. B2L and A2L both involve mild flammability, which does not match a refrigerant described as showing no flame propagation. B1 involves higher toxicity, which also does not match the lower toxicity description given.
  46. The correct answer is A (adequate service clearance is left on the side with the filter access and coil connections): air handling units require ongoing service such as filter changes, coil cleaning and future refrigerant connection work, so leaving the manufacturer's specified clearance on the access side is necessary before the unit becomes difficult to reach once piping and ductwork are finished. Pushing the unit flush against every wall can block filter access and make coil or piping service impossible without disassembly. Clearance needs typically extend beyond just the electrical disconnect, since piping and coil access are just as important for ongoing maintenance, so limiting the requirement to the disconnect alone understates what is actually needed.
  47. The correct answer is A (Test continuity between the equipment cabinet and the panel ground bar): with power safely disconnected, a continuity test between the equipment's metal cabinet and the grounding bar in the electrical panel confirms an intact, low resistance bonding path exists. A reading of infinite resistance or a very high value indicates a broken or missing ground connection that must be corrected before the unit is placed back in service. Voltage or current measurements are not the correct method for verifying this type of passive continuity path.
  48. The correct answer is C (The refrigerant is saturated liquid at its boiling point, at risk of flashing to vapour): When the liquid line temperature equals the saturation temperature at the high side pressure, the refrigerant sits exactly on the saturation curve with zero subcooling, meaning it is saturated liquid that can flash into vapour from any small pressure drop or heat gain downstream. Positive subcooling would instead show the liquid line temperature below the saturation temperature. Refrigerant does not become solid inside a liquid line under normal operating pressures.
  49. The correct answer is C (Recalculate the estimated charge using the actual measured liquid line length before ordering refrigerant): the volume of liquid refrigerant held in the liquid line is directly proportional to its length and internal diameter, so a 15 metre increase in actual line length meaningfully increases the required charge compared to the original drawing based estimate. Recalculating with the actual measured length before ordering refrigerant ensures the correct quantity is on hand for commissioning without relying on a trial and error top up. Assuming negligible effect or that longer lines hold less refrigerant is technically incorrect and could leave the system undercharged. Waiting to discover an undercharge through sight glass flashing during commissioning wastes time and delays project completion.
  50. The correct answer is D (Heat buildup at the contacts that can eventually cause a failure to close.): A measurable voltage drop across closed contacts indicates resistance at the contact surface caused by pitting and burning, and that resistance generates heat every time current flows through it under load. Over time this heat accelerates further contact degradation until the contactor can weld shut, fail to pull in, or fail to make a solid connection at all, leaving the compressor without power when called. This is an electrical wear mechanism, not a refrigerant leak path, and it degrades rather than improves electrical performance over time.
  51. The correct answer is A (A triple evacuation with nitrogen break between pulls, dropping to 500 microns each time): pulling vacuum to 500 microns, breaking it with dry nitrogen to atmospheric pressure, then repeating this cycle two more times dilutes and removes residual moisture far more effectively than a single deep pull, since trapped moisture in oil and metal surfaces releases more readily between breaks. A single ten-minute pull regardless of the reading achieved does not verify actual dryness. Pressurizing with refrigerant before evacuation risks contaminating the vacuum pump, and nitrogen purging during brazing does not eliminate the separate need for a proper evacuation afterward.
  52. The correct answer is B (Overtightening can crack the flare while undertightening allows it to leak): a flare connection has a narrow torque window where the joint seals properly, since tightening beyond spec can crack or deform the soft copper flare and tightening below spec leaves gaps that leak refrigerant under pressure. Using torque specs does not meaningfully speed up the job as suggested in option A, and even a properly torqued joint still requires a leak check afterward, which rules out option C. Option D is incorrect because torque specification is a real performance requirement, not just a preference.
  53. The correct answer is D (The total heat absorbed during evaporation is approximately 6,771 kilojoules.): Latent heat absorbed during a phase change is calculated by multiplying the mass of the substance by its latent heat value for that specific change of state. Multiplying 3 kilograms of water by 2,257 kilojoules per kilogram of latent heat of vaporization gives a total of 6,771 kilojoules absorbed during the boiling process. This heat is absorbed entirely at a constant 100°C, since no temperature rise occurs while both liquid and vapour phases coexist during the boiling process. This same principle explains why an evaporator coil can absorb very large amounts of heat from a space while the refrigerant temperature inside the coil stays nearly constant.
  54. The correct answer is C (The low pressure switch itself is faulty and tripping prematurely.): When gauges show suction pressure remaining in the normal operating range at the exact moment the low pressure switch opens and drops the contactor, the actual refrigerant pressure does not match the condition the switch is supposed to protect against, pointing to a faulty or misadjusted switch rather than a true low charge condition. If the charge were genuinely low or the evaporator iced over, suction pressure would actually be reading low at that moment, which contradicts the confirmed normal gauge reading. A lost condenser airflow would primarily affect head pressure, not this specific low side trip.
  55. The correct answer is B (The coil manufacturer's published pressure drop versus flow rate performance curve for that coil.): When a calibrated balancing valve with its own flow chart is not available, the coil manufacturer's own pressure drop versus flow performance curve provides the reference needed to convert a measured pressure drop into an actual flow rate through that specific coil. Refrigerant charts, electrical diagrams, and municipal water pressure ratings are unrelated to this calculation. Using the correct coil-specific curve is essential for an accurate flow estimate during commissioning.
  56. The correct answer is C (One BTU is the heat needed to raise one pound of water by one degree Fahrenheit.): The British Thermal Unit is the traditional imperial unit of heat energy used throughout the refrigeration and air conditioning trade, particularly on equipment nameplates and load calculations. It is formally defined as the amount of heat required to raise the temperature of one pound of water by one degree Fahrenheit. This unit forms the basis for the ton of refrigeration, which equals 12,000 BTU per hour. Understanding this base definition helps a technician move confidently between imperial nameplate values and the metric kilowatt figures used in most current specification sheets.
  57. The correct answer is A (Size the riser for minimum vapour velocity of the lowest expected load.): because this system never unloads below its constant full capacity, a single suction riser can be sized using minimum velocity tables for that one operating condition, ensuring vapour moves fast enough to entrain and lift oil back to the compressor. Oversizing the riser to match another line's diameter or an unrelated sizing table would reduce velocity and risk oil logging in the riser. A single riser is appropriate specifically because the load never drops to a point where velocity would fall below the oil carrying minimum.
  58. Option B describes the opposite of what actually happens as the angle widens. Option C is false since sling angle is one of the most significant factors affecting rigging capacity calculations.   60.   61. The correct answer is C (To limit pressure drop and maintain velocity high enough to return oil to the compressor): proper suction line sizing exists to balance two goals at once, keeping pressure drop low enough to avoid unnecessarily reducing compressor capacity and increasing compressor work, while keeping velocity high enough across the expected range of operation to carry entrained oil back to the compressor. Maximizing velocity without regard for pressure drop increases compressor work and noise, and minimizing cost by ignoring performance risks both inadequate capacity and oil return failures. Refrigerant lines are not sized identically across a system, since suction, discharge, and liquid lines each carry refrigerant under different conditions and require different diameters. Correct suction line sizing is therefore a deliberate balance, not a single maximized or minimized variable.
  62. The correct answer is C (The reading is higher than expected, suggesting air or another non-condensable gas is present): With the system off and fully equalized to the 24°C shop temperature, the pressure should settle very close to the 1480 kPa saturation pressure listed on the R-410A chart for that temperature, but the gauge in the diagram reads noticeably higher at 1650 kPa. This system would need to be properly evacuated with a vacuum pump before being charged, rather than simply topped up with more refrigerant.

  65. The correct answer is A (The compressor, because it is no longer producing a real pressure difference between the two lines): The compressor's basic job in the cycle is to create a pressure difference between the low side and the high side by pumping vapour from suction to discharge, so nearly equal readings on both gauges point to internal valve or reed damage that lets vapour leak straight through. A failed condenser fan or restricted evaporator airflow would change temperatures and superheat or subcooling values, but would not by itself equalize the two pressures like this.   66. A shorted winding is an electrical fault and would not produce this pattern of noise fading with runtime. A fan motor bearing issue would not transmit as compressor knocking tied to cold startup.   68. Option A overstates flux as mandatory in every case, contradicting the self fluxing property. Option B is wrong since flux never substitutes for proper mechanical cleaning of the joint surfaces. Option D misstates the purpose of flux, which manages oxidation, not the melting point of the base metal.
  69. The correct answer is A (Excessively low superheat and potential liquid flooding back to the compressor.): A fixed orifice sized larger than specified allows more refrigerant mass flow into the evaporator than the coil can fully vaporize under normal load, so liquid refrigerant reaches the far end of the coil and even the suction line. This shows up as abnormally low or even zero superheat, and in severe cases liquid can carry all the way back to the compressor. Compressor amperage typically rises rather than falls under this flooded condition, not the reverse. This is the opposite failure mode of an undersized orifice.
  70. The correct answer is B (Water-cooled shell and tube condenser served by the cooling tower loop): since a cooling tower is already planned for the building, a water-cooled shell and tube condenser can take direct advantage of that infrastructure to achieve consistently low and stable condensing temperatures across the operating season, improving chiller efficiency. An air-cooled condenser would not use the available tower and typically runs at higher condensing temperatures in warm weather. An evaporative or remote air-cooled option would duplicate infrastructure the building already provides through the tower loop.

  72. Increasing compressor displacement does not address pressure drop in the liquid line itself.   73.
