  74. The correct answer is A (Ream the cut end to remove the burr, preventing restriction and turbulence): A cutter always rolls a small burr inward as it scores and snaps the copper, and that burr narrows the bore right at the joint. Left in place it creates turbulence, adds pressure drop, and can trap oil or debris that eventually contaminates the metering device or compressor. A deburring tool or reamer clears it in seconds before the joint is fluxed and assembled.
  75. The correct answer is A (Liquid refrigerant may reach the compressor and cause damaging liquid slugging): Extremely low superheat means the evaporator coil is not fully boiling off the liquid refrigerant before it leaves the coil, so any drop in load or further reduction in superheat can send liquid refrigerant straight into the suction line and eventually the compressor. A compressor is built to pump vapour, not liquid, and liquid slugging can bend connecting rods, break valves, or otherwise destroy the compressor in a single event. Frost forming well before the suction line reaches the compressor is a visible warning sign of this exact condition.
  76. The correct answer is B (Route piping and locate joints away from unenclosed ignition sources per the equipment and code guidance): because A2L refrigerants carry a mild flammability classification, design guidance calls for keeping refrigerant piping joints, which are the most likely leak points, away from unenclosed ignition sources such as exposed electrical contacts, consistent with equipment listing and applicable code direction. Placing joints closer to electrical panels increases risk rather than reducing it. Treating A2L identically to non-flammable A1 ignores its classification, and deferring the review to another trade does not fulfill the mechanic's design responsibility.
  77. The correct answer is C (Issue a request for information to the design team to resolve the discrepancy): Issue a request for information to the design team to resolve the discrepancy. A mismatch between the equipment schedule and the floor plan tag is a drawing conflict that must be formally resolved by the design team through a request for information, since guessing which document is correct can lead to installing the wrong capacity or configuration of equipment. Neither the schedule nor the floor plan automatically takes precedence without confirmation, and ordering duplicate equipment or assuming a typo both risk cost and delay if the guess is wrong. Formal clarification protects the contractor and ensures the installed equipment matches the actual design intent.
  78. The correct answer is A (Heat that changes the temperature of a substance without changing its physical state): Sensible heat is measured with a thermometer because it produces a temperature change you can read directly, such as warming liquid refrigerant before it boils. Latent heat, by contrast, is the heat absorbed or released during a change of state, like boiling or condensing, at a constant temperature. A technician who confuses the two will misjudge how much heat a coil is actually removing from an airstream.
  79. The correct answer is B (The push-pull liquid recovery method using the system's own pressure differential.): The push-pull method uses the pressure differential between the system and the recovery cylinder to move liquid refrigerant quickly through the recovery machine, making it the most efficient choice for large liquid charges under pressure. Vapour-only recovery is far slower for bulk liquid removal. Venting is prohibited outright, and abandoning the liquid portion would leave a substantial charge unrecovered in violation of regulation.
  80. The correct answer is C (Superheat is below target, suggesting possible overcharge, so refrigerant should be carefully recovered): superheat here is 13°C minus 10°C, which equals 3°C, sitting below the 8°C to 12°C target range for this fixed-orifice system. Low superheat on this type of metering device generally points to too much refrigerant reaching the evaporator, so the correct response is to recover a small measured amount and recheck rather than assume the process is finished. Discharge line temperature is a useful compressor health indicator but is not required to interpret this specific superheat calculation against its target range.
  81. The correct answer is B (A temperature indicating controller that reads and controls a temperature control loop): on a piping and instrumentation diagram, instrument bubbles use letter codes to identify the variable being measured and the function performed. TIC stands for temperature indicating controller, meaning the device reads a temperature signal and actively controls part of the process based on that reading, rather than simply displaying a value. Manual isolation valves, pressure relief valves, and sight glasses each have their own distinct standardized symbols, none of which use the TIC letter code. Correctly reading these symbols is essential before a mechanic begins tracing control logic on an unfamiliar drawing.
  82. The correct answer is D (mechanical fittings can be disassembled for future service without cutting into the line): a component like a thermostatic expansion valve is sometimes replaced during the life of a system, and a flare or other mechanical connection at that point allows a mechanic to disconnect and reconnect it without cutting the tube and making a new brazed joint. A properly made brazed joint is generally stronger and more leak resistant over time than a mechanical fitting, not the reverse. Mechanical fittings still require periodic leak inspection since they can loosen with thermal cycling, so claims of no future inspection and no future leak testing both describe advantages that do not exist.
  83. The correct answer is B (A flare fitting can be disconnected and reconnected for future service access.): a mechanical flare fitting can be loosened and reassembled during future service, such as replacing a compressor or valve, without cutting the line and rebrazing a new joint. A brazed joint is permanent and must be cut apart for component replacement. Flare fittings are not inherently rated for higher pressure than a properly brazed joint, and both joint types still require leak checking and appropriate line insulation regardless of connection method.
  84. The correct answer is C (dry nitrogen only, because oxygen is an explosion hazard and compressed air introduces moisture and oil): oxygen must never be used to pressurize a refrigeration system because it can react violently with oil residue inside the piping, creating a serious explosion hazard. Shop compressed air is not inert, typically carries moisture and can carry oil from the compressor, both of which work against the later evacuation and dehydration steps. Dry nitrogen is inert, moisture free, and safe to use for pressure testing at the required test pressures, which is why it is the standard choice. Compressed air is not equivalent to nitrogen simply because both are gases, so treating the two as interchangeable does not hold up.
  85. The correct answer is C (Nothing about which refrigerant is in each cylinder, since both use the same recovery colour scheme.): Recovery cylinders all use the same conventional grey body with yellow shoulder regardless of the refrigerant recovered inside them, so colour provides no information about actual contents. The technician must read the attached tag or label, which records the specific refrigerant recovered, to correctly identify each cylinder before use. This is especially critical when one cylinder contains a mildly flammable A2L refrigerant and the other does not.
  86. The correct answer is D (A fixed orifice cannot self-adjust flow, so target superheat varies with load and airflow.): Unlike a TXV, a fixed orifice or piston has a set opening that cannot respond to changing conditions, so as outdoor and indoor wet bulb temperatures shift the evaporator load and refrigerant flow rate, the resulting superheat naturally shifts too. Manufacturers publish a target superheat chart cross-referencing both temperatures so the technician can find the correct target for that day's actual conditions rather than a single fixed number. This chart applies to fixed-orifice systems generally, not only blend refrigerants, and both indoor and outdoor conditions do influence suction-side superheat.
  87. The correct answer is D (Reclaimed refrigerant must be verified against AHRI purity specification before resale as reclaimed product.): Laboratory analysis is a required part of reclamation because refrigerant sold as reclaimed product must be verified to meet AHRI purity specification, not simply assumed to be clean based on appearance or cylinder markings. This requirement is not limited to refrigerant recovered from residential systems. Skipping this analysis would mean reselling refrigerant without the documented purity basis that reclamation status legally requires.
  88. The correct answer is A (The heater loses power at all times, even when the compressor is off): The heater loses power at all times, even when the compressor is off. Since this rung depends on a normally closed auxiliary contact that only carries power when it is physically closed, a contact stuck in the open position breaks that rung permanently regardless of whether the compressor contactor is energized or not. This leaves the crankcase heater without power at all times, including during the off cycle when it is most needed to keep refrigerant from migrating into and diluting the compressor oil. A technician troubleshooting a nuisance compressor failure after long off cycles should check this auxiliary contact for proper operation.
  89. The correct answer is B (Storage area ventilation and ignition source control appropriate for a mildly flammable refrigerant.): Because R-454B is an A2L mildly flammable refrigerant, its storage area specifically requires attention to ventilation and control of ignition sources that would not be a concern for A1 nonflammable cylinders stored nearby. Cylinder colour and weight limits are not driven by the A2L classification in this way. Treating A2L and A1 refrigerants as having identical hazards ignores the flammability distinction entirely.
  90. The correct answer is D (At both the regulator outlets and the torch handle inlets): installing flashback arrestors at both the regulator end and the torch handle end provides protection against a flame or pressure wave traveling backward through the hose from either direction, which is the accepted standard setup. Option A is incorrect because oxygen fed flashbacks are a real and serious hazard, not just fuel gas ones. Option B only protects at one end and leaves the regulators and cylinders exposed, and option C is wrong because arrestor placement at the connection points, not the middle of a hose run, is what actually provides protection.
  91. The correct answer is B (The motor speed drops to approximately 1800 RPM at 30 Hz): synchronous motor speed is proportional to supply frequency, so halving the frequency from 60 Hz to 30 Hz proportionally halves the synchronous speed from 3600 RPM down to approximately 1800 RPM. This direct relationship between frequency and speed is exactly what allows a variable frequency drive to modulate compressor capacity smoothly instead of relying only on fixed-speed cycling. The actual operating speed under load will be slightly below the calculated synchronous value due to normal motor slip. Frequency and speed always move in the same direction together, never opposite directions.
  92. The correct answer is B (The blend's composition has shifted due to fractionation from vapour charging, changing its behavior.): Drawing refrigerant off as vapour from a cylinder containing a zeotropic blend allows the more volatile component to boil off preferentially, gradually shifting the ratio of components remaining in both the cylinder and the charge already placed into the system. This process, called fractionation, means the refrigerant now circulating in the system no longer matches the pressure-temperature relationship published for the correctly proportioned blend. As a result, pressure and temperature readings taken during service will appear inconsistent with the manufacturer's standard chart values, confusing diagnosis if the technician does not recognize the root cause. The correct fix involves fully recovering the mismatched charge and recharging the system with a fresh, properly proportioned blend drawn as liquid from the start.
  93. The correct answer is A (the valve's internal plunger and coil rely on the specified orientation to seat and operate correctly): many refrigerant solenoid valves depend on gravity acting on an internal plunger or piston to fully seat and seal when de-energized, which is why manufacturers commonly specify an upright coil orientation. Mounting the same valve on its side can prevent the internal moving parts from returning fully to the closed position, allowing refrigerant to migrate through during the pump-down cycle. Orientation sensitivity is a real and commonly documented limitation for many solenoid valve designs, so treating solenoid valves as never orientation sensitive does not hold up. The valve's position relative to the filter drier does not explain a failure to close, and orientation affects mechanical seating, not coil resistance.
  94. The correct answer is A (The total subcooling present in this circuit is approximately 6°C.): Subcooling is found by subtracting the actual liquid line temperature from the saturated condensing temperature indicated by the high side pressure. Subtracting 41°C from 47°C gives a subcooling value of 6°C, confirming the refrigerant leaving the condenser is fully liquid and slightly below its saturation point before reaching the metering device. This measured value should then be compared against the manufacturer's specified subcooling target for that particular system and metering device type. A subcooling reading that is significantly lower or higher than the target range points toward a charge problem or a restriction that needs further diagnosis.
  95. The correct answer is D (Chemical splash goggles or a face shield rated for chemical exposure): Flux paste and any residual acidic contamination pose a chemical splash risk to the eyes and face during application, so protection specifically rated for chemical exposure is required before any heat source is even introduced. Option A offers no side or splash protection and would not stop flux from reaching the eyes. Option B is designed to filter intense arc light, not to block chemical splash, and provides the wrong type of protection entirely. Option C offers no eye protection at all and a dust mask does not address a liquid splash hazard.
  96. The correct answer is A (The type and quantity of refrigerant recovered or added during the visit): Refrigerant handling regulations require technicians to document the type and amount of refrigerant recovered from or added to a system during any service visit, since this information supports emissions tracking and cylinder inventory records. Option B records travel information that has no connection to refrigerant handling compliance. Option C lists tool brands, which is irrelevant to any regulatory documentation requirement. Option D records weather conditions, which may be noted informally but is not a required refrigerant tracking data point on a service report.
  97. The correct answer is B (The G terminal controls the indoor blower fan relay directly): The G terminal controls the indoor blower fan relay directly. When the thermostat fan switch is set to ON, the G terminal stays energized continuously, keeping the indoor blower relay closed regardless of any heating or cooling call. In AUTO mode, G only energizes together with a heating or cooling call, or briefly afterward. The outdoor condenser fan and reversing valve are controlled by separate circuits unrelated to the G terminal.
  98. The correct answer is C (10°C of superheat, found by subtracting negative 2°C from the suction line temperature of 8°C): Superheat is the actual suction line temperature minus the saturated evaporating temperature, so 8°C minus negative 2°C equals 10°C once the negative sign is correctly handled through subtraction. A common arithmetic mistake here is adding the two values instead of subtracting a negative number, which would produce an incorrect result. This 10°C reading tells the technician the evaporator is boiling off refrigerant well before the suction line and building a normal amount of superheat afterward.
  99. The correct answer is D (The calibration must be traceable to a recognized national or international measurement standard): Acceptable calibration documentation must show traceability back to a recognized national or international measurement standard, which is what allows the reading to be trusted and defended if the report is later questioned. The person using the instrument does not need to be the same person who calibrated it. Calibration intervals are set by the manufacturer or instrument type, not a fixed one week rule for every instrument. A verbal agreement provides no documented traceability and would not satisfy a formal commissioning record.
  100. The correct answer is C (Approximately 7.8 kilowatts of cooling capacity from latent heat alone): Capacity from latent heat equals mass flow rate multiplied by the latent heat of vaporization, so 0.04 kg/s times 195 kJ/kg equals 7.8 kW. The distractor values come from halving the mass flow rate or misapplying only part of the given latent heat figure. This calculation ignores any sensible heat picked up before or after the boiling process inside the coil.
  101. The correct answer is B (Weigh-in by mass, using a calibrated electronic refrigerant scale referenced to the nameplate charge): weighing in the exact factory charge listed on the nameplate is the most accurate way to restore a known critical charge after a full recovery. Superheat and subcooling methods are excellent for verifying and fine-tuning a charge but are normally used to check an existing charge rather than establish a first-fill mass from empty. A clear sight glass can be misleading on some systems and is not a substitute for a calibrated scale reading.
  102. The correct answer is C (Immerse the probe in a properly prepared ice water bath and confirm it reads 0 C): A properly prepared ice water bath, made with finely crushed ice and just enough water to cover it, is a reliable and repeatable 0 C reference for checking a thermometer's accuracy in the field. If the probe reads noticeably above or below 0 C, the offset must be accounted for or the instrument must be replaced before it is trusted for commissioning data. Comparing to an unrelated device like a phone weather app provides no verified reference point at all.
  103. The correct answer is B (The normally closed contact opens, disconnecting the start capacitor from the circuit): this arrangement describes a current-operated start relay, where the relay coil is wired in series with the run winding and senses current directly. As the motor accelerates toward running speed, current through the run winding rung drops, the relay coil de-energizes, and its normally closed contact opens on the start winding rung. This removes the start capacitor and start winding from the circuit once they are no longer needed, protecting the start capacitor from continuous duty it is not rated for. Leaving the start capacitor connected after startup would quickly overheat and destroy it.
  104. The correct answer is C (The expansion valve was stuck open and responded correctly once the bulb reacted): Near-zero superheat with high suction pressure and heavy suction line frost is the classic flooding pattern, and cooling the sensing bulb is the standard diagnostic test to confirm whether an overfeeding expansion valve can still close. A prompt drop in suction pressure after cooling the bulb confirms the valve mechanism can still move and was simply stuck in an overly open position rather than mechanically seized or damaged. Confirmed normal airflow and charge rule out an airflow or charge-related explanation for the flooding. Because the valve responded to the test, cleaning or replacing the valve and checking for debris holding it open is the appropriate next step.
  105. The correct answer is B (Fiberglass absorbs moisture readily and loses its insulating value once wet): Unlike closed-cell elastomeric foam, fiberglass has an open fibrous structure that wicks and holds moisture if water vapor reaches it, and wet fiberglass insulates poorly while also promoting corrosion under insulation on the tube surface. A continuous, well-sealed vapor barrier jacket is what keeps humid air from ever reaching the fiberglass on a line running below the surrounding dew point.
  106. The correct answer is B (The arrow must point in the same direction as the intended refrigerant flow.): the arrow stamped on a filter drier housing indicates the intended direction of refrigerant flow through the internal filtering and desiccant material, so the mechanic must install the drier with that arrow pointing the same direction the liquid refrigerant will actually flow, from the receiver or condenser toward the expansion valve. Installing it backward can restrict flow or reduce filtering effectiveness, since many driers are designed for flow in one direction only. The arrow is not related to compressor suction connections and does matter on liquid line driers specifically.
  107. The correct answer is C (Non-condensables are removed through purging as part of reclamation processing and testing.): Non-condensables are removed through purging as part of reclamation processing and testing. AHRI Standard 700 sets limits on non-condensable gas content, so a licensed reclaimer purges trapped air and other non-condensables from the refrigerant during processing and then verifies the result through laboratory analysis. Simply diluting contaminated refrigerant with virgin product does not reliably bring it into compliance and is not an accepted reclamation method. Non-condensables do not settle out on their own and must be actively removed and tested for.
  108. The correct answer is C (Providing water treatment and bleed off to control mineral scaling and biological growth in the sump): an evaporative condenser sprays recirculating water over the coil while air is drawn through it, and evaporation of that water is what allows it to condense at a lower temperature than an air-cooled unit exposed to the same dry bulb air. Because the water recirculates and concentrates minerals as it evaporates, the sump requires ongoing water treatment and a bleed off connection to prevent scale buildup and control biological growth such as legionella. Evaporative condensers are sized against wet bulb temperature rather than dry bulb alone, and they still rely on fan motors to move air across the wetted coil. Their filled sump also adds weight that must be accounted for in structural design.
  109. The correct answer is A (Closing the king valve isolates the stored charge for a system pump down.): closing the king valve at the receiver outlet allows the mechanic to perform a pump down, running the compressor briefly to draw refrigerant from the low side back into the receiver and condenser where it becomes trapped once the valve is closed, isolating the bulk of the charge there so downstream components can be safely opened for service with minimal refrigerant loss. The king valve has no electrical function and does not control flow into the receiver from the condenser side, nor does it bypass the filter drier.
  110. The correct answer is D (Both apply together, and the technician must meet whichever requirement is more stringent.): Both apply together, and the technician must meet whichever requirement is more stringent. Provincial trade certification and each province's own halocarbon or environmental protection regulation set the day-to-day baseline for refrigerant recovery, the venting prohibition, and general handling within that province, while the federal Ozone-depleting Substances and Halocarbon Alternatives Regulations made under CEPA 1999 separately control manufacture, import and export nationally, and the Federal Halocarbon Regulations apply on federal and Indigenous lands. Where any of these requirements overlap or differ, the technician must comply with whichever standard imposes the stricter requirement rather than picking the more convenient one. None of these rules cancels out the others, and all remain legally binding at the same time.
  111. The correct answer is B (Automatic calculation of superheat and subcooling directly from live pressure and temperature probes): a digital manifold gauge set typically pairs pressure transducers with clamp-on temperature probes and computes superheat and subcooling automatically on its display, saving the technician from manually cross-referencing a printed pressure-temperature chart. It does not eliminate the need to purge hoses of air and moisture before connecting, and refrigerant still has to move through an open service valve regardless of which gauge set is used. Refrigerant compatibility on a digital unit still depends on its stored refrigerant tables being current and correctly selected.
  112. The correct answer is C (Label it clearly as mixed, unknown composition, and route it for proper specialized disposal.): A cylinder holding refrigerant of unknown or mixed composition must be labeled accurately as such rather than guessed at, since an incorrect label could cause serious harm if the contents are later charged into an incompatible system. It should be routed to a facility equipped to handle unidentified or mixed refrigerant, which may involve specialized disposal rather than standard reclamation. Venting the contents or diluting them into a known virgin cylinder are both prohibited and unsafe responses to an identification problem.
  113. The correct answer is C (Removing refrigerant from a system and storing it in an approved container without required purity.): Recovery is the removal of refrigerant from a system into an approved container, without any required purity standard being met at that stage. Reclamation, by contrast, reprocesses refrigerant off site to a published industry purity standard, and recycling reduces contaminants on site to a lesser standard than reclamation. Incineration describes neither recovery nor either of the other two related processes.
  114. The correct answer is D (The quantity of refrigerant recovered and added, and the cylinder identification used): Environmental compliance recordkeeping expects documentation of refrigerant quantities recovered and added along with the cylinder used, so refrigerant movement can be traced and verified. Option A captures billing information but nothing about refrigerant handling. Option B identifies the equipment but not what happened to the refrigerant charge. Option C records logistics information that has no bearing on refrigerant compliance.
  115. The correct answer is D (1.2 metres of actual clearance between the condensing unit and the wall): a scale of 1:50 means every unit measured on the drawing represents 50 of the same unit in actual size. Multiplying the measured 24 millimetres by 50 gives 1200 millimetres, which converts to 1.2 metres of real clearance on site. Misreading a drawing scale is a common source of clearance errors that only surface during installation, when equipment does not actually fit as planned. Always confirm the stated scale before taking any dimension from a mechanical drawing.
  116. The correct answer is C (Zero the instrument according to the manufacturer's procedure before taking any readings): A digital manometer showing a nonzero value with both hoses open to atmosphere has an offset error that will carry through every reading taken until it is corrected. Most models include a simple zeroing function that must be performed before use, per the manufacturer's instructions. Ignoring the offset, manually adding it back later, or assuming a dead battery are not reliable substitutes for properly zeroing the instrument.
  117. The correct answer is B (To provide a low impedance path that clears a fault to ground safely): under CSA C22.1, bonding a metal equipment enclosure back to the grounding electrode system creates a low impedance path so that if a live conductor accidentally contacts the enclosure, enough fault current flows to operate the branch circuit protection quickly rather than energizing the enclosure indefinitely. This protects both technicians and building occupants from shock hazard. Grounding and bonding have no effect on running amperage or coil voltage, and they do not remove the separate requirement for an equipment disconnecting means.
  118. The correct answer is D (The refrigerant has already fully vaporized, so the added heat only raises its temperature): Once refrigerant has fully vaporized inside the evaporator, any further heat absorbed only raises the vapour's temperature since there is no liquid left to change state, which is the definition of sensible heat. If liquid droplets remained, that heat would instead be latent heat driving the phase change at constant temperature. Sensible heat is not restricted to any single component and pressure does not convert one heat type into the other.
  119. The correct answer is C (Flash gas occurs because a portion of liquid self-cools by boiling off during the pressure drop.): As refrigerant passes through the metering device, its pressure drops sharply and its saturation temperature drops along with it, but the liquid entering the device is still at the warmer, higher pressure saturation temperature from the condenser. Because the liquid is momentarily too warm for the new lower pressure, a portion of it immediately boils off into vapour, and the latent heat absorbed by that boiling cools the remaining liquid down to the new, lower saturation temperature. This self-cooling process is what produces flash gas, and it is a completely normal part of a properly operating system rather than a fault. Excessive flash gas, however, can reduce the evaporator's effective cooling capacity, which is why adequate subcooling entering the metering device is important to minimize it.
  120. The correct answer is A (Modulating control, because it can track a smoothly varying load without discrete capacity jumps): Modulating control, because it can track a smoothly varying load without discrete capacity jumps. A modulating compressor, whether through a variable frequency drive or a slide valve mechanism, can continuously adjust output to closely track a smoothly and continuously varying process load, avoiding the discrete capacity steps and associated temperature or pressure swings that staged on/off compressors produce as they cycle between fixed capacity levels. Staged control works well for loads that change in coarser steps but is a poorer match for a smoothly varying process load, the opposite of what option B claims. Capacity control strategy has a real and direct effect on process stability and energy use, and load calculation is still required regardless of which control strategy is chosen.
  121. The correct answer is B (The calculated purge rate will be too low if the room has significant ceiling height): the CSA B52 emergency purge formula is based on the actual internal volume of the machine room, and substituting floor area ignores ceiling height, which understates the true air volume that must be exchanged, resulting in an undersized and non-compliant ventilation design in any room with above-average ceiling height. The error does not automatically match the correct value. It affects the airflow rate calculation itself, not merely the electrical circuit sizing, and it does not automatically produce an oversized system since floor area alone typically understates true volume.
  122. The correct answer is B (The Canadian Environmental Protection Act, 1999, a federal statute): CEPA 1999 is the federal statute that gives Environment and Climate Change Canada authority to make the Ozone-depleting Substances and Halocarbon Alternatives Regulations, which control the manufacture, import and export of these substances nationally, and the Federal Halocarbon Regulations, which prohibit venting and require recovery specifically on federal and Indigenous lands and for federally owned equipment. CEPA 1999 is not, however, the source of a single nationwide venting prohibition covering every private-sector service call; each province and territory enacts its own halocarbon or environmental protection regulation requiring recovery and prohibiting venting for the refrigeration and air conditioning equipment within its jurisdiction. Option A incorrectly names a provincial statute as the source of the federal authority being asked about here. Option C describes a construction reference with no regulatory authority over refrigerant venting. Option D governs the transport of dangerous goods, not the venting prohibition itself.
  123. The correct answer is A (Superheat is 8°C, close to target, and charging can be considered complete.): The gauge reads 894 kPa, corresponding to a saturated evaporating temperature of 7°C on the R-410A chart. Subtracting that from the measured suction line temperature of 15°C gives a superheat of 8°C, which falls within the acceptable 8 to 12°C band around the 10°C target. Since the reading is inside the acceptable range, no further refrigerant needs to be added or removed at this point. The other options either misread the gauge or overstate how far off the reading is.
  124. The correct answer is C (Replace the stuck relay so the compressor circuit opens properly when satisfied): A relay contact welded closed keeps current flowing to the contactor coil regardless of what the thermostat is doing downstream, which explains why the compressor keeps running after the call for cooling ends. Recalibrating the thermostat would not correct a mechanically stuck contact further along the circuit, and a weak capacitor affects starting torque, not continuous unwanted running. A fouled condenser coil would reduce efficiency but would not prevent the unit from shutting off entirely. Replacing the failed relay restores proper control of the compressor circuit.
  125. The correct answer is B (a clear, bubble-free stream of liquid and a moisture indicator color matching the dry reference): a properly charged system with dry refrigerant shows a solid, clear column of liquid at the sight glass rather than bubbles, since bubbles at that point usually indicate flash gas from a low charge or a restriction upstream. The built-in moisture indicator changes color based on moisture content in the refrigerant, and it should settle at the color the manufacturer designates as dry once the drier has had time to work. Continuous bubbling is a symptom to investigate, not a confirmation of correct charge, and an empty sight glass at this location would indicate a serious problem rather than normal operation.
